## 2 Chronicles 1:1–6 (id: eng-2ch-p1-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 1:1–6 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 1:1–6 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 1:1–6 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

Solomon, the son of King David, became the new king of Israel after David died. This story happens soon after David died.

Solomon made himself strong as king and took full control of the kingdom of Israel. King Solomon made himself strong by defeating his enemies to become the only ruler of Israel. King Solomon's older brother, Adonijah, had tried to become king, but Solomon told Solomon's military leader, Benaiah, to kill Adonijah. Solomon also told Benaiah to kill Joab and Shimei. Joab had been King David's military leader and had supported Adonijah when Adonijah rebelled. Shimei was from the tribe of King Saul and had cursed King David when David ran away from Absalom, because Shimei hated David and blamed David for the death of King Saul's sons. Solomon had also banished Abiathar the priest from the priesthood. These men had all been enemies of King David and were dangerous to Solomon now as king. Solomon also began to build a wall around Jerusalem and likely made sure the army stayed loyal to Solomon.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Talk about a person who became the leader of your community or country after that person struggled with other people who also wanted to become the leader. Describe all the things that the new leader did after he became the leader to make sure that he remained the leader and became strong. Pause this audio here.

Solomon became strong because Yahweh, Solomon's God, was with Solomon. Yahweh was the true king of Israel and had chosen Solomon to rule. Yahweh blessed Solomon, protected Solomon, and helped Solomon take full control of the kingdom. Yahweh made Solomon a great and powerful king. Yahweh also gave Solomon wealth and honor during Solomon's rule.

After Solomon took control of the kingdom of Israel, Solomon spoke to the people of Israel. King Solomon called all the people of Israel and their leaders together. It was normal for a king to gather the people for important events. The storyteller lists the leaders to show that all of Israel supported Solomon. Solomon spoke to the army commanders, the judges, and the leaders of Israel who were heads of clans, or groups of families and tribes. The army commanders who led the military of Israel were in charge of groups of 100 and 1,000 soldiers.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Discuss how you organize your warriors and the different levels of people who lead the warriors. Tell of a time when these leaders of the warriors had a disagreement with the great chief of your people. Pause this audio here.

The judges were also leaders in Israel because they were responsible for making sure people followed the rules. The judges did this by listening to the people's problems and deciding what was right according to the law of Israel. The heads of families were wise and important men from each clan or large family group.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Discuss what your community's leadership structure looks like. For example, you may say that fathers lead their homes, clan leaders lead the clans, and the chiefs lead the community. Tell about a time when all these leaders, as well as the people, were gathered together. Pause this audio here.

We do not know what Solomon said in the gathering, but we know what happened next. Solomon and all the people went to the high place at Gibeon. High places were special places where people could worship, with altars for sacrifices. Before the Israelites built the temple, people used high places to worship Yahweh. People went to the high place at Gibeon because the Tent of Meeting that Moses had made was there. Yahweh had told Moses that Yahweh would meet the people there and that the people should bring their sacrifices to the tent.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of the Tent of Meeting. Talk about the place where the people of your community believe they can meet with God, the spirits, or the ancestors. Tell about a time that the leaders of your community went to that place, what they did, and why they did it. Pause this audio here.

The storyteller tells us that the ark of God was not in the Tent of Meeting at Gibeon. The ark was not there because King David had moved the ark from Kiriath Jearim to Jerusalem and placed it in a new tent. Normally, the ark stayed in the Tent of Meeting, but the Philistines had taken the ark of God during a battle. Later, the Philistines returned the ark, and then the people of Beth Shemesh took it and gave it to the people in Kiriath Jearim, where it stayed for 20 years. Then David brought the ark to Jerusalem. Even though the ark of God was not at Gibeon, the bronze altar was still there. This is the same altar that Yahweh had instructed Moses to make for burning sacrifices. Bezalel, the grandson of Hur, is the one who made this altar with the wisdom that Yahweh had given Bezalel.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of the ark of God and the bronze altar. Pause this audio here.

Solomon and the people worshiped Yahweh at the bronze altar. Solomon offered 1,000 burnt offerings. The priests likely killed the animals, cut them up, and burned them completely, except for the skin, which they kept. These offerings showed that the people wanted to dedicate themselves fully to Yahweh. It is likely that the people dedicated their new king, Solomon, to Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Describe the kind of offerings that the people of your community offer God, the spirits, or ancestors to please them. Tell of a time when a leader has offered a large number of sacrifices. Why did the leader do this?

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 1:1–6 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has four scenes.

**In the first scene:** King Solomon took firm control of the kingdom of Israel because Yahweh was with Solomon. Yahweh made Solomon very great.

**In the second scene:** King Solomon spoke to all the people of Israel and to their military leaders, judges, and the heads of the families of Israel.

**In the third scene:** King Solomon and all the people who had gathered went to the high place at Gibeon because the Tent of Meeting that Moses had made was there. The bronze altar that Bezalel, the son of Uri, had made was also there.

**In the fourth scene:** King Solomon and the people of Israel worshipped Yahweh there. And King Solomon offered 1,000 burnt offerings.

The characters in this passage are:

* Yahweh
* King Solomon
* King David
* All the people of Israel
* The military commanders of thousands
* The military commanders of hundreds
* The judges of Israel
* The leading men of Israel who are heads of the families, or clans
* Moses
* Bezalel, the son of Uri and grandson of Hur
* And the priest who offered Solomon's sacrifices—the storyteller does not mention the priests, but they were likely there

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

King David had died, and David's son Solomon had become the new king of Israel. It is not clear how long after King David died this story takes place, but it is not too long. In the first scene, King Solomon strengthened himself over his kingdom, or took firm control of Israel. A king is normally the most powerful man in the kingdom. The king rules with the help of other powerful people, who can be dangerous to the king's rule if they are not loyal to the king. King Solomon strengthened himself over his kingdom by making sure that there was no one to challenge Solomon's rule. Solomon also strengthened himself over Israel by doing things that would make the Kingdom of Israel safe from its enemies. You will remember from the story in 1 Kings that Solomon built the wall of Jerusalem and made a marriage alliance with Pharaoh king of Egypt.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Talk about a leader who ruled your community or country strongly. Talk about how the leader was in control of everything. List the things that the leader did to make himself strong from the day he first became the leader. Pay attention to the words and phrases you use to describe the way the leader made himself strong and took full control. Pause this audio here.

Although Solomon made himself strong over Israel, Solomon was only able to do so because Yahweh, Solomon's God, was with Solomon. When a king was successful, the Israelites said that it was because Yahweh was with that king. Yahweh was the true king of Israel, and Yahweh chose which man would rule as Yahweh's representative. Yahweh had chosen Solomon, and therefore Yahweh protected Solomon and made him able to take control of the kingdom of Israel. Yahweh made Solomon very great. Yahweh had promised that Yahweh would make Solomon great. It is possible that the storyteller is referring to how Yahweh made Solomon great and gave him power and honor throughout Solomon's rule, not just at the beginning of Solomon's rule.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Talk about a person who was very successful. Tell about how God, the spirits, or ancestors protected that person, blessed him, and helped him succeed. What special language would you use to say that God, the spirits, or the ancestors made that person able to become successful? Pause this audio here.

In the second scene, King Solomon spoke to all Israel. It is likely that King Solomon called a great gathering, or assembly, of all the people of Israel. It was common for kings to call for such gatherings at important times, especially at the beginning of the king's rule. The storyteller focuses on the levels of leaders who were there at the gathering. The storyteller likely does this to show that the people of Israel were united and loyal to King Solomon. King Solomon spoke to the leaders of Israel's military. Israel's army had fighting units made up of 1,000 soldiers each. Each unit of 1,000 soldiers had its leader. There were many of these units. Their leaders are the commanders of thousands. The military units of 1,000 were further broken down into units of 100 soldiers, each with its leader. These are the commanders of hundreds. King Solomon also spoke to the judges of Israel. The people often called rulers or leaders of Israel "judges." In this story, it is likely that the judges that King Solomon spoke to are the judges who decided which person was right or wrong when two people or groups had an argument. King Solomon also spoke to all the leading men of Israel who were heads of families. You will remember that there were 12 tribes in Israel, and each tribe was made up of clans or large family groups. From the days of Moses, the Israelites had tribal leaders as well as leaders of clans or large family groups.

Stop here and do this activity as a group: Act out a scene where a military commander has harmed a person or their property. The person whom the commander harmed or his family should go to their clan leader and explain what happened and ask for justice. Their clan leader should then take them before a judge to ask for justice. Pay attention to the words and phrases that you use for military commander, clan leader, and judge. Pause this audio here.

In the third scene, King Solomon and all the people who had gathered went to the high place that was at Gibeon. A high place usually refers to a dedicated place of worship set up on a high place, like a hill or mountain. But many of the high places of Israel were not on hills or mountains. It is better to think of a high place as a shrine or a sacred place of worship.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Describe a physical place that your community or another community you know goes to offer sacrifices. Talk about what the place looks like and how important it is. Pay attention to the words and phrases you use when you name this place. Make sure you choose a general name that you can also use to describe a similar worship place that belongs to another group of people. Pause this audio here.

King Solomon and the people went to the high place that was at Gibeon because the Tent of Meeting for God was there. Gibeon was one of the cities of the Levites, in the territory of Benjamin. Gibeon was about 10 kilometers northwest of Jerusalem.

Stop here and look at a map of the journey from Jerusalem to Gibeon. Pause this audio here.

This is the same tent that Moses, who served Yahweh faithfully, had made in the wilderness. Yahweh had asked Moses to make a temporary house that people made of skins. Yahweh said that Yahweh would speak with the people of Israel from the Tent of Meeting. The Israelites, therefore, called it the Tent of Meeting, the tabernacle, or the tent of Yahweh's presence. The storyteller adds a comment to explain to us that the ark of God was not in the Tent of Meeting like it was when the Israelites first made the tent. Earlier, King David had taken the ark from Kiriath Jearim to Jerusalem and put it in another tent at the place King David had prepared for it.

Stop here and do this activity as a group: Have someone tell a story. As the person is telling the story, he should pause the story to explain or add some information about what he has been saying. For example, the person could be speaking of the story of a great harvest that your community has enjoyed and then pause the story to make a comment about someone who did not plant anything at planting time. Pause this audio here.

But the bronze altar that Bezalel, the son of Uri and grandson of Hur, had made was there standing in front of the Tent of Meeting. Bezalel had made this altar by mixing two metals, copper and tin, to make a metal called bronze. The altar was standing before the Tent of Meeting because that is what Yahweh had commanded. Sometimes the word tabernacle refers to the entire Tent of Meeting, including the fence around it. In this case, it refers only to the two rooms in the main tent, the Holy Place and the Most Holy Place.

Stop here and, as a group, look at a picture of the entire Tent of Meeting with the bronze altar standing before the Tent of Meeting. Pause audio here.

In the fourth scene, King Solomon and the people with him worshipped or asked Yahweh for advice there. It is possible that the storyteller means that Solomon and the people went to sacrifice at Gibeon often. King Solomon went up to the bronze altar in front of Yahweh's Tent of Meeting and offered 1,000 burnt offerings on the altar. It is unlikely that Solomon himself made the offerings. Normally, priests would kill, cut up, and burn the entire animal on the altar, apart from its skin. It is likely that Solomon offered the burnt offerings to dedicate himself and his rule over Israel to Yahweh.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 1:1–6 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has four scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* Yahweh
* King Solomon
* King David
* All the people of Israel
* The military commanders of thousands
* The military commanders of hundreds
* The judges of Israel
* The leading men of Israel who are heads of the families, or clans
* Moses
* Bezalel, the son of Uri and grandson of Hur
* And the priest who offered Solomon's sacrifices—the storyteller does not mention the priests, but they were likely there

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

King Solomon took firm control of the kingdom of Israel because Yahweh was with Solomon. Yahweh made Solomon very great.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel happy and blessed. Yahweh is giving me control of Israel; or
* I am aware and alert for danger within Israel as well as from outside; or
* I am determined to secure myself as king.

Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel pleased with Solomon; or
* I am happy to give Solomon the power to defeat his enemies; or
* I feel loved by my obedient son Solomon. I will give Solomon favor and success.

Continue the drama.

King Solomon spoke to all the people of Israel and to their military leaders, judges, and the heads of the families of Israel.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel excited. This is a special day for Israel; or
* I am happy to honor Yahweh with all Israel; or
* I feel very happy to see the loyalty and unity my people have.

Ask the person playing the commanders, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* We feel honored by our king; or
* We love King Solomon, and we will serve Solomon loyally; or
* We are encouraged by our young king's wisdom. Solomon has done a very good job so far.

Ask the person playing judges and heads of families, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* We are very pleased with the purpose of this gathering. When Solomon's father became king, David called all the leaders of Israel, and they went and brought the ark of Yahweh. King Solomon wants us to worship Yahweh at Gibeon and ask for Yahweh's help; or
* We are proud of our young king's wisdom and success; or
* We feel great hope for the future of our people and nation.

Continue the drama.

King Solomon and all the people who had gathered went to the high place at Gibeon because the Tent of Meeting that Moses had made was there. The bronze altar that Bezalel, the son of Uri, had made was also there. But King David had brought the ark of God to Jerusalem.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing the people of Israel, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* We feel like rejoicing and celebrating; or
* We feel awe and amazement. We are at the tent of Yahweh's presence. We hope and pray that Solomon will serve Yahweh as faithfully as Moses did; or
* We feel excited to see the work Yahweh is doing in Israel today. Yahweh has given Solomon wisdom to rule just as Yahweh gave Bezalel wisdom to make the bronze altar.

Ask the person playing King Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel humbled to be in this holy place; or
* I feel sad that the ark was separated from the Tent of Meeting and that the people still have not got a permanent place to worship Yahweh; or
* I am determined to do the work Yahweh has given me. I will build the temple.

Continue the drama.

King Solomon and the people of Israel worshipped Yahweh there. And King Solomon offered 1,000 burnt offerings.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing the heads of families, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* We feel a great desire that Yahweh would guide our king; or
* We feel blessed to have a king like this who leads the whole nation to worship Yahweh.

Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am honored by my people; or
* I am pleased by the sweet smell of the burnt sacrifices; or
* I have heard and will answer Solomon.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 1:1–6 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

Solomon, the son of David, established himself in his kingdom, and Yahweh, Solomon's God, was with Solomon. A kingdom is the territory or the people that a king rules. Yahweh is a name that God gives himself. This name means something like "I am," or "I am who I am." God is a less personal or more general name that people sometimes used to refer to Yahweh. This is the God who created everything, the most powerful spiritual being.

Stop here and discuss as a group what words or phrases you will use for **kingdom**, **Yahweh**, and **God**. Look up kingdom, Yahweh, and God in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated these words in another book of the Bible, use the same words that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon spoke to all Israel, to the commanders, judges, leaders, and heads of families. Israel is the land that Yahweh promised to Abraham and his descendants. At some point, the people of Israel fought among themselves and divided the land into two kingdoms. At this point, Israel refers to the whole kingdom before it was divided. A commander is the military officer who is in charge of other soldiers. Israel's military commanders were in charge of either 1,000 or 100 soldiers. A judge is a person who judges actions that people do and decides if what those people have done is right or wrong, and therefore if a person is guilty or innocent of breaking a law. A judge will punish the person if the judge decides that the person is guilty of doing wrong. The leaders were the chiefs or leading men in all Israel. The heads of families were specifically the chiefs or the most important men from each clan, or large family group, in Israel. It is likely that leaders and heads of families refer to the same group of men.

Stop here and discuss as a group what words or phrases you will use for **Israel**, **commanders**, **judges**, **leaders**, and **heads**. Look up Israel and judges in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated these words in another book of the Bible, use the same words that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon and all the assembly went to the high place at Gibeon because the Tent of Meeting that Moses, the faithful or loyal servant of Yahweh, had made in the wilderness was there. "All the assembly" refers to all the people who had gathered together for a common purpose. Because Yahweh chose the people of Israel, people usually call gatherings of the people of Israel the "assembly" of Yahweh. The Tent of Meeting is another name for the tabernacle. This is where the Israelites worshipped Yahweh. The people called it the Tent of Meeting because it was where the Israelites could meet Yahweh. A servant is a person who does work for someone else. People sometimes call someone who worships and obeys Yahweh a servant of Yahweh. A wilderness is a lonely, barren place without water. There are almost no trees or bushes growing in a wilderness. The ground is dry and rocky, and there are wild animals roaming around. People do not live in the wilderness.

Stop here and discuss as a group what words or phrases you will use for **assembly**, **Tent of Meeting**, **servant**, and **wilderness**. Look up **tabernacle**, servant, and wilderness in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated these words in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

But David had brought the ark of God to Jerusalem. The bronze altar that Bezalel, the son of Uri, had made was also there in front of the tabernacle. The ark of God is the oblong box that people made of acacia wood that Yahweh asked Moses to make. Sometimes people call this box the ark of the covenant. Yahweh spoke to Moses from above the ark. Bronze is a metal that people made by combining copper and tin. An altar is a place where people make offerings or sacrifices to God. The people of Israel burned their animal sacrifices on the bronze altar. The tabernacle was a portable temple that the Israelites carried with them during their years of traveling in the wilderness. This was the place where the Israelites kept the ark of the covenant and where the people worshiped God.

Stop here and discuss as a group what words or phrases you will use for **ark**, **bronze**, **altar**, and **tabernacle**. Look up **ark of the covenant**, bronze, and altar in the Master Glossary for more information. Remember that the storyteller calls the ark the "ark of God" in this story. Remember that "tabernacle" is another way to say the Tent of Meeting. If you have already translated these words in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon offered 1,000 burnt offerings on the bronze altar. A burnt offering is a form of sacrifice that people bring to Yahweh. When a person brings Yahweh a burnt offering, he kills an animal and burns this animal completely on an altar. The people brought Yahweh burnt offerings as a way to completely dedicate themselves to Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **burnt offering**. Look up burnt offering in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

## 2 Chronicles 1:7–13 (id: eng-2ch-p2-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 1:7–13 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 1:7–13 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 1:7–13 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the last story, Solomon became the king of Israel after his father, David, died. Solomon gathered together the people of Israel and their leaders and went to make offerings to Yahweh at the bronze altar that was at the high place in Gibeon.

This story happened on the night of that same day that Solomon offered the burnt offerings to Yahweh. Solomon was still at the high place in Gibeon. That night, Yahweh appeared to Solomon. Yahweh told Solomon to ask Yahweh for anything that Solomon wanted. The storyteller does not tell us how Yahweh appeared to Solomon. The storyteller does say that it was at night. Solomon would have been asleep and dreaming. Yahweh sometimes appears to people in their dreams.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Tell stories of people in your community who received messages from God, the spirits, or the ancestors while they were dreaming. What messages did those people receive? Pause this audio here.

Solomon told Yahweh that Yahweh had already shown great kindness to Solomon's father, David, and that Yahweh had continued to be kind by making Solomon king after David. Yahweh had promised David that Yahweh would bless David and make some of David's descendants kings of Israel. Solomon told Yahweh that Yahweh had fulfilled Yahweh's word, or promise to David, because Yahweh had made Solomon king of the people of Israel. It is also possible that Solomon was actually asking Yahweh to fulfill Yahweh's promise rather than saying that Yahweh had already fulfilled it.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In the previous discussion, you talked about people who had received a message from God, the spirits, or the ancestors in a dream. Now discuss whether those messages were about true things that actually happened or not. Pause this audio here.

Solomon said that the people of Israel were as many as the dust of the earth or the grains of dust. Yahweh had promised Jacob, the ancestor of the 12 tribes of Israel, that the people of Israel would become as many as the dust of the earth. Solomon is saying that he has very many people to rule over.

Stop here and do this activity as a group: Collect some sand or dust in your hand and try to count the grains one by one. Pause audio here.

Solomon then asked Yahweh to give Solomon wisdom and knowledge to go out and to come in before the people of Israel. In Israel, because the leaders led the warriors out when they went to war and led the warriors back home after the war, to go out and to come in became another way to talk about leading people. Solomon is asking Yahweh to give Solomon wisdom or the ability to make good decisions that would make Yahweh happy. Solomon also asks for knowledge. Solomon wants to understand things about Yahweh, to know right from wrong, and to learn important truths. Knowledge is a special gift from Yahweh, but also something people try to learn. When people have knowledge, those people have the ability to make wise decisions. Knowledge is just the first step to becoming wise. Solomon is asking Yahweh to help Solomon think clearly so Solomon can be a good king and make smart, helpful choices. It is likely that when Solomon prays for wisdom, Solomon is also asking for the skills to build the temple.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Discuss the different ways in which a king or great chief leads his people. For example, how does a king lead in war, in building projects, in legal cases, or religiously? What skills or characteristics does a leader need to be successful in all these areas of leadership? Pause this audio here.

Solomon told Yahweh that Solomon asked for wisdom and knowledge because no one would be able to judge or rule the many and important people of Yahweh.

Yahweh was pleased that Solomon did not ask for possessions, riches, honor, or even for Yahweh to kill Solomon's enemies or to give Solomon a long life. Yahweh said that Solomon's heart desire was wisdom and knowledge to rule the people of Yahweh. The people of Israel believed that a person's heart controls that person's thinking, choices, and ability to know right from wrong. The heart's desire is the thing that someone really wants.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Which part of the body does your community believe our desires come from? Pause audio here.

Yahweh said that because of this, Yahweh would give Solomon the thing that Solomon had asked for—wisdom and knowledge. Yahweh said that Yahweh would also give Solomon the things that Solomon had not asked for—possessions, riches, and honor. Yahweh also said that Yahweh would give Solomon these things more than any king that had come before Solomon or any that would come after Solomon. It is not clear whether Yahweh meant that Yahweh would give Solomon more of these things than any of the kings of Israel or more than Yahweh would give the kings of all the nations.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Talk about the wisest, richest, and most respected king or leader that your community has ever had. Give examples of that person's wisdom, riches, and the respect people gave him. Where did the king or leader get these things from? Pause audio here.

Then Solomon left the Tent of Meeting that was at Gibeon, where he had gone to offer burnt offerings to Yahweh, and went back to Jerusalem. It is likely that Solomon went back to Jerusalem in the morning after the night Solomon had the dream.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of the Tent of Meeting and a map of the journey from Gibeon to Jerusalem. Pause this audio here.

Solomon ruled over Israel from Jerusalem.

This story is important because it shows the people of Israel that King Solomon cared more about being a good king and doing what Yahweh wanted than about being rich and powerful.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 1:7–13 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has two scenes.

**In the first scene:** Yahweh appeared to Solomon at night and told Solomon that Yahweh would give Solomon anything that Solomon asked for. Solomon asked Yahweh to give Solomon wisdom and knowledge to rule Yahweh's great people, Israel. And Yahweh said that Yahweh would give Solomon wisdom and knowledge and more possessions, riches, and honor than any other king before or after Solomon.

**In the second scene:** Solomon left Gibeon and the Tent of Meeting that was there and went back to Jerusalem. Solomon ruled Israel.

The characters in this passage are:

* Yahweh
* King Solomon
* King David
* The people of Israel
* And the other kings that came before and after Solomon

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In the first scene, God appears to Solomon. You will remember that in the last story, Solomon offered burnt offerings to Yahweh. In this story, Solomon is still at the high place in Gibeon, but it is now nighttime. Solomon is asleep and dreaming. God appears to Solomon and tells Solomon that God will give Solomon anything that Solomon asks for. God sometimes spoke to people in dreams.

Solomon answered Yahweh. Solomon began by reminding Yahweh how Yahweh had treated Solomon's father, David. Solomon said that Yahweh had shown great kindness to Solomon's father, David, and continued to be kind by making Solomon king after his father, David. Solomon is saying that Yahweh had shown steadfast love and was loyal to David because Yahweh had made a promise and agreement with David. Yahweh had promised David that David's family would rule Israel, and now Solomon, David's son, was king.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Talk about someone that God, the spirits, or ancestors loved and protected. Describe how God, the spirits, or the ancestors remained loyal to that person and never left the person. Pay attention to the words and phrases you use to describe this kind of love. Pause this audio here.

Solomon talks about how Solomon wants Yahweh to treat Solomon. The scene begins with a word that shows that Solomon is not talking about the past anymore. Solomon asks Yahweh God to fulfill his word because it is Yahweh who had made Solomon king of Israel. Yahweh's word is the promise that Yahweh had made to David. It is also possible that Solomon is saying that Yahweh had already fulfilled the word or promise that Yahweh had made to David because Solomon has become king. Solomon said the people of Israel were as many as the dust of the earth.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Talk about the largest group of people you have ever seen in your life. What special language can you use to say how many people they were? Use words or phrases that describe a number of people too many to count. Pause this audio here.

Solomon asked Yahweh for wisdom and knowledge to go out and come in before the people of Israel. The leaders of Israel went out before the people when going to war and came in before the people when returning from war. For this reason, people used the phrase "to go out and to come in" as a special way of saying to lead people.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Tell stories of the greatest leader that your community or country has ever had. Discuss and describe the different ways he led your people. How do you talk about the way this person led the people? How will you say that Solomon asked Yahweh for wisdom and knowledge to go out and to come in before the people of Israel? Pause this audio here.

Solomon wanted wisdom and knowledge so he could lead well. A person who has wisdom is able to make good decisions that lead to good results. People who have certain skills, like the ability to build or to make something beautiful, are also wise. It is Yahweh who gives wisdom to people. When a person has knowledge, that person has a good understanding of things. People gain knowledge by learning information or skills.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Talk about someone in your community who learned something that they did not know and continued learning for many years so that they became the most experienced or wisest person in that subject. For example, you may want to talk about someone who learned something like ruling or hunting. Pay attention to the words and phrases that you use to describe the knowledge this person learned and the wisdom they had after many years. Pause this audio here.

Solomon asked Yahweh a question to make a statement. Solomon said that no one could judge or rule Yahweh's great people. Solomon is saying that if Yahweh does not help Solomon, Solomon will not be able to be a good king. Solomon calls Israel Yahweh's great people. Solomon meant two things when he called Yahweh's people great. Firstly, Yahweh was the true king of Israel, and therefore the people of Israel were Yahweh's people, or very important people. Secondly, the people of Israel were very many.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Talk about how you will refer to Yahweh's great people. Remember that they are great because they are important as well as many. Pause this audio here.

Yahweh was happy because Solomon had not asked for possession, riches, honor or respect, long life, or for Solomon's enemies to die. Yahweh was happy because the desire of Solomon's heart was wisdom and knowledge to rule well. In Israel, people believed the heart helped you think, choose, and know right from wrong.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Talk about something you really want. Explain why you want it so much. How do you describe the feeling you have of wanting something so much? How will you talk about the desires of Solomon's heart? Pause this audio here.

Yahweh said Yahweh would give Solomon the wisdom and knowledge that Solomon had asked for. Yahweh also said Yahweh would give Solomon the things Solomon had not asked for. Yahweh said that Yahweh would give Solomon more wisdom, knowledge, possessions, riches, and honor than any king before or after Solomon. It is not clear if Yahweh meant only the kings of Israel or all kings. Yahweh does not say that Yahweh would give Solomon a long life or kill Solomon's enemies. Solomon had already killed some of Solomon's enemies.

In the second scene, Solomon left the Tent of Meeting in Gibeon and went back to Jerusalem. Solomon likely returned in the morning after the dream. Solomon had gone to Gibeon with the people of Israel and their leaders, but the storyteller does not mention those people here. Solomon ruled Israel from Jerusalem. Gibeon was one of the cities of the Levites, in the territory of Benjamin. It was about 10 kilometers northwest of Jerusalem.

Stop here and look at a map of the journey from Jerusalem to Gibeon. Pause this audio here.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 1:7–13 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has two scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* Yahweh
* King Solomon
* King David
* The people of Israel
* And the other kings that came before and after Solomon

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

In the first scene, Yahweh appeared to Solomon at night and told Solomon that Yahweh would give Solomon anything that Solomon asked for.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel honored by Solomon; or
* I am pleased with Solomon's burnt offerings and his dedication to me; or
* I love Solomon as I loved his father, David. I want to know if Solomon will be faithful to me as David was.

Ask the person playing Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am amazed by Yahweh; or
* I feel very afraid of Yahweh's glory; or
* I feel so happy. Yahweh has answered my prayers.

Continue the drama.

Solomon answered Yahweh and said that Yahweh had shown Solomon's father, David, great kindness.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel great joy. My heart is full of praise for Yahweh. Yahweh's word is true, and Yahweh keeps his promises; or
* Yahweh is a faithful God. Yahweh kept his word to David, and I desire the same love and loyalty Yahweh gave my father; or
* I love Yahweh. I feel that Yahweh loves me for the sake of my father, David.

Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel honored; or
* I love to keep my promises, and I love those who recognize my faithfulness; or
* I am happy that Solomon knows I am the one who made Solomon king of Israel.

Continue the drama.

Solomon asked Yahweh to give Solomon wisdom and knowledge to rule Yahweh's great people, Israel.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel overwhelmed by my job as the king of Yahweh's many important people; or
* I am scared that I will fail and make Yahweh angry; or
* I feel desperate for Yahweh's help.

Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am glad that Solomon has not been selfish and asked for things for himself, but has asked for what Solomon needs to rule My people well; or
* Solomon wants what I also want and this makes me very pleased with Solomon; or
* I love how Solomon loves me and loves people.

Continue the drama.

Yahweh said that Yahweh would give Solomon wisdom and knowledge and more possessions, riches, and honor than any other king before or after Solomon.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel very pleased with what Solomon has requested; or
* I love that Solomon is humble and knows that he needs my help to be a good king; or
* I am happy to bless Solomon more than any other king because Solomon's desire is to please me.

Ask the person playing Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Wow! I am amazed at Yahweh's generosity; or
* I feel loved by Yahweh; or
* I feel like I have a relationship with Yahweh, like the strong relationship my father, David, had with Yahweh.

Ask the person playing the other kings that came before or after, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am amazed by the wealth and greatness of King Solomon; or
* We wish we were as famous and that Yahweh would honor us like he honored Solomon; or
* We feel jealous of Solomon.

Continue the drama.

In the second scene, Solomon left Gibeon and went from the Tent of Meeting back to Jerusalem. Solomon ruled Israel.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am happy, blessed, and ready to rule; or
* I know Yahweh will be with me, and I feel more confident and prepared; or
* I feel loved by Yahweh. I feel secure and safe because Yahweh keeps his promises.

Ask the person playing the people of Israel, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* We are very happy. Our King Solomon rules us very well; or
* We feel blessed, rich, and prosperous; or
* We feel that we are a special nation that Yahweh loves. Yahweh has blessed us with a good king.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 1:7–13 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

That night, **God** appeared to Solomon and asked Solomon to ask for anything, and God would give it to Solomon. God is a general name for Yahweh. God refers to God who created everything. God is the most powerful spiritual being. Use the same word or phrase for God as you used in previous passages. For more information on God, refer to the Master Glossary.

Solomon told Yahweh that Yahweh had shown great kindness, or steadfast love, to Solomon's father, David, by making Solomon king. Kindness refers to how Yahweh treats people who are under Yahweh's authority. This kindness is the steadfast, or loyal, love of Yahweh. Yahweh treated David with kindness because Yahweh had promised to do so, and Yahweh never breaks Yahweh's promises.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **kindness** or **steadfast love**. Look up steadfast love in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Use the same word or phrase for **king** as you used in previous passages. For more information on king, refer to the Master Glossary.

Solomon asked **Yahweh** God to fulfill Yahweh's word or promise. Use the same word or phrase for Yahweh as you used in previous passages. For more information on Yahweh, refer to the Master Glossary.

Solomon said that it was Yahweh who had made Solomon king over a people who are as many as the dust of the earth. The people of Israel were too many to count.

Solomon asked Yahweh to give Solomon wisdom and knowledge so that Solomon might be able to go out and come in before the people. Solomon told Yahweh that no one would be able to judge Yahweh's great people. A person who has wisdom understands many things because they have experienced many things. That person is able to make good decisions that lead to good results. When a person has certain skills, like the ability to build or to make something beautiful, the Bible also says that this person has wisdom. It is Yahweh who gives wisdom to people. Knowledge is similar to wisdom; when someone has knowledge, that person has understanding. People can gain understanding more quickly, perhaps, than they can gain wisdom. This kind of knowledge also helps people lead others, make good choices, and understand the situation clearly. It is not just about knowing a lot of facts.

Stop here and discuss as a group what words or phrases you will use for **wisdom** and **knowledge**. Look up wisdom in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

To go out and come in before the people is to lead the people. It is special language that usually refers to a king or commander leading his warriors to war, but here it means all the different ways that king Solomon would lead Israel. When someone judges or rules, that person usually decides cases and punishes those who are wrong, but not in this case. Solomon asks Yahweh, "Who is able to judge your great people?" Solomon means that no one can rule the people fairly.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **judge**, especially when it means to govern or rule a group of people fairly. Look up judge in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Yahweh said that Solomon's **heart desire** was not **possessions**, **riches**, or **honor**, or for Yahweh to kill Solomon's enemies, or for a long life for himself, but for wisdom and knowledge. A person's heart desire is the thing that the person wants more than anything else. Possessions and riches refer to things like land, animals, silver, or gold. If you have different words in your language to refer to these things, use two different words. If not, use one word. Honor may refer to beauty, respect, riches, or signs that God is with Solomon.

Stop here and discuss as a group what words or phrases you will use for **heart desire**, **possessions**, **riches**, and **honor**. Look up **heart** in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated these words in another book of the Bible, use the same words that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Then Solomon went to Jerusalem from the **high place** at Gibeon, from before the **Tent of Meeting** or tabernacle. And Solomon ruled Israel. Use the same words or phrases for "high place" and "Tent of Meeting" as you used in previous passages. For more information on the Tent of Meeting, or tabernacle, refer to the Master Glossary.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 1:7-13

## 2 Chronicles 1:14–17 (id: eng-2ch-p3-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 1:14–17 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 1:14–17 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 1:14–17 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous story, Solomon asked God for wisdom and knowledge to be a good leader for Israel. God promised to make Solomon wise. God also promised that Solomon would be wealthy and famous. In this story, the storyteller describes the powerful army that Solomon built up for Israel.

Solomon's army owned many horses and chariots. At that time, having horses and chariots made an army more powerful. A soldier could ride a horse, and the horse could run faster than humans, so the soldier on a horse could catch and kill more people than a soldier on foot.

A chariot is a kind of cart that horses pull into battle. At least two soldiers ride in the chariot. One soldier controls the horses while the other soldier can shoot arrows or stab enemy soldiers with his sword. The soldiers inside the chariot are also somewhat protected by the walls of the chariot.

Stop here and look at a picture of a chariot with soldiers inside. Pause this audio here.

Solomon had 1,400 chariots. When you listened to three different versions of this passage, some might have said that Solomon had 12,000 horses, while others said that Solomon had 12,000 horsemen. A horseman is a person who knows how to handle horses. Bible scholars do not agree on whether the original language means that Solomon had 12,000 horses or that Solomon had 12,000 horsemen.

Solomon stationed some of his horses and chariots in Jerusalem, and Solomon stationed others in special cities in Israel that Solomon built just for them. This made it easier to defend the country from invaders, because no matter where an enemy attacked, there would always be a chariot city close by.

Solomon bought horses from Egypt and also from a place called Kue. Bible scholars are not certain exactly where Kue was located, but some think it might have been located northwest of Israel, in the southeastern part of the modern\-day country of Turkey. This area is sometimes called Cilicia. When you listened to three different versions of this passage, you might have heard the storyteller call this place Kue, or you might have heard the storyteller call this place Cilicia.

Stop here and look at a map of the Middle East that shows Israel and Turkey. Note the location of Cilicia. Pause this audio here.

Toward the end of this passage, the storyteller tells us how much Solomon paid for his chariots and horses. The price of a chariot was about 600 shekels of silver, and the price of a horse was about 150 shekels of silver. This was equal to about seven kilograms of silver for a chariot and about two kilograms of silver for a horse. The storyteller adds that Solomon's men sold chariots and horses to the kings of the Hittites and the kings of the Arameans.

As the storyteller tells us these things, the storyteller reminds us of just how rich Solomon was. Silver, gold, and cedar wood were all valuable, expensive items, but they were very common in Israel during Solomon's day. This meant that not only was Solomon very rich, but Solomon made his people quite rich as well.

Cedar wood was a kind of wood that was very good for building. Cedar was very strong, lasted a long time, and had a pleasant smell and color. The Israelites thought that the best cedar came from Lebanon, the country just north of Israel.

Stop here and look at a picture of a cedar tree. Have you ever seen a building that people made from cedar wood? What was it like?

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 1:14–17 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has one scene.

**In the first scene:** The storyteller tells us about Solomon's chariots and horses. The storyteller also tells us that Solomon was so rich that valuable materials like silver, gold, and cedar were extremely common in Israel.

The characters in this passage are:

* King Solomon
* Solomon's horsemen and charioteers
* Solomon's royal merchants
* And the kings of the Hittites and Arameans

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In the first scene, the author tells us about Solomon's chariots and horses. If you have already translated the book of 1 Chronicles, then you have already translated the word for chariot. It would be helpful to go back and listen to how you translated chariot in that book.

Stop here and listen to the first part of 1 Chronicles chapter 18, if it is available in your language. In this story, King David defeated King Hadadezer and took his chariots. Pay attention to how you translated the word chariot. Pause this audio here.

Solomon had 1,400 chariots, and either 12,000 horses or 12,000 horsemen. Bible scholars do not agree on whether the original language means to say "horses" or "horsemen" in this passage. Either translation choice is acceptable.

Stop here and discuss these questions: How common are horses in your culture? If horses are not common now, was there a time in the past when horses were common? What words do you use for a person who knows how to handle horses? What words do you have for a person who is a soldier who rides horses? Pause this audio here.

Solomon kept some of his horses, chariots, and charioteers in the chariot cities Solomon had built all over Israel, but Solomon kept some of them in Jerusalem to protect the capital city. Solomon bought horses from Egypt and from a place called Kue. Bible scholars are not certain exactly where Kue was. Bible scholars do know that during Solomon's time, an area in southeastern Turkey had a reputation for breeding excellent horses. Sometimes people called this area "Cilicia." When you listened to three different versions of this passage, you might have heard that Solomon bought horses from Egypt and Kue, or that Solomon bought horses from Egypt and Cilicia. Either translation choice is acceptable.

Stop here and look at a map that shows the location of Israel and the location of Cilicia. Pause this audio here.

Solomon bought a chariot for seven kilograms of silver and a horse for two kilograms of silver. Solomon's traders not only bought chariots and horses for Israel, but they also sold chariots and horses to the kings of the Hittites and the Arameans. The Hittites and Arameans were enemies of Israel, so it is not clear why Solomon's traders sold horses and chariots to these people. Perhaps Solomon was so rich and so powerful that Solomon no longer had to worry about attacks from the Hittites and the Arameans.

While the storyteller is describing Solomon's army, the storyteller mentions again that there was so much silver in Israel that it was as common as stones. The storyteller also claims that cedar trees were as common as sycamore trees in the lowlands. The Israelites had to import good\-quality cedar into Israel from Lebanon, and it was expensive. In contrast, sycamore trees were very common in an area called Shephelah, which was the low hills in the region of Judah. Both of these statements mean that Solomon was so rich that Solomon could buy a lot of expensive things.

Stop here and look at a picture of a cedar tree. Does the cedar tree look like any trees that grow in your area? Next, look at a picture of a sycamore tree. Does this tree look like any trees that grow in your area? Pause this audio here.

In your language, it may be unnatural or confusing for the storyteller to first talk about Solomon's chariots and horses, then talk about Solomon's silver, gold, and cedar, and then go back to talking about Solomon's chariots and horses again. If this is the case, it is acceptable to move the storyteller's comments about silver, gold, and cedar to either the beginning or the end of this story.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 1:14–17 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has one scene.

The characters in this passage are:

* King Solomon
* Solomon's horsemen and charioteers
* Solomon's royal merchants
* And the kings of the Hittites and Arameans

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

> Solomon built up a large force of 1,400 chariots and 12,000 horsemen. Solomon stationed some of them in the chariot cities and some near Solomon in Jerusalem. The king made silver and gold as common in Jerusalem as stone, and he made cedar as common as the sycamore trees that grow in the foothills of Judah. Solomon's horses came from Egypt and Kue, and the king's merchants bought them from Kue at the standard price. They bought a chariot from Egypt for about seven kilograms of silver, and a horse for about two kilograms of silver. And they also exported chariots and horses to the kings of the Hittites and the Arameans.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I hope that these chariots and horsemen will keep our nation safe for many years to come; or
* I am making Israel the most powerful nation on earth; or
* I am very happy that my people now have great riches.

Ask the people playing Solomon's royal merchants, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am so proud that our country has enough money to buy good horses and chariots; or
* I am happy that our people have such a great king as Solomon; or
* I am glad that we can make extra money by selling horses and chariots to the Hittites and Arameans.

Ask the people playing Solomon's horsemen and charioteers, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Finally, our country has an army as powerful as Egypt's; or
* I feel proud to serve in such a strong army; or
* I am grateful to King Solomon for providing such good chariots and horses for our army.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 1:14–17 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

Solomon built up a large force of 1,400 **chariots** and 12,000 horsemen. Be sure to translate the word chariot the same way that you did in previous books of the Bible. If necessary, listen again to the beginning of 1 Chronicles chapter 18, when King David defeats Hadadezer and takes his chariots.

Stop here. If you decide to use the word "**horsemen**," decide how you will translate it into your language. Pause this audio here.

Solomon stationed some of them in the chariot cities and some near Solomon in Jerusalem. Cities are larger settlements that often have a wall around them.

Stop here and discuss as a group what words or phrases you will use for **chariot cities**. Pause this audio here.

> The **king** made silver and gold as common in Jerusalem as stone, and the king made cedar as common as the sycamore trees that grow in the foothills of Judah. Be sure to translate king the same way that you did in previous passages. See the Master Glossary for more information on king.
> 
> Stop here and look again at a picture of a **cedar** tree and a **sycamore** tree. Decide what words you will use for these two trees. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **Judah**. Refer to the Master Glossary for more information about Judah. Pause this audio here.

> Solomon's horses came from Egypt and Kue, and the king's merchants bought them from Kue at the standard price.
> 
> Stop here and look again at a map of the Middle East that shows the locations of Israel, Turkey, and Cilicia. Decide whether you will use the name **Kue** or **Cilicia** for the place the horses came from. Pause this audio here.
> 
> Solomon's traders bought a chariot from Egypt for about seven kilograms of silver, and a horse for about two kilograms of silver. And the traders also exported chariots and horses to the kings of the **Hittites** and the **Arameans**. Be sure to translate Hittites and Arameans the same way you did in previous passages. If you have already translated the book of Genesis, then you have already translated the word Hittite. If necessary, go back and listen to the beginning of Genesis chapter 36, which lists the wives of Esau. Esau's wife, Adah, was the daughter of Elon the Hittite. You translated "Arameans" in 1 Chronicles chapter 18, in the story when King David defeats King Hadadezer and the Aramean army.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.
