## 2 Chronicles 2:1–18 (id: eng-2ch-p4-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 2:1–18 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 2:1–18 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 2:1–18 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

You can also listen to your translation of the same story in 1 Kings 5:13–18 if you have already translated it. Since the storyteller tells this story in another way, pay attention to the differences in the order of the scenes as well as some of the details.

Pause this audio here.

This story starts with King Solomon's order to build a temple where people can worship the Name of Yahweh and a royal palace for King Solomon. People can gather at the temple to worship Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your culture, where do people usually gather to worship their gods? Who builds those places? Pause this audio here.

Then King Solomon assigned workers to work on the project. Solomon assigned 70,000 workers to carry rocks that 80,000 other workers would cut out of the mountainside. The people will use these rocks to build the temple. Three thousand six hundred men will supervise these workers.

King Solomon sent a message to King Hiram of Tyre. Tyre was just north of Israel, in the present\-day country of Lebanon. King Solomon knows that his father, King David, had a good relationship with King Hiram. King Solomon asks King Hiram to send cedar wood, just like when King Hiram sent cedar wood for King David in the past. At that time, people knew about Lebanon for its thick forests, which grew valuable cedar trees. Cedar wood is tough and beautiful. King Hiram sent wood for David's palace before, but this time King Solomon asks King Hiram for timber because King Solomon is about to build a temple that Solomon will dedicate to Yahweh.

King Solomon explains what the people will do at the temple to obey Yahweh's law. \[The Israelites will burn incense, a mix of gum and spices that smell good. The Israelites will set out holy or special bread each week. The Israelites will offer burnt offerings every morning and evening, on every Sabbath, at the New Moon Festival, and on the other feast days.]{.mark} The Sabbath was the seventh day of the week, and the Israelites did not work on that day; instead, the Israelites rested and honored Yahweh. In Old Testament times, the Israelites celebrated the festival of the New Moon at the beginning of each month, when the people could see the new moon rise in the sky. During this festival, the Israelites stopped their work, offered special sacrifices, and blew ram's horn trumpets to celebrate. These are the same things that people have been doing at Yahweh's tabernacle.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your culture, what practices or rituals do you do to worship God? Pause this audio here.

King Solomon plans to build a great temple for Yahweh. Solomon knows that Yahweh is the greatest of all the gods. In fact, Solomon knows that Solomon cannot build a temple that Yahweh can live in—in fact, even the heavens where Yahweh lives are not big enough for Yahweh!

King Solomon asks King Hiram to send someone who is skilled in craftsmanship. King Solomon specifically asks for a skilled worker to work with precious metals like gold, silver, bronze, and iron.

Stop here and look at a picture of gold, silver, bronze, and iron. Pause this audio here.

The skilled worker should also know how to work with high\-quality materials like cloth or yarn, which people dyed to be the colors blue, purple, and red. These were expensive colors, so they often represented royalty. These were the same colors Yahweh had told the Israelites to use for the tabernacle.

King Solomon also wanted a skilled worker who knew how to do "engravings," or carvings into wood, stone, or metal. King David already prepared skilled workers in Judah and Jerusalem, his capital city, in the past to build the temple, but you will remember that Yahweh did not allow King David to build the temple for Yahweh.

Stop here and look at pictures of blue, purple, and red cloth. Pause this audio here.

Then King Solomon asks King Hiram to send King Solomon lots of wood from cedar trees, juniper trees, and algum trees. We do not know if the juniper trees are juniper, cypress, or fir trees. We also do not know what type of tree the algum trees are. Some people think algum was a type of juniper tree, while others think it was a sandalwood tree. Since people will use this wood to build a large and magnificent temple, it was probably high\-quality wood that people can polish and make shiny.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of cedar, Lebanese juniper, sandalwood, and cypress trees. Pause this audio here.

King Solomon is willing to pay King Hiram's workers with 2,000 tons of wheat grain, 2,000 tons of barley grain, 400,000 liters of wine, and 400,000 liters of oil. Israel produces a great amount of wheat, barley, wine, and olive oil. These were the most common foods that people in Israel ate.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: What are the most common things that you grow for food in your land? What things do you normally trade with other people for? Pause this audio here.

King Hiram replies to King Solomon through a letter. King Hiram starts his letter and declares that King Solomon is proof that Yahweh loves the people of Israel. Then King Hiram praises Yahweh, who is the God of Israel, and who made heaven and earth. King Hiram continues to praise Yahweh, who gave King David a wise son, who is intelligent and full of discernment.

King Hiram will send the skilled craftsman named Huram\-Abi. Huram\-Abi's mother was from the Israelite tribe of Dan, and Huram\-Abi's father was from Tyre. The tribe of Dan is one of the 12 tribes of Israel. Huram\-Abi works with precious metals, high\-quality yarn, and a special cloth that people wove from the flax plant called linen. Huram\-Abi can engrave and make any design that someone gives to him. Huram\-Abi will work with the skilled workers of King Solomon and those of King David to build the temple. Then King Hiram asks Solomon to send the wheat, barley, wine, and olive oil that King Solomon promised to give the workers. Finally, King Hiram tells how Hiram's workers will transport the timbers from Lebanon to Jerusalem. The workers will cut the timbers and will make wood into rafts to float on the sea south to Joppa, another town along the coast of the sea. Then, from Joppa, King Solomon's workers can take the timbers to Jerusalem.

Stop here and look at a map of Israel and Lebanon that shows Jerusalem, and also Tyre and Joppa along the coast. Pause this audio here.

King Solomon takes a census, or counts, all the foreigners who live in Israel, as King David had done before him. King Solomon counts 153,600 foreigners. King Solomon probably only counts the men. These are foreigners from other nations who live in Israel.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your culture, how do you gather or force workers to build something for your whole people group? What kinds of people have to do the work? How does the leader treat those people? Pause this audio here.

The storyteller reminds us that from these foreigners, King Solomon assigns 70,000 men to carry the rocks and 80,000 men to be stonecutters in the hills. King Solomon also assigns 3,600 foremen to supervise the workers.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your culture, how do you count how many people are in the nation? How do the leaders know who is part of different people groups? How do leaders treat the foreigners in their country?

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 2:1–18 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has four scenes.

**In the first scene:** King Solomon orders his people to build a temple for Yahweh and a palace for King Solomon.

**In the second scene:** King Solomon asks King Hiram of Tyre to send King Solomon an expert craftsman and a lot of wood.

**In the third scene:** King Hiram of Tyre agrees to Solomon's request.

**In the fourth scene:** King Solomon takes a census of the foreigners in Israel for the building project.

The characters in this passage are:

* King Solomon
* Yahweh
* King Hiram of Tyre
* Huram\-Abi the craftsman
* Workers who will carry large rocks
* Workers who will cut the large rocks
* Supervisors
* Workers of King Hiram
* And foreigners in Israel

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In the first scene, King Solomon orders the people to build a temple for the name of Yahweh and a royal palace for King Solomon. Though the storyteller mentions King Solomon's palace, the storyteller does not tell the story of how people built it. In that culture, someone's name represented who they were, so Solomon said that Solomon wanted Yahweh himself, or Yahweh's name, to live in the temple Solomon would build for Yahweh.

The storyteller first tells us about the workers that King Solomon forced to cut stones for the temple. However, we know from later in the story that King Solomon took a census, or counted all the foreigners, before Solomon chose these workers. King Solomon gathered 80,000 workers who would quarry and cut the large rocks, and 70,000 workers who would carry the rocks from the mountainside to Jerusalem. Solomon also assigned 3,600 officers who would supervise the workers. If it would be clearer in your language, you can talk about the workers who cut the stone before you talk about the workers who move the stones.

In the second scene, King Solomon sends a message to King Hiram of Tyre and asks King Hiram to send cedar timbers, just like what King Hiram did for King David when King David built a house for himself. Solomon emphasizes the fact that this time Solomon is building a temple for Yahweh. Solomon uses a word that means "Look!" or "Pay attention!" King Solomon will not only build his own house, but King Solomon will also build a temple for Yahweh, Solomon's God. Solomon emphasizes that Yahweh is Solomon's God. Solomon is completely devoted to Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss as a group how you will show that Solomon emphasizes that Solomon is building a temple for Yahweh, unlike what David built before. Pause this audio here.

It will be a temple where people can go, do their religious duties, and worship Yahweh. People will do all the important things that people did in the tabernacle. You will remember that the Israelites burn incense in the tabernacle, and they set out the special bread of the presence on the table. Every week, the priests set out 12 loaves of bread on the golden table in the Holy Place in the tabernacle. In this temple, people will make burnt offerings every morning and evening, just as you remember they did in the tabernacle. People will make these burnt offerings on the Sabbath, during the New Moon Festivals, and at every other special festival, just like they did in the tabernacle. Yahweh has told the Israelites that they should do these things forever.

Stop here and discuss as a group how you will show the regular actions that Solomon says the Israelites will do regularly in the temple. Review how you have translated incense, bread of the presence, burnt offerings, Sabbath, and New Moon Festivals in other books of the Bible. Pause this audio here.

This temple will be great because God is great, greater than other gods! Solomon asks several questions that Solomon does not expect anyone to answer. Solomon asks, "But who is able to build a temple for him, since the heavens, even the highest heavens, cannot contain him? Who then am I to build a temple for him, except as a place to burn sacrifices before him?" Solomon does this to show that Solomon understands that Solomon cannot build a house for Yahweh to live in. Even the heavens are not big enough to hold all of Yahweh. In this case, the "heavens, even the highest heavens," refer to the sky and everything above the earth.

Stop here and discuss as a group how you will show that Solomon understands that Solomon cannot build a house for Yahweh. How will you talk about the heavens being unable to hold all of Yahweh? Pause this audio here.

That is why King Solomon asks King Hiram to send a very skillful craftsman who can work with the other craftsmen and workers who build the temple. King Solomon wants a craftsman who is skilled in working with precious metals and precious fabrics and is also skillful in carving wood, stone, and metal. King Solomon also asks King Hiram to send a lot of wood for the building project. King Solomon wants the temple to be great and wonderful. King Solomon even assures King Hiram that King Solomon will send food to pay King Hiram's workers. Solomon promises to send 20,000 cors of ground wheat grain, 20,000 cors of barley grain, 20,000 baths of wine, and 20,000 baths of olive oil. This is about 2,000 tons of wheat, 2,000 tons of barley, and 400,000 liters of olive oil and wine. These are very large amounts of wheat, barley, wine, and oil! You may just say that King Solomon sent King Hiram 20,000 measures each of wheat, barley, wine, and oil because we are not sure exactly how much of each of these things Solomon sent.

Stop here and discuss as a group how you will talk about how much of each item Solomon will send to King Hiram. If you have already translated 1 Kings 5:1–12, use the same units of measurement that you used in that passage. Pause this audio here.

In the third scene, King Hiram agrees to Solomon's request. King Hiram sends a letter to King Solomon. In the letter, King Hiram recognizes that Yahweh loves the Israelites very much. Yahweh has made Solomon king of Israel because Yahweh loves them so much! Then King Hiram also blesses, or praises, Yahweh because Solomon is wise and has a lot of knowledge. After that, King Hiram informs King Solomon that King Hiram will send Huram\-Abi, who is a very skilled craftsman. Huram\-Abi will supervise and work with the other craftsmen. Then King Hiram agrees to Solomon's payments and asks Solomon to do what Solomon has promised. Hiram speaks very respectfully to Solomon. Hiram calls David and Solomon my lord, or master, and Hiram calls himself Solomon's servant!

Stop here and discuss as a group: How do two leaders of different people groups or countries usually talk to each other in a way that shows they respect each other? Pause this audio here.

King Hiram's workers are ready to supply all the wood that King Solomon needs. King Hiram's workers will make the wood into rafts and deliver them to Joppa. And then from Joppa, King Solomon's workers will bring the wood to Jerusalem.

Stop here and look at the map that shows the wood moving from Tyre to Joppa to Jerusalem. Pause this audio here.

In the fourth scene, King Solomon makes a record, or census, of all the male foreigners who are living in the land of Israel, as his father David did. We are not sure if this means that King Solomon uses the numbers David counted, or if King Solomon makes this record in a similar way to the way his father, David, did. The census numbers 153,600 people.

The storyteller reminds us that from this census, Solomon assigns 80,000 workers to quarry and cut the rocks. Then King Solomon assigns 70,000 workers to carry the rocks from the mountainside to Jerusalem, where they will build the temple. Then King Solomon assigns the remaining 3,600 as foremen to supervise the workers. These are the same groups of men that the storyteller told us about at the beginning of the passage. This means that Solomon must have taken the census before Solomon assigned the men to these jobs.

Stop here and discuss as a group: \[How will you make it clear that these are the same groups of men from the beginning of the story? Pause this audio here.]{.mark}

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 2:1–18 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has four scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* King Solomon
* Yahweh
* King Hiram of Tyre
* Huram\-Abi the craftsman
* Workers who will carry large rocks
* Workers who will cut the large rocks
* Supervisors
* Workers of King Hiram
* And foreigners in Israel

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

In the first scene, King Solomon orders the people to build a temple for Yahweh and a royal palace for King Solomon. King Solomon gathers the workers who will quarry and cut the large rocks, and then King Solomon gathers the workers who will carry the rocks from the mountainside to Jerusalem. Solomon assigns the supervisors to supervise the work.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Solomon, "What were you thinking when you decided to build a temple for Yahweh?" The person might answer things like:

* I must do what my father, David, wanted and build a temple for Yahweh; or
* I think I need to honor Yahweh because Yahweh brought peace to Israel. I will build a temple to honor Yahweh.

Continue the drama.

In the second scene, King Solomon sends a message to King Hiram of Tyre and asks King Hiram to give cedar timbers because King Solomon will build a temple for Yahweh and a palace for Solomon. King Solomon also asks King Hiram to send a very skillful craftsman who can work with the other craftsmen and workers to build the temple. In return, King Solomon will send food to the workers.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Solomon, "What were you feeling or thinking when you asked King Hiram for his support in building the temple?" The person might answer things like:

* I think King Hiram will not refuse me in honor of my father, King David's treaty with King Hiram;
* I think King Hiram has the workers and resources I need to help build the temple.

Continue the drama.

In the third scene, King Hiram supports King Solomon's plan to build the temple. King Hiram sends a letter that recognizes that Yahweh loves the Israelites very much. King Hiram also praises Yahweh because Yahweh chose a wise king to rule Israel. Then King Hiram informs King Solomon that King Hiram sent Huram\-Abi, a very skilled craftsman. Huram\-Abi will work alongside the craftsmen of King Solomon and King David as they build the temple.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Hiram, "What were you feeling or thinking when King Solomon asked you to support King Solomon as he builds a temple for Yahweh?" The person might answer things like:

* King Solomon honors me when he asks me to help build the temple for Yahweh; or
* I had a treaty with King David. Now I want to keep that treaty with his son, King Solomon.

Ask the person playing Huram\-Abi, "What were you feeling or thinking when you found out that you would help build the temple in Jerusalem?" The person might answer things like:

* King Hiram honors me because he has chosen me to help build the temple for Yahweh; or
* I think this is my chance to worship Yahweh through my skills.

Continue the drama.

In the fourth scene, King Solomon counts all the foreigners who are living in the land of Israel and assigns some of them as workers and foremen.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing foreigners that King Solomon assigns to work on building the temple, "What were you feeling or thinking when you found out that you would help build the temple?" The people might answer things like:

* We feel honored for such an opportunity to help build the temple for Yahweh; or
* We feel we belong to the people of Israel; or
* I do not like that Solomon is forcing us to do this very hard work. Let Solomon find someone else to do this work!

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 2:1–18 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

Solomon orders the people to build a temple for the **Name of Yahweh** and a royal palace for Solomon. Yahweh is God's personal name. In that culture, someone's name represented who that person was, so Solomon said that he wanted Yahweh himself, or Yahweh's Name, to live in the temple Solomon would build for Yahweh. A temple is a place where people worship God. It is a place where God meets with people. Kings and rulers live in a palace with their families.

Use the same word for Yahweh that you have used previously. For more information on Yahweh, refer to the Master Glossary.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for the **temple**. Look up the word temple in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for a **palace**, or a king's house. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

King Solomon gathers the workers who will quarry and cut the large rocks and the workers who will carry the rocks from the mountainside to Jerusalem. Solomon assigns the foremen or overseers to supervise the work.

Stop here and discuss as a group what words or phrases you will use for each group of **workers** and **overseers**. Pause this audio here.

Solomon sends a message to **King** Hiram of Tyre and asks King Hiram to give **cedar** timbers for building the temple and palace. Use the same words for king and cedar that you have used in previous passages. Refer to king in the Master Glossary for more information.

King Solomon says that people will burn incense in the temple. Incense is a mixture of sweet\-smelling spices that makes a sweet\-smelling smoke when people burn it.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for the **incense**. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. If you have translated the book of Exodus, listen to Exodus 30:34–38\. Pause this audio here.

Solomon also says that people will put the bread of the Presence in the temple. The bread of the Presence was 12 loaves of bread that priests put on the table in the Holy Place of the tabernacle, and later, in the temple.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for the **bread of the Presence**. Look up the phrase "bread of the Presence" in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. If you have translated 1 Chronicles 9:32, you may listen to your translation to hear how you translated this type of bread there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon says that people will give **burnt offerings** morning and evening, on the Sabbaths, new moons, and other special festivals that Yahweh, the **God** of **Israel**, commanded the people to celebrate. Burnt offerings are animal sacrifices that people give, which the priest completely burns on the altar. The Sabbath is a special day of rest and worship. The new moon is the first day when people see a new moon in the sky. The Israelites had a special festival on that day. There are also other festivals that Yahweh told the people to celebrate each year. Use the same words for burnt offering, God, and Israel that you have used previously. See the Master Glossary for more information about burnt offerings, God, and Israel.

Stop here and discuss as a group what words or phrases you will use for **Sabbath**, **new moon**, and **festivals**. Look up Sabbath and "festival of the new moon" in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated these words in another book of the Bible, use the same words that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon says that the house that Solomon will build for God is great because the God of Israel is greater than all other gods. But God is so great that no one can build a house big enough for God. God is bigger than the highest heavens. Some people worship other supernatural beings or gods who are not Yahweh. Yahweh is greater than these false gods. In this context, "heavens" refers to the sky and everything above the earth.

Stop here and discuss as a group what words or phrases you will use for **gods** and **heavens**. Look up gods and heavens in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated these words in another book of the Bible, use the same words that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon also asks King Hiram to send a very skillful craftsman who knows how to work with metals like gold, silver, **bronze**, and iron. The craftsman must be able to work with purple, red, and blue precious fabrics and who can engrave or carve in wood, stone, and metal. This craftsman will work with the other craftsmen in **Jerusalem** and **Judah** that Solomon's father, **David**, provided. Use the same words for bronze, Jerusalem, Judah, and David that you have used in previous passages. Refer to the Master Glossary for more information about bronze, Jerusalem, Judah, and David.

Stop here and look again at a photo of **purple**, **red**, and **blue** cloth. Discuss as a group how you will describe the purple, red, and blue cloth. Pause this audio here.

King Solomon asks King Hiram to send King Solomon lots of wood from cedar trees, juniper trees, and algum trees, because Hiram's **servants** are skilled at cutting trees. We do not know whether the trees are juniper, cypress, or fir. We also do not know what type of tree the algum trees are. Some people think algum was a type of juniper tree, while others think it was a sandalwood tree. Use the same word for servant that you have used in previous passages. Refer to servant in the Master Glossary for more information.

Stop here and look at a picture of cedar, Lebanese juniper, sandalwood, and cypress trees, if needed. How will you call **cedar**, **juniper**, and **cypress** trees in your language? If you have translated these words in another book of the Bible, use the same words that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and decide how you will translate **algum wood** into your language. If necessary, look again at the pictures of a sandalwood tree and a juniper tree, which might look like the trees that the storyteller mentions here. Will you use a word for an existing kind of wood from your language, or will you translate it to sound like the word in the original language? Pause this audio here.

.

Solomon promises to send 20,000 cors of ground wheat grain, 20,000 cors of barley grain, 20,000 baths of **wine**, and 20,000 baths of olive oil to the workers who cut the wood. A cor is a dry measurement equal to about 6 bushels or 220 liters. A bath is a liquid measure equal to about 22 liters. This is about 2,000 tons of wheat, 2,000 tons of barley, and 400,000 liters of olive oil and wine. These are very large amounts of wheat, barley, wine, and oil! You may just say that King Solomon sent King Hiram 20,000 measures each of wheat, barley, wine, and oil. Use the same word for wine that you have used in previous passages. Refer to the Master Glossary for more information about wine.

Stop here and discuss as a group how you will talk about how much of each item Solomon will send to King Hiram. If you have already translated 1 Kings 5:1–12, use the same units of measurement that you used in that passage. Pause this audio here.

King Hiram sends a letter back to King Solomon. Hiram says, "**Praise** or **bless** Yahweh, the God of Israel. God has given King David a **wise** son, who is intelligent and full of understanding." Use the same words for praise or bless and wise that you have used in previous passages. For more information, refer to praise, bless, and wisdom in the Master Glossary.

Stop here and discuss as a group how you will describe that Solomon is wise and understands a lot of things. The words for being **intelligent** and **understanding** describe someone who knows a lot of facts but also understands how to use that knowledge for the good of other people. Pause this audio here.

King Hiram tells Solomon that Hiram has sent Hamur\-Abi, a skilled craftsman, to work with Solomon's craftsmen and the craftsmen of "my lord David." When Hiram calls David "my **lord**," Hiram is using a term of respect and authority. Use the same word for lord that you have used in previous passages. For more information, refer to lord in the Master Glossary.

King Solomon takes a census of all the foreigners living in Israel, just like Solomon's father, King David, had done. A census is when a ruler or government counts the number of people. A person is a foreigner or a stranger in a country when they are in a country that is not where that person was born. There are different types of foreigners. In this context, foreigners are non\-Israelites who are living permanently in Israel.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **foreigner**. Use the same word for foreigners that you have used in other books of the Bible, when it is this kind of foreigner. For more information about foreigners, refer to the Master Glossary. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **census**, or when a leader counts the number of a certain type of people in his kingdom.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

## 2 Chronicles 3:1–14 (id: eng-2ch-p5-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 3:1–14 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 3:1–14 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 3:1–14 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous passage, you heard about Solomon's preparations to build the temple. Solomon got the materials and workers to help build a temple for Yahweh.

In this passage, Solomon begins to build a temple for Yahweh. This temple is a place where people will worship Yahweh.

The information in this passage is also in 1 Kings 6:1–13 and 1 Kings 6:14–38\. If you have already translated those passages, stop here and listen to your translation as a group. Pause this audio here.

Solomon builds the temple in Jerusalem on Mount Moriah, the place where Abraham went to sacrifice Abraham's son, Isaac. Solomon's father, David, chose this place to build Yahweh's temple because Yahweh appeared to David there. Before Yahweh appeared to David, this place belonged to a man named Araunah, who was a Jebusite, someone who was part of the Jebus people. Some translations call Araunah by the name Ornan. This was the threshing floor or the place where Araunah threshed or separated his grain from the rest of the plant. Solomon begins to build the temple on the second day of the second month of the year in Israel. Solomon begins to build during the fourth year Solomon is king over Israel.

Stop here as a group and look at a map of Jerusalem and Mount Moriah. Look at a picture of a threshing floor. Pause this audio here.

King Solomon lays the foundation for the temple of Yahweh, which is 60 cubits, or 30 meters, long and 20 cubits, or 10 meters, wide. A cubit is a way to measure how long something is. One cubit is about as long as the distance between a man's elbow and the tips of his fingers, or about 50 centimeters. At this time, the people of Israel probably had two different lengths for a cubit, and Solomon uses the older standard measurement for a cubit. The portico or porch at the front of the temple is 20 cubits, or 10 meters, wide, like the rest of the building. A portico, which people sometimes call a porch, is part of a structure that has columns instead of walls to hold up the roof. The portico is probably only 20 cubits high. Solomon uses pure gold to cover the inside of the portico.

Solomon covers the inside of the main area of the temple with wood planks. This wood could be from cypress, fir, or juniper trees. Those types of wood were very hard and lasted a long time. Solomon covers the wood planks with pure gold and makes designs of palm trees and decorative chains on the walls. Palm trees are tall trees in Israel, and palm trees can be symbols of fertility, life, plentiful crops, and Yahweh's blessing on Yahweh's people. Solomon uses expensive stones to make the main area of the temple beautiful and uses gold from the land of Parvaim, which probably means the gold was pure or of very good quality. We are not sure where Parvaim may have been located. Solomon covers the ceiling beams, door frames, walls, and doors in the main area of the temple with gold. Men carve pictures of angels called cherubim on the walls. You will remember from 1 Chronicles that cherubim are winged creatures that are part human and part animal. The cherubim represent Yahweh's majesty and Yahweh's presence. Cherubim also protect Yahweh's property and serve as a throne for a king.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of palm trees, expensive stones, and gold metal. Pause this audio here.

King Solomon also builds the room called the Most Holy Place. Any place, person, or object that people dedicate to Yahweh is holy. The Most Holy Place is a place set apart for Yahweh's presence to live.

Stop here and discuss as a group: What kinds of places or objects in your culture do people set apart to use for special reasons? Pause this audio here.

The Most Holy Place is 20 cubits, or 10 meters, long, just like the rest of the temple building. This room is also 20 cubits wide. Workmen use 600 talents of pure gold to cover the inside of the room. A talent is a way to measure how heavy something is, and 600 talents would weigh the same as about 21 metric tons. However, this amount may not be exact. Instead, 600 talents could represent how much gold there would be if each of the 12 tribes of Israel gave 50 tons of gold, or it could represent one year of income for Solomon.

Solomon uses gold nails for the Most Holy Place that weigh about 50 shekels. A shekel is another way to measure how heavy something is, and 50 shekels would weigh the same as about 600 grams. People probably made the nails out of iron and then covered the nails with gold. Solomon also covers the upper rooms with gold. We are not sure what these upper rooms may have been, but this may refer to the open space between the Most Holy Place and the ceiling of the rest of the temple.

For the Most Holy Place, men carve two cherubs out of wood, and the men cover the cherubim with gold. Together, the two cherubim's wings measure 20 cubits, or 10 meters, and the wings touched both sides of the room. The cherubim stand on their feet and look out from the Holy of Holies toward the main room of the temple. Solomon probably uses the cherubim to protect the Most Holy Place and to represent Yahweh's presence that lived there.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of cherubim. Pause this audio here.

Solomon weaves a curtain with fine linen and blue, purple, and red yarn and sews pictures of cherubim in it. Yarn is a thread that people spin and then use to weave cloth, and linen is cloth that people weave from flax plants. The curtain separates the main area of the temple from the Most Holy Place because Yahweh's presence lived in the Most Holy Place. Only the high priest, after he had made sacrifices for his sins, could enter Yahweh's presence one time per year.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of a curtain. Look at a picture of blue, purple, and red yarn.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 3:1–14 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has three scenes.

**In the first scene:** Solomon begins to build Yahweh's temple in Jerusalem, on Mount Moriah. David chose this place to build Yahweh's temple because Yahweh had appeared to David there.

**In the second scene:** Solomon builds the foundation of the temple. The foundation is 60 cubits long and 20 cubits wide. The portico at the front is also 20 cubits wide and 20 cubits high. Solomon covers the inside of the temple with gold and carves cherubim on the walls.

**In the third scene**: Solomon builds the Most Holy Place at the rear of the temple, and it is 20 cubits long and 20 cubits wide. Gold covers the upper parts of the room as well. Solomon builds two cherubim and covers them with gold. The wings touch opposite walls of the Most Holy Place, and the wings touch each other in the middle of the room. Solomon also weaves a curtain that separates the Most Holy Place from the main area of the temple.

The characters in this passage are:

* Solomon
* Workers who build the temple and decorate the temple
* And Yahweh

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In the first scene, Solomon begins to build a temple for Yahweh. Solomon builds the temple in Jerusalem, on Mount Moriah. Yahweh had sent Abraham to sacrifice Isaac on Mount Moriah, but we are not sure if this mountain in Jerusalem is the same Mount Moriah where Abraham traveled. However, this site in Jerusalem is the place that David, Solomon's father, chose as the place to build a temple for Yahweh. Yahweh appeared to David at this place. At the time that Yahweh appeared to David, Araunah the Jebusite used this site as a place to thresh grain. The storyteller talks about Mount Moriah and David because these things are important to the people of Israel. The place where Solomon builds the temple should show how important Yahweh's temple should be to the Israelites.

Stop here and discuss as a group: Talk about a place that is important to people in your culture. What kind of language do you use to let people know this place is important? Pause this audio here.

Solomon begins construction on the second day of the second month of the year, which would have been in the middle of spring, when the weather gets warmer and plants begin to grow. Solomon starts building in his fourth year as king over Israel.

In this passage, even though the storyteller talks about how Solomon builds the temple, Solomon actually directs many men to build and to decorate the temple. When the storyteller says Solomon built the temple, we know that Solomon directs his workers to do the work.

Stop here and discuss as a group: How will you talk about this so that people will understand that many people worked on the temple even though the passage only talks about Solomon? Pause this audio here.

In the second scene, Solomon begins to build the foundation of the temple. In Solomon's culture, a foundation is part of the structure of a building that supports the building and provides stability. People usually placed strong rocks at the bottom of the structure and built on top of that. The foundation of the temple is 60 cubits long and 20 cubits wide, using the older cubit measurement. During this time, people probably used two different lengths to measure a cubit, and Solomon used the older standard length. The portico or porch at the entrance to the temple was 20 cubits wide and 20 cubits high.

Solomon covers the inside of the temple with pure gold. First, Solomon covers the walls of the temple with wood planks, and then Solomon covers those planks with gold. The walls have pictures of palm trees and decorative chains on them. Solomon probably carves the pictures of palm trees and chains on the wooden walls. People made chains out of metal links that they connected together. Solomon uses expensive or precious stones to decorate the temple and to make it beautiful. The workmen may have laid these stones in patterns on the floor of the main area of the temple. The gold Solomon uses comes from the land of Parvaim, but we do not know where this land may have been. Solomon covers the ceiling beams, doorframes, walls, and doors with gold. Solomon orders craftsmen to carve pictures of cherubim, or angels, into the walls of the temple.

Stop here and discuss as a group: How do people in your culture decorate important religious places? How do people talk about these special places? Pause this audio here.

In the third scene, Solomon builds the Most Holy Place. The Most Holy Place is 20 cubits long and 20 cubits wide. From another passage in the Bible, we know that the Most Holy Place is also 20 cubits high. Solomon uses 600 talents of fine gold to cover the walls of the Most Holy Place. This amount of gold may be the exact amount used, or it may represent a gift from the 12 tribes of Israel or a donation from Solomon's income.

Solomon uses gold nails in the construction of the Most Holy Place. The gold nails weigh about 50 shekels. Workers probably make these nails from iron and cover them with gold. Solomon also covers the upper parts of the Most Holy Place with gold. We do not know exactly what the upper parts of the Most Holy Place were.

Inside the Most Holy Place, Solomon carves two cherubim, or angels. Men probably make the cherubim from wood and then cover the cherubim with gold. Each cherub has two wings. The total length of the cherubim's wings is 20 cubits. The first cherub has one wing that measures 5 cubits long, and the wing touches one wall of the Most Holy Place. The other wing of the first cherub also measures 5 cubits, and it touches one of the wings of the second cherub in the middle of the Most Holy Place. In the same way, the second cherub has one wing that measures 5 cubits long and touches the wall on the other side of the Most Holy Place. The second wing of the second cherub also measures 5 cubits long, and it touches the wing of the first cherub in the middle of the Most Holy Place. Again, the total length of all the cherubim's wings is 20 cubits. The cherubim stand on their feet, and the cherubim look out from the Most Holy Place toward the main area of the temple.

Solomon's workmen make a curtain that separates the main area of the temple from the Most Holy Place. Craftsmen weave the curtain with fine linen and cloth that people wove with blue, purple, and red yarn. The craftsmen weave cherubim into the design of the curtain.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 3:1–14 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has three scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* Solomon
* Workers who build the temple and decorate the temple
* And Yahweh

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

Solomon begins to build Yahweh's temple in Jerusalem, on Mount Moriah. David chose this place to build Yahweh's temple because Yahweh had appeared to David there, where Araunah the Jebusite threshed grain. Solomon begins to build the temple in mid\-spring, on the second day of the second month of the year. Solomon had been king for four years when Solomon began to build the temple.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am happy that David chose this place for my temple because this place is important; or
* I am pleased that Solomon is continuing the work of building my temple that Solomon's father, David, began.

Continue the drama.

Solomon builds the foundation of the temple using the old measure of a cubit. The foundation is 60 cubits long and 20 cubits wide. The portico at the front of the temple is also 20 cubits wide, and it is probably 20 cubits high. Solomon covers the entire inside of the temple with pure gold. Solomon uses wood planks for the walls of the temple and then covers those planks with gold. In the gold, Solomon's men carve palm trees and chain patterns to decorate the walls. Solomon uses expensive stones to decorate the temple. The pure gold Solomon uses comes from the land of Parvaim. Solomon covers the ceiling beams, doorframes, walls, and doors in the temple with gold and decorates the walls with cherubim.

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing workers who build and decorate the temple, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We are happy to get to build the temple for Yahweh; or
* We work hard to make Yahweh's temple beautiful according to Solomon's plans; or
* We are proud to use our strength and our skills to serve Yahweh.

Continue the drama.

Solomon builds the Most Holy Place at the rear of the temple. The Most Holy Place is 20 cubits long and 20 cubits wide. Solomon covers the inside with 600 talents of pure gold and uses nails that craftsmen covered with gold that weigh 50 shekels. Solomon covers the upper parts of the room with gold as well. Solomon builds two cherubim and covers each cherub with gold. Each cherub has two wings, and each wing is 5 cubits long. The first cherub has one wing that touches a wall, and its other wing touches a wing of the second cherub. The second cherub has one wing that touches the other wall, and one wing that touches the wing of the first cherub. The cherubim stand on their feet and look out from the Most Holy Place toward the main area of the temple. Solomon also weaves a curtain made of blue, purple, and red yarn and fine linen. Workers sew images of cherubim into the curtain, and people hang the curtain so that it separates the Most Holy Place from the main area of the temple.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel excited to build Yahweh's temple and to decorate it with such beautiful materials; or
* I feel humbled that Yahweh allows me to build the temple even though my father, David, planned to build it.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 3:1–14 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

If you have already translated 1 Kings 6:1–13 and 1 Kings 6:14–38, listen to your translation again to remind yourselves which words you used to translate that passage. Pause this audio here.

Solomon begins to build the **temple** for **Yahweh**. The temple is the place where the Israelites worshiped Yahweh. Yahweh is the name God gave himself. This name reminds people of God's covenant with the Israelites. Use the same words or phrases for temple and Yahweh that you used in previous passages. For more information on temple and Yahweh, refer to the Master Glossary.

Solomon builds the temple in **Jerusalem**, where Yahweh appeared to Solomon's father, **David**. David established Jerusalem as the capital of Israel. Use the same words or phrases for Jerusalem and David as you used in previous passages. For more information on Jerusalem and David, refer to the Master Glossary.

Solomon lays the foundation for the temple of **God**, and it is 60 cubits long and 20 cubits wide. Use the same word for God as you have used previously, and refer to God in the Master Glossary for more information.

The portico at the front of the temple is 20 cubits long and 20 cubits high. A cubit is a way to measure how long something is. One cubit is about as long as a man's arm from his elbow to his fingertips, or 50 centimeters. A portico, which people sometimes call a porch, is part of a structure that has columns instead of walls to hold up the roof.

Stop here and discuss as a group what words or phrases you will use for **cubit** and **portico**. Look up cubit in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon covers the entire main hall with wood planks that people made from **cypress**, **fir**, or **juniper** trees. Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for this wood. Use the same word you have used in the previous passage.

Solomon covers the entire inside of the temple with pure **gold**. Gold is a yellow precious metal that people use to make jewelry and for decoration. Use the same word or phrase for gold as you used in previous passages.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of gold metal. Pause this audio here.

Solomon covers every part of the inside of the temple with gold. Solomon covers the ceiling beams, door frames, walls, and doors with gold. People make ceiling beams from wood. Then, people place these beams across the tops of a building's walls to help support the roof. People make door frames out of wood to decorate and give stability to a doorway. People use walls to help give structure to a building and to hold up the roof. People use doors as openings to move into and out of a building, and as openings to move between rooms of a building.

Stop here and discuss as a group what words or phrases you will use for **ceiling beams**, **door frames**, **walls**, and **doors**. If you have already translated these words in another book of the Bible, use the same words or phrases that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon uses wood panels for the walls and covers them with gold. Solomon uses expensive stones to decorate the temple. Solomon also carves pictures of palm trees, chain patterns, and cherubim into the gold walls. A cherub is a type of angel, and cherubim means more than one angel. Cherubim seem to guard especially holy or sacred places.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of **cherubim**. Discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for cherubim. Look up cherubim in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon builds the Most Holy Place, and it is 20 cubits long and 20 cubits wide. The Most Holy Place contained the two cherubim that Solomon carved and the ark of the covenant. Yahweh lived in the Most Holy Place, and no one was allowed to enter the Most Holy Place except Yahweh's high priest.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **Most Holy Place**. Look up Most Holy Place in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this phrase in another book of the Bible, use the same words that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon weaves a curtain that Solomon made of fine linen and blue, purple, and red yarn to separate the Most Holy Place from the rest of the temple. A curtain is a piece of material that people hang from the top to create a covering or screen.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of blue, purple, and red yarn. Look at a picture of a **curtain**. Discuss what word or phrase you will use for curtain, and remind yourselves of the words you used for the colors blue, purple, and red in the previous passage. If you have already translated these words in another book of the Bible, use the same words that you have used there.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.
