## 2 Chronicles 3:15–4:10 (id: eng-2ch-p6-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 3:15–4:10 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 3:15–4:10 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 3:15–4:10 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous passage, you heard about how Solomon built Yahweh's temple at the site that David, Solomon's father, had chosen. Solomon covered the entire inside of the temple, which includes the main hall and the Most Holy Place, with gold. Solomon carved decorations into the walls and placed a curtain in front of the Most Holy Place.

Stop here and listen: If you have already translated 1 Kings 7:13–39, which contains the same information, listen to your translation of that passage as a group. Pause this audio here.

In this passage, the storyteller continues to describe how Solomon decorates the outside of the temple and makes objects for people to use in worship. However, the storyteller does not tell us exactly who does the work. From another passage in the Bible, we know Solomon commands a man named Huram from the city of Tyre to create all the bronze objects outside the temple.

In front of the temple, Solomon builds two pillars. We know from another passage in the Bible that Solomon makes these pillars out of bronze. Solomon probably makes these pillars for decoration, and the pillars are probably not connected to the temple building. These pillars measure about 35 cubits or 16 meters high. People use cubits to measure length. In another passage of the Bible, the storyteller measures each pillar to be 18 cubits high. In this passage, the storyteller might be talking about how long the two pillars are when someone lays them down one after the other. Together, the pillars measure 35 cubits long. Or the storyteller might be adding up different measurements of just one pillar, like the pillar's height, width, and circumference, to get the same measurement of 35 cubits.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of a pillar. Pause this audio here.

On top of each pillar, Solomon puts a bronze capital, or a special decorative top. These capitals measure 5 cubits or 2\.5 meters high. Solomon decorates the capitals with bronze chains that may have looked like braids. Solomon makes 100 bronze images that look like pomegranates and hangs them from the chains. Pomegranates are reddish fruits about the size of a human fist. Solomon attaches the pomegranates to the chains. From another passage in the Bible, we know Solomon decorates each capital with 2 chains and 200 pomegranates.

Stop here and look at a picture of metal braided chains and a picture of pomegranates. Pause this audio here.

Solomon sets the pillars in front of the temple. Solomon puts one pillar to the right or south of the temple entrance. Solomon names this pillar Jakin, which means, "Yahweh will establish." Solomon puts the other pillar to the left or north side of the temple entrance. Solomon names this pillar Boaz, which means, "strength is in Yahweh." Solomon may name the pillars this way to remind the people that Yahweh lives in the temple.

Solomon makes an altar out of bronze that measures 20 cubits or 9 meters long, 20 cubits or 9 meters wide, and 10 cubits or 4\.5 meters high. These measurements may be for the base or foundation of the altar, and stairs may have led up to a platform. An altar is a place where people make sacrifices to Yahweh. An altar is like a table or platform where people put sacrifices, and people often burn these sacrifices on the altar.

Stop here as a group and look at a drawing of the altar. Discuss as a group: How do people worship gods in your culture? Pause this audio here.

Then Solomon makes a large round basin from bronze, and Solomon names the basin "the Sea." The Sea measures 10 cubits, or 4\.5 meters, across from edge to edge and 5 cubits, or just over 2 meters, high. People use a piece of string to measure around the rim of the Sea. The rim is 30 cubits or 13\.5 meters around the circumference. Below the rim of the Sea, Solomon places figures to decorate the Sea. These figures may look like oxen bulls, but in another passage in the Bible, the storyteller says these decorations look like gourds, which are melon\-like fruits. Solomon uses 10 figures for every cubit all the way around the Sea and places them in two rows. Solomon casts the figures at the same time as the Sea and uses one mold for it all.

Solomon places the Sea on the figures of 12 large bulls that people probably make out of bronze or stone. These bulls are much larger than the decorations around the rim of the Sea. Three bulls face north, three face west, three face south, and three face east. The Sea sits on the backs of the bulls, and the bulls all face outward. The Sea is about a handbreadth thick, or about the width of four fingers or 8 centimeters. Solomon shapes the edge of the Sea to look like the rim of a cup or like the petals of a lily. A lily is a large red or white flower with petals that curve outward from the center. In this passage, the storyteller says the Sea holds 3,000 baths of water. In another passage in the Bible, the storyteller says the Sea holds 2,000 baths of water. We do not know exactly how many liters are in one bath, but the Sea probably holds between 40,000 and 80,000 liters of water.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of a bull and a picture of a lily. Pause this audio here.

Next, Solomon makes 10 smaller basins for washing. Solomon probably places five basins on the south side and five basins on the north side of the temple entrance, or Solomon may place the basins on the south and north sides of the Sea. People use the basins to wash either the things they use to offer burnt offerings or to wash the animals before people sacrifice the animals. A burnt offering is a form of sacrifice that people bring to Yahweh. However, priests use the Sea to wash their hands and feet.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of the smaller wash basins. Pause this audio here.

Solomon then makes 10 lampstands out of gold, and Solomon makes them exactly the way Yahweh says people should make these lampstands. Yahweh may have told King David how to make the lampstands, and then David told Solomon. We do not know exactly how the lampstands looked, but they probably had several wicks like candles that people could light. Solomon places the lampstands inside the main hall of the temple, with five on the south side and five on the north side. Solomon also makes 10 tables and places them in the main hall of the temple with the lampstands, 5 on the south side and 5 on the north side. From another passage in the Bible, we know Solomon probably makes the tables out of wood and covers them with gold. We do not know if Solomon puts the lampstands on the tables or if Solomon uses the tables for the bread that represents Yahweh's presence. Solomon also makes 100 small gold bowls. People probably use these bowls to hold the blood of animals before they sprinkle the blood on the altar.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of a gold lampstand. Pause this audio here.

Solomon builds walls around the inner court where only priests could enter. Solomon also builds walls and doors for the larger court, where any person could enter. Solomon covers the doors with bronze.

Stop here and look at a diagram of the two different courtyards in Solomon's temple. Pause this audio here.

Solomon places the Sea on the southeastern side of the temple.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 3:15–4:10 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has three scenes.

**In the first scene:** Solomon makes two large bronze pillars to stand in the front entrance of the temple. Solomon decorates the pillars with chains and pomegranates and names the pillars Jakin and Boaz.

**In the second scene:** Solomon makes the altar and the Sea. Solomon decorates the Sea with figures and places the Sea on the backs of 12 large bull statues that all face outward.

**In the third scene:** Solomon makes 10 wash basins, 10 gold lamp stands, 10 tables, and 100 small gold bowls. Solomon builds walls and doors for the inner and outer courtyards. Solomon puts the Sea at the southeast corner of the temple.

The characters in this passage are:

* Solomon
* Huram—even though the storyteller does not mention Huram's name in this passage, we know Huram is the main worker
* Workers who help Huram
* And Yahweh

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In scene one, Solomon makes two large bronze pillars for the front of the temple. We know from another passage in the Bible that Solomon did not actually make the pillars. Instead, Solomon commands a man named Huram from the city of Tyre to cast or mold objects out of bronze. Even though the storyteller says Solomon makes the pillars and many other bronze objects in this passage, we know that Solomon commands Huram to make things out of bronze.

Stop here and discuss as a group: How will you show that Solomon himself did not do the construction work on the temple, but Solomon ordered workers to do it? Pause this audio here.

As you translate this passage, you may find several different ways to interpret some of the words and measurements. You may want to follow the interpretation of the Bible you use for translation. Pause this audio here.

Solomon makes the pillars 35 cubits tall. Solomon also makes capitals, or decorative tops, for the tops of each pillar that measure 5 cubits high. Solomon decorates the capitals with bronze chains and images of pomegranates. Each pillar has two chains with 100 pomegranates attached to each chain. Solomon puts the pillars in front of the temple entrance. Solomon puts one pillar on the south or right side of the entrance, and Solomon names the pillar Jakin, which means "Yahweh will establish." Solomon places the other pillar on the north or left side of the temple entrance, and Solomon names this pillar Boaz, which means "strength is in Yahweh." We do not know if these pillars hold up the roof of the porch, or if they are standing on their own.

In the second scene, Solomon makes a bronze altar that measures 20 cubits long, 20 cubits wide, and 10 cubits high. You will remember that a cubit measures about 50 centimeters long. We do not know exactly how the altar looks, but we know people use it to offer animals as burnt offerings when they worship Yahweh. Next, Solomon makes a large, round bronze basin and names it "the Sea." The Sea measures 10 cubits across from rim to rim, and the Sea is 5 cubits high. To measure all the way around the Sea, people use a piece of string that measures 30 cubits.

Below the rim all the way around the Sea, Solomon places decorations in two rows. For every cubit around the Sea, Solomon uses 10 figures for decoration. In this passage, the storyteller says the figures look like bulls. Bulls are large animals with four legs. These bulls were probably oxen, which means someone had castrated them. In another passage in the Bible, however, the storyteller says the decorations around the Sea look like gourds, which are fruits that look like melons. Solomon makes the Sea and the figure decorations all at once with the same mold.

Stop here and discuss as a group: How will you describe the decorations Solomon uses around the Sea? Remember that you can follow the interpretation of the Bible you use for translation when different interpretations of the same words may exist. Pause this audio here.

Solomon places the Sea on 12 large figures that look like bulls. The Sea sits on the backs of the bulls, and the bulls face outward. Three bulls face in each direction, north, west, south, and east. The Sea measures a handbreadth thick, and Solomon makes the rim curve outward like the rim of a cup or the petals on a lily. The Sea can hold 3,000 baths. People use baths to measure the volume of liquids. We are not sure exactly how many liters may be in a bath, but the Sea probably held between 40,000 and 80,000 liters.

In the third scene, Solomon makes 10 smaller wash basins out of bronze. Solomon puts the wash basins outside the temple, 5 on the south side and 5 on the north side. People use these smaller wash basins to wash the things they use to sacrifice animals or to wash the animals that people will burn as offerings to Yahweh. But the priests use the Sea to wash their hands and feet.

Next, Solomon makes 10 lampstands out of gold according to the instructions Yahweh had given. Solomon puts these lampstands inside the main hall of the temple, 5 on the south side of the hall and 5 on the north side. Solomon also makes 10 tables for the main hall inside the temple. Solomon puts 5 on the south and 5 on the north side of the main hall. Solomon may have placed the lampstands on the tables.

Solomon makes 100 small gold bowls. People use these bowls to hold the blood from the animals they sacrifice, and then people sprinkle the blood on the altar.

Solomon makes walls for the inner courtyard where only priests could go. Solomon also makes walls and a door for the larger courtyard where any person could go. Solomon covers the doors to the courtyards with bronze. Solomon places the Sea outside the temple on the southeast side.

Stop here and discuss as a group: Solomon places each object in a specific location. Look at a diagram that shows where Solomon placed the objects. How will you describe the location of each object based on the diagram? Pause this audio here.

Cultures have different ways to describe directions like north, south, east, and west. You will remember that the sun rises in the east. How will you describe the different directions for your culture? Pause this audio here.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 3:15–4:10 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has three scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* Solomon
* Huram—even though the storyteller does not mention Huram's name in this passage, we know Huram is the main worker
* Workers who help Huram
* And Yahweh

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

Solomon tells Huram to make two bronze pillars for the front of the temple. On top of each pillar, Solomon puts a capital, bronze chains, and bronze images of pomegranates for decoration. Solomon places one pillar on the south side of the temple entrance, and Solomon names that pillar Jakin. Solomon places the other pillar on the north side of the entrance, and Solomon names that pillar Boaz.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Huram, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am happy that I can use my bronze\-working skills to help Solomon make Yahweh's temple beautiful; or
* I feel honored because I get to work on Yahweh's temple.

Continue the drama.

Solomon builds a bronze altar, and Solomon makes the large, round bronze basin called the Sea. The Sea measures 10 cubits rim to rim and 5 cubits high. People use a string that is 30 cubits long to measure around the Sea. Solomon decorates the Sea under its rim with two rows of figures that look like bulls. Solomon uses 10 bulls for every cubit around the Sea. Solomon makes the Sea and the decorative figures at the same time from the same mold.

Solomon places the Sea on 12 larger figures that look like bulls. The bulls face outward, three in each direction, and the sea sits on their backs. The Sea measures a handbreadth thick, and its rim curves out like the rim of a cup or the petal of a lily. The Sea can hold about 3,000 baths of water.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel pleased to see my people spend so much time and resources to honor me; or
* I feel happy because I know that, even though I could make my own house, Solomon and my people work hard to honor me by building this beautiful temple for me to live in.

Continue the drama.

Solomon makes 10 smaller wash basins for the outside of the temple. Solomon places 5 basins on the south side and 5 basins on the north side of the temple. People use these basins to wash the things they use to offer burnt offerings, but priests use the Sea to wash their hands and feet.

Next, Solomon makes 10 lampstands and 10 tables from gold. Solomon places these objects inside the main hall of the temple, 5 of each on the south side and 5 of each on the north side. Solomon also makes 100 gold bowls for sprinkling blood.

Solomon builds the walls of the inner courtyard that only priests can use. Solomon also builds walls and doors for the larger outer courtyard, where any person can enter. Solomon covers the courtyard doors with bronze. Solomon places the Sea outside the temple on the southeast corner.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel hopeful that Yahweh will like all the objects we make for Yahweh's temple; or
* I feel confident that Yahweh's temple will be beautiful and will use the best materials available.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 3:15–4:10 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

Stop here and listen: If you have already translated 1 Kings 7:13–39, which contains the same information, listen to your translation of that passage as a group. Pause this audio here.

Solomon makes two bronze metal pillars for the front entrance of the **temple**. People usually make tall vertical structures called "pillars" out of metal, wood, or stone. Pillars may help to support a building, or people may use pillars as decoration. Israelites worship Yahweh in the temple. Use the same word or phrase for temple as you have used in previous passages. For more information on temple, refer to the Master Glossary.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of a **pillar**. Discuss what word or phrase you will use for pillar. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon makes the pillars 35 cubits long. People use cubits to measure length, and a cubit measures about 50 centimeters. Use the same word or phrase for **cubit** as you used in previous passages. For more information on cubit, refer to the Master Glossary.

Solomon places a capital on top of each pillar. The word capital is special language that refers to an object that Solomon uses to decorate the top of each pillar. Because these pillars probably do not support the roof of the temple, the capitals sit on the top of each pillar.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **capital**. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon makes chains and images of pomegranates to decorate the capitals on the pillars. People probably weave bronze metal together to make chains that look like braids. Pomegranates are reddish fruits about the same size as a human's fist. People probably make images of these pomegranates out of bronze metal.

Stop here and look at a picture of **chains** and a **pomegranate**. Discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for chain and pomegranate. If you have already translated these words in another book of the Bible, use the same words that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon makes an **altar** out of **bronze**. An altar is a place where people make sacrifices to Yahweh. An altar is like a table or platform where people put sacrifices, and people often burn these sacrifices on the altar. Bronze is a metal that people make by combining copper and tin. Use the same word or phrase for altar and bronze as you used in previous passages. For more information on altar and bronze, refer to the Master Glossary.

Solomon makes the Sea out of metal and places decorations that may look like bulls just below the rim. A bull is a male ox.

Stop here and look at a picture of a **bull** as a group. If you choose to use the interpretation that says these are bulls, discuss what word or phrase you will use for bull. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon makes the Sea about one handbreadth thick. People use a handbreadth, which measures about 4 fingers or 8 centimeters, to measure how thick something is.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **handbreadth**. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon makes the rim of the Sea curve outward like the rim of a cup or the petals on a lily. A lily is a large red or white flower with petals that curve out away from its center.

Stop here and look at a picture of a **lily**. Discuss what word or phrase you will use for lily. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon makes the Sea large enough to hold 3,000 **baths**. We do not know exactly how much a bath might be, but the Sea probably holds between 40,000 and 80,000 liters of water. Baths measure the volume of liquid. Use the same word or phrase for bath as you used in previous passages.

Solomon makes 10 wash basins to place outside the temple. People use the wash basins to clean the things people use to make **burnt offerings**. A burnt offering is a form of sacrifice that people bring to Yahweh. Look up burnt offering in the master Glossary for more information. Use the same word that you have used in previous passages.

Although people use the wash basins to clean the things for burnt offerings, priests use the Sea to wash themselves. Priests are people who work in the temple of Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **priest**. Look up priest in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Solomon makes 10 lampstands and 100 small bowls out of **gold**. Gold is a precious yellow metal. Use the same word or phrase for gold as you have used in previous passages.

People light wicks on lampstands to use as a source of light.

Stop here and discuss what word or phrase you will use for **lampstand**. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 3:15-4:10

## 2 Chronicles 8:1–18 (id: eng-2ch-p15-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 8:1–18 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 8:1–18 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

B Setting the Stage (2 Chronicles 8:1–18\)

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 8:1–18 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous passage, Solomon finished building the temple of Yahweh and the royal palace. Yahweh appeared to Solomon and said that if Solomon was faithful to Yahweh, Solomon's descendants would reign over Israel forever. But if Solomon or his descendants turned away from Yahweh and worshiped other gods, Yahweh would remove the people of Israel from their land and reject the temple Solomon had built for Yahweh. In this passage, the storyteller describes Solomon's building projects.

If you have already translated the book of 1 Kings, then you have already translated one version of this story. It might be helpful to go back and listen to how you translated that story. If the book of 1 Kings is available in your language, stop here and listen to 1 Kings 9:10–25\. Pause this audio here.

It took Solomon 20 years to finish building the temple and the king's palace. Afterward, Solomon focused on building new cities and making existing cities stronger. King Hiram of Tyre gave Solomon some cities in new territory. This means that those cities used to be part of Hiram's territory, but now they were in Solomon's territory. Bible scholars do not know where these cities were located; most likely, they were located in the northern part of Israel, near the border with Hiram's territory.

Solomon's army attacked and conquered a place called Hamath Zobah. Bible scholars are not sure if this is the name of a town or a region. Solomon also rebuilt or fortified towns named Tadmor, Upper Beth Horon, Lower Beth Horon, and Baalath. Tadmor is located northeast of Israel, on the edge of a desert. Upper Beth Horon and Lower Beth Horon were two cities very close to each other. These cities were located in a valley near Jerusalem; Lower Beth Horon was down in the bottom of the valley, while Upper Beth Horon was located at the top of the valley. Bible scholars are not sure where Baalath was located.

Stop here and look at a map that shows the locations of the cities of Tadmor, Upper Beth Horon, and Lower Beth Horon. Pause this audio here.

Solomon built cities to store extra goods in. These cities are called storage cities or supply centers. A king might store things like grain, oil, or wine in a storage city. Or a king might use a city to store materials for battle, like weapons and things soldiers use to protect themselves in battle.

King Solomon used forced labor, or slaves, for his building projects. This was very common in the ancient world. The people Solomon used as slaves were the descendants of the Canaanites that the Israelites had not been able to drive out of the land of Israel. These people were descendants of the Hittites, Amorites, Perizzites, Hivites, and Jebusites. The Law that Yahweh gave to Moses for Israel did not allow Solomon to make Israelites slaves, so Solomon made the Canaanites do the hard labor of building cities. The Israelites served Solomon as soldiers, officers, government officials, and overseers.

Stop here and discuss these questions: How common is forced labor or slavery in your area? If it is not common now, was it ever common in the past? Which people were slaves? What do you think about the fact that King Solomon had slaves? Pause this audio here.

Solomon had a strong army, which included chariots and charioteers. A chariot is a kind of cart that horses pull, with at least two soldiers riding in the chariot. People used chariots to fight battles. One soldier controlled the horses, while the other soldier could shoot arrows or stab his opponents with his sword. A charioteer is a soldier who is specially trained to ride in a chariot.

Stop here and look at a picture of a chariot with its horses and charioteers. Pause this audio here.

Early in his reign, Solomon married the daughter of Pharaoh, the king of Egypt. Before Solomon finished building the king's house, Pharaoh's daughter lived in the City of David. Most Bible scholars believe that the City of David was an older part of Jerusalem, where King David once lived, and that Solomon built the king's house in a different part of Jerusalem. When Pharaoh's daughter moved into the house that Solomon built for her, Pharaoh's daughter just moved from one part of Jerusalem to another. In this scene, the storyteller explains that Solomon had another reason why Solomon wanted to move his Egyptian wife out of the City of David—the ark of the covenant had once stayed there in a tent. Solomon felt that the ark made that place holy. Because Solomon's wife was Egyptian, she was not a worshipper of Yahweh, and so Solomon felt it was not acceptable for her to be in a space where the ark of the covenant had once been.

Once Solomon had finished building the temple of Yahweh, Solomon assigned the priests and the Levites to do the daily activities in the temple. The Levites were one of the 12 tribes of Israel. Yahweh chose the Levites to be in charge of taking care of the temple and leading all of the activities there. The priests were descendants of Aaron, the brother of Moses. The gatekeepers of the temple were also Levites. The gatekeepers' most important job was to guard the entrances to the temple. But the gatekeepers also took care of all the things inside the Temple, including the special flour and wine that the priests used every day. Solomon did all of this just as Moses and King David had commanded. Solomon also made all the offerings and sacrifices at the temple that the Israelites' religious law required.

King Hiram of Tyre also gave Solomon ships to sail on the ocean. Solomon went to see these ships in two cities called Ezion Geber and Elath.

Stop here and look at a map of Israel that shows the locations of Ezion Geber and Elath. Keep looking at this map during the following discussion. Pause this audio here.

Ezion Geber and Elath were far to the south in the territory of Edom. Solomon controlled this territory, so it was no problem for Solomon to keep his ships there.

Stop here and look at a picture of a ship. Pause this audio here.

From Ezion Geber and Elath, Solomon sent ships to Ophir to bring back gold. Bible scholars do not agree about where Ophir was located, but it was probably somewhere in Arabia or northeastern Africa.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 8:1–18 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has four scenes.

**In the first scene:** After Solomon completed building the temple of Yahweh and the king's house, Solomon rebuilt and fortified many cities throughout Israel.

**In the second scene:** The storyteller describes the people who did the work of building the temple of Yahweh, the king's house, and building up cities. The storyteller also tells us that Solomon moved his wife from the City of David to the new palace Solomon had built for her when it was finished.

**In the third scene**: Solomon assigned the priests, Levites, and gatekeepers to do their duties in the temple of Yahweh. Solomon made offerings and sacrifices at the temple and observed all the religious holidays.

**In the fourth scene**: Solomon goes to two port cities in the far south of Israel to see the ships that Hiram, king of Tyre, sent Solomon. These ships sailed to Ophir and brought back 15,000 kilograms of gold.

The characters in this passage are:

* Solomon
* Hiram
* The Israelite people
* The Amorite, Hittite, Perizzite, Hivite, and Jebusite people
* Solomon's Israelite soldiers, servants, charioteers, and horsemen
* Pharaoh's daughter
* The Levites and priests
* The officers and captains on the ships that Hiram gave Solomon
* And Yahweh

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In the first scene, at the end of 20 years, King Solomon finished building the temple of Yahweh and Solomon's own palace. Hiram gave Solomon some cities. This means that those cities now became part of Solomon's kingdom. These cities existed before they became part of Solomon's kingdom, so Solomon only had to make the cities bigger, stronger, or better.

Stop here and discuss these questions: Think of as many ways as you can in your language to describe a situation where a person takes something that already exists and makes it bigger, stronger, or better. Which of these words could you use to describe Solomon making these cities bigger, stronger, or better? Pause this audio here.

If you have already translated the book of 1 Kings, you might remember that Solomon gave King Hiram 20 towns in the northern part of Israel. Here, we find out that Hiram also gave some towns to Solomon. This means that those towns used to be part of Hiram's territory, but now they were part of Solomon's territory. The storyteller does not mention why Solomon and Hiram exchanged these towns. Most Bible scholars think this exchange had something to do with payment for the cedar wood that Hiram gave to Solomon.

When the storyteller says that Solomon settled Israelites in these cities, it probably does not mean that Solomon forced these people to go to these cities against their will. Solomon probably just encouraged Israelites to go live in those cities.

Solomon conquered a place called Hamath Zobah. Bible scholars do not agree on what "Hamath Zobah" means. There was a town called Hamath north of Israel. But there was also a region called Hamath and a region called Zobah. These two regions were within the territory called Aram, which King David conquered before Solomon was born.

Stop here and look at a map that shows the locations of Israel, the town of Hamath, the region of Hamath, and the region of Zobah. Continue looking at this map during the following discussion. Pause this audio here.

Bible scholars are not sure if this statement means that Solomon conquered a town called Hamath in the region called Zobah, or if Solomon conquered the region called Hamath and the region called Zobah. Bible scholars are also not sure why Solomon had to conquer, or re\-conquer, territory that Solomon's father had already conquered. It is possible that either the town of Hamath Zobah tried to rebel against Solomon's rule, or that the entire region of Hamath and Zobah tried to rebel against Solomon's rule. Because we are not certain, most Bible translations simply say that Solomon conquered Hamath Zobah and do not say whether it was a town or a region.

Solomon rebuilt, or fortified, a city called Tadmor. Tadmor is located near a water source but is surrounded by desert. Tadmor was located about 485 kilometers north of Jerusalem. This city is also sometimes called Palmyra. You may have heard both names for this city when you listened to different versions of this passage. We recommend that you use the name Tadmor for this city.

Solomon built storage cities in the region of Hamath. Solomon built many other storage cities, or supply centers, all through the territory that Solomon controlled. A king might store things like grain, oil, salt, or wine in a storage city. The king stored these things for use in case there was not enough food. The king might also use a city to store supplies for battle, like weapons and protective items for soldiers. If the country went to war, these supplies would help the people to be better prepared than their enemy.

Stop here and discuss these questions: In your country, how do people store extra supplies? Where do people store the supplies? Who controls the supplies? How does the military or national government store its battle supplies? Pause this audio here.

Solomon made Upper Beth Horon and Lower Beth Horon into fortified cities. This means that Solomon built walls around the cities, with gates and bars to control who could go in and out. Walls, gates, and bars also made it easier to defend these cities from attackers.

Stop here and discuss how you will describe a fortified city. Pause this audio here.

Solomon also built up a town called Baalath. Bible scholars are not sure where Baalath was located. Baalath was somewhere in the territory of the tribe of Dan, which was west and northwest of Jerusalem. But the tribe of Dan was not able to stay in this land and lost it to the Philistines. The tribe of Dan moved north and eventually settled in a city called Dan.

In the second scene, the storyteller tells us about the people who did the building work for Solomon. The Hittites, Amorites, Perizzites, Hivites, and Jebusites were all descendants of a man named Canaan. Yahweh told the Israelites to drive all of the Canaanites out of the land, but the Israelites did not drive them all out. Some groups of Canaanites still lived in parts of the Israelite territory. These are the people Solomon used to do forced labor. It was against the Law that Moses gave to Israel for the Israelites to do forced labor. If Solomon used Israelites for his building projects, Solomon would have to hire these Israelites and pay them as regular workers. It cost less to use forced labor, so Solomon made the Canaanites do the construction work on Solomon's building projects.

You might have heard a few different ways to say this when you listened to different versions of this passage. Some translations say Solomon conscripted the forced laborers. Other translations say that Solomon raised slave labor, while yet others simply say that King Solomon used forced labor to build the cities. Each of these is just a different way to say that King Solomon forced the non\-Israelites in Solomon's territory to do this hard physical work, and that these people did not have the choice to say no.

Stop here and discuss this question: What are some ways in your language that you can say that Solomon forced the Canaanites to do this difficult work, and that the Canaanites did not have the choice to say no or change jobs? Pause this audio here.

The storyteller also says that the Amorites, Hittites, Perizzites, Hivites, and Jebusites are still slaves in Israel "to this day." This statement means that those people were enslaved in Israel at the time that the storyteller wrote this story down. It does not mean that those people are still slaves now.

Solomon could not make the Israelites do forced labor. The Israelites were the leaders and overseers of the people who did the work. In this scene, the storyteller uses four different words or phrases to describe the roles that the Israelites filled for Solomon's building projects. The first phrase is "men of war," which means men who fight in wars. You can also translate this as "soldiers" or "fighting men."

Stop here and try to think of as many words as you can in your language that have this meaning. Pause this audio here.

The second phrase is "chief captains." This is a phrase that describes the highest leaders of a group of people. You might have heard it translated as "officers," "chief officers," or "captains of his officers."

Stop here and try to think of as many words as you can in your language that have this meaning. Pause this audio here.

The third phrase is "commanders of chariots." If an army has many chariots, that army needs someone to tell the charioteers what to do in the battle. The commander of chariots probably rode in a chariot himself, but the commander's job was to tell the other charioteers where to go and what to do.

Stop here and discuss how to translate "commanders of chariots" in your language. Pause this audio here.

The fourth word the storyteller uses is a word for men who work with horses. This might be a soldier who rides on horses or a soldier who rides in a chariot. You might have heard this word translated as "cavalry," "horsemen," or "charioteer."

Stop here and try to think of as many words as you can in your language that have this meaning. Pause this audio here.

There were 250 chief officers who were overseers of the workers on Solomon's building projects.

When Solomon finished building the royal palace, Solomon moved his wife, Pharaoh's daughter, from her previous house in the City of David to the palace Solomon had built for her. Solomon had a religious motivation for this move. Solomon's father, King David, had brought the ark of the covenant into the City of David, where it stayed in a tent for many years. Solomon felt this made the City of David holy, because the ark represented the very presence of Yahweh. But Solomon's wife, who was Pharaoh's daughter, was Egyptian and did not worship Yahweh. Solomon felt it was not appropriate for Pharaoh's daughter to be in a place where the ark of the covenant had once been.

In the third scene, the storyteller says that Solomon regularly made burnt offerings to Yahweh on the altar in the temple for the Sabbaths, New Moon festivals, and three yearly feasts. Solomon did all of this in order to obey the Law of Moses, which describes how the Israelites had to worship Yahweh. The Sabbath was the seventh day of the week, and all the Israelites were supposed to rest and do no work on that day. The priests in the temple made a special offering on the Sabbath in addition to the normal daily offering. The storyteller tells us that Solomon came to the temple every Sabbath to participate in this offering. The night that there was a new moon was considered the beginning of a new month for the Israelites. At this time, the Israelites also made special sacrifices in the temple.

The three yearly feasts were called the Festival of Unleavened Bread, or Passover; the Festival of Weeks, or Pentecost; and the Festival of Tabernacles. If you have already translated the book of Deuteronomy, then you have already translated the names of these three festivals. It would be helpful to go back and listen to the part of Deuteronomy where Yahweh commands the Israelites to observe these three feasts.

Stop here and listen to Deuteronomy chapter 16 if it is available in your language. Pay close attention to the names you chose for the Festival of Unleavened Bread, the Festival of Weeks, and the Festival of Tabernacles. Pause this audio here.

Solomon was the one who assigned the priests and Levites to take care of the temple. Solomon followed instructions that Solomon's father, David, had left for Solomon. David had probably written these instructions down. One instruction was for the Levites to lead the people to praise Yahweh. Many of the Levites were musicians and singers, and one of their jobs was to lead the Israelites in singing songs of worship to Yahweh.

The storyteller calls David "the man of God." This means that David loved God with all his heart and was close to God. David was dedicated to doing what God wanted. In the Bible, the term "man of God" often refers to a prophet, and many people do consider King David to be a prophet. But in this passage, the phrase "man of God" describes David's devotion to God.

Stop here and talk about different ways that you could describe King David's devotion to God in your language. Pause this audio here.

At the end of this scene, the author provides a summary statement. The author says that all of the instructions that David had given concerning the priests, the Levites, and their work in the temple were done in detail. This shows that the author wants the audience to know for sure that people did everything correctly that David had said to do, and that all of Solomon's work was successful.

In scene four, Solomon traveled south to Ezion Geber and Elath to see the ships that King Hiram gave him. The two cities were located on the coast of a large, narrow body of water. You might have heard different names for this body of water when you listened to three different versions of the story. Some versions call it the Red Sea, others call it the Gulf of Aqaba. But most translations say that the cities were located on the coast or the seashore. Any of these translation choices is acceptable.

A ship is a very large boat, large enough to sail on the ocean. The boat had to be large enough to sail over large ocean waves and carry a lot of people and a lot of cargo for many days on the ocean.

Stop here and look at a picture of a ship on the ocean. Pause this audio here.

A sailor is a person who knows how to steer and control a ship. In the original language, the author calls these sailors "men, or servants, who know the sea." A large ship required many sailors to do different tasks to control the ship.

Stop here and discuss how you will translate "men who know the sea" into your language. Pause this audio here.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 8:1–18 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has four scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* Solomon
* Hiram
* The Israelite people
* The Amorite, Hittite, Perizzite, Hivite, and Jebusite people
* Solomon's Israelite soldiers, servants, charioteers, and horsemen
* Pharaoh's daughter
* The Levites and priests
* The officers and captains on the ships that Hiram gave Solomon
* And Yahweh

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

At the end of 20 years, during which Solomon built the temple of Yahweh and his own palace, Solomon built up the cities that Hiram had given him and settled Israelites in them. Solomon went to Hamath Zobah and captured it. Solomon also built up Tadmor in the desert and built storage cities in Hamath. Solomon rebuilt Upper Beth Horon and Lower Beth Horon as fortified cities, with walls, gates, and bars. And Solomon built Baalath and all his storage cities, and built cities for his chariots and horses. Solomon carried out all his plans for building in Jerusalem, Lebanon, and throughout all the territory that Solomon ruled over.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel confident that my country is strong and wealthy; or
* I feel happy that I was able to complete so many building projects; or
* I feel grateful to Yahweh for making me so successful.

Ask the person playing the Israelite people, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am happy that we have such a good king to lead our people; or
* I feel safe because we have a strong army and many storage cities; or
* I feel proud because my people are the strongest people in this part of the world; or
* I feel satisfied that all our hard work has brought good results.

Continue the drama.

Solomon used for forced labor all the people of Canaan, whom the Israelites had not killed when they took possession of the land. These included Hittites, Amorites, Perizzites, Hivites, and Jebusites, and their descendants are still slaves to this day. But Solomon did not use Israelites for forced labor. Israelites were the soldiers, officers, chariot commanders, and horsemen. There were 250 officials in charge of the forced labor who worked on the building projects. And Solomon moved his wife, the daughter of Pharaoh, from the City of David to the palace that Solomon built for her. Solomon said, "My wife must not live in King David's house, because the ark of Yahweh has been there, and it is holy ground."

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing the Hittites, Amorites, Perizzites, Hivites, and Jebusites, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am angry that my people are slaves; or
* I feel hopeless. First, our people lost our land, and now we are forced to build temples and palaces for our enemies; or
* I hate the Israelites. I wish my people could get away from the Israelites.

Ask the person playing King Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am relieved that we have finally completely conquered these Canaanites; or
* I feel hopeful that our people will continue to be wealthy and powerful for a long time; or
* I am pleased with the good work that my people have done.

Ask the people playing the Israelite soldiers, officers, commanders, and charioteers, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel happy and secure because our army is so big and strong; or
* I feel hopeful that our country has a bright future because of all the things that King Solomon has done; or
* I feel bad for these Canaanite slaves, but somebody has to do the hard labor; or
* I feel proud to serve such a good king in such a strong army.

Ask the person playing Pharaoh's daughter, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am angry that my father made me marry this Israelite and leave Egypt; or
* I feel honored to be a queen in Israel; or
* I miss worshipping my gods in the temples of Egypt; or
* I do not like that my own husband thinks I am unclean and not worthy to go into the temple of his God.

Continue the drama.

Then Solomon presented burnt offerings to Yahweh on the altar Solomon had built in front of the entry room of the temple. Solomon offered the sacrifices for the Sabbaths, the New Moon festivals, and the three yearly festivals—the Festival of Unleavened Bread, the Festival of Weeks, and the Festival of Tabernacles—just as Moses had commanded. Following the rules that Solomon's father, David, had made, Solomon organized the daily work of the priests in singing hymns and doing their work. Solomon also organized the gatekeepers into divisions for the gates of the temple, because this is what David, the man of God, had ordered. They did not turn aside from what the king had commanded concerning the priests and the Levites and the storehouses. So Solomon made sure that the people did all the work related to building the temple of Yahweh, from the day people laid the temple's foundation to the day people finished the building.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am so happy that the temple of Yahweh is finished and I can finally make sacrifices in it; or
* I know that my father, David, would be very happy to see this temple I have built and see all the worship people are doing in the temple; or
* I am grateful to Yahweh for allowing me to build Yahweh's temple.

Ask the people playing the priests and the Levites, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am amazed at how large and beautiful this temple is; or
* I am grateful that we have such a beautiful place to work and to worship Yahweh; or
* This temple is impressive, but it is nothing compared to how great Yahweh is.

Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am pleased that my people are following my directions and worshipping me; or
* This temple will be a place of great importance for thousands of years to come; or
* I am happy that everything is going according to plan.

Continue the drama.

Later, Solomon went to Ezion Geber and Elath, on the seashore in the land of Edom. Hiram sent him ships that Hiram's own officers commanded with experienced crews of sailors. These ships sailed to Ophir with Solomon's men and brought back to Solomon over 15,000 kilograms of gold.

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing Solomon's men, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* I am impressed because these ships are so big and valuable, and now they are ours! or
* I am amazed at how much gold we are bringing back to King Solomon; or
* I am happy because King Solomon will surely be pleased with all this gold; or
* It is a good time to be an Israelite! Our people are wealthy and powerful!

Ask the person playing King Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am grateful to Hiram for building these ships for me; or
* Now my people need to learn how to build our own ships and sail the ships; or
* I am happy with the large amount of gold that my sailors have brought back to Israel!

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 8:1–18 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

At the end of 20 years, during which Solomon built the **temple** of **Yahweh** and his own **palace**, Solomon built up the cities that Hiram had given him, and Solomon settled **Israelites** in them. Solomon went to **Hamath Zobah** and captured it. Be sure to translate temple, Yahweh, palace, and Israelites in the same way you did previously. See the Master Glossary for more information on temple, Yahweh, and Israelites. Be sure to translate the name Hamath the same way as you did in chapter seven. Be sure to translate the name Zobah the same way as you did in 1 Chronicles chapter 18\.

Solomon also fortified Tadmor in the **wilderness** and built storage cities in Hamath. Be sure to translate wilderness the same way you did in chapter one. See the Master Glossary for more information on wilderness. Remember that a storage city is a city where a king might store food and weapons. Be sure to translate "storage city" the same way you did previously.

Stop here and discuss how you will translate the name **Tadmor** into your language. Pause this audio here.

He rebuilt **Upper Beth Horon** and **Lower Beth Horon** as fortified cities, with walls, gates, and bars. Be sure to translate the name Beth Horon the same way as you did in chapter 6 of 1 Chronicles.

Stop here and decide how you will translate "**fortified cities**" into your language. Pause this audio here.

And Solomon built Baalath and all his storage cities, and all the cities for Solomon's **chariots** and horsemen. Be sure to translate chariot the same way you did in chapter one of this book.

Stop here and decide how you will translate the name **Baalath**. Pause this audio here.

Solomon finished all his plans for building in Jerusalem, Lebanon, and throughout all the territory that Solomon ruled over.

Solomon used for forced labor all the people of **Canaan**, whom the Israelites had not destroyed when they took possession of the land. These included the **descendants** of the **Hittites**, **Amorites**, **Perizzites**, **Hivites**, and **Jebusites**, as it is to this day. Be sure to translate descendants, Canaan, Israelites, Hittites, Amorites, Perizzites, Hivites, and Jebusites the same way that you have in previous books of the Bible. See the Master Glossary for more information on descendants and Canaan.

Stop here and decide how you will say in your language that Solomon made the Canaanites do **forced labor** to build his cities. Pause this audio here.

But Solomon did not use Israelites for forced labor. Israelites were the soldiers, officers, chariot commanders and horsemen. There were 250 officials in charge of the forced labor who worked on the building projects.

Stop here and decide how you will translate the word "**horsemen**" into your language. Pause this audio here.

And Solomon moved his wife, the daughter of **Pharaoh**, from the City of David to the palace that Solomon built for her. Solomon said, "My wife must not live in King David's house, for the **ark of Yahweh** has been there, and it is **holy** ground, or special ground for Yahweh." Make sure to translate Pharaoh the same way that you did in 1 Chronicles chapter four. Make sure to translate holy the same way that you have in previous passages. In this passage, the storyteller calls the ark of the covenant the "ark of Yahweh." See the Master Glossary for more information on Pharaoh, ark of the covenant, and holy.

Then Solomon presented **burnt offerings** to Yahweh on the **altar** Solomon had built in front of the entry room of the temple. Solomon offered the sacrifices for the **Sabbaths**, the New Moon festivals, and the three yearly festivals—the Festival of Unleavened Bread, the Festival of Weeks, and the Festival of Booths—just as Moses had commanded. Be sure to translate burnt offering and altar the same way you have in previous passages. Be sure to translate Sabbath the same way that you did in chapter two of this book. Refer to the Master Glossary for more information about burnt offerings, Sabbath, and altar.

The Master Glossary also has more information about the religious festivals of Israel that are mentioned here: the **New Moon festival**, the **Festival of Unleavened Bread**, the **Festival of Weeks**, and the **Festival of Tabernacles**. Be sure to translate the **Festival of Unleavened Bread**, the **Festival of Weeks**, and the **Festival of Tabernacles** the same way you did in Deuteronomy chapter 16\. If you have translated the book of Numbers, use the same term for "New Moon festival" that you used in Numbers 10:10\.

Following the rules that Solomon's father, David, had made, Solomon organized the daily work of the **priests** in singing hymns and doing their work. Solomon also organized the gatekeepers into divisions to guard the gates of the temple, because this is what David, the man of **God**, had ordered. The people did not turn aside from what the **king** had commanded concerning the priests and the **Levites** and the storehouses. So Solomon made sure that the people did all the work related to building the temple of Yahweh, from the day people laid the temple's **foundation** to the day people finished the building. Be sure to translate foundation, priests, Levites, God, and king in the same way that you did for previous passages. See the Master Glossary for more information on God, king, priests, and Levites.

Later, Solomon went to Ezion Geber and Elath, on the Red Sea in the land of Edom. If you have already translated the book of Deuteronomy, then you have translated the names Ezion Geber and Elath.

Stop here and listen to Deuteronomy chapter two, listening for how you translated the names Ezion Geber and Elath. Pause this audio here.

**Edom** is the name of the land where the descendants of Esau lived. Be sure to translate Edom the same way that you did in previous books of the Bible. Solomon built ships in Ezion Geber, and Hiram sent his own officers and servants who knew the sea to help Solomon's men sail the ships. These ships sailed to Ophir and brought back to Solomon over 450 **talents**, or 15,000 kilograms of gold. Use the same word or phrase for talent as you have used in previous passages, and refer to talent in the Master Glossary for more information.

Stop here and decide how you will translate "**servants who knew the sea**." See the Master Glossary for more information on **servant**.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 8:1-18
