## 2 Chronicles 9:1–12 (id: eng-2ch-p16-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 9:1–12 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 9:1–12 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 9:1–12 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous story, King Solomon built cities throughout the land of Israel to make the country stronger and to serve as storage cities and cities for Solomon's chariots and horsemen. Solomon also built ships that traveled to Ophir and brought back large amounts of gold. In this story, the Queen of Sheba comes and visits Solomon in Jerusalem. The Queen of Sheba asks Solomon questions to test Solomon's wisdom and is impressed by everything she sees. King Solomon and the Queen of Sheba give each other gifts, then the Queen of Sheba goes back to her own country.

In this story, the storyteller tells the events out of order. First, the storyteller tells us about the Queen of Sheba's visit to Solomon. Next, the storyteller says that Solomon's ships not only brought back gold from Ophir, but also wood and precious stones. At the end of the story, the storyteller adds that Solomon gave the Queen of Sheba more gifts than she gave Solomon. Then the Queen of Sheba left and went back to her country.

A king often hosted the leaders of other countries in his palace. One king might send his officials to another king's palace to make an agreement, end a conflict, or to show respect. Solomon probably hosted leaders from many other countries during Solomon's 40 years as king, but there was something special about the Queen of Sheba's visit. The Queen of Sheba heard that Solomon was not only very rich and had great wisdom, but she also heard about Solomon's God, Yahweh.

A queen is usually the wife of a king. But sometimes a queen could rule her country all by herself. In biblical times, some queens had a lot of power, while other queens did not. Bible scholars are not sure where Sheba was located. Some scholars think Sheba was located in Arabia, while other scholars think it was located in northeast Africa. The Queen of Sheba traveled with a large group of people and brought expensive gifts with her. This shows that Sheba was probably a wealthy and powerful nation.

The Queen of Sheba visited Solomon because she wanted to ask Solomon questions to see how wise Solomon really was. When the Queen of Sheba talked with Solomon, Solomon answered all the queen's questions and showed her that Solomon was indeed very wise.

Stop here and discuss these questions: Tell a story in your language about someone who tests the wisdom or knowledge of another person. How does this person test the other person? Does the person use riddles, stories, questions, or something else? If you do not know any stories like this, do you know any riddles or questions that people use to show whether a person is wise and educated? If so, say them out loud. Pause this audio here.

The Queen of Sheba brought gifts for Solomon. It was common at that time for kings and queens to give each other gifts to show respect to each other. It was also a way for the king or queen to show how wealthy he or she was. The Queen of Sheba brought gold, spices, and precious stones. The queen brought these things on animals called camels. A camel is a large animal that is common in places where the weather is hot and dry. Camels can survive for a long time without drinking water. Camels are also easy for people to train and can carry a lot of weight, so people in this part of the world often used camels to carry goods while they traveled.

Stop here and look at a picture of a camel next to a person. Pause this audio here.

The Queen of Sheba was very impressed by Solomon, his palace, and everything in Solomon's palace. The Queen of Sheba said that, although she had heard very good things about Solomon, what she saw in person was even better than what had been reported to her. The Queen of Sheba blessed Solomon and Solomon's officials, and she praised Yahweh who made Solomon king. Then the Queen of Sheba left and returned to her own country.

Stop here and discuss these questions: In your culture, when a person is successful, to whom do they usually give the credit for their success? Pause this audio here.

King Solomon also gave gifts to the Queen of Sheba. Either the number of gifts or the value of gifts King Solomon gave to the Queen of Sheba was greater than that which the queen gave to Solomon.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 9:1–12 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has three scenes.

**In the first scene:** The Queen of Sheba visits Solomon. The queen brings Solomon gifts and asks Solomon hard questions to see how wise Solomon really is.

**In the second scene:** The storyteller tells us that Solomon's ships had brought back not only a lot of gold, but also algum wood and precious stones.

**In the third scene:** King Solomon gives the Queen of Sheba more gifts than she gave Solomon. Then the Queen of Sheba goes back to her own country.

The characters in this passage are:

* The Queen of Sheba and her servants
* Solomon and Solomon's servants
* King Hiram's sailors
* Solomon's men
* And the musicians in the temple

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

If you have already translated the book of 1 Kings, then you have already translated a similar story. It would be helpful to go back and listen to how you translated that story.

Stop here and listen to the end of 1 Kings chapter 9 and the beginning of 1 Kings chapter 10 if it is available in your language. Pause this audio here.

In this story, the storyteller tells the events out of order. In your language, it might not be possible to tell a story out of order like this. Even if it is possible, it might be too confusing for your hearers to hear someone tell the story in this way. If this is the case, you may choose to rearrange the events of the story so that they are in the order in which they happened.

Stop here and discuss whether you will tell this story in the order that the events occurred, or if you will tell the story in the order the storyteller records the events in the Bible. Pause this audio here.

If you choose to tell the events of the story in the order in which they happened, this is the way you would tell it:

First, Solomon's men and Hiram's sailors brought back algum wood and precious stones in their ships. Solomon used the algum wood to build structures in the temple of Yahweh and the royal palace. Solomon also used some of the wood to make musical instruments. Then, the Queen of Sheba visited Solomon. The Queen of Sheba brought gifts to Solomon and tested his wisdom with hard questions. When the Queen of Sheba saw that Solomon truly was very wise, and that Jerusalem, the temple of Yahweh, and the palace were very beautiful, the queen was amazed. The queen praised Solomon and Yahweh. Then the Queen of Sheba said that even though she heard wonderful reports about Solomon and Yahweh, the reports were not half as good as what she saw when she went to Israel. Solomon gave the Queen of Sheba everything she wanted, then the queen went back to her own country.

Here is how the storyteller tells the story in 2 Chronicles:

In the first scene of this passage, the Queen of Sheba heard about the fame of King Solomon. A queen is usually the wife of a king, but it is possible that a queen could rule her country all by herself. The queen was usually the highest\-status female in the country, and she was often of higher status than everyone except the king.

Stop here and decide what word you will use for "queen." Pause this audio here.

The Queen of Sheba heard that Solomon was very famous. This means that people had spread the news about Solomon's wealth, power, and wisdom all over the world. People in faraway places knew about the things Solomon had achieved and were impressed. People also respected Solomon for his wisdom.

Stop here and discuss how you will say in your language that Solomon was famous. Pause this audio here.

The Queen of Sheba wanted to visit Solomon so that she could test Solomon's wisdom by asking him hard questions. The queen wanted to find out for herself if Solomon really was as wise as everyone said he was. The queen chose questions that would be difficult to answer because the answers needed good understanding or insight.

Stop here and discuss how you will say in your language that the Queen of Sheba tested Solomon's wisdom with difficult questions. Pause this audio here.

The Queen of Sheba did not travel by herself. The queen brought along a large number of servants and helpers. The queen probably also brought soldiers to protect her from attacks on the way. You might have heard different words or phrases for this group of people when you listened to three different versions of this story. These are different ways of saying that many people traveled with the Queen of Sheba.

Stop here and discuss how you could say in your language that many people traveled with the Queen of Sheba. Pause this audio here.

The Queen of Sheba brought many gifts to Solomon. The queen used camels to carry these goods. A camel is an animal that lives in hot, dry areas. A camel can travel for a long time without drinking water. Camels are very useful animals to help people travel through dry areas.

Stop here and look again at a picture of a camel. If your language does not have a word for "camel," discuss how you could translate the word "camel" into your language. Pause this audio here.

The Queen of Sheba brought gold, precious stones, and spices as a gift for Solomon. These spices were not the kind of spices people use to cook food. These were sweet\-smelling substances that people used for perfume, incense, or to prepare a body for burial.

Stop here and discuss this question: What words can you think of in your language for these kinds of spices? Pause this audio here.

The Queen of Sheba asked Solomon hard questions. The queen talked with Solomon about all the things that were in her heart, or on her mind. This means that the Queen of Sheba talked to Solomon about all the difficult topics that she wanted answers to. In the original language, a person's heart is where that person feels and thinks. What is in a person's heart shows who that person truly is.

Stop here and discuss these questions: In your culture, what part of a person's body do you say is the source of that person's feelings and thoughts? Do you have any sayings about how a person's feelings and thoughts come from this part of the body? How could you translate the phrase "the queen spoke to Solomon about all that was in the queen's heart" into your language? Pause this audio here.

King Solomon told the Queen of Sheba everything she wanted to know. Different Bible translations explain this in different ways. All of these translations mean the same thing—Solomon answered all of the queen's questions, and none of the queen's questions were too hard for Solomon.

After the Queen of Sheba talked with Solomon, the queen realized that Solomon truly was very wise. Some translations say that the Queen of Sheba saw Solomon's wisdom. This might sound awkward in your language, because no one can actually see wisdom. In the original language, to see something can also mean to experience something, to know something, or to understand something. So this is a way to say that the Queen of Sheba realized that Solomon was very wise.

Stop here and discuss how you will translate the phrase "the Queen of Sheba had seen all the wisdom of Solomon" into your language. Pause this audio here.

The Queen of Sheba saw Solomon's palace and thought it was beautiful. The queen was impressed by the quality, quantity, and variety of food that Solomon served to his guests and officials.

The Queen of Sheba was also impressed by "the seating of his servants." Bible scholars are not sure what this phrase means. It might mean that Solomon had many officials and that they all ate luxurious meals in Solomon's palace. This would show that Solomon was very rich, because the king had to pay for all those people to eat in his palace. It might mean that Solomon provided very beautiful places for Solomon's officials to live. This would also show that Solomon was rich and generous to the people who worked for Solomon. It might also mean that the offices and rooms where Solomon's officials worked were large and beautiful. Whatever this phrase means, it shows that the Queen of Sheba was impressed with the way that Solomon operated his palace.

The Queen of Sheba was also impressed by the clothing of Solomon's servants. One type of servant was a cupbearer. The cupbearer was a high\-ranking official in the king's court. The cupbearer's main job was to taste the king's drink before the king did to make sure it was not poisoned. But the cupbearer could have other jobs as well. If you have translated the book of Genesis, then you have already translated the word "cupbearer." It would be helpful to go back and listen to how you translated this word in Genesis.

Stop here and listen to Genesis chapter 40 if it is available in your language. Pay close attention to how you translated the word "cupbearer." Pause this audio here.

The Queen of Sheba was impressed by the sacrifices that Solomon made at the temple of Yahweh. Some translations include the idea that Solomon had to go up a stairway in order to go up to the temple and make the sacrifices.

After the Queen of Sheba saw how beautiful Solomon's palace was, the storyteller says that "there was no spirit left in her." In the original language, the word for spirit is also the word for breath. To have no breath left means the person is shocked by what they have just experienced. In this case, the Queen of Sheba was shocked in a good way. The queen was amazed by how beautiful Solomon's palace was and by how wise Solomon was. The Queen of Sheba had expected to see good things in Solomon's palace, but everything was much better than she thought it would be.

Stop here and try to think of ways you could say in your language that the Queen of Sheba was so impressed by everything in Solomon's palace that there was no breath left in her. Pause this audio here.

The Queen of Sheba told Solomon that she had heard reports of Solomon's wisdom and the things he had done, but the queen did not believe it until she came and saw it with her own eyes. This is a common way to say that the queen found out for herself that all the reports were true. In this case, the Queen of Sheba saw Solomon's palace, heard his wisdom, ate his food, and saw all the riches that he had.

Stop here and discuss how you would describe Solomon's wealth, wisdom, and achievements in your language. Pause this audio here.

After the Queen of Sheba talked to Solomon and heard Solomon's wisdom for herself, the queen said that people had not even told her half of what there was to tell. This means that although the queen had heard that Solomon was a wise person, Solomon's wisdom was far greater than what the queen had heard.

Stop here and discuss how you will describe what the Queen of Sheba meant when she said that people had not even told her half of what Solomon had done. Pause this audio here.

When you listened to three different versions of this story, you might have heard the Queen of Sheba say, "How happy your people must be!" or you might have heard her say, "How happy your wives must be!" The word for "people" and the word for "wives" are very similar in the original language, and Bible scholars do not agree on which one the Queen of Sheba meant here. Some translations leave this unclear by saying something like "How happy are the people who serve you." We recommend that you base your translation on whichever word is in the Bible your local church uses.

When the Queen of Sheba says that Solomon's people or wives must be happy, the queen means that they are very blessed or fortunate because of all the good things Solomon has done for them. The queen means that the people have good lives, with everything they need to be prosperous and secure. The people do not have to worry about their safety or their basic needs, and they can be proud of their people and their country. This would make the people feel happy most of the time.

Stop here and discuss how you might translate "How happy your people must be" into your language. Pause this audio here.

Next, the Queen of Sheba says, "Blessed be Yahweh your God." This statement has a different meaning than when the Queen of Sheba said that Solomon's people were blessed. This statement is a way to praise or worship Yahweh. This statement means that Yahweh is a very good God, and Yahweh deserves for people to praise Yahweh because of it.

The Queen of Sheba said that Yahweh set Solomon on the throne. This is a way to say that Yahweh made Solomon king of Israel. The queen also says that Yahweh set Solomon on the throne to rule for Yahweh. This means that Solomon's rule as king was supposed to reflect Yahweh's law and character. It is pretty amazing that a non\-Israelite would realize that Yahweh was the true King of Israel and that Solomon ruled on Yahweh's behalf.

Stop here and try to think of situations where a person of lower status represents a person of higher status, or does a task on behalf of a person of higher status. For example, an employee might purchase goods or arrange a sale on behalf of his employer; a messenger might deliver a message on behalf of a chief; a judge might make a decision on behalf of the government. How might these situations be similar to how Solomon rules Israel on Yahweh's behalf? Pause this audio here.

Finally, the Queen of Sheba said that Yahweh made Solomon king over Israel because Yahweh loved Israel. The queen realized that Yahweh's intention was for Israel to last forever. The queen said that Yahweh made Solomon king over Israel in order to maintain justice and righteousness. In ancient Israel, the king was the highest human authority in the land. The king was responsible for everything that happened in his country. The people could come to the king if they needed a fair judge to help with a conflict, solve a problem, or right a wrong. The king was supposed to protect those who were poor and weak and punish those who did wrong. The king was also supposed to always follow Yahweh's law and do what was right.

Stop here. Tell a story about a leader or ruler who did something wise and just. Then tell a story about a leader or ruler who did something evil or unjust. How important is it that leaders are just and righteous in all they do? What bad things might happen if leaders are not just and righteous? Pause this audio here.

Then the Queen of Sheba gave Solomon more than 4,000 kilograms of gold, as well as a large amount of spices and precious stones. When the storyteller says, "There have never been any other spices as fine as those that the Queen of Sheba gave Solomon," this could mean several things. It could mean that Solomon never received any other spices as good as those spices. Or it could mean that no king in Israel ever received spices as good as those spices.

Now the second scene takes us back to some things that happened long before the Queen of Sheba came to visit Solomon. The ships of King Hiram, which are the same ships that sailed to Ophir and brought back gold, also brought back precious stones and algum wood. When you listened to three different versions of this story, you might have heard the name Hiram pronounced "Huram." This is just a different way to say the same person's name.

Bible scholars are not sure exactly what kind of wood algum wood was, but it was good for constructing large buildings like Yahweh's temple and the royal palace. Most translations of the Bible call it "algum wood." The word "algum" sounds like the word for these trees in the original language. But a few translations call it "sandalwood" or "juniper wood." Sandalwood is a kind of tree that grows in Asia. Sandalwood is one of the most expensive kinds of wood in the world.

Stop here and look at a picture of a sandalwood tree. Pause this audio here.

Juniper trees grow all over the world. Some juniper trees have very strong wood that is useful for building.

Stop here and look at a picture of a juniper tree. Pause this audio here.

When you listened to three different versions of the passage, you might have heard that "the king used the algum wood to make supports for the house of Yahweh," or you might have heard that the king made railings for the house of Yahweh, or you might have heard that the king made steps for the house of Yahweh. Bible scholars are not sure what kind of structure Solomon made from the algum wood, so any of these translation choices is acceptable.

The Israelites also used algum wood to make musical instruments. King Solomon used the wood to make harps and lyres for his musicians. A harp is a musical instrument that people make of wood and use strings to make sound. A harp is usually shaped like a triangle. A lyre also uses strings to make sound, but people arrange the strings differently on the wood. A lyre is usually shaped more like a rectangle or oval.

Stop here and look at a picture of a harp and a lyre. What kind of stringed instruments do you have in your country? Are any of these instruments like a harp or a lyre? Pause this audio here.

Solomon's ships brought back precious stones. These are stones that are rare and very beautiful. Because these stones are rare and beautiful, people will pay a lot of money for these stones. Solomon used precious stones to decorate Yahweh's temple. The priests' garments also contained precious stones. Women liked to use precious stones to make jewelry.

Stop here and discuss these questions: What precious stones do your people value? What do you use the stones for? Pause this audio here.

In scene three, we return to the story of Solomon and the Queen of Sheba. Solomon gave the Queen of Sheba gifts. It was the normal custom for kings and queens to give each other gifts as a sign of respect and also to show how rich the king or queen was. This is what the storyteller calls the "royal generosity." But King Solomon gave the Queen of Sheba even more than the customary amount of gifts. Solomon gave the Queen of Sheba whatever she requested. Then the Queen of Sheba returned to her own land with her servants.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 9:1–12 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has three scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* The Queen of Sheba and her servants
* Solomon and Solomon's servants
* King Hiram's sailors
* Solomon's men
* And the musicians in the temple

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

When the Queen of Sheba heard that Solomon was very famous, she came to Jerusalem to test Solomon with difficult questions. The queen arrived with a large group of attendants, with camels loaded with spices, large quantities of gold, and precious stones. When the queen met with Solomon, the queen talked with Solomon about everything that was in her heart. Solomon had answers for all of the queen's questions—nothing was too hard for the king to explain to the queen. When the Queen of Sheba realized how wise Solomon was, and when she saw the palace Solomon had built, the excellent food on his tables, the seating of his officials, the service of his waiters and their excellent clothing, his cupbearers and the sacrifices he made at the temple of Yahweh, the queen was completely amazed.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am glad that this clever woman has come to visit me; or
* This queen has truly brought me excellent gifts; or
* I feel pleased to have such an excellent conversation with someone who values wisdom.

Ask the person playing the Queen of Sheba, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel happy and honored to have the chance to talk with such an insightful person; or
* It feels good to know someone who can answer these tricky questions; or
* I have never seen a person or a place like this before!

> Continue the drama.
> 
> The queen said to the king, "The report I heard in my country about your achievements and your wisdom is true. But I did not believe these things until I came and saw them myself. In fact, I had not even heard about half of your great wisdom! It is far beyond what I was told. How happy your people must be! How happy are your servants, who stand here day after day and listen to your wisdom! Blessed be Yahweh your God, who has delighted in you and has placed you on the throne as king to rule for him. Because your God loves Israel and desires for this kingdom to last forever, your God has made you king over the Israelites so you can rule with justice and righteousness." Then the queen gave the king a gift of more than 4,000 kilograms of gold, great quantities of spices, and precious jewels. Never before had there been spices as fine as those that the Queen of Sheba gave to King Solomon.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel proud that my country and I have made such a good impression on this woman; or
* I like the Queen of Sheba. Perhaps we can be allies with this queen's country; or
* I am glad that the Queen of Sheba is giving glory to Yahweh, because Yahweh is the one who made all of this possible.

Ask the person playing the Queen of Sheba, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Solomon's God has blessed Solomon abundantly; or
* I want my country to be as rich and as beautiful as Israel; or
* I can only say good things about Solomon and his wisdom; or

> Continue the drama.

Now the servants of Hiram and the servants of Solomon had brought gold from Ophir, and they also brought back algum wood and precious jewels. The king used the algum wood to make supports for the temple of Yahweh and the royal palace, and to make lyres and harps for the musicians. Never before had people seen such beautiful things in Judah.

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing King Hiram's sailors and Solomon's men, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We are happy that we are bringing back so many valuable things to King Solomon; or
* We are excited that our country is so rich and has so many beautiful things; or
* It was fun to sail out on the ocean!

Ask the person playing Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Hiram's sailors and my men have done a very good job; or
* I am happy that my country now owns so many valuable goods; or
* I want to make sure that I make the best use of these excellent ships.

Continue the drama.

So King Solomon gave the Queen of Sheba everything she asked for, besides all the gifts that Solomon had given her out of the royal bounty. Then the queen and all her servants returned to their own country.

Ask the person playing Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I want to show the Queen of Sheba that I am wealthy and generous, so I will give the queen more gifts than she gave me; or
* I hope that the Queen of Sheba will tell her people that I am even wiser than what they had heard; or
* I am truly happy that the Queen of Sheba visited and brought gifts.

Ask the person playing the Queen of Sheba, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Solomon is not only wise, but Solomon is generous too; or
* I wish I could stay in Israel longer; or
* I am grateful for all of Solomon's wonderful gifts.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 9:1–12 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage. If you have already translated the book of 1 Kings, you may go back and listen to how you translated this story in that book.

Stop here and listen to the end of 1 Kings chapter 9 and the beginning of chapter 10\. Pause this audio here.

When the Queen of Sheba heard that King Solomon was very famous, the queen came to Jerusalem to test Solomon with difficult questions.

Stop here and decide how you will translate "**queen**" and "**famous**." See the Master Glossary for more information on a queen. Pause this audio here.

The Queen of Sheba arrived with a large group of attendants, with camels loaded with spices, large quantities of gold, and precious stones. When the queen met with Solomon, she talked with Solomon about everything that was in her heart. Solomon had answers for all of the queen's questions. Nothing was too hard for Solomon to explain to the queen. When the Queen of Sheba realized how wise Solomon was, and when she saw the **palace** Solomon had built, the excellent food on his tables, the seating of his officials, the service of his waiters and their excellent clothing, his **cupbearers** and the **burnt offerings** he made at the **temple** of **Yahweh**, the queen was completely amazed. Be sure to translate burnt offering, palace, temple and Yahweh the same way you did in previous passages. See the Master Glossary for more information on burnt offering, temple and Yahweh. Be sure to translate cupbearer the same way you did in previous stories.

If necessary, listen again to Genesis chapter 40 to hear how you translated cupbearer in a previous story. Pause this audio here.

The Queen of Sheba said to the king, "The report that I heard in my country about your achievements and your **wisdom** is true. But I did not believe these things until I came and saw them myself. In fact, I had not even heard about half of your great wisdom! It is far beyond what I was told." Be sure to translate wisdom the same way that you have in previous passages. See the Master Glossary for more information on wisdom.

"How happy your people must be! How happy your officials must be, who continually stand in your presence and hear your wisdom! **Blessed** be Yahweh your **God**, who has delighted in you and has placed you on the **throne** as king to rule for him." In this case, to say "Blessed be Yahweh" is a way to praise or worship Yahweh. Be sure to translate "Blessed be Yahweh" the same way you have in previous passages. See the Master Glossary for more information on blessed when it refers to praising God. Also, be sure to translate God and throne the same way you have in previous passages. See the Master Glossary for more information on blessed and God.

"Because your God loves **Israel** and wants Israel to last forever, your God has made you **king** over the Israelites so you can rule with justice and righteousness." When a king rules with justice and righteousness, he rules fairly and rightly. The king punishes the people who have done wrong things, and the king protects the weak and poor people from other people who would harm the weak and poor. You may use the two words for justice and righteousness, or you may describe the way the king rules. See the Master Glossary for more information on king and Israel, and translate king and Israel in the same way as previously.

Stop here and discuss as a group how you will translate that the king rules with **justice** and **righteousness**. You can refer to justice and righteousness in the Master Glossary, but remember that you may also describe how the king rules fairly and rightly. Pause this audio here.

Then the Queen of Sheba gave the king a gift of more than 120 **talents**, or 4,000 kilograms, of gold, great quantities of spices, and precious jewels. Never before had there been spices as fine as those that the Queen of Sheba gave to King Solomon. A talent is a measure of weight. Refer to the Master Glossary for more information about talent, if you choose to use it here.

> And also, the ships of Hiram, which brought gold from Ophir, brought in from Ophir a very great amount of **algum wood** and precious stones. Use the same word or phrase for algum wood that you used in 2 Chronicles 2:8\.
> 
> The king made the algum wood into supports for the temple of Yahweh and for the palace, and the king also made lyres and harps for the musicians.
> 
> Stop here and decide what word you will use for "**lyre**" and what word you will use for "**harp**." Look again at pictures of a harp and a lyre if you need to. Pause this audio here.
> 
> And no one has seen anything like that in the land of Judah.
> 
> So King Solomon gave the Queen of Sheba everything she asked for, besides all the gifts that Solomon had given the queen out of the royal bounty. Then the Queen of Sheba and all her servants returned to their own country. Royal bounty is the gifts that a king would normally give to an important visitor.
> 
> Stop here and decide how you will translate "**royal bounty**" into your language.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

## 2 Chronicles 9:13–31 (id: eng-2ch-p17-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 9:13–31 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 9:13–31 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 9:13–31 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous story, the storyteller told us about the Queen of Sheba's visit to King Solomon. In this story, the storyteller tells us more about how rich Solomon was. The storyteller describes the throne that Solomon built inside the palace, the gifts Solomon received from visitors, and the number of chariots and horses that Solomon owned. At the end of the passage, Solomon dies, and people bury Solomon in Jerusalem. Solomon's son Rehoboam becomes king after Solomon.

In biblical times, kings were considered powerful and important if they were very rich. This passage shows us that King Solomon was extremely rich. However, Yahweh had told the Israelites that their king should not own a large number of horses, store up large amounts of silver and gold, or marry many wives. The kings of Israel were supposed to be different from the kings of the people around Israel. When Solomon bought many horses and chariots and collected large amounts of silver and gold, Solomon disobeyed Yahweh.

Solomon received almost 23,000 kilograms of gold each year. This is a very large amount of gold. In addition, Solomon received money and goods through taxes on Solomon's own people and from people in the lands around Israel.

Stop here and discuss these questions: How do the leaders of your country get money to run the country? Do you have to pay taxes? What sorts of things do you have to pay taxes on? Pause this audio here.

In this story, the storyteller describes some of the ways Solomon used his gold. Solomon used some of the gold to make large and small shields. A shield is a piece of equipment that soldiers use to defend themselves in battle. A shield could protect a soldier from arrows, swords, or objects that people threw at the soldier.

Stop here and look at a picture of a shield. Pause this audio here.

But the gold shields that Solomon made were not for soldiers to use in battle. Gold is a very soft metal and is not good to use as a shield. Solomon made these shields as a decoration for the House of the Forest of Lebanon. This house was a large building in Jerusalem. Bible scholars believe that Solomon sometimes took the shields out of the House of the Forest of Lebanon and used the shields during ceremonies. This would have shown the people how rich their king was.

Solomon used gold and ivory to make a large, beautiful throne in Solomon's palace. A throne is a special seat that only the king can sit on. Ivory is white and comes from the tusks of elephants. Sometimes, people collected the tusks from elephants that died of natural causes, but people also killed elephants just to get the tusks.

Stop here and look at a picture of an elephant. Note the size and color of the tusks. Pause this audio here.

Ivory is very beautiful and easy to carve. People used ivory to make jewelry and art. Solomon used ivory to decorate his throne. Solomon's throne was very large, with six steps leading up to the throne. The lions next to the armrests and on the steps were not real lions; they were statues of lions. A lion is a large, wild cat that was common in Israel during Solomon's time. Lions are very strong and can easily kill a human. There are very few animals that can kill a lion, so many Israelites considered lions to be the most powerful animals in Israel. Because of this, some people call the lion "the king of the animals" and use lions to symbolize kings. The lion statues around Solomon's throne were probably covered in gold, too.

Stop here and look at a picture that shows what Solomon's throne might have looked like. Pause this audio here.

Solomon had so much gold that Solomon was able to make everyday items, like cups, plates, forks, and spoons, out of gold. Solomon had so much gold that people considered silver, another valuable metal, relatively worthless in Solomon's time. The storyteller says that silver was as common as stones in Jerusalem. Jerusalem is built on hills that are very rocky, and Jerusalem stone is famous for its quality as a building material. So stones were extremely common in Jerusalem. Sycamore trees were common in the lowlands of Judah. Cedar trees were not common in Israel, and the Israelites had to import them from Tyre and other areas further north. Cedar was expensive, but Solomon was so rich that he was able to buy a large amount of cedar wood for Solomon's building projects.

Solomon had a fleet, or large group, of ships that sailed on the ocean with Hiram's servants. These ships brought goods and animals from faraway countries. It is likely that this fleet of ships sailed in the Mediterranean Sea. It was a different group of ships from the ships Solomon stationed at Ezion Geber in the far south of Israel.

Although Solomon was very rich, Solomon was also famous for his wisdom. People from all over the world came to Jerusalem to visit Solomon and to listen to Solomon's wisdom. And when the people came, they brought Solomon gifts. This is another way that Solomon became rich.

Solomon also owned many chariots and horses. This meant that Solomon had a powerful army. Throughout Israel, Solomon had 4,000 stalls for his horses. A stall is a small room inside a building where people can keep a horse. People put horses into stalls to protect the horses from wild animals and thieves. Usually, each stall contained one horse, so Solomon probably had 4,000 horses for his chariots. Solomon also had 12,000 charioteers. A charioteer is a soldier who rides in a chariot. Solomon stationed the chariots in Jerusalem and in other cities throughout Israel.

Next, the storyteller describes exactly how much territory Solomon controlled.

Stop here and look at a map of Solomon's kingdom. Note the location of the Euphrates River, the land of the Philistines, and the border with Egypt. Pause this audio here.

Solomon controlled the territory from the Euphrates River to the border of Egypt, except for the land of the Philistines.

While Solomon was king, people wrote down everything Solomon did in other books. The names of these books were "The Book of Nathan the prophet," "The Prophecy of Ahijah from Shiloh," and "The Visions of Iddo the Seer." These books were available to the Israelites at the time the storyteller was alive, but we no longer have these books. Some of the prophecy of Ahijah from Shiloh, and possibly some of the visions of Iddo the Seer, were about Jeroboam, the son of Nebat. Jeroboam was an enemy of Solomon and Solomon's son, Rehoboam. We will learn more about Jeroboam in the next few stories.

Solomon reigned as king over Israel for 40 years, the same length of time as his father, David. Solomon was buried in Jerusalem, and his son Rehoboam became king after Solomon.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 9:13–31 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has six scenes.

**In the first scene:** The storyteller tells us how much gold Solomon received each year and describes the shields Solomon made from the gold.

**In the second scene:** The storyteller tells us about the throne that Solomon built in the palace.

**In the third scene:** The storyteller tells us more about Solomon's wealth and the items that Solomon's trading ships brought to Solomon.

**In the fourth scene:** The storyteller tells us that many people came to visit Solomon in order to hear Solomon's wisdom, and the people brought marvelous gifts with them.

**In the fifth scene:** The storyteller tells us about Solomon's military power and all the land that Solomon ruled over.

**In the sixth scene:** The storyteller tells us that people record more stories about Solomon's reign as king in other books. After 40 years as king of Israel, Solomon died and was buried in Jerusalem. Solomon's son Rehoboam became king.

The characters in this passage are:

* King Solomon
* Traders, traveling merchants, kings of the Arabians, and governors of territories
* Solomon's sailors and Hiram's sailors
* The kings who came to Jerusalem to hear Solomon's wisdom
* Solomon's horsemen
* And Solomon's son Rehoboam

If you have already translated the book of 1 Kings, then you have already translated one version of this story. It might be helpful to go back and listen to how you translated that story.

Stop here and listen to the second half of 1 Kings chapter 10, after the Queen of Sheba's visit to Solomon. Pause this audio here.

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In the first scene, the storyteller says that Solomon received 666 talents, or almost 23,000 kilograms, of gold each year. Solomon might have received this amount of gold when the rulers of the countries around Israel gave valuable money and things to Solomon. Sometimes, a less powerful ruler gives valuable things to a more powerful ruler. If the less powerful ruler pays these things, the more powerful ruler agrees not to attack and invade the less powerful ruler's country.

In addition to this large amount of gold, Solomon also received money from taxes that Solomon charged merchants and traders. Anyone who did business in Israel had to pay a certain amount of tax to the king. Also, if any traders wanted to travel through Israel's land, Solomon's officials could charge the traders money to do so. This money became part of the king's income.

The Arabian kings were the rulers of the lands to the southeast of Israel. The storyteller says that the money they paid to Solomon was not included in the 23,000 kilograms of gold that Solomon received each year.

Stop here and look at a map of Israel and Arabia. What name does your language have for the place called Arabia? Pause this audio here.

The phrase "governors of the land" or "governors of the territories" probably refers to the governors of different areas inside Israel. A governor managed part of the country for the king. For example, in Israel, there might have been one governor who was responsible for managing the territory that belonged to each tribe. One job of a governor was to collect tax from the people and give it to the king.

Stop here and discuss these questions: In your country, what do you call the people who are responsible for managing different areas of the country? Could you use any of these words to translate the word "governor"? If not, what words could you use for a person who is responsible for managing one part of a country? Pause this audio here.

King Solomon hired artists to make golden shields. Solomon did not make the shields himself. The artists made large shields and small shields. Most Bible scholars believe that people made the shields from a strong yet lightweight material like wood and then overlaid the shields with gold. The larger shields required about seven kilograms of gold, and the smaller shields required about three kilograms of gold. Gold is a soft metal and is a poor material to use to make shields, so people probably did not use the shields in battle. Solomon probably used the shields as a decoration for the House of the Forest of Lebanon.

The House of the Forest of Lebanon was the largest building in Solomon's palace. Solomon built this building from cedar wood that came from Lebanon. People may have used this house as a gathering place for large groups of people, or it might have been a place to store weapons. If you have already translated the book of 1 Kings, then you have already translated the name of this building.

In the second scene, Solomon hires men to build a throne that is unlike any other throne in the world. Solomon did not build the throne himself. Solomon's throne was on a raised platform with six steps leading up to the throne. When you listened to three different versions of this passage, some of them might have said that Solomon made the throne from ivory and then overlaid it with the finest gold. Most Bible scholars think it was unlikely that Solomon would have covered ivory with gold, because ivory was expensive and also very beautiful.

Stop here and look at a picture of some ivory objects. Pause this audio here.

Most Bible scholars believe that Solomon built the throne, overlaid it with gold, and then placed ivory decorations on certain parts of the throne. There was a gold footstool in front of the throne. Each step leading up to Solomon's throne had a figure of a lion on each side, and there was also a figure of a lion next to each armrest on the throne. The lion was a symbol of the tribe of Judah, which was the tribe Solomon belonged to. The lion also represented royalty.

Stop here and look at a picture of a lion. Pause this audio here.

In scene three, the storyteller says that all of Solomon's drinking cups were gold and that all of the utensils in the House of the Forest of Lebanon were pure gold. This shows that Solomon had so much gold that Solomon could afford to use the gold for everyday items like cups and utensils.

Silver is another valuable metal that people commonly used in Biblical times to make coins, jewelry, utensils, or other items. In this scene, the storyteller says that people considered silver nothing during Solomon's days. This is a way to say that silver had very little value, or was worthless, during Solomon's reign as king, because everyone used so much gold. The people of Israel were very rich during Solomon's lifetime.

Solomon had a fleet of ships called "the ships of Tarshish." Tarshish was a city located on the far western end of the Mediterranean Sea. Israel was located on the far eastern end of the Mediterranean Sea. A ship had to sail about 1,600 kilometers to go from Israel to Tarshish.

Stop here and look at a map of the Mediterranean Sea that shows the location of Israel and the approximate location of Tarshish. Pause this audio here.

Solomon's fleet of ships sailed on the Mediterranean Sea and could sail very long distances. Some versions of this passage might have called these ships "merchant ships" or "trading ships." Every three years during Solomon's reign, these ships brought back gold, silver, ivory, and animals. Bible scholars are not certain what kind of animals these were. Some versions of this passage say that the animals were "apes and peacocks," while other versions say the animals were "apes and baboons" or "apes and monkeys."

Monkeys are small to medium\-sized, four\-legged animals that have hands similar to human hands. Monkeys also have tails.

Stop here and look at a picture of a monkey. Pause this audio here.

Apes are large monkeys. Some apes can be larger than humans. Apes do not have tails.

Stop here and look at a picture of an ape. Pause this audio here.

A baboon is a specific type of medium\-sized monkey.

Stop here and look at a picture of a baboon. Pause this audio here.

A peacock is a kind of bird. Peacocks have very colorful, beautiful tail feathers. People use these feathers for decorations. Women even wear peacock feathers on their hats or clothing.

Stop here and look at a picture of a peacock. Pause this audio here.

If your language does not have a word for any of these animals, you can provide a description. For example, instead of "peacocks," you could say "birds with brightly colored feathers." Or, if your language does not have a word for "apes," you could say "very large monkeys."

In scene four, the storyteller reminds us that Solomon was the richest and wisest king of his time. Kings from all over the world came to see Solomon and hear Solomon's wisdom. The storyteller reminds us that God had put this wisdom in Solomon's heart. The Israelites considered the heart to be the place where a person's thoughts, feelings, and wisdom come from.

Stop here and discuss this question: In your culture, what part of the body do you consider thoughts, feelings, and wisdom to come from? Pause this audio here.

Each person who came to see Solomon brought a gift. Some people brought silver and gold; others brought clothes, weapons or spices. Some people brought horses and mules. A mule is an animal that is very similar to a horse, but mules can work harder and are easier to handle than horses. To produce a mule, people mate a female horse with a male donkey.

Stop here and look at a picture of a mule. Pause this audio here.

In the fifth scene, the storyteller describes Solomon's military power and the area Solomon ruled over. Solomon had 4,000 stalls for his horses and chariots. When you listened to three different versions of this passage, some might have said that Solomon had 12,000 horses, while others said that Solomon had 12,000 horsemen. A horseman is a person who knows how to handle horses. Bible scholars do not agree on whether the original language means that Solomon had 12,000 horses or that Solomon had 12,000 horsemen. Some scholars think that translating the word as "horses" makes more sense in this passage than translating the word as "horsemen," but either choice is acceptable.

Solomon ruled over a large territory that stretched beyond the borders of the land that Yahweh had given to the 12 tribes of Israel. The northern border of Solomon's territory was the Euphrates River. This is the modern\-day name of a large river that flows through the modern\-day countries of Syria and Iraq. Some translations of this passage just say "the river," but we recommend that you include the name of the river so that people understand where this river is.

Stop here and look at a map of the Middle East that shows the Euphrates River. Pause this audio here.

Even Solomon, with all his power, did not conquer the territory of the Philistines. The Philistines were a people group that lived along the coast of the Mediterranean Sea between the territory of Israel and the territory of Egypt. The Israelites often had conflict with the Philistines.

In the middle of this scene, the storyteller mentions again that silver was so abundant in Israel that it was as common as stones. The storyteller also says that cedars were as common as sycamore trees in the lowlands. The Israelites had to import good\-quality cedar into Israel from Lebanon, and this cedar was expensive. In contrast, sycamore trees were very common in an area called Shephelah, which was in the foothills of Judah. Both of these statements mean that Solomon was so rich that he could buy a lot of expensive things.

In the sixth scene, the storyteller mentions that other people wrote down all the stories about Solomon in three other books. The storyteller introduces this scene using a question that does not expect an answer: "Now the rest of the acts of Solomon, first and last, are they not written in the book, or words, of Nathan the prophet, in the prophecy of Ahijah the Shilonite and in the visions of Iddo the Seer concerning Jeroboam the son of Nebat?" This question means that a hearer can read more about Solomon's reign in those three books. Nathan the prophet is most likely the same Nathan who was also alive during King David's lifetime. A prophet was a person who received messages from Yahweh to give to other people.

Ahijah the Shilonite was an Israelite prophet who lived at the same time as Solomon and his son Rehoboam. Ahijah was from the town of Shiloh. Ahijah received prophecy from Yahweh about Jeroboam, the son of Nebat. "Prophecy" is a word for revelation from God. A prophecy can be an instruction from God to do something, a warning from God to stop doing something bad, information about what will happen in the future, or it could be words of encouragement and hope. Prophets can receive prophecies in different ways, but all true prophecy comes from God.

Stop here and discuss these questions: In your language, how do you talk about messages that come from God? How do you make sure that a message is really from God? Pause this audio here.

Jeroboam was an Israelite who did not like Solomon and Solomon's son, Rehoboam.

Iddo the Seer lived a little bit later, during the reign of Rehoboam and his son Abijah. "Seer" is another word for "prophet." "Seer" means "someone who sees," as in, that person can see revelations from Yahweh.

Iddo the Seer had enough visions to collect the visions into a book. In this case, a vision is a revelation from God.

After 40 years as king of Israel, Solomon "slept with his fathers" or "rested with his ancestors." This is a way to say that Solomon died, just as all of Solomon's ancestors had died. This phrase shows that the Israelites believed that after a person died, that person's soul was at rest, like being asleep. The person's soul was not active or aware of things happening on earth.

Stop here and discuss these questions: What different ways do you have in your language to say that someone died? Which of these sayings describes that the person's soul is at rest or asleep? Which of these sayings describes that the person's ancestors have also died and are at rest? Decide how you will translate this phrase. Pause this audio here.

Solomon's son Rehoboam became the next king after Rehoboam's father died.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 9:13–31 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has six scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* King Solomon
* Traders, traveling merchants, kings of the Arabians, and governors of territories
* Solomon's sailors and Hiram's sailors
* The kings who came to Jerusalem to hear Solomon's wisdom
* Solomon's horsemen
* And Solomon's son Rehoboam

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

Every year, King Solomon received around 23,000 kilograms of gold. This was in addition to the taxes from the merchants and traders. And all the kings of Arabia and the governors of the country brought gold and silver to Solomon. Solomon made 200 large shields and covered each shield with about seven kilograms of hammered gold. And Solomon made 300 small shields, and overlaid each shield with about three kilograms of hammered gold. The king placed these shields in the House of the Forest of Lebanon.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am happy that I have so much gold and can make all these beautiful things; or
* All this gold and silver will make our country more secure; or
* I feel powerful and important because I have so much wealth.

Ask the people playing the traders and merchants, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* These taxes and fees are really a burden; or
* I need to find a way not to pay so much tax; or
* It is not fair that we work so hard only for so much of our money to go to the king.

Ask the people playing the kings of Arabia, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* It makes me angry to pay tribute to Solomon, but our army cannot defeat Solomon's army; or
* I feel sad that we are losing our gold to a foreigner; or
* I resent the fact that Solomon is more powerful than we are.

Ask the people playing the governors of the country, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am happy that our country is so prosperous; or
* I do not like paying taxes when the king wastes it on making golden shields; or
* Did Yahweh not say that our king should not collect a lot of gold?

> Continue the drama.
> 
> Then the king made a large throne decorated with ivory and overlaid with the finest gold. The throne had six steps and a footstool covered with gold in front of it. There were armrests at either side of the seat, and a lion stood next to each armrest. Twelve lions stood on the steps, one at each end of each step. No one had ever made anything like this throne for any other kingdom.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am so proud of this beautiful throne that my workers have made! or
* I feel strong and powerful when I sit on this throne; or
* I hope other kings will come visit me and be impressed by my throne.

> Continue the drama.

People made all of King Solomon's cups from gold, and people made all of the utensils in the House of the Forest of Lebanon from pure gold. No one made anything from silver, because silver had very little value in Solomon's day. The king had a fleet of trading ships at sea with the ships of King Hiram. Once every three years, the ships came back bringing gold, silver, ivory, apes, and peacocks.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am glad that I have so much gold, silver, and other valuable goods; or
* Our partnership with Hiram has brought us a lot of valuable goods; or
* I wish my father, David, could have seen these days when Israel is so rich and powerful.

Ask the people playing Solomon and Hiram's sailors, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I really enjoy sailing to Tarshish and bringing back so many good things to the king; or
* I am glad that our country has so much gold; or
* I wonder why the king needs these apes and peacocks?

> Continue the drama.

King Solomon was greater than all the kings of the earth in riches and in wisdom. All the kings on the earth came to see Solomon and to hear the wisdom that God had put in Solomon's heart. Year after year, everyone who visited Solomon brought a gift—items that people made of silver and gold, clothing, weapons, spices, horses, and mules.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am glad that my wisdom can help so many people; or
* I am thankful that Yahweh gave me the gift of wisdom; or
* I am amazed by all the gifts the people are bringing to me.

Ask the people playing the kings who came to hear Solomon's wisdom, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel honored that the great King Solomon is willing to see me; or
* I hope that King Solomon can tell me what to do about the problems in my country; or
* It was definitely worth the trip and the cost to come see such a wise king!

> Continue the drama.

Solomon had 4,000 stalls for horses and chariots, and 12,000 horsemen. Solomon kept them in the chariot cities and also with Solomon in Jerusalem. Solomon ruled over all the kings from the Euphrates River to the land of the Philistines, as far as the border of Egypt. The king made silver as common as stones in Jerusalem, and the king made cedar as common as sycamore trees in the foothills. Solomon imported his horses from Egypt and many other countries.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel proud that our army has grown so large; or
* I hope that these chariots and horsemen will keep our nation safe for many years to come; or
* I want Israel to be the most powerful nation on earth.

Ask the people playing Solomon's horsemen and charioteers, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Finally, our country has an army as powerful as Egypt; or
* I feel proud to serve in such a strong army; or
* I am grateful to King Solomon for providing such good chariots and horses for our army.

> Continue the drama.
> 
> The rest of the events in Solomon's reign, from beginning to end, are recorded in The Book of Nathan the Prophet, and the book of prophecy of Ahijah from Shiloh, and also in The Visions of Iddo the Seer, concerning Jeroboam son of Nebat. Solomon reigned in Jerusalem over all Israel for 40 years. Then Solomon rested with his fathers, and people buried Solomon in Jerusalem, the City of David his father. Then Solomon's son Rehoboam became the next king.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Solomon, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am grateful to Yahweh for giving me such a long life as king; or
* I hope my son Rehoboam will be a good king of Israel; or
* I hope that I did enough to make Israel strong and secure for a long time.

Ask the person playing Rehoboam, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel sad that my father has died; or
* I am happy to become king of Israel; or
* I am worried about taking care of such a large territory and so many people.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 9:13–31 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

The book of 1 Kings contains a similar story to this story.

If you want to go back and listen to that story in your language, then stop here and listen to the second half of 1 Kings chapter 10, after the Queen of Sheba's visit to Solomon. Pause this audio here.

Every year, **King** Solomon received 666 **talents**, or around 23,000 kilograms of gold. This was in addition to the taxes from the merchants and traders. And all the kings of Arabia and the governors of the country brought gold and silver to Solomon. Be sure to translate king the same way you have in previous passages. If you use the word for "talent" instead of the metric weight, use the same word for talent as you have used previously. See the Master Glossary for more information on king and talent.

Stop here. If your language does not have a name for the place called "**Arabia**," you may translate this name to sound like the original name. Also, decide how you will translate "**governor**" into your language. A governor managed part of the country for the king. Pause this audio here.

King Solomon made 200 large shields and covered each one with about 600 **shekels**, or seven kilograms, of hammered gold. And Solomon made 300 small shields, and overlaid each one with about three kilograms of hammered gold. If you use the word for shekel instead of metric weight, use the same word for shekel that you have used previously. See the Master Glossary for more information about shekel.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of a **shield**. Decide how you will translate "shield" into your language. Pause this audio here.

The king placed these shields in the **House of the Forest of Lebanon**. Be sure to translate "House of the Forest of Lebanon" the same way you did in previous books of the Bible.

Stop here if necessary and listen to the beginning of 1 Kings chapter 7, if it is available in your language. If 1 Kings is not available in your language, then decide how you will translate "The House of the Forest of Lebanon." Pause this audio here.

Then the king made a large **throne** decorated with ivory and overlaid with the finest gold. Be sure to translate throne the same way you did in previous passages.

Stop here and decide how you will translate "**ivory**" into your language. Pause this audio here.

The throne had six steps and a footstool covered with gold in front of the throne. There were armrests at either side of the seat, and a lion stood next to each armrest. Twelve lions stood on the steps, one at each end of each step. No one had ever made anything like this throne for any other **kingdom**. Be sure to translate kingdom the same way you did in previous passages. See the Master Glossary for more information about kingdom.

Stop here. If your language does not have a word for **lion**, decide how you will describe a lion for your hearers. Pause this audio here.

The king had a fleet of trading **ships** at sea with the ships of King Hiram. Once every three years, the ships came back bringing gold, silver, ivory, apes, and peacocks. Use the same word for ships that you have used in previous stories in 2 Chronicles.

Stop here and decide how you will translate "**apes**" and "**peacocks**" into your language. You may also use the words "baboons" and "monkeys." Pause this audio here.

King Solomon was greater than all the kings of the earth in riches and in **wisdom**. All the kings on the earth came to see Solomon and to hear the wisdom that **God** had put in Solomon's **heart**. Year after year, everyone who visited Solomon brought a gift—items made of silver and gold, clothing, weapons, spices, horses, and mules. Be sure to use the same words for wisdom, heart, and God as you did in previous passages. See the Master Glossary for more information on wisdom, heart, and God.

Stop here and decide what word you will use for "**mule**." If necessary, look again at a picture of a mule. Pause this audio here.

> Solomon had 4,000 stalls for horses and **chariots**, and 12,000 horsemen. Solomon kept them in the chariot cities and also with Solomon in Jerusalem. Solomon ruled over all the kings from the **Euphrates River** to the land of the **Philistines**, as far as the border of Egypt. Be sure to use the same words for chariots, "Euphrates River," and Philistines that you have in previous books of the Bible.
> 
> If necessary, stop here and listen again to Genesis chapter 2 to remember how you translated the name "Euphrates River." Pay close attention to the middle of the chapters when the author names the four rivers. The Euphrates is the fourth river. Pause this audio here.
> 
> If you need to remember how you translated "Philistines," then stop here and listen to Genesis chapter 26 if it is available in your language. At the beginning of the story, Isaac goes to Gerar, to Abimelech, king of the Philistines. Pause this audio here.
> 
> People recorded the rest of the events in Solomon's reign, from beginning to end, in the book, or word, of Nathan the **prophet**, and the book of prophecy of Ahijah from Shiloh, and also in The Visions of Iddo the Seer, concerning Jeroboam, the son of Nebat. Be sure to translate "prophet" the same way as you did in previous books of the Bible. Remember that most people wrote histories like this down on scrolls, which were long pieces of animal skin or paper that people rolled around two long sticks on either end.
> 
> If necessary, stop here and listen to the beginning of 1 Chronicles 17 to remember how you translated the word "prophet." Prophet is in the Master Glossary. Pause this audio here.

"Prophecy" is another word for a message or revelation from God. There are different kinds of prophecies in the Bible. One of the most common kinds of prophecies was messages that told the people to stop disobeying God. But a prophecy could also describe something that was going to happen in the future, give people instructions about how to do things in a way that pleased God, or give the people encouragement.

Stop here and decide how you will translate the word "**prophecy**." See the Master Glossary for more information on prophecy. Pause this audio here.

The word "**seer**" is another word for prophet. This word is based on the word "to see." This word means that the person can see things that other people cannot see, such as visions from Yahweh or messages from Yahweh.

If necessary, stop here and listen to the beginning of 1 Samuel 9 to remember how you translated "seer" in that book. Look in the Master Glossary under the word "prophet" for more information on "seer." Pause this audio here.

A **vision** is a kind of prophecy, or message, from Yahweh. When a prophet has a vision, the prophet sees something that other people cannot see. If necessary, stop here and listen again to the beginning of Genesis chapter 15, where Abram has a vision. See the Master Glossary for more information on vision.

If you have already translated 1 Kings, be sure to translate the names **Ahijah**, **Shiloh**, **Jeroboam**, and **Nebat** the same way you did there.

Stop here and listen again to the first half of 1 Kings chapter 11 to hear how you previously translated those names. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss as a group: What will you call these histories? If you use the word for **scroll** or book in the titles, refer to scroll in the Master Glossary. Pause this audio here.

Then Solomon's son **Rehoboam** became the next king. If you have already translated 1 Kings, then you have already translated the name Rehoboam.

Stop here, if necessary, and listen to the beginning of 1 Kings chapter 12 to remember how you translated this name. If 1 Kings chapter 12 is not available in your language, then decide how you will translate the name Rehoboam.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 9:13-31

## 2 Chronicles 9:13–31 (id: eng-2ch-p17-v1-audio)
