## 2 Chronicles 10:1–7 (id: eng-2ch-p18a-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 10:1–7 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 10:1–7 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 10:1–7 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous story, King Solomon died, and Solomon's son, Rehoboam, became the new king. Solomon's temple was in Jerusalem, Israel's capital city, and Rehoboam also lived in Jerusalem. Now, the northern tribes of Israel gather in Shechem in the north of Israel to recognize Rehoboam as their new king. Jeroboam leads the northern tribes as they ask Rehoboam to give them less work and make their lives easier. In this story, Rehoboam asks his older advisers how Rehoboam should answer the people of Israel. This story is the first part in a two\-part story.

This story is the start of a new section in the book of 2 Chronicles and a new part of the history of the people of Israel as they lived with a divided kingdom. This story is very similar to the story in 1 Kings 12:1–15\. The storyteller knows that most people who listen to this story in 2 Chronicles already have heard the similar story in 1 Kings. If you have already translated the similar story in 1 Kings 12:1–15, you should translate this story the same way as the story in 1 Kings in most places, except for the places where there are some small differences in the story.

Stop here and listen to your translations of the passages in 1 Kings 12:1–15 now, if your team has already translated these similar passages in 1 Kings. Pause this audio here.

As the story begins, Rehoboam goes to Shechem, which is an important religious city in the north of Israel in the land of the tribe of Ephraim. Shechem was the first religious center of the 12 tribes of Israel when the Israelites first came into the land of Canaan. The northern tribes of Israel had already gathered in Shechem to renew their loyalty to David's grandson, Rehoboam, as their new king. However, some people think that because the northern tribes did not travel to Jerusalem to make Rehoboam king, this shows that these tribes already planned to leave Rehoboam and make their own kingdom.

Stop here as a group and look at a map that includes Shechem. Pause this audio here.

Also, stop here and discuss this question as a group: Talk about a time when your community, village, city, or nation chose a new leader. How did your community choose this leader? What did you like or dislike about the new leader? Pause this audio here.

Jeroboam, the son of Nebat, is another important person in this story. We know from some similar stories in 1 Kings that Jeroboam was from the tribe of Ephraim and that Jeroboam was a servant of King Solomon in the past. King Solomon tried to kill Jeroboam, so Jeroboam ran away to live in Egypt. Now, in this story, Jeroboam hears the news that King Solomon has died and that Solomon's son, Rehoboam, is the new king in Israel. The storyteller gives background information that Jeroboam was living in exile in Egypt while King Solomon was still alive.

Stop here as a group and look at a map that includes Egypt. Pause this audio here.

The storyteller says that Jeroboam returns from Egypt when Jeroboam hears the news about the death of Solomon and the new king. Remember that in the similar story in 1 Kings, it was not clear if Jeroboam immediately returned from Egypt at this point in the story, or if the storyteller said that Jeroboam had been living in Egypt in exile for a while. But either way, the storyteller now says that the northern tribes send for Jeroboam to come help them. We are not sure if Jeroboam is still in Egypt or already in Israel when the tribes ask Jeroboam to come help them.

In the next part of the story, Jeroboam and the northern tribes of Israel ask Rehoboam to lighten their heavy load of work. The people say that Rehoboam's father, Solomon, put a heavy yoke on them, and the people want Rehoboam to make their yoke, or burden, lighter. A yoke is a wooden bar that joins together the necks of two cows or oxen so that the two animals can work together. King Solomon forced the people to build Solomon's temple, his palace, and other buildings. It is likely that the people also had to pay heavy taxes to Solomon.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of a yoke. Pause this audio here.

Jeroboam and the people tell Rehoboam they will be loyal to Rehoboam if Rehoboam makes their lives easier. The people do not ask Rehoboam to take away their burdens completely, but to make the people's burdens less than their burdens were during the rule of Rehoboam's father, Solomon. It was common during Rehoboam's time for a new king to do what the people requested in exchange for their loyalty to him as their new king.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Think about your previous discussion about a time when your community chose a new leader. Describe how your new leader was different from your previous leader. What did the new leader do or say to make the people like the leader? Pause this audio here.

Rehoboam tells Jeroboam and the people to leave for three days, probably so that Rehoboam can think about how to answer the people's request to lighten their heavy load of work. Rehoboam does not pray to Yahweh or talk to a prophet about what Rehoboam should do. Instead, Rehoboam talks with two different groups of advisors during these three days. First, Rehoboam consults the elders, or older advisers, who served his father, King Solomon. These older, experienced advisers tell Rehoboam to be a servant to the people today, so that in the future, the people will always be Rehoboam's servants. In this story, the advisors want Rehoboam to act like a servant to his people and speak kindly to the people in order to keep the loyalty of the northern tribes. Some people think the older advisers want Rehoboam to be kind to the people now, and then later, Rehoboam will be free to be harsh to the people.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 10:1–7 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has four scenes.

**In the first scene:** The northern tribes of Israel prepare to make Rehoboam their new king in the city of Shechem.

**In the second scene:** The northern tribes of Israel send for Jeroboam to come back from his exile in Egypt. Jeroboam comes back to Israel.

**In the third scene:** Jeroboam and the people ask Rehoboam to take away some of their hard work. Rehoboam says to come back to Rehoboam in three days.

**In the fourth scene:** Rehoboam asks the older advisers for advice. The older advisers tell Rehoboam to answer the people with kind words.

The characters in this passage are:

* Rehoboam
* The northern tribes of Israel
* Jeroboam, the son of Nebat
* Older advisers who served King Solomon in the past
* And Yahweh

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

Remember that before this story, King Solomon died, and now King Solomon's son, Rehoboam, is the new king of Israel. This story is the start of a new section in the book of 2 Chronicles. In this story, and in the following stories, we will see that because of King Rehoboam's choices, Israel divides into a northern kingdom and a southern kingdom. Remember, this story is very similar to two stories in 1 Kings 12:1–15, and it is often best to translate the story in the same way that you translated the two stories in 1 Kings. Remember, this is the first part in a two\-part story.

In the first scene, the northern tribes of Israel gather in Shechem, a city in the north of Israel. Shechem is 65 kilometers north of Jerusalem, between Mount Ebal and Mount Gerizim. Shechem is in the land of the tribes of Ephraim and Manasseh. Rehoboam also goes to Shechem because the northern tribes want to recognize Rehoboam as their new king.

Stop here as a group and look at a map that includes Shechem again, if needed. Pause this audio here.

Also, stop here and discuss this question as a group: What is the most natural order to translate this scene of the story? It is probably best to translate this in the same way that you did in the 1 Kings story. Some translation examples include:

* All Israel gathered in Shechem to make Rehoboam their king, so then Rehoboam went to Shechem too; or
* Rehoboam went to Shechem because all Israel had gathered in Shechem to make Rehoboam their king.

Pause this audio here.

In the second scene, Jeroboam hears the news that King Solomon died and that Rehoboam is the new king of Israel. Remember that the storyteller gives background information that Jeroboam was living in exile in Egypt because King Solomon tried to kill Jeroboam while King Solomon was still alive.

Stop here as a group and look at a map that includes Egypt again, if needed. Pause this audio here.

Also, stop here and discuss this question as a group: Tell a story that includes some background information about one of the characters. Pay attention to how you introduce the background information about the character. How will you translate this background information about Jeroboam? It might be better to say first that Jeroboam fled to Egypt, and then later Jeroboam heard the news. It is probably best to translate this in the same way that you did in the 1 Kings story. Pause this audio here.

Jeroboam returns from Egypt to Israel. Remember that it may be that some people go to Egypt to get Jeroboam, or that Jeroboam has already come back to Israel before this story happens. Most translations do not say that Jeroboam comes back to Israel, but we know from the next scene that Jeroboam is in Shechem now. In any case, the Israelites have asked Jeroboam to come with them to talk to King Rehoboam.

In the third scene, Jeroboam and the people of Israel talk to King Rehoboam. Jeroboam and the people remind Rehoboam that Solomon, Rehoboam's father, gave the people a heavy yoke of work. Remember that people put a yoke on two animals to help the animals work together.

During the time of Rehoboam, people used a yoke as special language for heavy burdens of work. Remember also that the people do not have a physical yoke on their necks. Solomon gave the people a lot of work, and Solomon made the people pay a lot of taxes to Solomon.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of a yoke again, if needed. Pause this audio here.

Also, stop here and discuss as a group: Talk about hard work in your culture. How will you translate this part of the story about the heavy yoke of work that King Solomon put on the people of Israel? It is probably best to translate this in the same way that you did in the 1 Kings 12:1–15 story. Pause this audio here.

Now the people want Rehoboam to lessen their harsh labor and the heavy yoke of work. Some people think that harsh labor and heavy yoke are two ways to say the same thing about the hard work that Solomon forced the people to do. Other people think the heavy yoke means the heavy taxes that the king made the people pay, in addition to the harsh labor as well. The people say that if Rehoboam lessens their work, the people will serve Rehoboam as their king. The people want Rehoboam to be a different kind of king than Solomon was.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How will you translate harsh labor and heavy yoke in this story? Also, how will you show the contrast between the hard work that Solomon made the people do in the past and the lighter load of work that the people want Rehoboam to give to them now? It is probably best to translate this in the same way that you did in the 1 Kings story. A few translation examples are:

* Now, if you lighten the demands your father made and do not make us work as hard, we will serve you; or
* Lighten the harsh labor demands and the heavy taxes that your father imposed on us. Then we will be your loyal subjects; or
* But if you make our work easier, we will serve you and do whatever you ask.

Pause this audio here.

Rehoboam tells Jeroboam and the people to leave for three days. Remember that Rehoboam probably wants three days to think about how to answer the people's request. The people agree and leave for three days. It may be more natural to translate this part of the scene with the exact words that Rehoboam said to the people, or with a description of what Rehoboam said to the people.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Tell a story that includes some parts where the characters talk to each other. Do you say the exact words that the characters say to each other, or do you tell a description of what they said? What is the best way to translate this part of the story where Rehoboam tells the people to come back in three days? It is probably best to translate this in the same way that you did in the 1 Kings story. Some translation examples include:

* Rehoboam told the people, "Go away for three days and then come back"; or
* Rehoboam told the people to come back in three days to hear Rehoboam's answer.

Pause this audio here.

In the fourth scene of this story and the first scene of the following story, Rehoboam asks two different groups for advice about how to answer the people's request. You could also translate this as "consulted" different groups of people.

In the fourth scene, Rehoboam talks with the older advisers who were alive during Solomon's time. Other ways to translate older advisers could be "elders" or "older men who advised Solomon." In the original language, it says these advisers stood before Solomon, which means these advisers gave Solomon advice about what to do. Rehoboam asks the older advisers, "How do you advise me to answer these people?" Again, it may be better to translate this as a description of what Rehoboam said: "Rehoboam asks the older advisers how they thought Rehoboam should respond to the people."

The older advisers tell Rehoboam that Rehoboam should serve the people and that Rehoboam should speak good words to the people. The older advisers want Rehoboam to be kind to the people and lessen the people's harsh load of work. The older advisers say that if Rehoboam is kind to the people, then the people will always serve Rehoboam.

Stop here and discuss as a group: Think about a time when you or someone you know gave conditions to someone else in order to get the person to do something. For example, a father might tell his child that if his child helps with the father's work, the father will give the child a gift. What is the best way to translate this part of the story about the way that the older advisers tell Rehoboam that Rehoboam must act so that the people will always serve Rehoboam? Some translation choices include:

* If you are a servant for the people today, and if you give the people a good answer, then the people will always serve you; or
* Serve the people and give the people a good answer today. Then the people will always serve you.

Also, decide if you want to include the exact words that the older advisers told Rehoboam to say or if you want to say, "The older advisers told Rehoboam that he should help the people and give the people a good answer, and then the people will serve Rehoboam." It is probably best to translate this in the same way that you did in the 1 Kings story. Pause this audio here.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the story and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 10:1–7 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has four scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* Rehoboam
* The northern tribes of Israel
* Jeroboam, the son of Nebat
* Older advisers who served King Solomon in the past
* And Yahweh

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The people of Israel traveled to Shechem to make Rehoboam their king. So, Rehoboam went to Shechem too.

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing the people of Israel, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We are not sure if we can trust Rehoboam as our new king. We need to talk with Rehoboam before we decide to make Rehoboam our king; or
* It is our duty to follow Rehoboam as our new king. Rehoboam's father, Solomon, was our king before, and now we must follow Solomon's son, Rehoboam; or
* We hope that Rehoboam will be kinder to us than his father, Solomon, was.

Continue the drama.

In the past, King Solomon tried to kill Jeroboam, so Jeroboam fled to live in Egypt. Now Jeroboam hears the news that Rehoboam is the new king of Israel, and Jeroboam returns from Egypt to Israel. The people of Israel ask Jeroboam to come back to help Israel, so Jeroboam comes back to Israel.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Jeroboam, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am happy to finally come back to my home in Israel; or
* I was afraid that Solomon would kill me, but now it is safe for me to come home because I am not afraid of this new king, Rehoboam; or
* I wonder why the people of Israel want me to come help them now. Could this be part of the prophecy that the prophet Ahijah made about me?

> Continue the drama.

Jeroboam and the people of Israel talk to King Rehoboam. The people say, "Your father, King Solomon, made us work very hard. But if you make our work easier, we will serve you and do whatever you ask." Rehoboam tells the people to come back in three days to hear Rehoboam's answer to the people's request. So, the people leave for three days.

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing the people of Israel, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We think that our request is reasonable for King Rehoboam to lessen our heavy work. We are not asking Rehoboam to take away our work, but to lessen the work; or
* We hope that Rehoboam listens to our request because we cannot keep working this hard and paying this many taxes as we did for Rehoboam's father, King Solomon; or
* This is our chance to finally get the king to listen to us! We will be loyal to this new king if he gives us something in return; or
* We are hopeful that the king will think carefully about our request during these three days.

Also, ask the person playing Rehoboam, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Why are these people coming to talk to me like this? The people should make me king and then let me go back home to Jerusalem. The people should be grateful that I traveled here to Shechem to see the people; or
* I need to be careful how I answer the people because I want their loyalty and support. I know that the people worked very hard for my father, King Solomon, but I also enjoy the life that I have as king in Jerusalem, and I do not want the people to stop working or paying taxes to me; or
* I am not sure what to do yet, so I will take a few days to think about my answer and ask some advisers for advice about what to do.

Continue the drama.

King Rehoboam talks with the older advisers who served Rehoboam's father, King Solomon, when King Solomon was alive. Rehoboam asks the older advisers, "What answer do you think I should give to the people?" The older advisers tell Rehoboam, "If you are a servant for the people today, and if you give the people a good answer, then the people will always serve you."

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing the older advisers, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We think that Rehoboam should say anything he can to make the people happy, so the people will support Rehoboam as their new king. It does not matter if Rehoboam plans to keep his promises to the people or not. In the future, Rehoboam can be harsher with the people, but today, Rehoboam should speak kindly to the people; or
* We think it is important to carefully listen to the people and speak kindly to the people. Rehoboam needs the support of the northern tribes of Israel. Rehoboam should not do anything now that would make these people of Israel mad at Rehoboam. Otherwise, the people may not support Rehoboam as their new king; or
* We are honored that the new king came to ask us for advice first. We have the most experience, and we know how to handle the requests of the different tribes of Israel.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 10:1–7 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

Rehoboam became the new king of the Israelites in the previous passage. The Israelites are the descendants of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob. David was the first king of Israel, and David's son, Solomon, was the second king of Israel. Rehoboam is Solomon's son. We know from a different passage in 1 Kings that Rehoboam was the son of an Ammonite woman whose name was Naamah.

In this passage, **Israel** and **Israelites** refer to the northern tribes of Israel. These northern tribes of Israel talk to King Rehoboam at Shechem in the north of Israel. Shechem later becomes the first capital of the northern kingdom of Israel. Translate Israel and Israelites the same as you have in previous passages. For more information on Israel and Israelites, refer to Israel and Israelites in the Master Glossary.

Jeroboam returns from his exile in **Egypt**. Egypt is the land where the Israelites lived in slavery for 400 years before Yahweh rescued the people during the time of Moses and Aaron. Jeroboam was in Egypt because, in the past, **King** Solomon tried to kill Jeroboam. A king is someone who rules over a city or territory. Translate Egypt and king the same as you have in previous passages. For more information on king, refer to the Master Glossary.

The people of Israel ask King Rehoboam to lighten their heavy yoke of work that King Solomon gave to the people. A yoke is a wooden collar with two openings that people use to tie two oxen or two other animals together. In the Bible, sometimes a yoke means slavery or hard work.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How will you translate **yoke** in this passage? Remember your discussion from step three about how to translate this part of the passage. Will you use the word yoke here, or translate it as heavy work, harsh demands, or something else? It is probably best to translate this in the same way that you did in the 1 Kings passage.

Pause this audio here.

King Rehoboam first asks the **elders**, or the older advisers, for advice on how to answer the people. Elders are people with authority in the community who are usually leaders of their families. These elders were advisers to King Solomon while Solomon was alive.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How will you translate **elders** in this passage? You may want to translate "elders" the same as in previous passages, but these elders may have a more specific job to advise the king. It is probably best to translate this in the same way that you did in the 1 Kings passage. For more information on elders, refer to the Master Glossary.

Pause this audio here.

The older advisers tell King Rehoboam that if Rehoboam is a **servant** to the people today, then the people will always be the king's servants in the future. A servant is someone who does work for their master. Translate servant the same as you have in previous passages. For more information on servant, refer to the Master Glossary.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 10:1-7

## 2 Chronicles 10:8–19 (id: eng-2ch-p18b-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 10:8–19 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 10:8–19 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 10:8–19 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous story, Jeroboam and the northern tribes of Israel ask Rehoboam to give the people less work and make their lives easier. Rehoboam asks his older advisers how Rehoboam should answer the people of Israel. Now, Rehoboam asks his younger advisers for their advice, and Rehoboam decides to listen to the advice of his younger advisers instead of the advice of his older advisers. This is the second part of a two\-part story.

This story is very similar to two passages in 1 Kings. The storyteller knows that most people who listen to these stories in 2 Chronicles already have heard the similar stories in 1 Kings 12:1–15 and 1 Kings 12:16–24\. If you have already translated the similar passages in 1 Kings, you should translate this passage in the same way as the passages in 1 Kings in most places, except for the places where there are some small differences in the passage.

Stop here and listen to your translation of the passages in 1 Kings 12:1–15 and 1 Kings 12:16–24 now, if your team has already translated these similar passages in 1 Kings. Pause this audio here.

Rehoboam does not listen to the advice of Rehoboam's older advisers, which is surprising because people in Israel respected the advice of older advisers. Instead, Rehoboam asks his younger advisers what they recommend Rehoboam should do. It is likely that Rehoboam already plans to follow the advice of the younger advisers before Rehoboam hears their answer. We know from a story in 1 Kings that Rehoboam was 41 years old when he became king, and these younger advisers are the same age as Rehoboam. These young men grew up with Rehoboam and do not have the same years of experience and wisdom as Rehoboam's older advisers.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Who do you talk to for advice in your community? Talk about a time when you asked a few different people for advice about the same situation. How was the advice different? How did you choose which advice to follow? Pause this audio here.

The younger advisers tell Rehoboam to be even harsher than Rehoboam's father, King Solomon. The younger advisers want Rehoboam to show the people that Rehoboam is very strong and show that Rehoboam's easiest demands will be worse than the hardest work Solomon made the people do. The younger advisers use a proverb, or wise saying, that Rehoboam's little finger is thicker than his father Solomon's loins, or waist. Some people think the little finger is a polite way to refer to the male sexual organ.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Tell some proverbs, or wise sayings, in your language. Practice explaining what each proverb means. Pause this audio here.

The younger advisers also say that Rehoboam should make the yoke even heavier for the people. The men say Solomon punished, or disciplined, the people with whips, which means Solomon was harsh on the people. A whip has a long handle, which people often made of wood, and a flexible cord that a person used to hit another person.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of a whip. Pause this audio here.

The advisers say that Rehoboam should punish the people with whips that sting like scorpions.

A scorpion is an insect in Israel that has a poisonous sting. This could mean that Rehoboam should punish the people more than Solomon did. Or it could mean that Rehoboam will punish the people with whips that have pieces of sharp metal in them, which will cut into a person's body like the point of a scorpion's sting. If the tribes of Israel do not listen to King Rehoboam, then King Rehoboam will painfully punish the people.

Stop here and look at a picture of a scorpion and a picture of a whip with metal in the cord. Pause this audio here.

Jeroboam and the people come back to Rehoboam after three days, and King Rehoboam chooses to follow the advice of the younger advisers. King Rehoboam speaks harshly, or unkindly, to the people, and Rehoboam rejects the advice of the older advisers. This also shows that King Rehoboam rejects the wisdom of his father, King Solomon, whom people knew was very wise. If Rehoboam had listened to his people, Israel would have had an opportunity to remain one unified nation, but because King Rehoboam did not listen to his people, the nation of Israel later divided into two parts. Rehoboam adds to people's oppression. This is similar to what Pharaoh, the king of Egypt, did when Pharaoh made the Israelites work harder instead of making their burdens lighter.

Next, the storyteller tells us that the events in this story are really from Yahweh. The reason Yahweh allowed these things to happen was that these things fulfilled the promise that Yahweh made through Ahijah the prophet in a story in 1 Kings. The prophet Ahijah was from Shiloh, and Ahijah had told Jeroboam that Yahweh would give Jeroboam 10 of the tribes of Israel. In the prophecy, Ahijah said this was a punishment on Solomon because Solomon stopped following Yahweh and Solomon followed the gods of the other nations around Solomon. We see that Rehoboam acted foolishly when Rehoboam listened to bad advice, but the final reason that the kingdom of Israel split is that Yahweh made it happen. Yahweh is in control of everything.

The northern tribes of Israel realize that Rehoboam will not listen to their request to lessen their heavy load of work. The people tell King Rehoboam that the people have no place in the kingdom of David now. In the original translation, the people ask King Rehoboam, "What part do we have in David, and what part do we have in the son of Jesse?" The people do not want an answer to their question. The people know that David was Rehoboam's grandfather, the son of Jesse, and a descendant of the tribe of Judah. David and David's family have nothing to do with the people in the north of Israel.

The people from the northern tribes of Israel also say that they should all go home to their tents. A tent is a temporary home where people live. Tents are another way to talk about home. The people want the descendants of David to take care of themselves and their own people. The northern tribes no longer feel like they are a part of the people of Israel.

Stop here and look at a picture of a tent. Pause this audio here.

The northern Israelites go home. However, King Rehoboam still rules over the Israelites, who were originally from the northern tribes but who now live in the cities and towns of Judah in the south of Israel. We know that Rehoboam also rules over all the other people in Judah as well.

Stop here and look at a map that shows the kingdoms of Israel and Judah. Pause this audio here.

Rehoboam sends out Adoniram, the man who was in charge of forcing the people to build buildings for the kings of Israel. Rehoboam probably sends Adoniram because Rehoboam wants the people of Israel to know that Rehoboam will be harsh with the people and increase their heavy yoke of work. But the Israelites do not listen to Adoniram, and the people kill Adoniram by throwing stones at him. The people probably do this to show Rehoboam that the people do not respect Rehoboam as their king, and the people will not do any more building projects for Rehoboam.

As the story ends, Rehoboam quickly gets in his chariot and flees to Jerusalem, the capital of Israel. This means that Rehoboam was in the northern part of Israel before this, maybe to go with Adoniram. A chariot is a two\-wheeled cart that horses pull in battle, but sometimes kings rode on chariots, too.

Stop here and look at a picture of a chariot and a map that includes Jerusalem. Pause this audio here.

The storyteller says that Israel, meaning the northern tribes of Israel, has been rebelling against the family of David since the time when Rehoboam refused to listen to the people. The northern tribes of Israel refused to let a descendant of David be their king. The storyteller told these stories long after the stories happened, so the people of Israel had been rebelling for a long time.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 10:8–19 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has six scenes.

**In the first scene:** Rehoboam rejects the advice of the older advisers. Instead, Rehoboam asks his younger advisers for advice. The younger advisers tell Rehoboam to be harsh to the people.

**In the second scene:** Jeroboam and the people come back to Rehoboam after three days. Rehoboam speaks harshly to the people. Rehoboam says he will be even harsher than his father, Solomon, was to the people.

**In the third scene:** King Rehoboam does not listen to the people. Yahweh causes this to happen in order to fulfill the promise that Yahweh made to Jeroboam through the prophet Ahijah.

**In the fourth scene:** The people say that they have no place in the Kingdom of David now. The people go home to their tents. However, Rehoboam still rules over the people from the northern tribes of Israel who now live in the land of the tribe of Judah.

**In the fifth scene:** Rehoboam sends Adoniram, the man in charge of forcing the people to build buildings. The people kill Adoniram with stones. Rehoboam escapes and flees in his chariot to Jerusalem.

**In the sixth scene:** The northern tribes of Israel have been rebelling against the family of David ever since these stories happened.

The characters in this passage are:

* Rehoboam
* The northern tribes of Israel
* Jeroboam, the son of Nebat
* Older advisers who served King Solomon in the past
* Younger advisers who are Rehoboam's age
* Yahweh
* The prophet Ahijah, the Shilonite
* And Adoniram

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

Remember that in the previous story, Jeroboam and the northern tribes of Israel asked Rehoboam to give the people less work. Rehoboam asked his older advisers for advice, and now, Rehoboam asks his younger advisers for their advice. Rehoboam decides to listen to the advice of his younger advisers instead of the advice of Rehoboam's older advisers. This is the second part in a two\-part story.

In the first scene, Rehoboam rejects the advice of the older advisers. Instead, Rehoboam asks the younger advisers what to do. In the original language, the storyteller calls these younger advisers "young boys" or "children" because these advisers are inexperienced. But these younger advisers are actually grown men like Rehoboam. It may be more natural in your language if you include the information about what the people asked Rehoboam to do before Rehoboam asks the younger advisers for advice.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How will you show the contrast between the advice of the older advisers and what Rehoboam decides to do? It is probably best to translate this in the same way that you did in the 1 Kings story. Pause this audio here.

The younger advisers repeat what the people said to Rehoboam. Again, you can include the exact words that the people said to Rehoboam, or a description of the words. It might be good to do the same thing here that you did earlier when Rehoboam asked the younger advisers for advice. It is probably also best to translate this in the same way that you did in the 1 Kings story.

The younger advisers tell Rehoboam a proverb, or wise saying, to show that Rehoboam should act even harsher than Rehoboam's father, Solomon. The younger advisers say that Rehoboam's little finger is thicker than his father Solomon's "loins," or waist. Remember that the proverb means that the easiest work that Rehoboam requires will be worse than the hardest things that Solomon required. Remember that some people think that "little finger" means the male sexual organ.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How will you translate this proverb? It might be best to translate the meaning of the proverb, as in: "I will be much harsher than my father was!" It is probably best to translate this in the same way that you did in the 1 Kings story.

Pause this audio here.

The younger advisers give two different examples of how Rehoboam will be harsher than his father, Solomon. The advisers say that Solomon gave a heavy yoke of work to the people. Rehoboam should make the yoke even heavier for the people. The men also say that Solomon punished the people with ordinary whips, but Rehoboam should punish the people with whips that sting like scorpions. Remember, this probably means that Rehoboam will punish the people even more than Solomon did. In each example, the younger advisers emphasize the difference between how Solomon acted in the past and how the younger advisers think Rehoboam should act now.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How will you translate this part of the story to show the contrast between the work that Solomon gave to the people and the work that Rehoboam will give to the people? It is probably best to translate this in the same way that you did in the 1 Kings story. Pause this audio here.

In the second scene, Jeroboam and the people come back to Rehoboam after three days. Remember that this is when Rehoboam told Jeroboam and the people to come back. Rehoboam speaks harshly to the people. Rehoboam rejects the advice of the older advisers and instead, Rehoboam follows the advice of the younger advisers. Rehoboam does not repeat the proverb again, but Rehoboam does repeat the two examples that the younger advisers gave to him about how Rehoboam will make life even harder for the people.

In the third scene, the storyteller gives more information about the ultimate reason for all of the events in this story. This comment from the storyteller is not a part of the main action of the story. Yahweh caused these things to happen, meaning Yahweh is the one controlling everything, including the unhappiness of the people of Israel and Rehoboam's choice not to listen to the people. Remember that we know from 1 Kings that Yahweh spoke through the prophet Ahijah to Jeroboam. Ahijah told Jeroboam that Yahweh would give 10 of the tribes of Israel to Jeroboam.

Now, in this story, we see that Yahweh will fulfill the promise to Jeroboam because Rehoboam did not listen to the people of Israel. Remember that we will learn in later stories about how the tribes of Israel split into two kingdoms, with Rehoboam leading the southern kingdom and Jeroboam leading the northern kingdom.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Tell a story that includes a statement about why the event in the story happened. How will you translate the end of this story to show how Yahweh accomplished Yahweh's word, or Yahweh's will? It is probably best to translate this in the same way that you did in the 1 Kings story. Pause this audio here.

In the fourth scene, the northern tribes of Israel tell Rehoboam that they have no place in the kingdom of David. The people ask, "What part do we have in David, and what part do we have in the son of Jesse?" Remember that the people do not want an answer to this question, but the people want to show Rehoboam that they feel there is no place for them now in the Kingdom of David.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Would it be best to translate this as a question or as a statement? Another way to translate this part of the story is: "We have nothing to do with King David, and we have nothing to do with this grandson of Jesse." Again, it is probably best to translate this in the same way that you did in the 1 Kings story. Pause this audio here.

The people of Israel also say they should go back to their own homes. Then, the people go home. Remember that the northern tribes of Israel do not accept a descendant of David as their king, but Rehoboam does rule over some of the people in the northern tribes who live in the land of the tribe of Judah in the south of Israel.

In the fifth scene, Rehoboam sends Adoniram to the people. Remember that Adoniram was in charge of forcing people to build buildings for Israel. The people of Israel kill Adoniram by throwing stones at Adoniram. Rehoboam escapes and quickly runs away in his chariot to Jerusalem.

Finally, in the last scene, the storyteller says that the northern tribes of Israel have been in rebellion against the house, or family, of David from that time until now. Remember that this shows that the events in this story are very important for the rest of the story of the divided kingdom of Israel.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the story and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 10:8–19 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has six scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* Rehoboam
* The northern tribes of Israel
* Jeroboam, the son of Nebat
* Older advisers who served King Solomon in the past
* Younger advisers who are Rehoboam's age
* Yahweh
* The prophet Ahijah, the Shilonite
* And Adoniram

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

But Rehoboam rejects the advice of the older advisers. Instead, Rehoboam asks the younger advisers what to do. These younger advisers also served Rehoboam, and they were the same age as Rehoboam. Rehoboam asks the younger advisers, "What should I answer the people who asked me to give them less work than my father gave them?"

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Rehoboam, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I do not want the people to think I am weak. If I follow the advice of the older advisers, people will say I am weaker than my father, King Solomon; or
* I prefer to see what the advisers of my own age think about this situation. I trust the people my own age more than the older advisers that my father had; or
* The people of Israel seem to complain a lot. I do not think that my father made the people work very hard; or
* I hope that my younger advisers give me better advice than the older advisers gave me. I think I will decide to follow the advice of the younger advisers, even before I know what their advice is.

Continue the drama.

The younger advisers tell Rehoboam that Rehoboam should reject the request of the people. The people complained that Rehoboam's father, Solomon, made them work very hard. The people wanted Rehoboam to give the people less work. The younger advisers say that Rehoboam should be even harsher than Solomon was. The younger advisers say that Rehoboam should tell the people, "My little finger is thicker than my father's waist!" The younger advisers also think Rehoboam should make the people's work even harder. Solomon punished the people with ordinary whips, but Rehoboam should punish the people with whips that sting like scorpions!

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing the younger advisers, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* Rehoboam should listen to our advice because Rehoboam knows us, and we know Rehoboam. We have the best ideas for how Rehoboam can be a strong king of Israel; or
* We think it would be a bad idea to follow the advice of the older advisers. Those old men do not know the new way of doing things. Rehoboam will show them what a real king looks like, one who is even tougher than King Solomon was; or
* These people of Israel need to know that they cannot come and talk to King Rehoboam like this. The people should respect the new king and not ask the king for things. Rehoboam will be even stronger and harsher than Rehoboam's father was!

Continue the drama.

Jeroboam and the people come back to Rehoboam on the third day. This is just as King Rehoboam told the people to do. Rehoboam talks harshly to the people. Rehoboam rejects the advice of the older advisers and instead follows the advice of the younger advisers. Rehoboam tells the people, "My father made you work hard, but I will make you work even harder! My father punished you with ordinary whips, but I will punish you with whips that sting like scorpions!"

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Rehoboam, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I like the advice of my younger advisers better than the advice of my older advisers. I am the king, so I can decide what I want to do; or
* I am ready to be an even harsher and more demanding king than my father was! or
* These people need to respect me. These people should never have asked me to lessen their heavy load of work because now they will need to work even harder.

Also, ask the people playing the people of Israel, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We are very disappointed that Rehoboam did not listen to us; or
* We are shocked that Rehoboam plans to be even harsher than his father was; or
* We do not plan to serve Rehoboam as our king now. We gave Rehoboam a chance to be kind to us in return for our loyalty. Rehoboam did not take the chance, and now we know that we are not welcome in the kingdom of Rehoboam. We must start to make other plans with Jeroboam as our leader.

Continue the drama.

Rehoboam refused to listen to the people of Israel. Yahweh caused this to happen in order to fulfill the promise that Ahijah the Shilonite spoke to Jeroboam, the son of Nebat.

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing the people of Israel who are listening to this story, generations after it happened, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We wish that Rehoboam had chosen to listen to the people of Israel. Maybe then there would never have been a divided kingdom of Israel; or
* We trust that Yahweh's ways are always good, even though we do not understand why Yahweh makes things happen; or
* We remember the promise that Ahijah the Shilonite made to Jeroboam in a previous story, and now we see that Yahweh keeps his promises that Yahweh makes.

Continue the drama.

The people of Israel realize that King Rehoboam has refused to listen to their requests to lessen their heavy load of work. The people say, "King David was not from one of our families, and the family of Jesse has nothing to do with us. Let us all go home. The family of David can take care of his own people." So, the people of Israel went to their homes. Rehoboam still rules over the northern tribes of Israel, who live in the cities of the tribe of Judah in the south of Israel.

Ask the people playing the people of Israel, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* Rehoboam will be sorry that he did not listen to us; or
* We will never let a man from the family of David rule as our king. We have nothing in common with the people of Israel from the south, from David's family; or
* We wonder who our new king will be? We do not want Rehoboam as our king, but we do not know who might be our king; or
* Those of us who live in the south, in the land of the tribe of Judah, are not happy that Rehoboam is ruling over us. But we do not have a choice because we are so close to Jerusalem.

Continue the drama.

King Rehoboam sends out Adoniram, the man who was in charge of forced labor. The people of Israel killed Adoniram with stones. Rehoboam hurried to get on his chariot, and Rehoboam fled to Jerusalem. Israel has been rebelling against the family of David since the time of this passage.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Rehoboam, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I will show the people of Israel that they must listen to me, and the people must do my building projects for me! or
* I am shocked that the people of Israel killed Adoniram! How will I complete my building projects now? or
* I want nothing to do with the people of the northern tribes of Israel. I had to escape quickly so that the people would not try to kill me as well! or
* I am afraid that the people of the northern tribes of Israel will kill me!

Also, ask the people playing the people of Israel, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We tried to ask Rehoboam for lighter work, but Rehoboam refused. Now we are ready to fight back against Rehoboam; or
* Rehoboam will never be our king; or
* Adoniram deserved to die because Adoniram forced us to do many work projects to build things for Solomon and Rehoboam; or
* We wish that we had been able to catch Rehoboam before he ran away to Jerusalem.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 10:8–19 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

The people of the northern tribes of Israel ask Rehoboam to lighten their heavy **yoke** of work.

Remember that a yoke is a wooden collar with two openings that people use to tie two oxen or two other animals together. In the Bible, sometimes a yoke means slavery or hard work.

It is probably best to translate this in the same way that you did in the previous 2 Chronicles passage and in the 1 Kings passage.

Rehoboam rejects the advice of the older advisors, or **elders**. Translate elders in the same way you have previously, and refer to elder in the Master Glossary for more information about elder.

Rehoboam also asks the young men with whom he grew up for advice. Other ways to translate this could be younger advisers, young advisers, or younger officials.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How will you translate **young men** in this passage? Pause this audio here.

Jeroboam and the people of the northern tribes of Israel return to **King** Rehoboam after three days. A king is someone who rules over a city or territory. Translate king the same as you have in previous passages. For more information on king, refer to the Master Glossary.

The events in this passage were from **Yahweh**. Yahweh is God's personal name. Translate Yahweh the same as you have in previous passages. For more information on Yahweh, refer to the Master Glossary.

Yahweh caused these events to happen in order to fulfill the **word** that Yahweh spoke to Jeroboam through Ahijah the Shilonite. The word that Yahweh spoke to Jeroboam was a promise from Yahweh. Translate "word" the same as you have in previous passages. For more information on word, refer to the Master Glossary.

The people of Israel say that they have nothing to do with **David**, the son of Jesse. In this passage, **Israel** and **Israelites** refer to the northern tribes of Israel. David was the first king of Israel. Translate Israel, Israelites, and David the same as you have in previous passages. For more information on Israel and Israelites, refer to the Master Glossary.

Rehoboam rules over the people from the northern tribes of Israel who now live in the cities of **Judah**. A **city** is a settlement of people with a wall around it. Judah is one of the tribes of Israel, but people often used the name "Judah" to talk about the two tribes in the south that still followed Rehoboam as their king. Translate city and Judah the same as you have in previous passages. Refer to Judah in the Master Glossary for more information.

Rehoboam escaped in his **chariot** to **Jerusalem**. A chariot is a two\-wheeled cart that horses pull. Jerusalem is the capital city of Israel. Translate chariot and Jerusalem the same as you have in previous passages.

Israel rebelled or revolted against the house, or family, of David from that time until now. The "house of David" means the descendants of David, the first king of Israel.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How will you translate "**house of David**" in this passage? For more information on "house of David," refer to the Master Glossary.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 10:8-19
