## 2 Chronicles 18:1–8 (id: eng-2ch-p30-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 18:1–8 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 18:1–8 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 18:1–8 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous passage, the storyteller told us about when Jehoshaphat followed Yahweh and was a good king of Judah. Now, the storyteller starts to tell us of a time when Jehoshaphat makes mistakes and visits King Ahab in Israel. We can also find this story in 1 Kings 22:1–9, but with some differences.

Stop here and do this activity as a group: If you have already translated 1 Kings 22:1–9, listen to your translation of that passage. Pause this audio here.

The storyteller starts the story by telling us again that Jehoshaphat had lots of money and many people respected Jehoshaphat. Even though Jehoshaphat did not need money or respect, Jehoshaphat made an alliance, a formal relationship, with Israel by marrying his son to Ahab's daughter. The alliance meant Jehoshaphat and Ahab did not fight each other. The two kings would work together and support each other.

Stop here and do this activity as a group: Tell a story of a time when two people married or made a formal relationship to make two families or groups of people work together and support each other. Pause this audio here.

After Jehoshaphat married his son to Ahab's daughter, Jehoshaphat goes to visit Ahab. We do not know exactly how many years there were between the marriage and the visit, but it was probably more than three years. Jehoshaphat leaves Jerusalem and goes to visit Ahab in the city of Samaria, in Israel. Jehoshaphat probably travels to Samaria with a large number of people. In the time of King Jehoshaphat, it was normal for kings to travel with many servants and officials. When Jehoshaphat is in Samaria, Ahab tells his people to kill lots of sheep and "cattle," or oxen. Sheep and cattle are both large animals, and when people cook one of these animals, the animal will feed lots of people.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of sheep. Pause this audio here.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of cattle. Pause this audio here.

The people probably do not kill the animals for a sacrifice to please Yahweh. Ahab probably gives the sheep and cattle to Jehoshaphat and the people with Jehoshaphat as part of a big feast. The feast is to celebrate the visit of Jehoshaphat and all the people with Jehoshaphat. Ahab wants Jehoshaphat to feel special and important. Ahab wants Jehoshaphat to help Ahab attack Ramoth in Gilead.

Stop here as a group and look at a map of Israel, Judah, and western Syria. Find the cities of Jerusalem, Samaria, and Ramoth in Gilead. Pause this audio here.

You may remember that Ramoth Gilead was part of the land Yahweh gave to the tribe of Gad and was a city of refuge where the Levites lived. However, the people of Aram attacked and now control Ramoth Gilead. After the last war between Israel and Aram, the king of Aram said he would give Ramoth Gilead back to the king of Israel. The king of Aram did not give Ramoth Gilead back to Ahab, and now Ahab wants the city back. Syria is another name for Aram. The Aramean people are the same people as the Syrian people.

Ahab wants Jehoshaphat to help to fight the Arameans at Ramoth Gilead and to help get back control of Ramoth Gilead. Jehoshaphat tells Ahab that Jehoshaphat and Ahab are united like being one person. Jehoshaphat says that the fighting people of Judah and Israel are one people together. Jehoshaphat will support Ahab in the war to fight the Arameans. Jehoshaphat tells the king of Israel that the two kings must ask Yahweh for guidance before the kings do anything else. In the time of the kings of Israel and Judah, the kings used prophets to ask for guidance from God.

The king of Israel gathers the prophets so that the kings can ask for guidance. There are about 400 prophets. We do not know if the prophets were all men or if they were both men and women. The prophets were people who said they spoke messages from God. It is possible that the prophets all served the king of Israel. The king of Israel asks if the kings should go to war to fight against the people in Ramoth Gilead or hold back and not attack. The king of Israel does not expect the prophets to go to war.

The prophets tell the kings that God will give Ramoth Gilead into the king of Israel's "hand" or control. The prophets use polite language and do not address the kings directly. Jehoshaphat does not trust the prophets' answer. Jehoshaphat does not think any of the prophets bring a message from Yahweh. Jehoshaphat asks the king of Israel if all the prophets of Yahweh are gone or if there is still one prophet that the kings can ask for guidance. The king of Israel tells Jehoshaphat about the one prophet of Yahweh whom the kings can talk to. The king of Israel hates the prophet of Yahweh because, in all the time the prophet has brought messages, the messages for the king are never good news. The prophet always prophesies bad things to happen to the king of Israel. The king of Israel names the prophet as Micaiah, who is the son of Imlah. Imlah was probably Micaiah's father.

Jehoshaphat replies directly to the king of Israel but addresses him as the king. Jehoshaphat uses polite language to tell the king of Israel that the king should not say bad things about a prophet of Yahweh.

Stop here and do this activity as a group: Tell a story about a time when you needed to tell someone politely that the person was doing something wrong. Maybe the person was older than you or a very important person in your community. Pause this audio here.

The king of Israel calls one of his officials over to him and asks the official to bring Micaiah, son of Imlah, to the two kings. The king wants Micaiah to come to the two kings as soon as possible.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 18:1–8 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has three scenes.

**In the first scene:** The storyteller tells us that Jehoshaphat is rich, well respected, and has a formal relationship with Ahab.

**In the second scene:** Jehoshaphat visits Ahab in Samaria, and Ahab gives a big feast for Jehoshaphat. At the feast, Ahab persuades Jehoshaphat to fight with Ahab against Ramoth Gilead. Jehoshaphat wants to help Ahab but wants to hear from Yahweh first.

**In the third scene:** The king of Israel gathers 400 prophets and asks the prophets if Israel should fight Ramoth Gilead. The prophets tell the king of Israel to fight because God will give Ramoth Gilead to the king of Israel. Jehoshaphat wants to hear from a prophet of Yahweh. The king of Israel says that Micaiah is the only prophet of Yahweh. The king of Israel hates Micaiah because Micaiah always prophesies bad things for the king of Israel. The king of Israel asks an official to bring Micaiah to the two kings.

The characters in this passage are:

* The storyteller
* Jehoshaphat, king of Judah
* Ahab, king of Israel
* Jehoshaphat's people
* Four hundred prophets
* And the king of Israel's official

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In the previous passage, the storyteller told us Jehoshaphat followed Yahweh, became powerful, and was a successful king of Judah. Now the storyteller wants to tell us about the time Jehoshaphat visits Ahab, the king of Israel. The storyteller begins the story by reminding us that Jehoshaphat is wealthy, with lots of money and valuable things. The storyteller also tells us that Jehoshaphat has a lot of honor, has respect from lots of people, and is well known by lots of people. Jehoshaphat has allied himself with Ahab; Jehoshaphat has made a formal relationship with Ahab by marrying Jehoshaphat's son to Ahab's daughter.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: What do you call a formal relationship between two leaders where the leaders will help each other and possibly fight to protect each other's countries? Pause this audio here.

A few years after Jehoshaphat and Ahab became allies, Jehoshaphat visits Ahab in Samaria. You may remember that the storyteller in 1 Kings tells us that Jehoshaphat visits Ahab in the third year after Aram made peace with Israel. But the storyteller in this story only gives a general idea of when Jehoshaphat visits Ahab. Jehoshaphat goes down from Jerusalem, which is on top of a hill, to Samaria, which is lower down. Samaria is north of Jerusalem.

Stop here as a group and, if needed, look at a map of Israel, Judah, and western Syria. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How will you say Jehoshaphat went down to see the king of Israel? Remember that "going down" means that Jehoshaphat traveled from a higher place to a lower place. Pause this audio here.

When Jehoshaphat visits Ahab, Ahab kills sheep and cattle to celebrate the visit and to provide food for a feast.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of sheep if needed. Pause this audio here.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of cattle if needed. Pause this audio here.

The storyteller tells us that Ahab urges Jehoshaphat to help Ahab attack Ramoth Gilead. The storyteller uses language to tell us that Ahab wants Jehoshaphat to do something that is not good.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How do you talk about someone who tries to get someone to do something that is not good or the right thing to do? Pause this audio here.

The storyteller uses Ahab's and Jehoshaphat's titles as kings of Israel and Judah when the storyteller says what Ahab asks Jehoshaphat. Ahab wants Jehoshaphat and Jehoshaphat's fighting men to help attack the people at Ramoth Gilead. Now, the storyteller stops naming Ahab as the king of Israel. Jehoshaphat replies to say that the two kings are united and will work together as one fighting people. The people of Judah will join the people of Israel in a war against Aram at Ramoth Gilead.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: If you have one, what particular saying or phrase do you use to say that you and another person or group of people will do something together, like you are one person or group? Pause this audio here.

At the same time as Jehoshaphat agrees to fight with Ahab, Jehoshaphat also says that the kings should ask Yahweh for guidance. The storyteller stops naming Ahab and only uses the title "the king of Israel."

The king of Israel gathers together prophets. We do not know how long it takes for the prophets to come to the kings. The prophets are probably part of the people living in the king's household and probably come to the kings on the same day or the next day. The group of prophets probably included men and women. The king of Israel asks the prophets if Israel and Judah should go to war to attack and fight the people of Ramoth Gilead, or not go to war. The prophets all reply that God will give Ramoth Gilead into the king's hand. The prophets use the word "hand" to symbolize power. The prophets say the king of Israel will have power over Ramoth Gilead. The prophets do not call God by God's personal name.

Jehoshaphat asks if there is no prophet of Yahweh in the group that the kings can ask for guidance. Jehoshaphat thinks that it is possible that there are no prophets of Yahweh advising the king of Israel.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How do you ask someone politely if a person is in a town or village if you do not know if that person is still there or has gone away? Pause this audio here.

The king of Israel tells Jehoshaphat that there is one prophet whom the kings can use to ask questions to Yahweh. The king of Israel tells Jehoshaphat about the one prophet of Yahweh who is in Israel. The king of Israel says that he hates the prophet and sees the prophet as an enemy. The prophet's messages for the king of Israel are never good. The king of Israel names the prophet as Micaiah, son of Imlah. Jehoshaphat tells the king of Israel that the king of Israel should not speak in that way. The king of Israel should not say bad things about Micaiah because Micaiah is a prophet of Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How do you tell somebody not to say bad things

about another person? Pause this audio here.

The king of Israel asks one of his officials to come to the king and asks the official to bring Micaiah to the two kings.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 18:1–8 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has three scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* The storyteller
* Jehoshaphat, king of Judah
* Ahab, king of Israel
* Jehoshaphat's people
* Four hundred prophets
* And the king of Israel's official

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

The storyteller tells us that Jehoshaphat is rich, that everyone respects Jehoshaphat, and that Jehoshaphat has a formal relationship with Ahab.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing the storyteller, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Jehoshaphat is a powerful king of Judah; or
* I told you Jehoshaphat followed Yahweh. But Jehoshaphat made an alliance with Ahab, who did not follow Yahweh; or
* Now I will tell you about a mistake Jehoshaphat made.

Continue the drama.

Jehoshaphat visits Ahab in Samaria, and Ahab gives a big feast for Jehoshaphat and his people. At the feast, Ahab persuades Jehoshaphat to fight with Ahab against Ramoth Gilead. Jehoshaphat wants to help Ahab but wants to hear from Yahweh first.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Jehoshaphat, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Ahab has made a big feast for us. Ahab is trying to impress us and make us feel welcome; or
* I am happy to help Ahab because our families are connected; or
* It is important to consult Yahweh. I do not want to fight if Yahweh does not tell us to fight; or
* I am worried that Ahab does not care about Yahweh.

Ask the person playing Jehoshaphat's people, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* We are very impressed by this amazing big feast for us; or
* We feel very welcome in Samaria.

Ask the person playing the king of Israel, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I want Jehoshaphat to help me get control of Ramoth Gilead and make it part of Israel again; or
* I am happy that Jehoshaphat wants to help me; or
* I have lots of prophets who will bring messages to me from God. I do not care which god the prophets talk to.

Continue the drama.

The king of Israel gathers 400 prophets and asks the prophets if Israel should fight Ramoth Gilead. The prophets tell the king of Israel to fight because God will give Ramoth Gilead to the king of Israel. Jehoshaphat wants to hear from a prophet of Yahweh. The king of Israel says that Micaiah is the only prophet of Yahweh. The king of Israel hates Micaiah because Micaiah always prophesies bad things for the king of Israel. The king of Israel asks an official to bring Micaiah to the two kings.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing the king of Israel, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am excited. All the prophets say that I should attack Ramoth Gilead. God is with me and will give me the victory; or
* Why does Jehoshaphat care about Yahweh? Yahweh's prophet never says anything good to me. I do not want to talk to Yahweh's prophet; or
* If I want Jehoshaphat to help me, I will need to get Micaiah to come and prophesy.

Ask the person playing the 400 prophets, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* We want the king to like us, so we tell the king, "Yes, go and fight Ramoth Gilead"; or
* You, the king of Israel, are powerful. Of course, you will win; or
* Listen to all of us. We can tell you what God is saying.

Ask the person playing Jehoshaphat, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* There are so many prophets, but I do not think these prophets speak messages from Yahweh; or
* I do not trust these prophets; or
* I must hear from Yahweh before I go with Israel and take my army to fight Aram in Ramoth Gilead; or
* The king of Israel does not respect Micaiah, Yahweh's prophet. I do not like what the king of Israel says about Micaiah.

Ask the person playing the king of Israel's official, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I must find Micaiah quickly; or
* I wonder what Micaiah will say this time. I hope Micaiah does not make my king angry.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 18:1–8 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

Jehoshaphat has great **wealth** and **honor**. Wealth is when a person has lots of money and possessions. A person with honor is a person whom people respect and value because the person makes good choices and has good behavior. Use the same words or phrases for wealth and honor as you used in previous passages.

Jehoshaphat allied himself with Ahab through marriage. Jehoshaphat made a formal relationship between Judah and Israel by marrying Jehoshaphat's son to Ahab's daughter.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **allied through marriage**. Pause this audio here.

Ahab slaughtered **sheep** and **cattle** for Jehoshaphat. Use the same words or phrases for sheep and cattle as you used in previous passages. Ahab killed the animals for the group of visitors to eat as part of a feast.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for "**slaughtered**," or killed, an animal. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

The **king** of **Israel** asked the king of **Judah** to go to attack Ramoth Gilead. Use the same words or phrases for king, Israel, and Judah as you used in previous passages. For more information on king, Israel, and Judah, refer to the Master Glossary.

Jehoshaphat told the king of Israel to seek counsel or advice from **Yahweh**. Yahweh is God's personal name. In this passage, God is called Yahweh. Be sure to translate Yahweh in the same way as you have in previous passages, and remember that Yahweh is in the Master Glossary.

The king of Israel gathered the **prophets**. Use the same word or phrase for prophets as you used in previous passages. For more information on prophet, refer to the Master Glossary.

The prophets say that **God**, the most powerful being, will give Syria to the king of Israel. Use the same word or phrase for God as you used in previous passages. For more information on God, refer to the Master Glossary.

God will give Ramoth Gilead into the king's "**hand**" or into the king's power. Use the same word or phrase for "into the king's hand" as you used in previous passages.

The king of Israel says that Micaiah never **prophesies** anything good about the king. Use the same word or phrase for prophesies as you used in previous passages. For more information on prophecy, refer to the Master Glossary.

The king of Israel asks an official to bring Micaiah to the kings. An official is a person who serves the king and helps the king run the country. An official has high status in the king's household and does not do domestic tasks.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **official**. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 18:1-8

## 2 Chronicles 18:9–27 (id: eng-2ch-p31-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 18:9–27 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 18:9–27 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 18:9–27 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

This passage continues the story where the kings of Judah and Israel meet together in Samaria, in Israel. The kings want to know if the fighters of Judah and Israel should attack the Arameans at Ramoth Gilead. Syria is another name for Aram, and the Aramean people are the same people as the Syrian people. The kings heard 400 prophets tell the kings to attack, but Jehoshaphat, the king of Judah, wanted to hear from Yahweh.

Stop here and look at a map that shows Israel and the cities of Samaria and Ramoth Gilead. Pause this audio here.

We can also find this story in 1 Kings 22:10–28, but with some differences.

Stop here and do this activity as a group: If you have already translated 1 Kings 22:10–28, listen to your translation of that passage. Pause this audio here.

The kings wear special clothes or robes that show they are kings. Special servants made the robes from fine material and decorated the robes with special signs and patterns.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: What special clothes or decorations does your king or most important leader wear to show that they are the king? Pause this audio here.

The kings are sitting on their thrones, their special seats. The kings are at the threshing floor near the gate of the city of Samaria. The threshing floor is an open area with a flat, level, stone surface. People went to the threshing floor to beat or break the outer coating off the grains of wheat or other grains.

Stop here and look at a picture of people using a threshing floor to separate wheat grains from other parts of the plant. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: What open spaces do your leaders use for special times when the leaders are with your people? Are these spaces inside or outside of buildings, or inside or outside of the cities or towns? Pause this audio here.

The threshing floor was probably outside the city gate so that the wind blew the light outer coating of the grain away. Big walls surrounded the city of Samaria to protect the city. People used big gates in the city walls to go in and out of the city.

Stop here and look at a picture of a city gate in the wall of a city. Pause this audio here.

There is lots of space for people at the threshing floor. You will remember that in David's time, two important events with the ark of the covenant happened on a threshing floor. The kings probably think the threshing floor is an important place to hear from God.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: What place or places in your city, or where you live, are special for you to hear from God? Pause this audio here.

The prophets are with the kings and are giving their messages and talking, or possibly dancing and shouting. One of the prophets is called Zedekiah and is the son of Kenaanah. Kenaanah is probably the father of Zedekiah. Zedekiah made horns from iron. Iron is a hard metal, and people use iron to make tools and weapons. Zedekiah probably shaped the iron to look like the horns on a bull, which is an adult male cow.

Stop here and look at a picture of a bull with horns and imagine the horns made from iron. Pause this audio here.

Sometimes bulls are dangerous. If a bull attacks a person, the bull hits a person with its horns and pushes its horns into the person's body. A bull might hurt or gore a person and cause the person to die. Zedekiah probably put the iron horns on a helmet. Zedekiah tells the king of Israel that Yahweh said the king of Israel would use the horns to gore or make a big, bloody hole in the Arameans. The king would destroy or completely defeat the Arameans. Zedekiah probably gave the helmet to the king as a symbol of the words. You might remember that Zedekiah's words are very similar to the blessing Moses gave to the tribes descended from Joseph in Deuteronomy 33:17\. But Zedekiah does not say that the king must be faithful to Yahweh like Moses said the tribes should be.

Stop here and do this activity as a group: Listen to Deuteronomy 33:17 in your own language, if possible. Pause this audio here.

The storyteller tells us that all the other prophets at the threshing floor say the same thing as Zedekiah. All the prophets encourage the king of Israel to attack the Syrians at Ramoth Gilead and win the battle. The prophets say Yahweh will give Ramoth Gilead into the king of Israel's hand, into the king's power.

A messenger, a person who carries a message, went to order Micaiah to come to the kings. The messenger speaks to Micaiah and tells Micaiah to pay special attention and know that all the prophets are saying the same thing. The prophets are all saying what the king of Israel wants to hear. The messenger tells Micaiah to give a message like the other prophets and say what the king wants to hear. Micaiah replies to the messenger and uses the words of a strong oath or promise. Micaiah will only say what God wants Micaiah to say. Micaiah says, "My God," the God whom Micaiah worships. In 1 Kings, the storyteller says "Yahweh" instead of God.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Talk about a time when someone has told you to say something to keep another person happy. Maybe the person asked you to say something that was not true or to leave out some important information. Why did the person not want you to say the truth? Pause this audio here.

Micaiah arrives at the threshing floor and meets with the kings. The king of Israel asks Micaiah the same question that he asked the 400 prophets. Micaiah answers the king and tells the king to attack and win. Micaiah says someone will give the Arameans into the king's hand or power. Micaiah does not mean what he says. The king of Israel knew that Micaiah was not giving a true prophecy.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Talk about a time when you heard someone talk, and you knew that the person did not mean what they said. The way the person said it showed that the person did not expect people to believe the person. How did the person sound when the person said the untrue thing? Pause this audio here.

The king of Israel is frustrated with Micaiah. The king speaks harshly to Micaiah for not telling the truth. We do not know when or if Micaiah has told the king other things that are not true.

Micaiah answers the king of Israel's question with what Micaiah saw in a vision. We do not know when Micaiah saw this vision. Yahweh sends a vision to Micaiah to be a message for people. Micaiah saw the people of Israel spread out all over a hill. Micaiah uses special language to describe how the people look on the hill. Micaiah says the people look like a group of sheep who do not have a shepherd. Sheep live in open places with lots of grass and small plants. Normally, a shepherd works to keep sheep together to help protect the sheep and move them from one place to another. When no shepherd is with the sheep, the sheep wander all over the place, do not stay together, and are not safe. Micaiah sees people, not sheep. The people listening to Micaiah are used to seeing sheep on hills and know that it is wrong for a group of sheep not to have a shepherd.

Stop here and look at a picture of a group of sheep on a hillside, and imagine the sheep all over the hillside instead of together in a group. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: If you see a group of people scattered over a large area, what might you compare those people to? How would you help someone understand what the group of people looked like? Pause this audio here.

Micaiah tells the king what Yahweh says to Micaiah. Yahweh says the people have no master, no person to tell the people what to do and to look after the people. Yahweh says that each person is to go home in peace or safely and not fight.

The king of Israel then talks to Jehoshaphat, the king of Judah. The king of Israel uses a question that he does not expect Jehoshaphat to answer. The king of Israel uses the question to say that Micaiah has done just what the king of Israel expected Micaiah to do.

Micaiah tells the king of Israel about another vision from Yahweh. Micaiah tells the king of Israel to hear a message from Yahweh. Micaiah talks about a vision. We do not know when Micaiah saw the vision. In the vision, Micaiah saw Yahweh sitting on a throne, a special seat for a king. Many heavenly beings stood around Yahweh on all sides of the throne. Yahweh asked the heavenly beings to say who would go to Ahab, the king of Israel, and try to persuade Ahab to attack Ramoth Gilead. Yahweh said that Ahab will die if Ahab attacks Ramoth Gilead. The heavenly beings gave different responses to Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Talk about a time someone asked a group of people to do something that the people did not want to do or would find difficult to do. How did the group of people respond? What did the people say to each other or the person who asked the question? Pause this audio here.

In the vision, a spirit, a heavenly being, stood in front of Yahweh and said it will entice the king of Israel. The spirit is a supernatural being who lives and serves Yahweh in heaven. The spirit is not the Holy Spirit or a demon. The spirit said it will be a deceiving spirit in the mouths of the King of Israel's prophets. Someone who deceives a person makes a person believe something that is not true. The spirit made the prophets believe what the prophets said. Yahweh knew that the spirit would persuade Ahab to attack Ramoth Gilead. Micaiah tells the king of Israel that Yahweh sent the spirit who made the prophets tell Ahab to attack Ramoth Gilead. Yahweh has decided and declared that Ahab will have a terrible end.

Zedekiah, son of Kenaanah, is angry. Zedekiah slaps Micaiah on the face to insult Micaiah.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: What ways do people use to insult other people in your community? Pause this audio here.

Zedekiah also uses a question that he does not expect Micaiah to answer. Zedekiah asks how or why the spirit of Yahweh has gone from Zedekiah to Micaiah. Zedekiah is not talking about the Holy Spirit or a demon. Zedekiah is talking about a spiritual being who carries the message from Yahweh. Zedekiah does not think that Micaiah has replaced Zedekiah as the main person to receive messages from Yahweh. Zedekiah thinks that Zedekiah is better than Micaiah. Micaiah responds to Zedekiah and tells Zedekiah that Zedekiah will know the answer to his question on a day in the future. In the future, there will be a time when Zedekiah will hide in the most protected room inside a building or in the middle of the city. This may be when the Arameans defeat the Israelites, or a time when Zedekiah hides from Israelites because Zedekiah gives false prophecies to the Israelites.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: If you want to hide from people who want to hurt you, where would you go to hide? Pause this audio here.

After Micaiah told the king a prophecy that the king did not like, the king of Israel gives an order to the people who serve the king. The king of Israel tells people to take Micaiah away and take Micaiah back to Amon and Joash. Amon was the governor, or the most important official, of the city of Samaria. We do not know if Joash was a male offspring of the king. The king's son might be a special title for someone who is a royal official and responsible for safety in the city.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Who in your community is responsible for looking after the safety of your people? What title or special name, if any, do they have? Pause this audio here.

The king of Israel commands Amon and Joash to put Micaiah in prison. A prison is a strong building. If someone puts a person in a prison, the person cannot leave the building. People will guard the person in the prison, and there are locks or barriers on the door to stop the person from opening the door and leaving.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your community, what do you do with people who disobey the rules in your community or who might be a danger to other people in the community? Pause this audio here.

The king of Israel orders Amon and Joash to only feed Micaiah enough food and water for Micaiah to stay alive. In Israel, bread that people made from wheat flour was not a special food.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: What do you do with people who disobey your most important leader and say bad things about your most important leader? Pause this audio here.

The king wants the order to last until the time the king of Israel returns safely from attacking Ramoth Gilead.

Micaiah responds to the king of Israel's order to say that if the king comes back safely from the battle, Yahweh did not speak through Micaiah. Micaiah uses the outcome of the battle to show the people of Israel whether Micaiah is a prophet of Yahweh or a false prophet. Micaiah then speaks to everyone who can hear him. Micaiah tells everybody to hear the warning from Yahweh.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 18:9–27 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has five scenes.

**In the first scene:** As the kings wait for Micaiah to arrive, the two kings sit outside the city of Samaria with many prophets in front of them. One of the prophets, Zedekiah, has iron horns like a bull. Zedekiah says Yahweh says the kings will defeat the Arameans with the iron horns. The prophets make a loud noise and tell the kings to attack Aram at Ramoth Gilead because the kings will win the battle.

**In the second scene:** The messenger tells Micaiah to give the king the same prophecy as the other prophets. Micaiah says he will say what Yahweh tells him to say.

**In the third scene:** Micaiah says the false prophecy, but the king of Israel knows Micaiah is not giving a prophecy from Yahweh. Micaiah speaks about a vision from Yahweh where Micaiah sees Israelites who have lost their leader. The king of Israel is angry.

**In the fourth scene:** Micaiah speaks about another vision where Yahweh wants a spirit to persuade Ahab to attack Ramoth Gilead. Yahweh says Ahab will die if Ahab attacks Ramoth Gilead. A spirit agrees to deceive the king. Micaiah tells the king and everyone around the king that Yahweh put a spirit in the prophets to speak false prophecies. Zedekiah slaps and insults Micaiah. Micaiah says Zedekiah will understand one day in the future when Zedekiah hides away in an inner room.

**In the fifth scene:** The king of Israel orders people to take Micaiah back to Amon and Joash, and for Amon and Joash to put Micaiah in prison with basic food and water until the king of Israel returns from attacking Ramoth Gilead. Micaiah says if the king returns, Yahweh did not speak through Micaiah and warns everyone to listen to the prophecy.

The characters in this passage are:

* King of Israel
* Jehoshaphat, king of Judah
* 400 prophets
* Zedekiah, son of Kenaanah
* A messenger
* Micaiah
* Yahweh
* Heavenly beings
* A spirit, one of the heavenly beings
* And the king of Israel's officials

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

This story follows immediately after the previous story, when all the prophets prophesied that the kings would be successful if the kings attacked Ramoth Gilead. The two kings are now sitting together outside the gates of Samaria at an open space where the people of Samaria can thresh or beat their grain. The kings are wearing special clothes and sitting on special seats, called thrones. We do not know how much time has passed between the first time the prophets gathered and this time at the threshing floor. All the prophets make a lot of noise and dance and chant as they prophesy. One prophet is called Zedekiah. Zedekiah has made two horns from iron. Zedekiah says that Yahweh has a message. When Zedekiah says "you," it is only to one person, the king of Israel. As a bull gores with its horns, so will the king of Israel push or gore the men of Aram until the king destroys them.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of a bull with horns if needed. Pause this audio here.

At the threshing floor, in front of the kings, all the prophets are saying similar things. All the prophets tell the king of Israel to attack Ramoth Gilead and win. Yahweh will give Ramoth Gilead into the hand or the power of the king of Israel. The prophets use polite language and do not address the king directly. The prophets do not tell Jehoshaphat to attack.

A messenger for the king of Israel had gone to get Micaiah and bring Micaiah to the kings. As the messenger and Micaiah are coming back to the kings, the messenger tries to give advice to Micaiah. The messenger uses special language to draw attention to the importance of what the messenger tells Micaiah. The messenger wants Micaiah to know that the words of the prophets are in one accord; all the prophets say the same thing. The messenger wants Micaiah to say the same prophecy as the 400 prophets. The storyteller indicates that Micaiah's response will not be what the messenger wants to hear. Micaiah will not agree with the messenger. Micaiah uses a strong promise to say that Micaiah will only say what Micaiah's God tells Micaiah to say.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: What words or phrases do you use to make a strong promise or oath that you will do something? Pause this audio here.

Micaiah comes to the king of Israel. The king of Israel asks the same question to Micaiah as he asked the 400 prophets. Micaiah tells the king to go up, to attack and be victorious or win. Micaiah says Ramoth Gilead will be given into the king's hand or his power. Micaiah uses words that mean the opposite of what Micaiah seems to say. Usually, a person talks like this to mock or insult someone. The king hears how Micaiah talks and is frustrated with Micaiah.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How do you use your voice tone and body language to obviously show your audience that you are saying something that is not true or that you do not believe is correct? Try out saying things to each other in this way. Pause this audio here.

The king uses the question, "How many times must I make you swear to only tell the truth?" to say how the king feels about Micaiah.

Stop here and do this activity as a group: Have one person pretend to be frustrated with another person in the group and use questions to express how they feel about something the person has done. An example might be for the person to say, "How many times do I need to ask you to put away your clothes?" or "Why do you always forget to do what I ask?" Notice if you use questions to show that frustration or if you show frustration in another way. Pause this audio here.

Micaiah then speaks about a vision from Yahweh. Micaiah saw the people of Israel scattered on the hills. The people are not together in one group but spread out. Micaiah says the people were like sheep without a shepherd. Yahweh told Micaiah that the people have no master or leader. Yahweh said to let each person go home in peace. When a person goes somewhere in peace, that person does not fight and is safe.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How can you help people to understand that Micaiah is describing a vision that Yahweh gave to Micaiah rather than something that is actually happening in the story? Pause this audio here.

After Micaiah tells the king of Israel about the vision, the king of Israel speaks to Jehoshaphat, king of Judah. The king of Israel speaks about Micaiah. The king of Israel does not expect Jehoshaphat to answer the question, "Did I not tell you that Micaiah never prophesies anything good about me?"

Micaiah gives another prophecy. We do not know if the story of Micaiah giving this prophecy follows immediately from the story before, or if it is a completely different time Micaiah gave a prophecy to the king of Israel. We do not know when Micaiah saw the second vision. Micaiah tells the people listening to hear the word or the message from Yahweh. In Micaiah's vision, Yahweh sits on his throne. There are heavenly beings around Yahweh on the left and right of Yahweh. Micaiah describes the group of heavenly beings as a group that contains many, many beings. The heavenly beings serve Yahweh and do what Yahweh wants to be done. Yahweh asks all the heavenly beings if one of the beings will go to persuade Ahab to attack the people in Ramoth Gilead using words or actions that are not true. But Yahweh says Ahab will die if Ahab attacks Ramoth Gilead.

Micaiah uses special language to say the heavenly beings speak and that each being says a different thing. Each being says a different response to Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your language, how would you describe it when lots of people talk at one time and give different responses to a question? Pause this audio here.

Then one of the beings, a spirit, moves to be in front of Yahweh. The spirit is a heavenly being, not the Holy Spirit or a demon. The spirit says that it will persuade Ahab to attack Ramoth Gilead. Yahweh asks the spirit how the spirit will persuade Ahab. The spirit tells Yahweh that it will go from heaven to Ahab's prophets in Israel. In Israel, the spirit will make all Ahab's prophets say lies. Yahweh tells the spirit that the spirit will be successful and to go persuade Ahab.

Micaiah tells the king of Israel and the other people listening to Micaiah that the spirit came to the prophets. The spirit made the prophets say untrue prophecies. Yahweh has decided that other nations will destroy and kill the king of Israel. Zedekiah, the son of Kenaanah, is one of the people listening to Micaiah. Zedekiah moves close to Micaiah and slaps or hits Micaiah in the face. A person slaps another person with an open hand, and it makes a loud noise when the hand hits the face.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: What different words do you use when one person hits another person in the face? Pause this audio here.

Zedekiah uses a question to insult Micaiah. Zedekiah asks how the spirit from Yahweh went from Zedekiah to Micaiah. Zedekiah questions why Yahweh would speak to Micaiah and not Zedekiah. Micaiah replies to Zedekiah to say that Zedekiah will know the answer to Zedekiah's question one day in the future. Zedekiah will know when he goes to hide away from people in a room away from the outside walls of the city or building. We do not know when Zedekiah knows the answer to his question or what happens to Zedekiah at that time.

After Micaiah tells the king of Israel about the visions, the king of Israel is angry. The king of Israel tells his officials to take hold of Micaiah and take Micaiah back to Amon and Joash. The king of Israel tells the people who hold Micaiah to give a message to Amon and Joash. The king of Israel uses special words to show that what follows is a royal command for Amon and Joash. The king wants Amon and Joash to put Micaiah in prison and only give Micaiah small amounts of bread and water to eat and drink. The king wants Micaiah to only have enough bread and water to keep Micaiah alive.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How would you describe the amount of food that Amon and Joash will give to Micaiah in prison? Pause this audio here.

The king wants Micaiah to be in prison until the king comes back safely after fighting the Arameans. Micaiah speaks to the king of Israel to say that if the king comes back, Yahweh did not speak through Micaiah. Micaiah then speaks to everybody to tell everybody to hear or remember what Micaiah said.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How would you tell a large group of people to listen carefully and remember something that you say? Pause this audio here.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 18: 9–27 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has five scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* King of Israel
* Jehoshaphat, king of Judah
* 400 prophets
* Zedekiah, son of Kenaanah
* A messenger
* Micaiah
* Yahweh
* Heavenly beings
* A spirit, one of the heavenly beings
* And the king of Israel's officials

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

As the kings wait for Micaiah to arrive, the two kings sit outside the city of Samaria with many prophets in front of them. One of the prophets, Zedekiah, has iron horns like a bull. Zedekiah says Yahweh says the kings will defeat the Arameans with the iron horns. The prophets make a loud noise and tell the kings to attack Aram at Ramoth Gilead because the kings will win the battle.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Jehoshaphat, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* What a noise and strange sight. Are these men speaking from Yahweh or something else? or
* This man, Zedekiah, is sure that Israel will win the battle; or
* I need to hear from a prophet who speaks messages from Yahweh.

Ask the person playing the king of Israel, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* All these prophets are saying the same thing. These prophets must be right! or
* I feel excited and very important. Zedekiah says that I will win if I fight at Ramoth Gilead; or
* Look at the iron horns that Zedekiah wears! I will push back and hurt the Arameans like a bull pushes and hurts people.

Ask the person playing Zedekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Look at me! I know that Israel will be strong and dangerous like a bull; or
* Listen to me and my message. It is time for Israel to take back Ramoth Gilead; or
* The other prophets say the same as me. I feel powerful and excited.

Continue the drama.

The messenger tells Micaiah to give the king the same prophecy as the other prophets. Micaiah says he will say what Yahweh tells Micaiah to say.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing the messenger, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am worried that Micaiah will make the king angry; or
* I hope Micaiah will give a good prophecy like the other prophets; or
* I do not think Micaiah will please my king today.

Ask the person playing Micaiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I know that I will make the king angry again; or
* I want to be faithful to Yahweh; or
* Yahweh, please give me your words to speak today.

Continue the drama.

Micaiah says the false prophecy, but the king of Israel knows Micaiah is not giving a prophecy from Yahweh. Micaiah speaks about a vision from Yahweh where Micaiah sees Israelites who have lost their leader. The king of Israel is angry.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Micaiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I know that these words are not true. All of your prophets are telling you lies; or
* I am worried and sad because I can see that my king will die; or
* Thank you, Yahweh, for showing me your vision and giving me the words to tell the king.

Ask the person playing the king of Israel, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I do not like Micaiah; or
* I want to hear what Yahweh thinks; or
* This is what I expected. Now I feel angry and frustrated; or
* Micaiah is embarrassing me in front of Jehoshaphat.

Ask the person playing Jehoshaphat, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* This will be interesting. Is Micaiah a prophet from Yahweh or just like all the other prophets? or
* Micaiah is making his king angry. I want to hear from Yahweh; or
* I feel relieved. This man is a prophet of Yahweh.

Continue the drama.

Micaiah speaks about another vision where Yahweh wants a spirit to persuade Ahab to attack Ramoth Gilead. Yahweh says Ahab will die if Ahab attacks Ramoth Gilead. A spirit agrees to deceive the king. Micaiah tells the king and everyone around the king that Yahweh put a spirit in the prophets to speak false prophecies. Zedekiah slaps and insults Micaiah. Micaiah says Zedekiah will understand one day in the future when Zedekiah hides away in an inner room.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Micaiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am scared, but I must tell the king about my vision from Yahweh; or
* I know telling this truth offends everyone; or
* Zedekiah hates me; or
* One day, Zedekiah will know everything that he has done wrong; or
* I feel sorry for Zedekiah. Zedekiah will be afraid, and I hope he asks Yahweh for help.

Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Here is a final test for Ahab. I know Ahab will fail and people will kill him; or
* Here are my words for Ahab and his people; or
* I am pleased with Micaiah, who speaks the truth and brings my warning to Ahab.

Ask the person playing the heavenly beings, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* We are here to serve Yahweh; or
* How can we persuade Ahab? What is the best way to tempt Ahab to attack Ramoth Gilead?

Ask the person playing the spirit, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I have a good idea and know how I can entice Ahab; or
* I am sad to know that I will be successful; or
* It is always good to do what Yahweh wants.

Ask the person playing Zedekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel horrified. How can Micaiah say this about all of us? or
* I am so angry with Micaiah; or
* What does Micaiah mean? What day will come when I hide somewhere?

Continue the drama.

The king of Israel orders people to take Micaiah back to Amon and Joash, and for Amon and Joash to put Micaiah into prison with basic food and water until the king of Israel returns from attacking Ramoth Gilead. Micaiah says if the king returns, Yahweh did not speak through Micaiah and warns everyone to listen to the prophecy.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing the king of Israel, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Micaiah has gone too far. How can Micaiah say that all my prophets lied to me? or
* I do not believe Micaiah; or
* I have heard enough. Micaiah makes me angry; or
* I hope Micaiah suffers while I go to fight Ramoth Gilead.

Ask the person playing the king of Israel's officials, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Micaiah has offended our king; or
* We must tell Amon and Joash the king's punishment of Micaiah; or
* We are pleased to take Micaiah to be punished.

Ask the person playing Micaiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I knew the king would be angry; or
* I feel relieved that I spoke the truth, but I am frightened; or
* Everyone will know that I spoke the truth when the king dies at Ramoth Gilead.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 18:9–27 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

The **kings** of **Israel** and **Judah** sit on their **thrones** and wear **royal robes**. Use the same words or phrases for king, Israel, Judah, throne, royal, and robes as you used in previous passages. For more information on king, Israel, Judah, and robe, refer to the Master Glossary.

The kings are at the threshing floor, the open space for people to separate grains from the outer parts of the plant.

Stop here and look at a picture of people using a threshing floor to separate wheat grains from other parts of the plant. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **threshing floor**. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

The threshing floor is next to the entrance **gate** of the city. Use the same word or phrase for "gate of the city" as you used in previous passages.

The **prophets** are **prophesying**. Use the same words or phrases for prophets and prophesy as you used in previous passages. For more information on prophets and prophesy, refer to the Master Glossary.

Zedekiah made **iron** horns. Zedekiah made from iron two horns like the horns on a bull. Use the same word or phrase for iron as you used in previous passages. The horns were not musical instruments.

Stop here and look at a picture of a bull with horns. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **horns**. For more information about horns, refer to the Master Glossary. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Zedekiah tells the kings that Yahweh says the kings will gore or badly wound the **Arameans** with the horns. Use the same word or phrase for Arameans as you used in previous passages, and remember that another word for Arameans is Syrians.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **gore**. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

**Yahweh** will give Ramoth Gilead into the king's **hand** or into the king's power. Yahweh is God's personal name. Use the same words or phrases for Yahweh and "into the king's hand" as you used in previous passages.

The **messenger** spoke to Micaiah. Use the same word or phrase for messenger as you used in previous passages.

Micaiah will only say what **God** says to Micaiah. Use the same word or phrase for God as you used in previous passages. For more information on God, refer to the Master Glossary.

Micaiah uses the words "**As surely as the Yahweh lives**," as a promise or oath.

Stop here and discuss as a group what words or phrase you will use for this phrase. Look up **oath** in the Master Glossary. If you have already translated this phrase in another book of the Bible, use the same phrase that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Micaiah says he saw the people of Israel scattered on the hills. The people were spread out over the hill.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **scattered**. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

The people looked like **sheep** without a shepherd. Use the same word or phrase for sheep as you used in previous passages. A shepherd is a person who protects and cares for sheep.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **shepherd**. Look up shepherd in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Each person can go home in **peace**. The people can go home without fighting and be safe. Use the same word or phrase for peace as you used in previous passages. For more information on peace, refer to the Master Glossary.

Micaiah tells the people to hear the **word** of Yahweh. Micaiah wants the people to hear Yahweh's message. Use the same word or phrase for word as you used in previous passages. For more information on word, refer to the Master Glossary.

The host of heaven is around Yahweh. The host of heaven is the heavenly beings who do Yahweh's work.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **host of heaven**. Look up **heaven** in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this phrase in another book of the Bible, use the same word or phrase that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

A spirit came to Yahweh. The spirit is a supernatural being that Yahweh created who lives in heaven with Yahweh. This spirit is not the Holy Spirit, and it is not evil.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **spirit**. Look up spirit in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Zedekiah is the **son** of Kenaanah. Use the same word or phrase for son as you used in previous passages. For more information on son, refer to the Master Glossary.

Zedekiah slapped Micaiah in the face.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **slapped**. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Zedekiah will one day hide in an **inner room**. An inner room is a safe place inside a building. Use the same word or phrase for "inner room" as you used in previous passages.

Amon is the **governor** of the city. A governor is a senior official in charge of a city. Use the same word or phrase for governor as you used in previous passages.

The king of Israel wants Amon to put Micaiah in prison. A prison is a solid building that stops a person who is inside the building from going outside the building.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **prison**. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

The king of Israel wants Micaiah to have a small amount of simple food to eat, like **bread** and water. Use the same word or phrase for bread as you used in previous passages.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 18:9-27
