## 2 Chronicles 26:1–15 (id: eng-2ch-p47-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 26:1–15 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 26:1–15 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 26:1–15 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous story, the storyteller told about the end of King Amaziah of Judah. This passage tells about some of the life of Amaziah's son, King Uzziah, and what Uzziah did as the king of Judah. In other stories in the Bible, storytellers call Uzziah by another name, "Azariah."

The people of Judah made Uzziah king in place of his father, Amaziah. Uzziah was 16 years old. Uzziah is the king who rebuilt Elath and restored control of Elath to Judah. Uzziah did this after Amaziah had died.

Elath was a city more than 300 kilometers to the south of Jerusalem in the territory of Edom. Elath was a port on a large body of water called the Gulf of Aqaba. The Gulf of Aqaba is the eastern branch of the Red Sea. Whichever kingdom controlled the port could become more wealthy by trading with faraway nations.

Stop here as a group and look at a map of the kingdom of Judah in the context of the larger region. Note where Jerusalem is and where the boundary of Judah is compared to the city of Elath on the Gulf of Aqaba. Note where Mount Sinai, the Red Sea, and Egypt are. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: What goods do your community trade with others? What locations are important for trading? Pause this audio here.

Uzziah was 16 years old when he became king. Uzziah was king in Jerusalem for 52 years. Uzziah's mother's name was Jecoliah. Jecoliah was from Jerusalem. Uzziah did things that pleased Yahweh in the same way Uzziah's father did. A man named Zechariah taught Uzziah to respect and obey God. It is likely Zechariah was a priest or a trusted advisor, but we do not know more information about Zechariah. Uzziah served God during the time Zechariah was with Uzziah. During the time Uzziah served Yahweh, God made Uzziah successful.

King Uzziah and his army warred against the Philistines and broke the walls that protected three Philistine cities: Gath, Jabneh, and Ashdod. The Philistines had been enemies of Israel for centuries. Uzziah ordered the people of Judah to rebuild cities of Judah near Ashdod and in other places in the Philistine land. God helped Uzziah's kingdom fight against the Philistines.

Stop here as a group and look at the map from earlier of the kingdom of Judah in the context of the larger region. Note where the Philistine territory is and the cities of Ashdod, Gath, and Jabneh. Pause this audio here.

God helped Uzziah and his army fight against the Arabs who lived in Gur Baal. God helped Uzziah and his army fight against the Meunites. The Meunites or Ammonites regularly paid tribute to Uzziah since Uzziah was very powerful. The tribute would have been money or something valuable. In that time, a weak nation would give tribute to a stronger nation so that the stronger nation would not destroy the weak nation. People who lived throughout the land from Jerusalem to Egypt knew that Uzziah was very powerful.

Stop here as a group and look at the map from earlier of the kingdom of Judah in the context of the larger region. Note the probable locations of the Arabs and Meunites that Uzziah's army defeated. These places were likely along the trade route from Jerusalem to Elath. So Uzziah's victory in these areas gave Uzziah greater control of Elath and made Uzziah more powerful. Pause this audio here.

Uzziah ordered his people to build towers in Jerusalem at the Corner Gate, the Valley Gate, and at the angle in the wall. A tower was a tall structure that soldiers could stand on top of to help the soldiers see the enemy from far away. Soldiers who stood on the tower could attack the enemy before the enemy broke into the city. Uzziah did this to protect his city and make it stronger.

Stop here as a group and discuss this question: Where would people in your culture go to protect themselves? What do people in your culture do to see far away, hide, or attack enemies? Pause this audio here.

Uzziah ordered the people of Judah to build towers in the open land away from the towns. These towers would stand tall like trees in a field. People would stand on top of the towers in the field to watch their livestock, protect their animals from wild beasts, and see enemies coming from far away.

Stop here as a group and do this activity: Have someone in your group stand on the ground and describe what they can see far away. Now have that person safely go to a higher level, look in the same direction as the first person, and describe what they can see far away. What difference do you hear? How could this help when you or your animals are in an open field? Pause this audio here.

Uzziah ordered the people to dig many cisterns. Cisterns were large holes in rocks that the people used to catch rainwater. People would cut or dig out a large hole in a giant rock. Then the people would use another material to seal the rock so the cistern would not leak water. Uzziah had the people dig cisterns because Uzziah had a lot of livestock in the low hills and in the open plain. Uzziah had people work his fields and grape vines in the hills and in the fertile fields. Uzziah had a lot of farms and vineyards because Uzziah loved to grow food from the ground.

Uzziah had a strong army that practiced for war. The army was always ready to go to war. The army stayed organized with groups of soldiers. Jeiel kept count of the soldiers and supplies. Maaseiah was also an army leader, but we do not know what Maaseiah's job was. Hananiah was one of the royal generals, and he led Jeiel, Maaseiah, and the whole army. There were 2,600 family leaders in charge of the fighting men. These family leaders had authority over 307,500 men. These soldiers trained for war and stayed ready to fight King Uzziah's enemies.

Uzziah provided equipment to his whole army. Uzziah gave the soldiers shields, helmets, and armor to put over their bodies and protect themselves. People made small shields out of wood or metal. People made the helmets and coats of armor out of leather or metal. The helmets and coats of armor protected people's bodies. Uzziah gave the soldiers weapons to fight enemies. Uzziah gave the soldiers spears, bows and arrows, and slings to throw stones. People made the weapons out of wood, stone, leather, and metal.

Stop here as a group and look at pictures of the army's equipment in this passage. What kind of equipment does your culture use to protect soldiers? What kind of weapons does your culture use to fight enemies or animals? How would you describe the army's equipment? Pause this audio here.

Skillful men of Judah invented powerful weapons of war. In Jerusalem, Uzziah ordered men to build these devices on top of the towers and corners of the city walls. These clever inventions enabled soldiers to shoot arrows and throw large stones down onto enemies. Uzziah became very famous in faraway places. God helped Uzziah until Uzziah became powerful.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 26:1–15 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has three scenes.

**In the first scene:** Uzziah becomes king and is famous because Uzziah rebuilds Elath. Uzziah has a mother named Jecoliah and an advisor named Zechariah. The storyteller says Uzziah followed God as long as Zechariah was alive.

**In the second scene:** Uzziah's army makes war against other nations. The army of Judah wins the battles because God is with the army.

**In the third scene:** Uzziah makes his kingdom more secure by ordering the people of Judah to build towers of defense in the city and the fields. Uzziah has a lot of workers on his farms and in his vineyards. Uzziah develops a strong and organized army and gives the army good equipment to defend Judah and attack enemies. God is with Uzziah until Uzziah becomes powerful.

The characters in this passage are:

* King Amaziah, Uzziah's father
* King Uzziah
* The people of Judah
* Jecoliah, Uzziah's mother
* Zechariah, Uzziah's advisor
* Yahweh
* The Philistines
* The Arabs
* The Meunites, and possibly the Ammonites
* Jeiel, the secretary of Judah's army
* Maaseiah, the officer of Judah's army
* Hananiah, the royal general of Judah's army
* Family leaders of Judah's army
* And Judah's soldiers

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In the first scene, the storyteller transitions from King Amaziah to Amaziah's son, Uzziah. When Uzziah was 16 years old, all the people of Judah made Uzziah king in place of his father. The Bible does not say Amaziah died before Uzziah became king. This is different from the formula of previous passages, where a king rests with his fathers, people bury the king, and the king's son becomes the new king. We know from other passages that Amaziah was a prisoner after he lost a battle. It is possible that the people of Judah made Uzziah king instead of Amaziah because the Israelite army captured Amaziah.

Stop here and discuss as a group how you will translate the idea that people made Uzziah king "in place of" Uzziah's father. How will you show the exchange of kings without saying that the previous king died? Pause this audio here.

Uzziah was the king who rebuilt Elath and restored Elath to Judah's control after Amaziah's death. Uzziah's army would have recaptured the city of Elath before Uzziah had his people rebuild the city. Throughout this passage, the storyteller emphasizes Uzziah's victories. Uzziah did not win the wars and build defenses by himself, but Uzziah led the people of Judah to do these good things for the kingdom of Judah.

Stop here and look at a map of the kingdom of Judah. Note the locations of Jerusalem and the city of Elath on the Gulf of Aqaba. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss this as a group: How do people in your culture tell the culture's history to younger generations? How do people talk about important victories from the past? Pause this audio here.

The storyteller repeats a familiar formula in Chronicles for introducing a new king. The storyteller states the new king's age, the number of years the new king reigned, where the king lived, who the new king's mother was, and where the mother was from. Uzziah became king at 16 years old, and Uzziah reigned in Jerusalem for 52 years. Jecoliah was Uzziah's mother's name, and Jecoliah was from Jerusalem. The storyteller continues the formula and states whether the king followed Yahweh: "Uzziah did right things in the eyes of Yahweh, just like Amaziah, Uzziah's father, had done."

Uzziah sought God during the days of Zechariah. In the books of Chronicles, a king who seeks God is a way to say that the king served Yahweh or followed Yahweh's commands. Zechariah taught Uzziah to fear God, or to honor God and do what God commands. During the time that Uzziah sought Yahweh, or tried to follow Yahweh, while Zechariah was alive, God gave Uzziah success.

In the second scene, we learn about Uzziah's wars and that God helped the kingdom of Judah defeat enemies. King Uzziah and his army attacked the Philistines and broke the walls of three Philistine cities: Gath, Jabneh, and Ashdod. Usually, an army would not destroy the whole wall, but the army would create a large hole in the wall so soldiers could run through the hole.

Stop and discuss how you will describe what King Uzziah and his army did to the walls of the three cities: they broke enough of the wall to allow the army to go through. Pause this audio here.

Uzziah commanded his people to rebuild cities of Judah near Ashdod and among other Philistine lands.

Stop here as a group and look again at the map of the kingdom of Judah in the context of the larger region. Note where the Philistine territory is and the cities of Ashdod, Gath, and Jabneh. Pause this audio here.

God helped Uzziah's kingdom fight against the Philistines. God helped Uzziah and Uzziah's army fight against the Arabs who lived in Gur Baal. God helped Uzziah and Uzziah's army fight against the Meunites. We do not know the specific ways God helped Uzziah win these wars. The Meunites or Ammonites regularly paid tribute to Uzziah since Uzziah was very powerful. A tribute is money or other valuable things that a weaker nation gives to a stronger nation so that the stronger nation does not destroy the weaker nation. A nation will give a tribute to another nation as a payment for protection or to keep peace.

Stop and discuss how you will translate the idea of paying tribute to another country. Pause this audio here.

The Meunites and Ammonites are two different people groups. Many translations of the Bible mention both groups, while other translations only mention the Meunites. In the original language, the spelling of these two people groups is almost identical, and we do not know which one paid tribute to King Uzziah. If the Meunites paid tribute to Uzziah, the Meunites probably began paying Uzziah after they lost the war. People living from Jerusalem to Egypt knew that Uzziah was very powerful.

Stop and discuss: What does the translation you listened to call these two groups? Did the translation use the same name or different names? Discuss what names you will use in your translation for these groups. If you do not know if you should use the same name or two different names here, we recommend you follow the example of the accepted translation in your area. Pause this audio here.

In the third scene, we learn ways Uzziah made his kingdom safer and stronger.

Uzziah commanded his people to build towers in the city of Jerusalem. The people built the towers at the Corner Gate, the Valley Gate, and at the angle in the wall. Uzziah had the people make those places stronger. The Corner Gate was at the northwest corner of Jerusalem. The Valley Gate was on the western side of Jerusalem. Uzziah made the Corner and Valley Gates to the west, facing the land of the Philistines, stronger. The new towers protected these western entrances to Jerusalem. We do not know where the angle in the wall was, but it is where the city wall turned or curved in a different direction, in a way that was probably different from a corner.

Stop here and, as a group, look at a picture of a city wall with a tower. Pause this audio here.

Uzziah commanded the people to build towers in the open land away from the towns. The passage uses the same word for towers in the field as towers at the gates and on the wall. Soldiers stood on top of these towers to watch for approaching enemies. The soldiers would then send a message to the city that enemies were approaching. The soldiers could also shoot their arrows at the enemy soldiers when the enemy was close.

Uzziah also had the people dig many cisterns out of large rocks in the open land in order to catch water when it rained. In this way, Uzziah's livestock could receive water out in the western foothills and plains where there were no reliable water sources.

Stop here as a group and discuss how you will translate "cisterns." Pause this audio here.

Uzziah had a lot of livestock in the western foothills and plains. Uzziah also had a lot of people working in Uzziah's fields and in Uzziah's vineyards because Uzziah loved to grow crops.

Uzziah had a strong army that practiced for war. The army was always ready to go to war. The army stayed organized with groups of soldiers. Jeiel counted and organized the soldiers and the supplies. Maaseiah was an army leader, commander, or officer with some authority. Hananiah was one of the royal generals, and he led Jeiel, Maaseiah, and the whole army. There were 2,600 family leaders in charge of the mighty fighting men. These family leaders had authority over 307,500 men. The whole army trained for war and stayed ready to fight King Uzziah's enemies.

Stop here as a group and discuss how you translate types of military officers or positions. Pause this audio here.

Uzziah gave to the soldiers shields, helmets, armor, spears, bows and arrows, and slings to throw stones. Skillful men of Judah invented powerful weapons of war. In Jerusalem, Uzziah ordered men to build these devices on top of the towers and corners of the city walls. We do not know what these weapons looked like. We only know that soldiers used these devices to shoot arrows and throw large stones down from the towers onto the enemies. Uzziah became very famous in faraway places. To end the passage, the storyteller repeats an idea but uses new words. God helped Uzziah until Uzziah became powerful.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 26:1–15 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has 3 scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* King Amaziah, Uzziah's father
* King Uzziah
* The people of Judah
* Jecoliah, Uzziah's mother
* Zechariah, Uzziah's advisor
* Yahweh
* The Philistines
* The Arabs
* The Meunites, and possibly the Ammonites
* Jeiel, the secretary of Judah's army
* Maaseiah, the officer of Judah's army
* Hananiah, the royal general of Judah's army
* Family leaders of Judah's army
* And Judah's soldiers

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

The people of Judah made Uzziah king in place of Uzziah's father when Uzziah was 16 years old.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing the people of Judah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel desperate. I do not want a 16\-year\-old to rule us, but we need a king now; or
* I am confident that Uzziah will be a great king for us.

> Continue the drama.

After Amaziah died, King Uzziah was the one who regained control of Elath and rebuilt Elath for Judah. Uzziah became king at 16 years old, and Uzziah ruled in Jerusalem for 52 years. Jecoliah was Uzziah's mother's name, and Jecoliah was from Jerusalem. Uzziah did right in the eyes of Yahweh, just like Uzziah's father, Amaziah.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Jekoliah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* My son is too young to be king! I am scared that people will hate my son; or
* My son is smart, and I am happy that my son is following Yahweh like Amaziah; or
* I am concerned my son will be too ambitious like Amaziah.

Continue the drama.

During the days of Zechariah, Uzziah sought God and obeyed God. Zechariah instructed Uzziah in the fear of Yahweh. As long as Uzziah sought God, God made Uzziah prosperous.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Zechariah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am honored that Uzziah trusts me to teach him about Yahweh; or
* I am happy Uzziah is making good choices to follow Yahweh; or
* I hope Uzziah continues to trust Yahweh even after I am gone.

Continue the drama.

Uzziah and his army went to war against the Philistines and broke through the walls of Gath, Jabneh, and Ashdod. Uzziah had people rebuild a town of Judah near Ashdod and in other places in the Philistine territory.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing the Philistines, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am sad we lost three cities to Uzziah; or
* Uzziah's kingdom is trying to take over our land. I am angry and I want to get revenge.

> Continue the drama.

God helped Uzziah and his army fight the Philistines, the Arabs who lived in Gur Baal, and the Meunites. The Meunites or Ammonites regularly paid taxes to Uzziah. People from Judah all the way to Egypt knew Uzziah's name and how powerful Uzziah became.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing the Meunites, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I will gladly pay Uzziah a lot of money so that Uzziah does not totally destroy our culture; or
* I hate to pay Uzziah, but I will pay Uzziah because we need access to Elath to sell our produce.

Continue the drama.

King Uzziah had people build towers at the Corner Gate, the Valley Gate, and the curve in the wall. Uzziah had the people make the towers stronger. Uzziah had people build towers and hewn cisterns in the open fields because Uzziah had a lot of livestock in the western foothills and plains. Uzziah had people farm his fields and work in his vineyards because Uzziah loved the land of Judah.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing the people of Judah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* King Uzziah makes us feel safe with all these towers. No enemies would dare attack us now; or
* Look at all the good food we are producing for the king and all the animals we are herding for the king. I hope the king will share some of the wealth with us.

> Continue the drama.

Uzziah had a strong army that practiced for war. The army was always ready to go to war. The army stayed organized with groups of soldiers. Jeiel counted and organized the soldiers and the supplies. Maaseiah was an army leader. Hananiah was one of the royal generals, and he led Jeiel, Maaseiah, and the whole army. There were 2,600 family leaders in charge of the mighty fighting men. These family leaders had authority over 307,500 men. The whole army trained for war and stayed ready to fight King Uzziah's enemies.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Jeiel, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* King Uzziah is a mighty king because we have so many men in our army; or
* I feel confident our army is ready for war.

> Continue the drama.

Uzziah gave to the soldiers shields, helmets, armor, spears, bows and arrows, and slings to throw stones. Skillful men of Judah invented powerful weapons of war. In Jerusalem, Uzziah ordered men to build these devices on top of the towers and corners of the city walls. These clever inventions enabled soldiers to shoot arrows and throw large stones down onto enemies. Uzziah became very famous in far away places. God helped Uzziah until Uzziah became powerful.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Uzziah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* God has made me powerful. No one can take what belongs to Judah; or
* I feel proud. Look at my army, my defense towers, my farms, and all I accomplished at Elath.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 26:1–15 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

All the people of **Judah** made Uzziah **king** in place of Uzziah's father, Amaziah. Uzziah had authority over all the land, people, and resources of the kingdom of Judah. Use the same words for Judah and king that you have used previously. For more information on Judah and king, refer to the Master Glossary.

Uzziah reigned in **Jerusalem** and did what was right in the eyes of **Yahweh.** Yahweh is God's personal name. Use the same words for Jerusalem and Yahweh that you have used before. For more information on Jerusalem and Yahweh, refer to the Master Glossary.

Uzziah sought **God**, and Zechariah taught Uzziah to **fear God**, or to respect God's commands and follow God. Use the same words or phrases for God and "the fear of God" as you have before. For more information on God and "fear of God," refer to the Master Glossary.

Uzziah went to war against the Philistines. Uzziah built **cities** in the region of Ashod and in the Philistine territory. Use the same word for cities that you have used previously.

Uzziah had people build **towers** at the city gates, along the city wall, and out in the fields, or **wilderness**, where no one lived. The storyteller uses the same word for these tall, protective structures in the field and near the city wall. Use the same word for tower and wilderness that you have translated previously, and for more information, refer to "lookout tower" and wilderness in the Master Glossary.

Stop here and look at a picture of a city wall with a tower, if needed. Pause this audio here.

Uzziah had a lot of livestock. Livestock includes the kinds of animals people keep for food and milk, like cows, bulls, oxen, sheep, and goats.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **livestock**. Pause this audio here.

Uzziah had many people work in his fields and **vineyards**. You will remember from other stories in 1 Chronicles and other books of the Old Testament that a vineyard is a type of farmland where grapes grow on a vine. People use grapes to make wine. Use the same word for vineyard that you have used previously, and remember that vineyard is in the Master Glossary.

Uzziah had a **scribe** named Jeiel. Use the same word or phrase for scribe as you used in 2 Chronicles 24:11, and refer to scribe in the Master Glossary for more information.

Hananiah was the king's **high official**, and Maaseiah was an **army officer** who worked for Hananiah. Use the same words or phrases for a king's high official and an army officer as you have used in previous passages.

Uzziah gave to the soldiers **shields**, **helmets**, **armor**, **spears**, **bows** and **arrows**, and **slings** to throw stones. Translate the names of these words in the same way that you have previously.

Stop here as a group and, if needed, look at pictures of the army's equipment: a shield, a helmet, armor, a spear, a bow and arrows, and a sling.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 26:1-15
* **Associated Articles:** Jerusalem (ID: `eng-t76-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); Judea (ID: `eng-t81-v2@FIAKeyTerms`); vineyard (ID: `eng-t142-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); wilderness (ID: `eng-t145-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); scribe (ID: `eng-t155-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); Yahweh (ID: `eng-t174-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); fear of God (ID: `eng-t185-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); God (ID: `eng-t189-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); king (ID: `eng-t226-v2@FIAKeyTerms`); 2 Chronicles 26:1–15 (ID: `eng-2ch-p47-v1-audio@FIATranslationGuide`)

## 2 Chronicles 26:16–23 (id: eng-2ch-p48-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 26:16–23 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 26:16–23 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 26:16–23 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

The previous passage tells of the good things King Uzziah did to protect Judah and make it prosperous. God helped Uzziah until Uzziah became powerful. This passage tells how King Uzziah became prideful and failed to obey God. Uzziah became sick for the rest of Uzziah's life.

After Uzziah became powerful, Uzziah became too prideful. Uzziah's pride destroyed Uzziah. King Uzziah disobeyed Yahweh, Uzziah's God. Uzziah entered the temple of Yahweh to burn incense, or sweet\-smelling spices, on the altar of incense. You will remember that Yahweh told Moses that only priests could enter Yahweh's temple to serve Yahweh with incense.

But Azariah the priest followed Uzziah into the temple with 80 other brave priests of Yahweh. All the priests confronted King Uzziah. The priests told Uzziah that he was not supposed to offer incense to Yahweh. Only the priests, descendants of Aaron, could burn incense. God chose only the priests to serve Yahweh in this way. The priests told Uzziah to leave the holy place because Uzziah had acted unfaithfully toward God. The priests said that Yahweh God would not honor Uzziah or give Uzziah a good reputation.

Stop and look at a diagram of the temple and pictures of incense, a censer for incense, and the altar of incense, if needed. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Tell about a time when a leader or important person in your culture did something wrong. What did others around the leader do about it? How is talking to leaders in your culture the same or different from what the priests did to Uzziah? Pause this audio here.

Uzziah became very angry at the priests. Uzziah had a bowl of incense in his hands. Uzziah was ready to burn the incense. While Uzziah was angry at the priests, leprosy or skin disease appeared on Uzziah's forehead. The priests saw the leprosy appear while Uzziah stood near the altar of incense. Azariah the high priest and all the priests saw the leprosy appear on Uzziah's forehead. The priests hurried Uzziah out of the temple. Uzziah himself went quickly when Uzziah realized Yahweh had punished Uzziah.

King Uzziah had leprosy or a skin disease until the day Uzziah died. For the rest of Uzziah's life, Uzziah lived in a house separated from other people. Because Uzziah had this skin disease, Uzziah could never go back to Yahweh's house, the temple. You will remember that Yahweh gave the law to Moses that no one with a skin disease could enter the temple. A person with a skin disease, like King Uzziah, was ritually unclean. Yahweh said in Yahweh's law that someone with a skin disease had to live away from that person's family and other people.

Stop here as a group and discuss this question: When in your culture does someone have to be alone or stay away from other people? What do you do when someone has a contagious disease or is ritually unclean? Pause this audio here.

Because Uzziah had leprosy or a skin disease, Uzziah was unable to act like a king would normally do. Uzziah's son, Jotham, managed the royal household and ruled the nation of Judah.

The prophet Isaiah, son of Amoz, wrote about all the things that King Uzziah did. This is not the same book as the prophecy of Isaiah that we have in the Bible. We do not know what happened to these missing writings from Isaiah.

So Uzziah died, and people buried Uzziah in the field that belonged to the kings. This field was near the royal burial place. The people did not bury Uzziah's body with the other kings of Judah because Uzziah had leprosy. Jotham, Uzziah's son, became king in Uzziah's place.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 26:16–23 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has two scenes.

**In the first scene:** King Uzziah goes into Yahweh's temple to burn incense, which is lawful only for priests to do. Azariah and 80 other priests confront Uzziah. Uzziah gets angry at the priests, and Yahweh makes Uzziah sick with a skin disease. Uzziah quickly leaves the temple.

**In the second scene:** Uzziah lives alone for the rest of his life because Uzziah has leprosy. Uzziah never goes back to the temple. Uzziah's son Jotham rules the kingdom instead of Uzziah. Uzziah dies, and people bury Uzziah in a field. Jotham becomes king.

The characters in this passage are:

* Yahweh
* King Uzziah
* Azariah, Yahweh's high priest
* Eighty priests of Yahweh
* Jotham, Uzziah's son
* And the people of Judah

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

"But King Uzziah became too prideful after Uzziah became powerful." The first scene begins with a word that shows the story is continuing from the previous story, but in a different way. The storyteller contrasts Uzziah's sin with Uzziah's victories from the previous passage. Pride is a sin against Yahweh. In the original language, the storyteller says that Uzziah's "heart was lifted up" to show that Uzziah became arrogant. Someone who is prideful, or arrogant, is convinced they are good and that they can do no wrong. When Uzziah first became king, Uzziah followed Yahweh closely. After Uzziah became powerful, Uzziah did not obey Yahweh. Uzziah followed his own way.

Stop here as a group and tell a story from your culture where a character is doing something, and then the character suddenly does something different that seems against what the character first did. Pay attention to the word or phrase you use to show the story continues in the same way, as well as the word or phrase you use to show the story suddenly changes. Pause this audio here.

King Uzziah acted in a proud way when Uzziah thought he did not have to follow Yahweh's rules. Uzziah acted unfaithfully, or disobeyed Yahweh. King Uzziah's sin caused Yahweh to destroy the rest of King Uzziah's reign as king. Uzziah was unfaithful to Yahweh, Uzziah's God, when Uzziah went into the temple of Yahweh to burn incense on the altar of incense. You will remember from earlier stories in the Bible that the incense altar was inside the holy place, the front room of the temple. God commanded only the priests to enter the holy place to burn incense, keep the lamps burning, and do other rituals of worship to Yahweh. Before the priests could enter the temple, the priests had to wash themselves according to Yahweh's commands in order to become ritually clean. Uzziah was prideful because Uzziah thought he did not have to follow Yahweh's rules.

Stop here as a group and discuss this question: What places in your culture can only certain people go to? What responsibilities or actions can only certain people do? Consider places and actions for only men, only women, or perhaps only the elderly or the leaders. What happens if someone does what that person is not supposed to do? Pause this audio here.

But Azariah the high priest and 80 other priests of Yahweh had great courage and went into the temple after the king. The storyteller uses the same word as at the start of this passage to show that the story is continuing but in an opposite way than before. The storyteller calls the priests men of great courage, which usually describes brave soldiers. The priests stood against Uzziah and told Uzziah that he could not offer incense to Yahweh because that duty was only for priests, the descendants of Aaron. Only priests are able to burn incense because Yahweh has consecrated or set priests apart from other people for this special purpose. The priests told the king that the king must leave the temple and that Yahweh God would not honor Uzziah for burning incense.

The king already had a "censer" or bowl of incense in his hands, and the king became very angry at the priests. A censer is a small bowl or pan that the priests used to burn incense. A craftsman made the censer from metal. The censer had a handle so a person could carry the censer to the altar.

Stop here and look at a picture of a censer of incense. Pause this audio here.

We do not know what Uzziah said or did when he was angry, but Uzziah did not do what the priests said. Uzziah was standing near the altar of incense. While Uzziah was showing his anger, the priests suddenly saw a skin disease appear on Uzziah's forehead. We do not know exactly what the skin disease looked like. Perhaps Uzziah's skin changed color or blisters formed. The priests immediately recognized the leprosy, and the priests moved quickly to get Uzziah out of the temple. Uzziah quickly moved out of the temple when he realized Yahweh had struck Uzziah with leprosy. This is special language to show that Yahweh had punished Uzziah with leprosy. Yahweh made Uzziah sick in an instant. It happened in a brief moment. You may remember that leprosy is what the people called a contagious skin disease that caused people to be ceremonially unclean. You may simply call this a contagious skin disease.

In the second scene, King Uzziah had leprosy or a skin disease for the rest of his life. Uzziah died with the disease. Uzziah lived by himself in a separate house for the rest of Uzziah's life. Uzziah could not have contact with anyone. Uzziah could never return to the temple, or house of Yahweh. Jotham, Uzziah's son, ruled the kingdom and managed everyone in the king's house.

The storyteller uses a common formula to end the stories of a king. The storyteller says that someone wrote about the king's life, and then the storyteller says the king died, people buried the king's body, and the king's son became the next king. The prophet Isaiah, son of Amoz, wrote down everything King Uzziah did from beginning to end. Uzziah "slept with his fathers" or died. People buried Uzziah in a cemetery, or a burial field that belonged to the kings, rather than in the burial caves where people buried the other kings. In the original language, the word refers to a field or an open place where people buried dead bodies. This field was near the kings' burial caves. People had to bury Uzziah's body in a separate place from the other kings since Uzziah had a skin disease. Uzziah's son, Jotham, became the new king.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 26:16–23 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has two scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* Yahweh
* King Uzziah
* Azariah, Yahweh's high priest
* Eighty priests of Yahweh
* Jotham, Uzziah's son
* And the people of Judah

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

"But King Uzziah became too prideful after he became powerful." King Uzziah's pride caused Uzziah to act unfaithfully, and then destroyed Uzziah. Uzziah sinned against Yahweh his God when Uzziah went into Yahweh's temple to burn incense on the altar of incense.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing the people of Judah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am worried. Our king is so powerful and he is ignoring God; or
* When you are powerful, you get to do whatever you want. That is just how kings act.

> Continue the drama.

But Azariah the priest followed Uzziah into the temple with 80 other brave priests of Yahweh. All the priests confronted King Uzziah. The priests told Uzziah that he was not supposed to offer incense to Yahweh. Only the priests, descendants of Aaron, could burn incense. God chose only the priests to serve Yahweh in this way. The priests told Uzziah to leave the holy place because Uzziah acted unfaithfully toward God. The priests said that Yahweh God would not honor Uzziah or give Uzziah a good reputation.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing the priests, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* The king is powerful, but we must obey God even if it is hard; or
* I am scared to confront the king by myself, but with Azariah and the other priests, I think Uzziah will listen to us.

> Continue the drama.

Uzziah became furious at the priests. Uzziah had a bowl of incense in his hands. Uzziah was ready to burn the incense. While Uzziah was angry at the priests, leprosy or skin disease appeared on Uzziah's forehead.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing the Uzziah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am the king! I defeated our enemies and I can worship God however I want to; or
* These priests are wrong to talk against me. God will show these priests that I am in charge.

Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* My priests honor my name, and I will be strong to defend my priests; or
* I am sad and angry that Uzziah has stopped obeying me. I cannot let Uzziah ignore my laws.

Continue the drama.

The priests saw the leprosy appear while Uzziah stood near the altar of incense. Azariah the high priest and all the priests saw the leprosy appear on Uzziah's forehead. The priests hurried Uzziah out of the temple. Uzziah himself went quickly when he realized Yahweh had punished Uzziah.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Azariah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am sad that King Uzziah ignored Yahweh's laws and can never come back to the temple; or
* I was shocked when I saw the leprosy on Uzziah's forehead!

Continue the drama.

King Uzziah was a leper until the day he died. For the rest of Uzziah's life, Uzziah lived in a house separated from other people. Because Uzziah was a leper, Uzziah could never go back to Yahweh's house, or temple. Uzziah's son, Jotham, managed the royal household and ruled the nation of Judah.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Uzziah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I regret that I did not follow Yahweh like Zechariah taught me to do. I wish Zechariah were still alive to help me; or
* I am so angry that I can never go back to the temple again or rule like a good king. Now everyone will think I am weak.

Continue the drama.

All the other things King Uzziah did, from beginning to end, are recorded in another book. The prophet Isaiah, son of Amoz, wrote everything down. So Uzziah died, and people buried Uzziah in the burial field that belonged to the kings. The people did not bury Uzziah's body with the other kings of Judah because Uzziah had leprosy. Jotham, Uzziah's son, became king in Uzziah's place.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 26:16–23 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

This passage uses both God's personal name, **Yahweh**, and the word for the creator, **God.** Sometimes the storyteller uses these words together, such as "Yahweh, Uzziah's God," to emphasize Yahweh's power or Yahweh's special relationship with Yahweh's people. Use the same words for Yahweh and God that you have used before. For more information on Yahweh and God, refer to the Master Glossary.

"Uzziah entered the **temple** of Yahweh." You will remember that the temple of Yahweh is the special place where God promised that God's presence would live. Sometimes in the Bible and in this passage, the temple is called "the house of Yahweh." Use the same words or phrases for temple or "house of Yahweh" as you have done before. For more information, refer to temple or "**house of God**" in the Master Glossary.

Uzziah went into the temple to burn incense, or sweet\-smelling spices, on the **altar of incense** or the altar for burning incense. You will remember that Yahweh instructed Moses to build an altar for burning sacrifices of grain and animals. Yahweh also instructed Moses to build an altar for burning incense, which is the altar Uzziah went towards. Translate "altar" the same way you have previously, and specify in your language that this is the altar for burning incense. For more information on altars, refer to the Master Glossary.

Stop here and look at a picture of the altar of incense, if needed. Pause this audio here.

Azariah was the **high priest**, and Azariah went into the temple with 80 other **priests**. All the priests confronted **King** Uzziah. Translate high priest, priest, and king in the same way you have previously. For more information on high priest, priest, and king, refer to the Master Glossary.

The king already had a censer, or bowl of incense, in his hands, and he became very angry at the priests. A censer is a small bowl or pan that the priests used to burn incense. A craftsman made the censer from metal. It had a handle so a person could carry the censer to the altar.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **censer**. Look at a picture of a censer of incense, if needed. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

God **consecrated** or set apart only the descendants of Aaron, who were priests, to serve in the temple. No one besides priests had this special job. God also consecrated the **sanctuary** or **holy place** where the altar of incense was. Uzziah went into the sanctuary, where it was unlawful for Uzziah to go. Use the same words for consecrated and sanctuary or holy place as you have used before. For more information on consecrate, sanctuary, and holy place, refer to the Master Glossary.

Stop and look at a diagram of the temple that shows the location of the holy place, if needed. Pause this audio here.

The priests saw leprosy appear on Uzziah's forehead. In the Bible, leprosy refers to a skin disease. Yahweh said in his law that someone with leprosy was ceremonially unclean and unable to worship Yahweh in Yahweh's temple.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for leprosy. Look up **leprosy** in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Jotham, Uzziah's son, was in charge of the **palace** or the king's **household**. Use the same word for palace or household that you have used previously. You may use whichever word—palace or household—works best in your language. For more information on household, refer to the Master Glossary.

"The **prophet** Isaiah, son of Amoz, wrote everything down." A prophet was a messenger of Yahweh. Use the same word for prophet as you have used before. For more information on prophet, refer to the Master Glossary.

Uzziah "slept with his fathers." This is a common phrase in the books of Kings and Chronicles to say that a king died. Use the same phrase you have used previously to say that a king died. In Uzziah's case, people did not bury him with the other kings because Uzziah had leprosy. People buried Uzziah in a burial field that belonged to the kings, instead of the burial caves with the other kings. In the original language, the word refers to a field or an open place where people were buried. It is important in your translation to make clear that Uzziah "sleeping with his fathers" is different from people burying Uzziah with his fathers.

Stop here as a group and discuss what words or phrases you will use to describe the place where Uzziah was buried.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 26:16-23
* **Associated Articles:** Bronze Utensils in the Tabernacle (ID: `a1632@FIAImages`); altar (ID: `eng-t3-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); household (ID: `eng-t70-v2@FIAKeyTerms`); leprosy (ID: `eng-t85-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); priest (ID: `eng-t103-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); prophet (ID: `eng-t104-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); sanctuary (ID: `eng-t117-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); temple (ID: `eng-t138-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); Yahweh (ID: `eng-t174-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); God (ID: `eng-t189-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); king (ID: `eng-t226-v2@FIAKeyTerms`); high priest (ID: `eng-t246-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); consecrate (ID: `eng-t254-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); holy place (ID: `eng-t256-v1@FIAKeyTerms`); 2 Chronicles 26:16–23 (ID: `eng-2ch-p48-v1-audio@FIATranslationGuide`)

* **Associated Articles:** 2 Chronicles 26:16–23 (ID: `eng-2ch-p48-v1@FIATranslationGuide`)


## 2 Chronicles 28:9–15 (id: eng-2ch-p51-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 28:9–15 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 28:9–15 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 28:9–15 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the last story, we heard how King Ahaz of Judah, the southern kingdom, did not follow Yahweh but instead worshipped foreign gods. So Yahweh allowed Judah's enemies, including Israel, the northern kingdom, to defeat Judah. The soldiers of Israel killed many of Judah's soldiers. Israel's army also brought 200,000 people, including women and children, from Judah back to the city of Samaria as prisoners. In this story, we will now hear what Yahweh tells the people of Israel to do with those prisoners.

The story begins when one of Yahweh's prophets, Oded, comes out of the city of Samaria to speak to the army of Israel. The Israelite army has just returned from Judah with the prisoners. You will remember that Samaria is the capital city of the northern kingdom of Israel.

Stop here as a group and look at a map that shows the southern kingdom of Judah and the northern kingdom of Israel. Find the city of Samaria on the map. Pause this audio here.

Oded tells the army that Yahweh was angry with Judah, and that is why Yahweh allowed the army of Israel to defeat Judah. But now, Yahweh is angry with Israel because Israel's soldiers killed Judah's people in a very angry and cruel way.

Stop here and discuss as a group: Tell a story about some soldiers who fought a battle in a very angry and cruel way. What did those soldiers do that was different from how people normally expect soldiers to behave in battle? Pause this audio here.

Oded also says that the soldiers now plan to make all of these people from Judah Israel's slaves. You will remember that in the law that Yahweh gave to Moses, in the book of Leviticus, Yahweh allowed an Israelite to work as a servant to another Israelite for a time to pay off a debt. But Yahweh said the people were never to make another Israelite a slave. So it would be very wrong for the soldiers to do this.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: What do people think about slavery in your culture? If people take slaves, how do those people treat those slaves? Which types of people are you allowed to take slaves from? Would you ever take slaves from people who are related to you? Pause this audio here.

Oded also reminds the soldiers that although the people of Judah sinned, the people of Israel have sinned too. You will remember the story from earlier in this book and in 1 Kings of how the 10 northern tribes first formed their own kingdom, with Jeroboam as king. At that time, King Jeroboam created idols for the people to worship so that the people would not have to go to Jerusalem in Judah to worship at Yahweh's temple. So Israel has done the same kinds of sinful things that Judah has done.

Oded warns the soldiers that Yahweh is very angry with Israel, just as Yahweh was angry with Judah. Oded says the soldiers must release these people and let the people go back home to Judah.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your culture, when an army wins a battle against another army, how do people explain why those people were able to win? Pause this audio here.

Four of Israel's leaders now speak to the soldiers and repeat the same warning Oded just gave. The leaders say that the soldiers cannot bring these prisoners to Samaria. The leaders say that the people of Israel are already very guilty of sin against Yahweh and that Yahweh is very angry.

So the soldiers let the prisoners go free. The soldiers also give everything the soldiers took during the battle to the leaders. Then, from those things, the leaders give the people from Judah clothing to cover their bodies and sandals to put on their feet. The leaders also give the people food and drink and take care of the people's wounds from the battle. The leaders give anyone who is unable to walk a donkey to ride. Then the leaders take all of the people back to the city of Jericho in Judah so the people can be with their relatives. People call Jericho the city of palm trees.

Stop here as a group and again look at the map of Israel that shows the southern kingdom of Judah and the northern kingdom of Israel. Find Jericho on the map. Pause this audio here.

Stop here as a group and look at pictures of sandals, a donkey, and palm trees. Pause this audio here.

Finally, the leaders return to Samaria, their home in the kingdom of Israel.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 28:9–15 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has four scenes.

**In the first scene:** Oded, one of Yahweh's prophets, comes out of the city of Samaria to speak to the army of Israel as the army returns with prisoners from Judah. Oded says that Yahweh let Israel defeat Judah because Yahweh was angry with Judah. But Israel has sinned too, so Yahweh will punish Israel if they keep these people from Judah as prisoners.

**In the second scene:** Four of Israel's leaders speak to Israel's army. The leaders repeat Oded's warning.

**In the third scene:** Israel's army lets Judah's people go free.

**In the fourth scene:** Israel's leaders give the people from Judah clothes and sandals to wear and food and drink. The leaders also take care of the people's battle wounds. Then the leaders take all of the people back home to Judah.

The characters in this passage are:

* Oded, Yahweh's prophet
* Yahweh
* Soldiers from the northern kingdom of Israel
* All the people of the southern kingdom of Judah
* The prisoners from Judah
* And four leaders from the northern kingdom: Azariah, Jehohanan's son; Berekiah, Meshillemoth's son; Jehizkiah, Shallum's son; and Amasa, Hadlai's son

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

You will remember that in the last story, the army of Israel, the northern kingdom, killed many people in Judah during a battle. Then the army brought many prisoners back to the city of Samaria, in the northern kingdom.

Stop here as a group and again find the city of Samaria on the map of Judah and Israel, if needed. Pause this audio here.

Now, as the first scene begins, the storyteller explains that one of Yahweh's prophets, Oded, lives in Samaria. You will remember that a prophet is a person who gives messages from God to the people. Oded comes out of the city to meet the army as the army approaches Samaria. Then Oded speaks to the army. In the original language, Oded begins with a word that means "Look!" or "Pay attention!" Then Oded tells the army why Israel was able to defeat Judah in the battle. Oded says that Yahweh, the God of the people's ancestors, was angry with Judah. You will remember that although Yahweh has always been the Israelites' God, the people have worshiped other gods instead of Yahweh for a long time. So Oded probably calls Yahweh the God of the Israelites to remind the people that Yahweh is the God their ancestors worshiped.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: The storyteller refers to Yahweh as the God of the people's ancestors. How do you talk about who your ancestors worshiped, whether it was God or the local gods or spirits? Pause this audio here.

Oded says that it was because Yahweh was angry with Judah that Yahweh gave Judah into Israel's hand. This is special language that means that Yahweh gave the army of Israel power and control over Judah. But although Yahweh allowed this to happen, Israel's army killed the army of Judah in a very angry and cruel way, without mercy. Oded says this reached up to heaven, which is special language that means that God, who is in heaven, saw what the army did.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How do you talk about soldiers who fight in a way that is very cruel or who show no mercy even when they win the battle? If needed, think about the story you told in step two and how you described the soldiers in your story. Pause this audio here.

Now, Oded says that the army plans to make these people of Judah and Jerusalem, both male and female, slaves to the people of the northern kingdom of Israel. You will remember that Judah is one of the tribes of Israel, but Judah is also the name of the southern kingdom. Jerusalem is the capital city of Judah. Jerusalem was also the capital city of all of Israel before the people divided into two different kingdoms. Jerusalem is where Solomon built Yahweh's temple. Oded asks the army a question that Oded does not expect anyone to answer. Oded asks the men, "But are you not also guilty of sins against Yahweh your God?" Oded asks this question to show the army that just like Yahweh punished Judah, Yahweh will also punish Israel if the army disobeys Yahweh and takes these people as slaves.

Oded tells the army to hear Oded, which means to pay attention to Oded. Oded says to send all of these prisoners back home. Oded reminds the army that these people are the men's relatives. You will remember from a previous story that most of the people who live in the southern kingdom are from the tribes of Judah and Benjamin, but some people from other Israelite tribes also live in Judah. Either way, all of these people come from the same 12 tribes of Israel, and all of the people are related. Oded says the army must send all of the people back home because Yahweh's wrath or burning anger is on the army, which means that Yahweh feels very angry with the army and plans to punish the people.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your language, how do you talk about someone who feels very, very angry? Pause this audio here.

In the second scene, the storyteller says that some chiefs or leaders of Ephraim confront the soldiers. You will remember that Ephraim is one of the tribes that live in the northern kingdom, but here the storyteller uses Ephraim to mean the entire northern kingdom. The storyteller says these leaders are Azariah, Jehohanan's son; Berekiah, Meshillemoth's son; Jehizkiah, Shallum's son; and Amasa, Hadlai's son. These four leaders speak to the army and repeat the same warning that Oded just gave. The leaders say that the army must not bring the prisoners into the city, because if they do, it will make the people's sin and guilt before Yahweh even worse than it already is. The leaders say that the people are already very guilty and that Yahweh's wrath, or anger, is very great toward the people of the northern kingdom.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How do you talk about people who deserve punishment because of the wrong things those people have done? Pause this audio here.

In the third scene, the soldiers release the prisoners and all the things the soldiers took during the battle. The soldiers do this in front of the leaders and all of the other people who have gathered together.

In the fourth scene, the storyteller says that the men whom people mentioned by name rise up and take the prisoners. We are not sure what the storyteller means by "the men whom people mentioned by name." The storyteller may use this phrase to refer to the four leaders, Azariah, Berekiah, Jehizkiah, and Amasa. Or the storyteller may mean that the leaders specifically choose other men to help the prisoners. Also, "rose up" does not mean that the men were first lying down. This is special language that means the men take action. First, the men give the prisoners clothes and sandals to wear from the spoils, or all the things the army took from Judah during the battle. The men also give the people food and drink because the people are probably very hungry and thirsty after the battle and the journey to Samaria. The men also anoint the people. This means the men put olive oil on people's wounds. Many people were probably injured in the battle, and at this time, people often used oil from olives, a type of fruit, to put on wounds as medicine.

Then the men carried the feeble people on donkeys. This means that people who were weak or injured and could not walk rode on donkeys, which are large animals with four legs. The men took all of the people, whom the storyteller reminds us are kinfolk or relatives of the men, to Jericho, which people call "the city of palm trees."

Stop here as a group and again find the city of Jericho on the map of Judah and Israel, if needed. Pause this audio here.

Stop here as a group and look again at pictures of sandals, a donkey, and palm trees, if needed. Pause this audio here.

Finally, the men returned home to Samaria in the northern kingdom of Israel.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 28:9–15 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has four scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* Oded, Yahweh's prophet
* Yahweh
* Soldiers from the northern kingdom of Israel
* All the people of the southern kingdom of Judah
* The prisoners from Judah
* And four leaders from the northern kingdom: Azariah, Jehohanan's son; Berekiah, Meshillemoth's son; Jehizkiah, Shallum's son; and Amasa, Hadlai's son

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

Oded is one of Yahweh's prophets, and Oded lives in Samaria. Oded goes out to meet Israel's army as they return to Samaria. Oded says to the army, "Yahweh, the God of your ancestors, was angry with Judah. That is why Yahweh let you defeat Judah. But you killed the people with so much violence that even God in heaven is shocked. And now you want to make these people from Judah and Jerusalem your slaves. But you have sinned too, and you are guilty before Yahweh your God. Now listen to me! You have the same ancestors as these people you have captured. So free these people and let them go home. Otherwise, Yahweh will be very angry and will punish you."

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing Israel's army, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We must be a very good army, because we completely defeated Judah's army; or
* The gods that we worship must be very pleased with us, because we won a great victory today! or
* We will show these people from Judah how strong we are. We will take everything Judah's people have and make them our slaves.

Ask the people playing the prisoners from Judah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We feel very scared. These men killed many of our families in a brutal way, and we do not know what these men will do to us now; or
* Even though we often fight with our relatives, we did not expect these men to treat us this badly.

Ask the person playing Oded, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* These soldiers think they won this battle by their own strength, but it was Yahweh who let them win; or
* Yahweh was angry with Judah because they served other gods, but the northern kingdom is guilty of the same sins; or
* These people have served other gods for so long that I have to remind them that Yahweh is their God; or
* These soldiers think that they are successful today, but they do not realize that God is about to punish them.

> Continue the drama.

Now, four of the leaders of the northern kingdom of Israel speak to the army and tell the army that the army is doing wrong. These leaders are Azariah, son of Jehohanan; Berekiah, son of Meshillemoth; Jehizkiah, son of Shallum; and Amasa, son of Hadlai. These men tell the army, "Do not bring these prisoners here. If you do that, Yahweh will consider us guilty of sin. You are making our sins and faults even greater. We are already very guilty, and Yahweh is very angry with us."

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing the soldiers, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We feel surprised. We thought our leaders would be happy that we won the battle and that we brought back all of these good things and these people to be our slaves; or
* We feel shame. We were very strong and powerful in battle, but Oded and our leaders have made us look foolish; or
* We feel afraid. We thought the gods were happy with us, but now our ancestors' God, Yahweh, might punish us!

Ask the people playing the four leaders, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We feel afraid that Yahweh will punish our people; or
* All of this wealth and power that the army has taken will do us no good if Yahweh punishes us.

> Continue the drama.

So, in front of the leaders and all of the people, the soldiers let all of the prisoners go free, and the soldiers laid down all of the things they had taken during the battle.

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing soldiers, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We fought very hard to win, and we feel like we deserve to keep these things, but we do not want to receive punishment; or
* I think Oded was right. These people are our relatives, and we should not have been so harsh with these people.

Continue the drama.

The leaders choose some men to help the people from Judah. Out of all of the things the soldiers brought back from battle, the men take clothes and give them to the people from Judah who are naked. The men also give people sandals to put on their feet. The men give people food and drink, and the men put oil on people's wounds. The men put people who are weak on donkeys, and then the men bring all of these people, who are the men's relatives, back to Jericho, the city of the palms.

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing the people from Judah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We are surprised that the soldiers and the leaders listened to Oded; or
* We are so happy that we do not have to be slaves, and that we can go home.

Continue the drama.

> Then the men from Israel go back home to the city of Samaria.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 28:9–15 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

Oded, a **prophet** of **Yahweh**, comes out to meet the army when the army comes back to **Samaria**. A prophet is a person who gives messages from God to the people. Yahweh is God's personal name. Use the same words or phrases for prophet, Samaria, and Yahweh as you used in previous passages. For more information on prophet, Samaria, and Yahweh, refer to the Master Glossary.

Oded reminds the soldiers that Yahweh is the **God** of their **fathers**. In the Bible, "God" refers to God who created everything. This is the general word for God. Here, the word "fathers" means ancestors. Use the same words or phrases for God and fathers as you used in previous passages. For more information on God, refer to the Master Glossary.

Oded says that the Israel army killed the Judah army in a rage that reached up to **heaven**. This is special language that means that God in heaven saw what the army did. Heaven refers to the place where God lives with his angels. Use the same word or phrase for heaven as you used in previous passages. For more information on heaven, refer to the Master Glossary.

Oded says the army now plans to make these people slaves, or to force these people to serve the Israelites. A slave is the property of a master. The master can sell the slave to someone else, just as he can sell his other property. A slave works without pay and is not free to leave their master. Some masters treated slaves cruelly. The storyteller just says that the Israelites want to make the people they captured from Judah serve them forcefully. You may need to say that the Israelites wanted to make the captives from Judah their slaves. If so, translate slave in the same way you have previously, and refer to **slave** in the Master Glossary for more information.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use to show that the soldiers wanted to force the people from Judah to serve the Israelites. Pause this audio here.

Oded reminds the army that these people are their **brothers**, which means relatives. Use the same word or phrase for brothers or relatives as you used in previous passages.

Some chiefs or leaders of **Ephraim** speak to the army. Ephraim is one of the tribes of Israel, but here Ephraim refers to the whole northern kingdom of Israel. Use the same word or phrase for Ephraim as you used in previous passages.

The leaders say that the people's **sins** and **guilt** are very great. Sin is rebellion against God. Guilt means that someone has done something wrong and deserves punishment. Use the same words or phrases for sins and guilt as you used in previous passages. For more information on sin and guilt, refer to the Master Glossary.

So the soldiers let go of the prisoners and the **plunder** in the presence of the **princes**, or high officials, and all of the **assembly**. Plunder means all of the valuable things that an army takes from the enemy in battle. "Princes" is another word for important leaders, and "assembly" means a large group of people who gather together for a special purpose. Use the same words or phrases for plunder, princes, and assembly as you used in previous passages.

The leaders give the people from Judah clothing, sandals, and food and drink from the plunder the army took during the battle. Sandals are a type of footwear with a leather sole that people tie to the bottom of their foot with leather thongs or straps.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **sandals**. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

The leaders anoint the people. To anoint someone means to pour sweet\-smelling oil over that person. Many times in the Bible, to anoint meant to pour oil over a person or object to show that the person or thing was holy, or dedicated to God. But here, to anoint means to put oil on people's wounds to help the wounds heal.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **anoint**. Look up anoint in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

The men carry the feeble people on donkeys. To be feeble means to fall down from weakness or to be unable to walk. A donkey is a large animal with four legs that people in Israel often rode or used to carry heavy burdens.

Stop here and discuss as a group what words or phrases you will use for **feeble** and **donkeys**. If you have already translated these words in another book of the Bible, use the same words that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

The leaders take the people back to Jericho, the "city of palm trees." These are date palm trees. Date palms are slow\-growing, evergreen trees with arching gray\-green fronds that can grow up to 10 feet long. This kind of palm tree grows a sweet fruit that people call dates.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for "**city of palm trees**." If you have already translated this phrase in another book of the Bible, use the same words that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Finally, the men return to Samaria in the northern kingdom of Israel.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.
