## 2 Chronicles 28:16–27 (id: eng-2ch-p52-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 28:16–27 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 28:16–27 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 28:16–27 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the last story, we learned that Yahweh was angry with the kingdom of Judah because the people had done evil and worshiped other gods. As punishment, Yahweh began to allow Judah's enemies, including the northern kingdom of Israel, to defeat Judah in battle. Now, in this story, we will learn how Judah's king, Ahaz, continues to reject Yahweh and turn to foreign gods for help, and how this hurts Ahaz even more.

As the story begins, we learn that Judah's enemies have continued to attack and defeat Judah, so King Ahaz sends someone to take a message to the king of Assyria to ask for help. We know this is Tiglath Pileser III, who was king of Assyria from 744 B.C. to 727 B.C. Assyria was a country in northern Mesopotamia, the area that today is northern Iran.

Stop here as a group and look at a map that shows Assyria and Judah. Pause this audio here.

The storyteller specifically mentions how the Edomites had attacked Judah and carried away prisoners, and how the Philistines had raided or attacked some of Judah's cities in the lower hills to the west side of Judah and also to the south of Judah. You will remember that the Edomites lived in the land south of Judah, and the Philistines lived in the land west of Judah along the Mediterranean Sea. The storyteller says that the Philistines had taken control of the cities of Beth Shemesh, Aijalon, Gederoth, Soco, Timnah, and Gimzo and also the smaller villages that surrounded those cities.

Stop here as a group and look at a map of Judah that also shows Edom and Philistia. Find each of these cities on the map. Pause this audio here.

Now we learn why these countries were able to defeat Judah. The storyteller says that Yahweh humbled the king of Judah, King Ahaz, because the king turned away from Yahweh and encouraged the people to do wicked things. And so, when King Ahaz sends word to the king of Assyria, Tiglath\-Pileser, to ask for help, Tiglath\-Pileser oppresses Ahaz instead of helping. Ahaz takes many expensive things from Yahweh's temple, from Ahaz's own house, and even from the leaders who work for King Ahaz, and gives these things to Tiglath\-Pileser as payment for his help, but this does not help Ahaz.

When all of these bad things happened, King Ahaz should have asked Yahweh for help, but instead, Ahaz asked the gods of Judah's enemies for help. Ahaz offered sacrifices to the gods that people worshiped in Damascus, the capital city of Aram, or Syria. Ahaz said that if those gods had helped Ahaz's enemies, then Ahaz would ask those gods to help Ahaz, too. But the storyteller says that those gods ruined Ahaz and Judah rather than helping.

Stop here and discuss as a group: Tell about a time someone tried to fix a bad choice they had made, but that person just made it worse. Pause this audio here.

Ahaz took all of the valuable things out of God's temple and destroyed those things. Ahaz also shut the doors of Yahweh's temple so that no one could go into the temple anymore. Instead, Ahaz had people build altars where people could worship foreign gods at the corner of every street in Jerusalem. And in every town in Judah, Ahaz had people build places to sacrifice to foreign gods there, too. All of these things made Yahweh, the God that Ahaz's ancestors worshiped, very angry.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Where do people worship God, or local spirits or gods, in your community? What are the right ways to worship those local spirits or gods? What happens if someone decides to worship another way? Pause this audio here.

The storyteller finishes his story by reminding us that people wrote other important stories about King Ahaz in the book, or scroll, about the kings of Judah and Israel. You will remember that this book is not in the Bible and no longer exists. The storyteller also says that when Ahaz died, people buried Ahaz in Jerusalem, but the people did not put Ahaz's body in the tomb of the kings of Israel. The storyteller probably says this to show that people did not honor Ahaz because Ahaz was wicked and disobeyed Yahweh. However, this is different than what we hear in 2 Kings 16, where that storyteller says that people buried Ahaz with his ancestors. We are not sure why these are different, but it may mean that the people buried Ahaz near his ancestors, but not in the royal tomb. Finally, after Ahaz died, Ahaz's son Hezekiah became the next king.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 28:16–27 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has six scenes.

**In the first scene:** The Edomites and Philistines have once again attacked and defeated Judah, so King Ahaz asks the king of Assyria to help protect Judah.

**In the second scene:** The storyteller reminds us that Yahweh allowed these enemies to defeat Judah because King Ahaz had turned away from Yahweh and caused the people to sin.

**In the third scene:** Instead of protecting Judah, Assyria causes trouble, even though Ahaz gives the king of Assyria expensive things from Yahweh's temple as payment.

**In the fourth scene:** King Ahaz sacrifices to the gods of his enemy, so that those gods will help Ahaz. But instead, those gods destroy Ahaz.

**In the fifth scene:** Ahaz destroys the objects in Yahweh's temple and shuts the doors to the temple so no one will worship there. Instead, Ahaz makes sacred places where people can worship foreign gods. All of these actions make Yahweh very angry.

**In the sixth scene:** The storyteller tells how King Ahaz dies and how Ahaz's son Hezekiah becomes the next king.

The characters in this passage are:

* King Ahaz of Judah
* Edomite army
* Prisoners from Judah
* Philistine army
* People who live in and near Judah's cities that the Philistines capture
* King Tiglath\-Pileser of Assyria and his army, although the storyteller does not mention the army
* Yahweh
* Foreign gods
* And Hezekiah, Ahaz's son

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In the first scene, the storyteller begins by saying that at that time, King Ahaz sent to the king of Assyria for help. Here, the phrase "at that time" does not refer to the same time as the last story, when Syria and the king of Israel attacked Judah. Instead, it refers to a later time when Edom and Philistia attacked. Also, although the storyteller only says Ahaz "sent word," we know that this means the king sent someone to take a message from Ahaz to the king of Assyria. The storyteller will say later in the story that this is King Tiglath\-Pileser.

Stop here as a group and again look at a map that shows Assyria and Judah, if needed. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How do you talk about when one person sends another person to carry a message to a third person who is far away? How will you translate the way King Ahaz sends this message here? Pause this audio here.

The storyteller says that King Ahaz sent this message because the Edomites had again invaded Judah and taken people away as captives or prisoners. Also, the Philistines had attacked cities in the Shephelah and the Negev of Judah. The Shephelah refers to the land in Judah where the hills are not very high. The Shephelah is west of the hill country of Judah but east of the Mediterranean seacoast. The Shephelah is south of the Aijalon Valley in an area about 65 kilometers long and 20 kilometers wide. The Negev refers to the southern part of Judah, where the land is very dry, or like a desert.

Stop here as a group and look at pictures of the Shephelah and the Negev wilderness. Pause this audio here.

The Philistines took control of Beth Shemesh. There are four different cities in the Old Testament called Beth Shemesh, but most people believe that this Beth Shemesh was located on the northwest border of Judah, about 24 kilometers west of Jerusalem. The Philistines also took Aijalon, which people believe was about 20 kilometers northwest of Jerusalem, and Gederoth, which people believe was \[about 18 kilometers southwest of Lod on the Ramle\-Al Mughar road. The Philistines took Soco with its villages.]{.mark} There are three different cities in the Old Testament called Soco, but this is likely the Soco located in the Shephelah, about 30 kilometers southwest of Jerusalem. The Philistines took Timnah with its villages. Timnah was located on the northern border of Judah, about 6\.5 kilometers north of Beth Shemesh. And finally, the Philistines took Gimzo, which was located in the lowland region near the Philistine plain, about 5 kilometers southeast of Lod off the Jerusalem road in the Aijalon Valley. The storyteller says that the Philistines settled in these cities and the villages around these cities, which means to live in those places permanently.

Stop here as a group and again look at a map of Judah that shows the likely location of each of these cities. Pause this audio here.

In the second scene, the storyteller says that Yahweh humbled Judah because Ahaz, king of Israel, had encouraged the people to do wicked things, and because Ahaz himself had been unfaithful to Yahweh. You will remember that the nation of Israel was divided into two different kingdoms at this time. One of these kingdoms was the northern kingdom, which people called Israel. The other kingdom was the southern kingdom, which people called Judah. We know that Ahaz was the king of the southern kingdom of Judah, but the storyteller may call Ahaz the "king of Israel" to remind us that Ahaz is acting just as badly as the people of the northern kingdom, who worshiped other gods.

In the third scene, because Ahaz has done these wicked things, when Ahaz asks King Tiglath\-Pileser of Assyria for help, Tiglath\-Pileser instead comes against Ahaz, which means he attacks Ahaz instead of helping. In the original language, we are not sure if the storyteller says that Tiglath\-Pileser did not make Ahaz strong by helping Ahaz, or if the storyteller says that Tiglath\-Pileser could not overpower Ahaz or defeat him. Either way, we understand that Tiglath\-Pileser made things worse for Ahaz and not better.

The storyteller says that Ahaz took or stole things from Yahweh's house, the temple. Ahaz also took things from his own palace, the house where Ahaz lived as the king. And Ahaz also took things from the "princes" or leaders of Judah who worked for the king. Ahaz then gave all of these expensive things to Tiglath\-Pileser as a way to pay for Assyria's help, but the storyteller says this did not help Ahaz. In other words, Tiglath\-Pileser did not help, even though Ahaz paid Tiglath\-Pileser to help.

In the fourth scene, the storyteller says that when Ahaz is distressed, or when all these bad things happen, Ahaz acts even more unfaithfully to Yahweh. Ahaz makes sacrifices to the gods of Damascus that defeated Ahaz. Remember that Damascus is the capital city of Syria, so these are the gods that the Syrians worship. And Ahaz said, "Because the gods of the kings of Syria helped those kings, I will sacrifice to those gods so that those gods may help me too." Here, the storyteller may mean that Ahaz said this out loud to himself, or it may mean that Ahaz simply thought this thing. Either way, the storyteller tells us this so we will know why Ahaz made sacrifices to these other gods. However, the storyteller says these gods ruined Ahaz and all of Israel, which means that the gods only made things worse, not better. When the storyteller says Israel here, we do not know if he refers to Judah, the kingdom where Ahaz rules, or to the northern kingdom, where people also worshiped foreign gods.

Stop here and discuss as a group: Tell a story about a person who does something that people would not expect. Then tell people what that person thought that caused the person to act that way. Pay attention to the way you explain this. Pause this audio here.

In the fifth scene, the storyteller says that Ahaz gathered together the vessels of the house of God and cut the vessels into pieces. You will remember that the house of God means Yahweh's temple in Jerusalem. The "vessels" refer to the various holy items in the temple that the priests used to worship Yahweh. Apparently, Ahaz destroys these things to prevent people from worshiping Yahweh. Then Ahaz closes the doors to the temple. This probably means that Ahaz locks the doors so that no one can enter the temple to worship Yahweh anymore. Instead, Ahaz makes altars in every corner in Jerusalem. This probably means that Ahaz commands people to build these altars, or places for people to worship these gods, in many places around the city of Jerusalem. Also, in every city of Judah, Ahaz makes high places to burn incense to other gods. High places probably refers to places on hills where people would make special shrines to foreign gods. The storyteller says that all of these things provoked Yahweh, the God of Ahaz's ancestors, to anger. This means that Yahweh felt very angry that Ahaz did all of this.

Stop here and discuss as a group: Tell a story about a time when someone in your community did something that people thought angered God or the local gods or spirits. What did that person do? How did people talk about those things the person did? How did people feel about those actions? Pause this audio here.

In the last scene, the storyteller uses the same kind of formal talk he used to describe other kings. First, the storyteller says that everything that happened when Ahaz was king, and everything Ahaz did, is in the scroll or book of the kings of Judah and Israel. You will remember that a scroll is a type of ancient book where people wrote everything on one very long piece of paper and then rolled that paper around a stick. The storyteller says that Ahaz "slept with his fathers." This is a nice way to say that Ahaz died, just as Ahaz's ancestors before Ahaz did. People buried Ahaz in the city of Jerusalem, but people did not place Ahaz's body in the tombs of the kings of Israel. And finally, Hezekiah, Ahaz's son, succeeded Ahaz as king. This means that Hezekiah became the next king after Ahaz.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 28:16–27 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has six scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* King Ahaz of Judah
* Edomite army
* Prisoners from Judah
* Philistine army
* People who live in and near Judah's cities that the Philistines capture
* King Tiglath\-Pileser of Assyria and his army, although the storyteller does not mention the army
* Yahweh
* Foreign gods
* And Hezekiah, Ahaz's son

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

Then King Ahaz sent people to King Tiglath\-Pileser, the king of Assyria, to ask for help. Ahaz did this because the Edomites had again come and attacked Judah and taken people away as prisoners. And at the same time, the Philistines had raided the cities and towns in Judah's low hills and also the Negev. The Philistines captured and settled in Beth Shemesh, Aijalon, and Gederoth, as well as Soco, Timnah, and Gimzo, along with the villages around those cities.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Ahaz, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I keep losing battles to my enemies. I need to do something to stop this! or
* Now we have even fewer people and cities to protect ourselves; or
* I am not sure if I can trust the Assyrians, but I know they have a strong army. Maybe I can pay the Assyrians to help us.

Continue the drama.

Because of the evil things King Ahaz did, Yahweh made the kingdom of Judah low. Ahaz had led his people to do evil things, and Ahaz himself disobeyed Yahweh.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am the one who protects my people, but now my people worship other gods instead of me. These other gods cannot help the people! or
* I will stop protecting my people so that the people will see that these gods can do nothing for them; or
* Ahaz has rejected me, so now I will reject Ahaz.

Continue the drama.

King Tiglath\-Pileser of Assyria comes to Ahaz. But instead of helping, Tiglath\-Pileser gives Ahaz even more trouble. Ahaz takes some of the things from Yahweh's temple, from Ahaz's palace, and from the officials of Judah, and Ahaz gives these expensive things to Tiglath\-Pileser as payment. But this does not help Ahaz.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Ahaz, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I do not want to give these expensive things away, but if it can help save my kingdom, it is worth it!

Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I allowed these things to happen so Ahaz would remember to obey and worship me. But instead, Ahaz steals things from my temple to pay a human king to help.

Ask the person playing King Tiglath\-Pileser, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* My army is bigger and stronger than Judah's. I cannot completely defeat Judah in battle, but I will gladly take these expensive things from them.

Continue the drama.

King Ahaz disobeyed Yahweh even more when all of these bad things happened. Ahaz made sacrifices to the gods of Damascus, who had defeated Ahaz in battle, because Ahaz thought, "The gods of the kings of Aram have helped those kings, so I will also sacrifice to those gods. Then those gods will help me too." But worshiping those gods ruined Ahaz and all the people of Judah.

Pause the drama. Ask the people of Judah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* Our enemies stole some of our people and cities. We must do something to protect ourselves! or
* If Yahweh allowed these bad things to happen, maybe other gods can help instead; or
* We will do whatever our king tells us to do.

> Continue the drama.

King Ahaz gathered up and destroyed all of the sacred things from Yahweh's temple. Ahaz shut and locked the doors of the temple so that no one could go in to worship Yahweh anymore. Then Ahaz set up altars at the corner of every street in Jerusalem, and Ahaz built sacred places to burn sacrifices to foreign gods in every town in Judah. This made Yahweh, the God that Ahaz's ancestors worshiped, very angry.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Ahaz continues to disobey me and treat me with great disrespect; or
* Ahaz not only disobeys me and worships other gods, but Ahaz makes other people disobey me too; or
* I will punish Ahaz and the people of Judah for doing these things.

> Continue the drama.

King Ahaz did many things while he was king, and people wrote about all of these things in the book "The History of the Kings of Judah and Israel." Then Ahaz died, and people buried Ahaz with his ancestors. People buried Ahaz in Jerusalem, but the people did not bury Ahaz in the tombs of the kings of Israel. After Ahaz died, Ahaz's son Hezekiah became the next king of Judah.

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing the people of Judah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* The good kings of Judah in the past always worshiped Yahweh, but Ahaz changed all of that; or
* It seems like all of the changes Ahaz made in Judah made things worse for us, not better; or
* We need to show respect, but Ahaz was not a very good king. So we will bury Ahaz in Jerusalem, but not in the tombs where people buried the most important kings.

Ask the person playing Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* My father stopped people from worshiping Yahweh, our ancestors' God; or
* My father thought these other gods would make us stronger, but worshiping those gods made everything worse; or
* I do not want to be like my father. My father did not leave a good legacy as king.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 28:16–27 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

As the story begins, **King** Ahaz sends a message to the king of Assyria to ask for help. Use the same word or phrase for king as you used in previous passages. King is in the Master Glossary.

Assyria was a country in northern Mesopotamia, the area that today is northern Iran.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **Assyria**. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

The **Edomites** had once again defeated Judah and carried away captives or prisoners. You will remember that the Edomites were the ancestors of Esau, Jacob's brother. Jacob, who God later named Israel, was the ancestor of the Israelites. Use the same word or phrase for Edomites as you used in previous passages.

The Philistines had also made raids and taken several **cities** in the **foothills** and the Negev wilderness. Translate cities the same as you have in previous passages. The foothills refer to the land in Judah where the hills are not very high. These foothills are west of the hill country of Judah but east of the Mediterranean seacoast. Use the same word or phrase for foothills as you used in previous passages.

Stop here as a group and look again at a picture of the foothills, if needed. Pause this audio here.

The Negev refers to the Negev wilderness, the southern part of Judah, where the land is very dry, or like a desert.

Stop here as a group and look again at a picture of the Negev wilderness, if needed. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for the **Negev wilderness**. If you use the word for wilderness, refer to wilderness in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Yahweh **humbled** Judah. **Yahweh** is God's personal name, and to humble people means to make those people submit, to bring them low, or to make them lose face. Use the same words or phrases for Yahweh and humbled as you used in previous passages. For more information on Yahweh, refer to the Master Glossary.

Yahweh humbled Judah because King Ahaz had promoted wickedness or caused Judah to act sinfully. Wickedness means evil, or the opposite of good, and to sin means to rebel against God. But here, King Ahaz does not just do wicked or sinful things himself. Instead, Ahaz does wrong as the leader of Judah and causes other people to do sinful or wicked things.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **promoted wickedness** or **caused Judah to act sinfully**. Look up **wickedness** and **sin** in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated these words in another book of the Bible, use the same words that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

King Tiglath\-Pileser of Assyria **afflicted** Ahaz, even though Ahaz paid him with many things. To afflict means to oppress or restrict either physically or emotionally. This does not necessarily mean that the Assyrians made war with Judah, but it does mean that Tiglath\-Pileser made things even harder for Ahaz. Use the same word or phrase for afflicted as you used in previous passages.

King Ahaz **sacrificed** to the **gods** of Damascus. When the Israelites would make a sacrifice to Yahweh, it either meant to kill an animal and then burn the animal on an altar, or to give a gift to Yahweh of food items, oil, or sweet\-smelling spices. But here, King Ahaz instead makes sacrifices to the foreign gods of Judah's enemies. Use the same words or phrases for sacrifice and gods as you used in previous passages. For more information on gods and on the kinds of sacrifices that people made to Yahweh, refer to the Master Glossary.

Ahaz destroyed the **vessels** in Yahweh's **temple**. Vessels refer to the items the priests would use in the temple for worship. Use the same words or phrases for vessels and temple as you used in previous passages. For more information, look up temple in the Master Glossary.

Ahaz made **altars** all around Jerusalem. An altar is a place where people make offerings or sacrifices to God, but Ahaz made these altars to make offerings to foreign gods. Use the same word or phrase for altars as you used in previous passages. For more information on the kind of altar that people made to worship Yahweh, refer to the Master Glossary.

Ahaz also made **high places**. People from these other countries would often make the shrines or places where they worshiped their gods on naturally high places, like hills, so Ahaz did the same thing in Judah. Use the same word or phrase for high places as you used in previous passages.

People wrote about everything King Ahaz did in a **book** or **scroll** about the kings of Judah and Israel. You will remember that in that time, a book or "scroll" was usually a long piece of leather or paper that people wrote on and then rolled up around a stick. This way, people could easily carry or store the scroll until they wanted to read it. Use the same word or phrase for book or scroll as you used in previous passages, and refer to the Master Glossary for more information about scroll.

Ahaz **slept with his fathers**. Slept is a nice way to say that Ahaz died, and "fathers" means Ahaz's ancestors. Use the same word or phrase for "slept with his fathers" as you used in previous passages.

The storyteller says that people did not bury Ahaz in the **tombs** of the kings. A tomb is a place where people bury a dead body. These were often caves or rooms that people would hollow out of rock to hide the body from view. Use the same word or phrase for tomb as you used in previous passages. For more information about tomb, refer to the Master Glossary.

Finally, after Ahaz died, Ahaz's son Hezekiah **reigned** in Ahaz's place. To reign means to rule or act with power as king. Use the same word or phrase for reign as you used in previous passages.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 28:16-27

## 2 Chronicles 29:1–17 (id: eng-2ch-p53-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 29:1–17 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 29:1–17 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 29:1–17 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous story, we learned how King Ahaz served other gods and kings instead of Yahweh and caused the people of Judah to sin, too. These things made Yahweh very angry. When Ahaz died, people did not honor Ahaz by burying his body with the other kings in Jerusalem. Then Ahaz's son, Hezekiah, became the new king of Judah.

In this story, we learn more about King Hezekiah. Remember that 2 Kings 18:1–12 has similar information about Hezekiah.

Stop here as a group and listen to your translation of 2 Kings 18:1–12 if you have already translated that passage. Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah was 25 years old when he became king, and Hezekiah was king for 29 years. Hezekiah lived in Jerusalem. Hezekiah's mother was Abijah, and Abijah's father was Zechariah. Many men in the Bible had the name Zechariah, so we are not sure which Zechariah this is. However, we know this is not the man who wrote the book of Zechariah in the Bible.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your community, what are some common names that more than one person has? Tell a story about a time when someone talked about one person, but people thought that person talked about a different person who had the same name. Tell what happened because people misunderstood. Pause this audio here.

King Hezekiah does good things that please Yahweh. Shortly after Hezekiah becomes king, Hezekiah opens the doors of Yahweh's temple. Hezekiah also has people repair these doors. You will remember from the previous stories that it was Hezekiah's father, King Ahaz, who shut the temple doors so people could not come to worship Yahweh anymore. But now, Hezekiah begins to undo the evil things Ahaz did.

Hezekiah gathers the Levites and priests outside the temple. You will remember that the Israelites were 12 tribes. One tribe was the Levites, and the Levites who descended from Moses' brother Aaron were the priests. The priests were responsible for offering sacrifices to Yahweh on the people's behalf. The priests took care of the temple and taught the people how to worship God. The other Levites helped the priests.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your community, which people are responsible for religious duties? How do people get those responsibilities? Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah tells the Levites and priests to make themselves holy and to make the temple holy. Hezekiah says the ancestors of Judah's people were unfaithful to Yahweh and did evil things. Those people closed the temple doors and stopped serving Yahweh. So Yahweh became very angry with all the people. Yahweh punished Judah's people. The surrounding nations were horrified and surprised when they saw how Yahweh punished Judah. The people of those other nations talked badly about the people of Judah. Hezekiah says that the Levites and priests know that these things are true because they saw them happen. The people of Judah's ancestors died in battle, and the people's sons, daughters, and wives became prisoners in other countries. All of these things happened because of the evil things the people of Judah did.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your community, how do people act toward a person when they believe that God is punishing that person? What do people say to each other about that person? Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah has decided that the people of Judah must make a covenant or agreement with Yahweh that the people will once again serve Yahweh and only Yahweh. Hezekiah hopes that when the people of Judah do this, Yahweh will stop being angry at the people.

Hezekiah tells the Levites and priests that they must begin working at the temple because Yahweh chose these men to serve Yahweh and offer sacrifices. So the Levites prepare themselves to work in the temple. Here, the storyteller gives the name of each of the Levite men who serve as a leader of a family group. These leaders organize all the men in their groups, and together, all the men ritually cleanse themselves and the temple.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Tell a story about a large group of people with many smaller groups who all needed to work together. Tell how each smaller group had its own leader and how the leaders worked together to organize all of the people. Pause this audio here.

You will remember that the law that Yahweh gave to Moses included detailed instructions for how to make people and things holy before Yahweh. In the law, Yahweh said that only the priests could go into the inner part of the temple, so here, it is the priests who cleanse these parts. The priests bring out everything that is unclean. We do not know what these things are, but these are probably things Ahaz brought into the temple to worship other gods. The men bring all these things to the Kidron Valley. The Kidron Valley was a place on the east side of Jerusalem where people buried dead bodies. You will remember that the law said dead bodies were unclean, so we are not surprised that the men take these unclean things to this unclean place. You may also remember from an earlier story in 2 Chronicles 15:16 how King Asa brought idols to this valley to destroy the idols with fire.

Stop here as a group and look at a map that shows Jerusalem and the Kidron Valley. Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your culture, what makes a person or thing ceremonially unclean or unholy? How do people treat a person or thing that is unholy? Pause this audio here.

The storyteller says the priests and Levites began purifying, or making the temple holy, on the first day of the month. It took eight days to purify the temple courtyards and another eight days to purify the temple itself. So the men finished the work on the 16th day of that same month.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 29:1–17 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has five scenes.

**In the first scene:** The storyteller gives general information about Hezekiah's time as king. We also learn that Hezekiah does what Yahweh says is right, just as King David did.

**In the second scene:** Hezekiah opens the temple so people can go inside, and Hezekiah repairs the temple.

**In the third scene:** Hezekiah tells the Levites and priests to purify themselves and the temple. Hezekiah reminds the men of the evil things the people's ancestors did. Hezekiah says it is because people did these evil things that Yahweh became angry and Judah's people suffered and died. But Hezekiah says the people will make a covenant with Yahweh so that Yahweh will stop being angry with Judah.

**In the fourth scene:** Hezekiah tells the Levites and priests to begin serving Yahweh and to help the people worship Yahweh.

**In the fifth scene:** All the Levites and priests begin to do their priestly work again. The men purify themselves and the temple. The men follow all of Yahweh's instructions.

The characters in this passage are:

* Hezekiah, Judah's king
* Abijah, who is Zechariah's daughter and Hezekiah's mother
* Yahweh
* King David, Hezekiah's ancestor
* The Levites
* The priests, who are also Levites
* The people of Judah
* The people's ancestors
* And the leaders of the Levite clans

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In the first scene, the storyteller gives the same information about Hezekiah as the previous kings. We hear that Hezekiah was 25 years old when Hezekiah became king. Hezekiah reigned or was in power as king in Jerusalem for 29 years. Hezekiah's mother was Abijah, and Abijah was the daughter of Zechariah. We are not sure which Zechariah this was.

Hezekiah did what was right in Yahweh's eyes, just as Hezekiah's father, King David, had done. This is special language that means that Hezekiah did the things Yahweh approved of. David was Hezekiah's ancestor. You will remember that many times in the Bible, "father" means an ancestor, not a person's literal father.

In the second scene, we hear that in the first year Hezekiah reigned as king, in the first month, Hezekiah opened the doors of Yahweh's temple and repaired the doors. This could mean that Hezekiah became king sometime during one year, and then, when the next year began, Hezekiah opened the temple. Or, more likely, this means Hezekiah began to reign in the first month of the year and immediately opened the temple. You will remember that for the Israelites, each year began at the time of year we call mid\-March. So the storyteller shows that Hezekiah began to care for Yahweh's temple very soon after Hezekiah became king. You will remember that when the storyteller says Hezekiah repaired the temple doors, this probably means that Hezekiah told his workers to repair the doors, not that Hezekiah did the work himself.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How will you talk about when Hezekiah began to work on the temple in your translation? Pause this audio here.

In the third scene, Hezekiah gathers the priests and Levites in the open place on the east. We are not sure what this place is, but we know it is east of the temple. This may refer to a place near one of the eastern gates of Jerusalem or perhaps the eastern courtyard in front of the temple, which faced east. We also do not know how Hezekiah gathers the men together. It may be that Hezekiah tells some of his workers to travel around and tell all of the priests and Levites to come to the temple.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How will you talk about the place where Hezekiah gathers the men? You should translate this so it does not say an exact location, just like the storyteller does not say the exact location. For example, you might say something like, "the open place east of the temple" or "the gathering place east of the temple." Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah speaks to this large group of men. First, Hezekiah tells the Levites that they must pay attention to what Hezekiah says. You will remember that priests are a special kind of Levite, so when Hezekiah says "Levites," Hezekiah means all of the men, including the priests. Hezekiah tells the men to consecrate themselves, or to make themselves holy. Hezekiah also tells the men to consecrate the temple. Hezekiah calls Yahweh "the God of your fathers." Again, "fathers" means ancestors. Hezekiah probably says this to remind the Levites that although the people of Judah have been worshiping other nations' gods, Yahweh has always been the Israelites' God, and this is Yahweh's temple.

Hezekiah says the men must carry the dirty things out of the Holy Place. Hezekiah is not saying there is literally garbage or dirt in the temple. Hezekiah means the men must remove the idols and other unholy things from the temple. The sanctuary, or Holy Place, refers to the middle part of the temple, not the Most Holy Place, where only the high priest could enter once a year.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your culture, how do you talk about things that are ceremonially unclean, or unfit for people to use in worship? Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah says the men's fathers were unfaithful. Again, "fathers" means ancestors, so Hezekiah refers to the way the previous generations of people worshipped other gods instead of Yahweh. The people did what was evil in Yahweh's sight. This is special language that means the people did what Yahweh said was very wrong.

Again, Hezekiah calls Yahweh the people's God. But Hezekiah says the people have left Yahweh, have turned their faces from where Yahweh lives, and have turned their backs. These are three similar ways to say the same idea. It seems Hezekiah wants the people to remember that Yahweh did not leave the people, but the people left Yahweh. Yahweh's presence is at the temple, but the people quit going to the temple. Instead, the people worshipped idols. Literally, when people quit going to the temple to worship Yahweh, the people no longer faced toward the temple. However, Hezekiah also uses this as a symbol to show how the people's relationship to Yahweh changed. When the people worshipped idols instead of Yahweh, the people no longer knew Yahweh, like a person who walks away from another person so that only their back faces that person.

Stop here and do this activity as a group: Mark out an area on the ground to represent a person's house. Choose one person from your group to stand in that house. Next, have some people from your group come to that house to visit the person and become friends. Now, choose another person from your group and have that person stand some distance away from the house. Have the people who had been visiting the person at the house leave that person and instead go visit the new person. Pay attention to how the people walk away from the house and how they stand with their backs facing the house. Pause this audio here.

The people's ancestors also shut the doors of the vestibule or porch. This was the open area right in front of the doors that opened into the temple. You will remember that in the last story, we learned how Hezekiah's father, King Ahaz, shut these doors so people could not go into the temple.

The people's ancestors also put out the lamps and no longer burned incense or offered burnt offerings in the sanctuary, or Holy Place, to Israel's God. You will remember in the books of Exodus and Leviticus how Yahweh instructed the priests to always keep these lamps burning in the temple. Yahweh had also given the priests the responsibility to burn incense and make burnt offerings to Yahweh on behalf of the people. The Holy Place refers to the whole temple area, not just a specific part of the temple. And Israel refers specifically to the kingdom of Judah. You will remember that at this time, the nation of Israel was divided into two kingdoms: the kingdom that people called Judah in the south, and the kingdom that people called Israel in the north. But both kingdoms were part of the nation of Israel.

Stop here as a group and tell a story about two separate communities that were the same people group but did not get along with each other. How did the people talk about each other? Pause this audio here.

Because of the wrong things people did, Yahweh became very, very angry at the people of Judah and Jerusalem. Yahweh made people from other nations feel horrified and surprised when they saw how Yahweh punished Judah. People hissed or made the whistling sound that people make when they want someone to feel ashamed. Hezekiah says the Levites know these things are true because they have seen it with their own eyes. This is special language that means the men have seen these things happen.

In the original language, Hezekiah uses a special word to draw extra attention to what Hezekiah says next. The men's fathers, or ancestors, have "fallen by the sword." This phrase refers to the way an enemy uses a sword, a sharp weapon, to badly wound a person in battle so that the person falls down on the ground and dies. Also, the men's sons, daughters, and wives are in captivity. This means that Judah's enemies took the men's wives and children away to other countries as prisoners. Hezekiah reminds the men that this happened because Yahweh was very angry with them.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How do you talk about the way a person dies in a violent way in battle? Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah says it is in Hezekiah's "heart" to make a covenant with Yahweh, Israel's God. In the Bible, the heart is special language for the place where a person's thoughts, feelings, and decisions happen. A covenant is a special promise that people make to each other. So Hezekiah has decided that the people of Judah must promise to be faithful to Yahweh. The people must do this so that Yahweh will no longer be angry with the people.

In the fourth scene, Hezekiah calls the men "my sons." This is special language that shows the close relationship the Levites have with the king. Hezekiah says not to be lazy or neglect the work Yahweh gave the men. Yahweh chose these men to stand in Yahweh's presence, to minister to Yahweh, and to be Yahweh's ministers. Each of these phrases means almost the same thing: to serve Yahweh and lead other people in worship. Hezekiah says these three different ways to emphasize it more strongly. Hezekiah also says Yahweh chose these men to burn incense to Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How do you emphasize something in a stronger way by the way you say it? If you do not have similar phrases like this in your language, how will you emphasize what Hezekiah says? Pause this audio here.

In the fifth scene, the storyteller says the Levites arise. This is special language that means the men got ready to do what Hezekiah said to do. The storyteller gives a list of each Levite clan and its leaders. From the Kohathite clan, the leaders are Mahath, Amasai's son, and Joel, Azariah's son. From the Merarite clan, the leaders are Kish, Abdi's son, and Azariah, Jehallel's son. From the Gershonite clan, the leaders are Joah, Zimmah's son, and Eden, Joah's son. From Elizaphan's descendants, the leaders are Shimri and Jeiel. From Asaph's descendants, the leaders are Zechariah and Mattaniah. From Heman's descendants, the leaders are Jehiel and Shimei. And from Jeduthan's descendants, the leaders are Shemaiah and Uzziel.

Each of these leaders gathers the men of their clan together. The men make themselves ritually clean and holy to serve Yahweh. Then the men go in to clean Yahweh's temple. The men do what King Hezekiah commands, according to Yahweh's law. This could refer to how Yahweh instructed Hezekiah to cleanse the temple, but it more likely refers to Yahweh's instructions in the law. So the priests go into Yahweh's temple and remove the things that make the temple impure. The priests bring these things out to the temple courtyard. Then the Levites carry these things away to the Kidron Valley. You will remember that the Kidron Valley was a valley to the east of Jerusalem.

Stop here as a group and again look at a map that shows Jerusalem and the Kidron Valley, if needed. Pause this audio here.

The storyteller ends by telling how long it took the priests and other Levites to finish this work. The men began to consecrate, or make the temple holy, on the first day of the first month. On the eighth day of the month, the men finished making the courtyard holy and reached the porch of Yahweh's temple. Then, for eight more days, the men continued to make holy the inside of Yahweh's temple. Finally, the men finished on the 16th day of the first month.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 29:1–17 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has five scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* Hezekiah, Judah's king
* Abijah, who is Zechariah's daughter and Hezekiah's mother
* Yahweh
* King David, Hezekiah's ancestor
* The Levites
* The priests, who are also Levites
* The people of Judah
* The people's ancestors
* And the leaders of the Levite clans

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

Hezekiah becomes Judah's king when Hezekiah is 25 years old. Hezekiah rules Judah from the city of Jerusalem for 29 years. Hezekiah's mother's name is Abijah. Abijah is the daughter of a man whose name is Zechariah. Hezekiah does things that please Yahweh, just like Hezekiah's ancestor, King David, did.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am happy that Hezekiah wants to serve me and do what is right; or
* I will allow Hezekiah to be Judah's king for a long time.

Ask the person playing Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I want to be a better king than my father, Ahaz, was; or
* I want to serve Yahweh. I will try to be like my ancestor, King David.

Continue the drama.

During King Hezekiah's first month as king, Hezekiah opens the doors of Yahweh's temple and has people fix the doors.

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing the workers, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We are happy that we can help make these doors beautiful again.

Ask the people playing the people of Judah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* Some of us remember when King Ahaz closed Yahweh's temple. We are glad to see the temple open again; or
* Some of us are too young to remember when people worshipped at this temple. We do not know Yahweh.

Continue the drama.

Hezekiah gathers the priests and Levites on the east side of the temple. Then Hezekiah speaks to all of the men: "You people from Levi's tribe, listen to me! Make yourselves holy for Yahweh, and also make Yahweh's temple holy. Yahweh was your ancestors' God. Get rid of the dirty things from the inner room of the temple. Our ancestors did not keep their promises to Yahweh. Our ancestors did evil things that Yahweh, our God, did not like. Our ancestors abandoned Yahweh. Our ancestors turned their backs on the place where Yahweh lives. Our ancestors ignored Yahweh altogether. Our ancestors also closed the doors at the entrance of the temple and made the fire stop burning in the lamps. Our ancestors no longer burned incense for Yahweh in the inner room or made burnt offerings to Israel's God. Because of this, Yahweh has become angry with Judah and the city of Jerusalem. Yahweh has made people from other nations feel shocked and horrified when they see Yahweh's people, so that those people greatly disrespect Yahweh's people. You have seen this yourselves. This is why people killed our ancestors in battle, and why people have taken our sons, our daughters, and our wives as prisoners. So today, I want to renew the strong agreement we made with Yahweh, the God of Israel's people, so that Yahweh will stop being angry with us."

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel ashamed that my father, Ahaz, led the people of Judah to worship idols; or
* I do not want Yahweh to be angry with us. I hope Yahweh will forgive us.

Ask the people playing the Levites, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* I think King Hezekiah is right. Yahweh has been punishing us; or
* I agree with King Ahaz. We must obey and serve Yahweh; or
* King Hezekiah acts very differently than King Ahaz did. Hezekiah is a wise king.

Continue the drama.

"Do not be lazy any longer, my sons. Yahweh chose you to stand in Yahweh's presence and to serve Yahweh. Yahweh chose you to be Yahweh's workers and to burn Yahweh's offerings."

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing the Levites, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We can tell that Hezekiah cares about us very much. Hezekiah speaks to us like we are Hezekiah's family or close friends; or
* We feel honored that Yahweh chose us to do this special work to serve Yahweh; or
* It has been a long time since our people worked in the temple. We hope the work is not too difficult.

Continue the drama.

After King Hezekiah finishes speaking, the Levites start to work. From Kohath's clan, the leaders are Mahath, Amasai's son, and Joel, Azariah's son. From Merari's clan, the leaders are Kish, Abdi's son, and Azariah, Jehallel's son. From Gershon's clan, the leaders are Joah, Zimmah's son, and Eden, Joah's son. From Elizaphan's descendants, the leaders are Shimri and Jeiel. From Asaph's descendants, the leaders are Zechariah and Mattaniah. From Heman's descendants, the leaders are Jehiel and Shimei. And from Jeduthun's descendants, the leaders are Shemaiah and Uzziel.

After the leaders brought all the other Levites together, the Levites all purified themselves to serve Yahweh. Then the Levites purified Yahweh's temple, just as the king told them to do, according to Yahweh's instructions. The priests entered Yahweh's temple to make the temple holy. The priests brought everything from inside that was unclean out to the courtyard. Then the Levites took those things away to the Kidron Valley.

The priests and Levites began to purify Yahweh's temple on the first day of the first month. By the eighth day of the month, the priests and Levites finished with the courtyard and reached the entrance to Yahweh's temple. Then for eight more days, the priests and Levites purified Yahweh's temple itself to make it holy, and they finished on the first month's 16th day.

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing the priests, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* I am surprised that there are so many things in the temple that we need to remove; or
* I did not expect it to take us this many days to clean the temple!

Ask the people playing the Levites who are not priests, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We are disappointed that we are not allowed to go into the temple like the priests; or
* The priests had a lot of work to do in the temple! or
* We are glad we do not have to stay in the Kidron Valley very long. The Kidron Valley is not a very nice place. We would rather be at Yahweh's temple, where we feel close to Yahweh.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 29:1–17 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah began to **reign** when Hezekiah was 25 years old. You will remember that to reign means to rule or act with power as king. Use the same word or phrase for reign as you used in previous passages.

Hezekiah opens the doors of Yahweh's house. Yahweh's house is another way to say Yahweh's **temple**. Use the same words or phrases for Yahweh's house or temple as you used in previous passages. For more information on temple, refer to the Master Glossary.

Hezekiah gathered the **priests** and the **Levites**. Use the same words or phrases for priests and Levites as you used in previous passages. For more information on priest and Levite, refer to the Master Glossary.

The priests and Levites must **consecrate** themselves and the temple. To consecrate a person or thing means to set that person or thing apart for special service to God or to make it holy. In the Old Testament, when a priest or Levite consecrated themself, it meant they did not have sexual relations with their wife for a certain amount of time, and then they had to take a special bath and put on fresh clothes. To consecrate the temple and all the objects in the temple, the priests sprinkled the blood of animals they had sacrificed in the temple and over the objects in the temple. Use the same word or phrase for consecrate as you used in previous passages. For more information, refer to consecrate in the Master Glossary.

Hezekiah refers to **Yahweh** as the **God** of the people's **fathers**. God is the general name for the most powerful spiritual being who created everything, and Yahweh is God's personal name. "Fathers" means ancestors. Use the same words or phrases for Yahweh, God, and fathers as you have used in previous passages. For more information, look up Yahweh and God in the Master Glossary.

The people's ancestors did **evil**. Use the same word or phrase for evil as you used in previous passages. For more information on evil, refer to the Master Glossary.

The Levites must take the dirty things out of the **Holy Place**. This refers to the middle room of the temple. Use the same word or phrase for Holy Place as you used in previous passages, where Holy Place referred to this room. For more information on Holy Place, refer to the Master Glossary.

The people's ancestors turned their faces away from Yahweh's **dwelling place**, or the place where Yahweh lives. In this passage, this refers to the temple, but in the original language, the same phrase can refer to the tabernacle, which was Yahweh's dwelling place or house before the temple. For more information on temple, refer to the Master Glossary. Use the same word or phrase for dwelling place or house as you used in previous passages.

The people's ancestors put out the **lamps**. You will remember that Yahweh instructed Moses how to make the lampstand in Exodus 25:31–40, and that this lampstand held seven oil lamps. These lamps symbolized the way that Yahweh led the people of Israel, and the priests were supposed to make sure these lamps always kept burning. Use the same word or phrase for lamps as you used in previous passages. For more information about the kind of lamps people used in Judah at this time, look up **oil lamp** in the Master Glossary.

Stop here as a group and look at a drawing of what the lampstand in the temple might have looked like. Pause this audio here.

The priests had also stopped **burning incense** and offering **burnt offerings** in the **sanctuary**. People make incense from things like spices and wood that smell very good when people burn the incense in a fire. The priests would burn incense as an offering to Yahweh. A burnt offering is a form of sacrifice where a person kills an animal and burns this animal completely on an altar, which is a special place meant for bringing gifts to Yahweh. Here, the sanctuary refers to the whole temple complex. Use the same words or phrases for burning incense, burnt offerings, and sanctuary as you used in previous passages. For more information on burnt offerings and sanctuary, refer to the Master Glossary.

Yahweh's **wrath** came on Judah and Jerusalem. Wrath is the attitude Yahweh has when Yahweh sees sin and evil. Wrath is stronger than anger. Look up wrath in the Master Glossary for more information. Use the same word or phrase for wrath as you used in previous passages.

Yahweh made people from other nations feel horrified when those people saw how Yahweh punished Judah. In the original language, this literally means the people trembled or felt very afraid when they saw how Yahweh punished the people of Judah.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **horrified**. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

The people of other nations also felt "astonished," or surprised. This is a similar idea to horrified. This word means to feel shocked by a bad thing that happens.

Stop here and discuss as a group how you will describe how the people felt **astonished**. Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah says the people's ancestors were killed by the **sword**. A sword is a long, sharp weapon that people make from metal. People use a sword to fight and injure others in battle. A sword has a handle to hold and a blade to cut or stab. A sword is usually longer than a knife. Use the same word or phrase for sword as you used in previous passages.

It is in Hezekiah's **heart** to make a **covenant** with Yahweh. "Heart" is special language for the place where a person's thoughts, feelings, and decisions happen. When people make a covenant, those people make a solemn promise to each other that they cannot break. Use the same words or phrases for heart and covenant as you used in previous passages. For more information on heart and covenant, refer to the Master Glossary.

Yahweh has chosen the Levites to minister to Yahweh and to make offerings to Yahweh. To minister means to do the work of a servant, or to do work for someone who is more important.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **minister**. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

An **offering** is a gift or sacrifice to Yahweh. People can make offerings for many reasons, including to give thanks, to be purified, or to ask forgiveness for sin. Use the same word or phrase for offering as you used in previous passages. For more information on offering, refer to the Master Glossary.

The priests and Levites cleanse, or purify, the temple. To cleanse means to make something ritually clean or holy.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **cleanse**, or **purify**. Look up purify in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.
