## 2 Chronicles 29:18–30 (id: eng-2ch-p54a-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 29:18–30 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 29:18–30 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 29:18–30 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

The events in this story happened some time after Hezekiah, king of Judah, had told the priests and the Levites to consecrate themselves and the temple of Yahweh, and after the priests and the Levites had finished consecrating themselves and the temple. The events in this story happened in Jerusalem.

This story is about how King Hezekiah, the priests, and the Levites started doing the worship service at the temple again. King Hezekiah did this because Hezekiah wanted to make a covenant with Yahweh so that Yahweh would stop being angry at Hezekiah and at the Israelites, because the ancestors of the Israelites had done evil things.

The priests and the Levites go to King Hezekiah and report that they have purified the entire temple of Yahweh, the altar of burnt offering with all its utensils, and the table for setting out the bread of the presence, with all the utensils that the priests used in the temple.

Stop here and, as a group, look at a picture of the altar with all the utensils and the table for the bread of the presence with all the utensils. Pause this audio here.

When you consecrate or sanctify someone, you set the person apart for special service to Yahweh. People can also consecrate or sanctify an object to do a special task for Yahweh. At that time, the priests and Levites consecrated an object by sprinkling the blood of animals they had sacrificed in the temple over that object. These objects were now holy—they belonged to Yahweh. People could not use these objects for ordinary use. If people did, that would be a terrible insult to Yahweh. These utensils are the things the priests used during the worship time at the temple of Yahweh, including the special tools and dishes.

The priests and the Levites say that they have prepared and consecrated all the utensils that King Ahaz removed when Ahaz was unfaithful to Yahweh. All the articles are now again in front of Yahweh's altar. You will remember in earlier stories that when King Ahaz was ruling as the king, King Ahaz took these utensils from the temple to use the utensils to worship idols. The priests and the Levites have brought back all these utensils into the temple.

Early the next morning, King Hezekiah gathers the officials or leaders of the city together and goes up to Yahweh's temple. King Hezekiah and the officials bring seven bulls, seven rams, seven male lambs, and seven male goats as a sin offering for the royal family of Judah, for Yahweh's temple, and for the rest of the kingdom. We know from the context of the passage that the male goats were for the sin offering. The priests sacrifice the bulls, rams, and male lambs as a burnt offering. A ram is an adult male sheep, usually over one year old. A lamb refers specifically to a young sheep that is less than one year old.

Stop here and, as a group, look at pictures of a bull, sheep, ram, lamb, and goat. Pause this audio here.

King Hezekiah and the officials brought these animals to sacrifice as offerings to remove the sin people had done in the royal family, in Yahweh's temple, and in the land of Judah.

King Hezekiah commands the priests, the descendants of Aaron, to offer the animals on the altar of Yahweh. So the people slaughter, or kill, the bulls, and the priests take the blood, and splash the blood against the altar. Next, the priests slaughter the rams and splash the rams' blood against the altar. Then the priests slaughter the lambs and splash the lambs' blood against the altar.

The priests then bring the goats for the sin or purification offering before the king and the people, and the king and the people lay their hands on the goats. Laying hands on the goats symbolizes that the people have transferred their sins onto the animals. The animals die in place of the people who have sinned so that the people do not have to receive God's punishment for the people's sin.

The priests then kill the goats and present the blood of the goats as a sin offering on the altar to make atonement for all Israel. When someone makes atonement, they restore the relationship between people or groups of people. The king had commanded the priests to make the burnt offering and the sin or purification offering for all the people of Israel. The people had broken their relationship with Yahweh because of the sin the people committed. By making atonement, the people restore their relationship with Yahweh, and Yahweh cleanses the people of their sins.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your culture, what do people do to restore the broken relationship between a person and God, a person and gods, or a person and spirits? Pause this audio here.

King Hezekiah positions the Levites in the temple of Yahweh with cymbals, harps, and lyres. Hezekiah followed the same placement that David used for the temple musicians. Yahweh had commanded David to do so through the prophet Gad, who was King David's seer, and through Nathan the prophet. \[You will remember that Gad was a prophet who served in David's royal court.]{.mark} You may also remember that people frequently used cymbals, harps, and lyres as musical instruments in worship.

A cymbal was a musical instrument that consisted of two metal discs that a person struck together in order to make a shrill, clashing sound. People made cymbals with copper or bronze at that time.

Stop here and look at a picture of a cymbal. Pause this audio here.

The harp and lyre are stringed instruments. The harp has 10 to 13 strings of unequal length. A harp does not have a connecting yoke like a lyre. A person plays a harp by plucking its strings to make music. A lyre has a rectangular sound box with two arms and a yoke that connects the two arms. Lyres had from four to eight strings of about the same length. A lyre is usually smaller than a harp, and a person plays a lyre to make music by plucking the strings.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of a harp and a lyre. Pause this audio here.

So the Levites stand ready with the same kinds of instruments that King David had used, and the priests stand with their trumpets. It is unlikely that King David's own instruments were still in working condition after 300 years. A trumpet is a long, straight instrument that musicians blow air into. The trumpet had a limited range of notes. People made trumpets from hammered metal, either silver or bronze. Priests played these trumpets to sound an alarm in times of war or to produce music in times of celebration.

Stop here as a group and look at a picture of a trumpet. Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah gives the priests the order to sacrifice the burnt offering on the altar. The priests sacrifice the seven bulls, seven rams, and seven male lambs on the altar, as prescribed by the Law of Moses. As the priests begin the burnt offering, the musicians also begin singing to Yahweh. The priests join the singing by blowing the trumpets, and the Levites join the singing by playing the musical instruments of David, king of Israel.

All the people there bow in worship while the musicians play and the priests blow the trumpets. The people continue doing all these things until the priests have finished burning the offerings. When the sacrifice of burnt offerings is finished, King Hezekiah and everyone present with Hezekiah kneel down and worship Yahweh.

King Hezekiah and his officials order the Levites to sing praise songs to Yahweh with the words of David and the seer or prophet Asaph. So the Levites sing praise songs joyfully and bow down and worship Yahweh.

King David and the prophet Asaph were the authors of many of the Psalms, so it is probable that "the words of David and Asaph" refers to the songs that David and Asaph composed. Asaph was the Levite whom David appointed to oversee the musical parts of the worship in the temple.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 29:18–30 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has five scenes.

**In the first scene:** The priests and the Levites go to King Hezekiah and tell King Hezekiah that they have purified the entire temple.

**In the second scene:** Early the next morning, King Hezekiah gathers officials of the city and goes to Yahweh's temple with animals for a sin offering.

**In the third scene:** King Hezekiah commands the priests to offer the animals on the altar. The priests kill the bulls, rams, and lambs, and throw the blood on the altar. Then the priests kill the male goats and offer the male goats as sin offerings to make atonement for all the people of Israel.

**In the fourth scene:** King Hezekiah positions the Levites in the temple with different musical instruments. King Hezekiah commands the priests to sacrifice the burnt offerings on the altar. While the priests sacrifice the burnt offerings, the musicians begin to sing and play the musical instruments. The people bow in worship to Yahweh.

**In the fifth scene:** When the offerings finish burning, King Hezekiah and everyone present with Hezekiah kneel down and worship Yahweh. Then, King Hezekiah and his officials order the Levites to sing praise songs to Yahweh.

The characters in this passage are:

* The priests
* The Levites
* King Hezekiah
* The city officials
* The people who gathered at the temple of Yahweh
* And the musicians

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In scene one, the priests and the Levites go to King Hezekiah and report that they have purified the entire temple of Yahweh, the altar of burnt offering with all its utensils, and the table for setting out the bread of the presence, with all the equipment associated with it. The priests and Levites had made holy all the things that King Ahaz removed from the temple when King Ahaz was unfaithful to Yahweh, or betrayed Yahweh. All the actions in scene one probably happened at King Hezekiah's palace.

In scene two, King Hezekiah gathers the city officials together and goes up to Yahweh's temple early the next morning. King Hezekiah and the officials bring seven bulls, seven rams, seven male lambs, and seven male goats as a sin or purification offering for the kingdom, for the sanctuary or temple, and for Judah. In this context, the kingdom refers to the royal family, while Judah refers to the rest of the people in the kingdom of Judah. When the storyteller says "the sanctuary," the storyteller is referring to the temple and the people who serve in the temple.

Stop and discuss: How will you translate each of these groups of people so that people in your culture understand who the storyteller is referring to? Pause this audio here.

The number seven is a special number in many cultures of this area, including the people of Judah. The number seven is also an important number in the Bible as a symbol of completeness or fullness. By offering seven of each animal, King Hezekiah is emphasizing the importance of the sacrifice.

In scene three, King Hezekiah commands the priests, who are the descendants of Aaron, to offer the animals on the altar of Yahweh. In the original language, it is not clear who killed the bulls. It may be the priests, or it may be King Hezekiah and his officials. It may also be King Hezekiah, his officials, and the priests. However, according to the instructions Yahweh gave to Moses, the people making the burnt offering were usually the ones who actually slaughtered the animals. So, it is most likely that King Hezekiah and his officials killed the bulls. The priests take the blood from the slaughtered bulls and splash the blood against the altar. Next, the priests slaughter the rams and splash the rams' blood against the altar. Then, the priests slaughter the lambs and splash the lambs' blood against the altar.

The priests then bring the goats for the sin or purification offering before King Hezekiah and the people there, and King Hezekiah and the people lay their hands on the goats. Then, the priests kill the goats and present the blood of the goats as a sin or purification offering on the altar to make atonement for all Israel, because King Hezekiah has commanded the priests to make the burnt offering and the sin or purification offering for all the people of Israel.

Stop here and discuss as a group: If you have already translated Leviticus into your language, listen to your translation of Leviticus 4\. Discuss how you will translate this section about the sin or purification offering. Pause this audio here.

After the priests kill the animals, the storyteller repeats King Hezekiah's command to emphasize that the king commanded the priests to make burnt offerings and sin or purification offerings for all of Israel.

In scene four, King Hezekiah positions the Levites in the temple of Yahweh with cymbals, harps, and lyres, in the way David and Gad, King David's seer, and Nathan the prophet had ordered many years before. A seer is another word for "prophet." People called Gad King David's seer, because Gad served in David's royal court as an advisor. Here, the storyteller explains that Yahweh, through Yahweh's prophets, commanded the Israelites to have Levites with musical instruments in Yahweh's temple. Yahweh commanded the Israelites to do so about 300 years ago, during the time of King David.

Then, the Levites stand ready with the instruments like those King David had used, and the priests stand with their trumpets. Hezekiah gives the priests the order to sacrifice the burnt offering on the altar. As the priests begin offering, the musicians also begin singing to Yahweh. The priests join the singing by blowing the trumpets, and the Levites join the singing by playing the musical instruments like those King David used.

Stop here and discuss as a group: Tell a story in which two events happened at the same time. In your language, how do people usually talk about two events that occur at the same time? Discuss how you will tell that the musical worship happened at the same time as the sacrifices. Pause this audio here.

All the people there bow in worship, while the musicians play and the priests blow the trumpets. The people continue doing all these things until the priests have finished burning the offerings.

Stop here and discuss as a group: In your culture, how do people use music in worship? When does music accompany worship rituals and activities? Pause this audio here.

In the fifth scene, when the offerings finish burning, King Hezekiah and everyone present with Hezekiah kneel down and worship Yahweh. King Hezekiah and his officials order the Levites to sing praise songs to Yahweh with the words of David and the seer Asaph. So the Levites sing praise songs joyfully and bow down and worship Yahweh.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 29:18–30 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has five scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* The priests
* The Levites
* King Hezekiah
* The city officials
* The people who gathered at the temple of Yahweh
* And the musicians

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

All the actions in scene one probably happened at King Hezekiah's palace.

The priests and the Levites go to King Hezekiah and report that they have purified the entire temple of Yahweh, the altar of burnt offering with all its utensils, and the table for setting out the bread of the presence, with all the equipment associated with it.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing the priest, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am very tired but excited to let King Hezekiah know what we have accomplished; or
* I am looking forward to leading the worship service at the temple of Yahweh soon; or
* I will need to study all the laws for offering sacrifices so that I do not miss any steps.

> Continue the drama.

King Hezekiah gathers the city officials together and goes up to Yahweh's temple early the next morning. King Hezekiah and the officials bring seven bulls, seven rams, seven male lambs, and seven male goats as a sin offering for the royal family of Judah, for Yahweh's temple, and for the rest of the kingdom.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing one of the city officials, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am very happy to get to serve a leader like King Hezekiah; or
* I have not worshipped Yahweh in many years. I am very excited now; or
* I hope Yahweh will accept our offerings and forgive our sins.

> Continue the drama.

King Hezekiah commands the priests, the descendants of Aaron, to offer the animals on the altar of Yahweh. So the people slaughter the bulls, and the priests take the blood and splash the blood against the altar. Next, the priests slaughter the rams and splash the rams' blood against the altar. Then the priests slaughter the lambs and splash the lambs' blood against the altar.

The priests then bring the goats for the sin offering before the king and the people there, and the king and the people lay their hands on the goats. Then the priests kill the goats and present the blood of the goats as a sin offering on the altar to make atonement for all Israel because King Hezekiah commanded the priests to make the burnt offering and the sin offering for all the people of Israel.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing one of the priests, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* King Hezekiah and the people are serious about making atonement for their sins. They brought seven of each kind of animal; or
* I am grateful that King Hezekiah is helping us return to the proper worship of Yahweh.

Ask the person playing one of the people gathered at the temple, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am happy that we now have a restored relationship with Yahweh; or
* I am so excited that the priests have made these offerings for us. Now Yahweh has forgiven our sins. It has been a long time!

> Continue the drama.

King Hezekiah positions the Levites in the temple of Yahweh with cymbals, harps, and lyres in the way David and Gad, who was king David's seer and Nathan the prophet, prescribed. Yahweh commanded the Israelites to do so through Yahweh's prophets. So the Levites stand ready with musical instruments like those David used, and the priests stand with their trumpets.

Hezekiah commands the priests to sacrifice the burnt offering on the altar. As the priests begin offering, the musicians also begin singing to Yahweh. The priests join the singing by blowing the trumpets and the Levites join the singing by playing the musical instruments of David, king of Israel.

All the people there bow in worship, while the musicians play and the priests blow the trumpets. The people continue doing all these things until the priests have finished burning the offerings. When the offerings finish burning, King Hezekiah and everyone present with the king kneel down and worship Yahweh.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing a Levite who plays a musical instrument, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel peace and joy when I play music to worship Yahweh; or
* I am very happy to use my skill as a musician during the worship service; or
* Yahweh is worthy of our worship and praise. I cannot help but play my instrument for Yahweh.

> Continue the drama.

King Hezekiah and his officials order the Levites to sing praise songs to Yahweh with the words of David and the seer or the prophet Asaph. So the Levites sing praise songs joyfully and bow down and worship Yahweh.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 29:18–30 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

The **priests** and the **Levites** go to **King** Hezekiah and report that they have **purified** the entire **temple** of **Yahweh**, the **altar** of burnt offering with all its utensils, and the table for setting out the **bread of the presence**, with all the utensils associated with it. Use the same words for priests, Levites, King, purify, temple, Yahweh, altar, and bread of the presence as you used in the previous passages. For more information on priests, Levites, king, purify, temple, Yahweh, altar, and "bread of the presence," refer to the Master Glossary.

A **burnt offering** is a form of sacrifice that people bring to Yahweh. When a person brings Yahweh a burnt offering, that person kills an animal and burns this animal completely on an altar. When a person brings Yahweh a burnt offering, that person is saying to Yahweh, "Just as I am completely burning this animal, I am completely devoted to you." Use the same word or phrase for burnt offering as you used in previous passages. For more information on burnt offering, refer to the Master Glossary.

The priests said that they **consecrated** all of the utensils that King Ahaz had taken out of the temple. When you consecrate someone, you set the person apart for special service to Yahweh. You could also consecrate an object to do a special task for Yahweh. Use the same word or phrase for consecrate as you used in previous passages. For more information on consecrate, refer to the Master Glossary.

King Hezekiah and the officials bring seven bulls, seven rams, seven lambs, and seven male goats as a sin or purification offering for the "kingdom" or royal family of Judah, for Yahweh's temple, and for the rest of the kingdom of Judah. People also called the sin offering a purification offering or a purification sacrifice because the purpose of this offering was to purify someone who had become unclean or unfit to serve Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **sin or purification offering**. Look up "sin or purification offering" in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

If you use the term for "**kingdom**" instead of royal family, refer to kingdom in the Master Glossary for more information and translate kingdom in the same way you have previously. Use the same word for important **officials** and **Judah** as you have used previously, and refer to Judah in the Master Glossary for more information.

Use the same word or phrase for **slaughter**, or kill an animal, as you have used in previous passages.

Later, the priests bring the goats to the **assembly**, or gathering of people, and they kill the goats and present the blood of the goats as a sin offering on the altar to make atonement for all Israel. The people had broken their relationship with Yahweh because of the sin the people committed. By making atonement, the people restore their relationship with Yahweh, and Yahweh cleanses the people of their sins. Use the same word or phrase for assembly, or "special gathering of people," as you have used previously.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **atonement**. Look up atonement in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

King Hezekiah positions the Levites in the temple of Yahweh with **cymbals**, **harps**, and **lyres** in the way David and Gad, who was king David's **seer** and Nathan the **prophet**, prescribed. A prophet is a person who gives messages from Yahweh to the people. A seer is another word for a prophet. People called Gad King David's seer because Gad served as an advisor in David's royal court. Use the same words or phrases for cymbals, harps, lyres, prophet, and seer as you used in previous passages. For more information on prophet, refer to the Master Glossary.

Later, all the people at the temple of Yahweh **bow** in **worship** while the musicians play and the priests blow the **trumpets**. In the original language, the storyteller simply says "bow down," meaning that the people worshiped. When a person bows down before someone else, that person lowers their head or the whole top of their body forward. Sometimes people even stretched themselves out flat on the ground in front of a person. People do this to show respect and humility. When a person worships Yahweh, that person gives honor to Yahweh. That person is saying that Yahweh deserves all of that person's thanks, praise, and respect. Worship is not just performing rituals for Yahweh; it is about having a heart of awe and wonder towards Yahweh. Use the same words for trumpets, bow, and worship as you used in previous passages. For more information on bow and worship, refer to the Master Glossary.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 29:18-30

## 2 Chronicles 29:31–36 (id: eng-2ch-p54b-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 29:31–36 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 29:31–36 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 29:31–36 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous passage, King Hezekiah commanded the priests to offer burnt offerings and sin or purification offerings for the royal family, the temple, and the people of Judah. Through these offerings, the people made atonement for their sin. The people bowed in worship while the priests made the offerings, and the Levites praised Yahweh with instruments and singing.

Now, Hezekiah says to the people that since the people have consecrated themselves to Yahweh, the people should come and bring sacrifices and thank offerings to Yahweh's temple. So the people there bring sacrifices and thank offerings. Sacrifices are offerings of sheep, goats, or cattle that people give to worship Yahweh, confirm their relationship with Yahweh, or ask forgiveness for something people have done wrong. A sacrifice could involve the killing of an animal, and then the priest would burn some parts of the animal on the altar. Thank offerings are sacrifices that people give to thank Yahweh for answered prayer.

Some people have a willing heart and make a voluntary sacrifice of burnt offerings. The people who brought voluntary burnt offerings were considered generous because the priests burned all the meat from those offerings on the altar. You will remember that for other sacrifices and thank offerings, the person who gave the offering ate what the priests did not burn.

Stop here and discuss as a group: Tell a story about someone who did a good thing they did not have to do, or about someone who did more than was expected. Pay attention to how the storyteller describes this kind of person. What do people in your culture think about this kind of voluntary action? Pause this audio here.

The people bring 70 bulls, 100 rams, and 200 male lambs as burnt offerings. All these animals are for burnt offerings to Yahweh. When a person brings Yahweh a burnt offering, that person kills an animal, and the priest burns this animal completely on the altar. The people bring 600 bulls and 3,000 sheep and goats as consecrated or holy sacrifices.

However, there are not enough priests who are ritually clean to skin all of the animals for burnt offerings. So the Levites help the priests finish skinning the animals until other priests have consecrated themselves. Levites have done better at keeping themselves pure for Yahweh than the priests have.

According to the instructions Yahweh gave to Moses, the people who brought the sacrifices were the ones who had to skin the animals, not the priests. There were so many offerings this day that there was not enough space in the temple courtyard for all the people to skin the animals. This is probably the reason why the Levites helped the priests finish skinning the animals.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your culture, what do people usually do to purify themselves to worship God, gods, or the spirits? Pause this audio here.

There is a large number of burnt offerings, together with the fat of peace or fellowship offerings and the drink offerings that go with the burnt offerings. In the peace or fellowship offerings, the priests burned some of the offering on the altar, and the priests and the persons making the offering ate the rest of the offering. The purpose of peace or fellowship offerings was to establish or maintain good relations with Yahweh. The fat of the peace or fellowship offerings refers to the fatty animal parts of these offerings. Drink offerings are gifts of wine that priests pour onto the altar along with the other offerings. Wine is fermented grape juice.

So, the people begin to worship Yahweh again in the temple. Then Hezekiah rejoices along with all of Yahweh's people because Yahweh has helped them to renew worship in the temple very quickly.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 29:31–36 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has one scene.

**In the scene:** Hezekiah says to the people that they should bring sacrifices and thank offerings to Yahweh's temple. So the people do as Hezekiah says. The Levites help the priests skin the animals. Then, Hezekiah rejoices along with all of Yahweh's people.

The characters in this passage are:

* The priests
* The Levites
* King Hezekiah
* The city officials who are probably still there, although the storyteller does not mention them
* The people who gathered at Yahweh's temple
* And the musicians who are probably still there, although the storyteller does not mention them

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

Hezekiah says to the people that since they have now consecrated themselves to Yahweh, the people should come and bring sacrifices and thank offerings to Yahweh's temple. So the people there bring sacrifices and thank offerings, and all who are of a willing heart also bring burnt offerings. The sacrifices and thank offerings required that the priests burn only part of the meat. The people who made the offerings ate what the priests did not burn. But the burnt offerings indicated a special generosity since the priests burned all of the meat on the altar. Offerings that people made "with a willing heart" refer to sacrifices that people made because they wanted to, not because Yahweh required the people to. In other words, the storyteller is saying that the people who brought burnt offerings were generous.

Stop here and discuss as a group: Think back to your discussion about people who do more than expected. How will you translate the idea that some people gave from a willing heart? Pause this audio here.

The story action stops briefly while the storyteller shares the total number of animals the assembly, or group of people, gave as burnt offerings and sacrifices. The number of burnt offerings the people bring is 70 bulls, 100 rams, and 200 male lambs. The number of animals the people "consecrated," or set apart as sacrifices, is 600 bulls and 3,000 sheep and goats.

You may remember that according to the instructions Yahweh gave to Moses, the people who brought the sacrifices were the ones who had to skin the animals, not the priests. There were so many offerings this day that there probably was not enough space in the temple courtyard for all the people to skin the animals. The storyteller continues and notes that there are not enough priests to skin all the animals for burnt offerings. So the Levites help the priests finish skinning the animals until other priests have consecrated themselves.

The Levites helped the priests because the Levites had done better at keeping themselves pure for Yahweh than the priests had. In the original language, the storyteller says that the Levites are more "upright in heart" than the priests. This is special language that people used to describe obedience. The Levites were more faithful and more diligent to keep themselves ritually pure than the priests were.

Stop here and discuss as a group: How do people in your culture describe someone who is faithful and diligent? How will you translate the idea that the Levites were more "upright in heart"? Pause this audio here

In addition to the large number of burnt offerings, there was the fat of the peace or fellowship offerings and the drink offerings that went along with the burnt offerings. The priests were very busy with the added work responsibilities of these offerings.

So, the people began to worship Yahweh again in the temple. Then Hezekiah and all the people rejoiced because God had helped them do all these things very quickly.

King Hezekiah rejoiced because Yahweh helped King Hezekiah, the priests, the Levites, and the Israelites to restart or renew worship in the temple in the first month of the first year that Hezekiah was king. That is why the storyteller said that all the things happened very quickly.

Stop here and discuss as a group: How do people in your culture express their joy when they are happy about something? Pause this audio here.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 29:31–36 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has one scene.

The characters in this passage are:

* The priests
* The Levites
* King Hezekiah
* The city officials who are probably still there, although the storyteller does not mention them
* The people who gathered at Yahweh's temple
* And the musicians who are probably still there, although the storyteller does not mention them

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

Hezekiah says to the people, "You have now consecrated yourselves to Yahweh. Come and bring sacrifices and thank offerings to the temple of Yahweh." So the people there bring sacrifices and thank offerings, and all who are of a willing heart also bring burnt offerings.

The number of burnt offerings the people bring is 70 bulls, 100 rams, and 200 male lambs. All these animals are for burnt offerings to Yahweh. The number of animals people set apart as sacrifices is 600 bulls and 3,000 sheep and goats.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing one of the people gathered at the temple, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am so happy to be able to worship Yahweh like this again. I am going to bring a special burnt offering to thank Yahweh; or
* This is so exciting. I have never seen so many people in the temple at one time; or
* There sure are a lot of animals. I hope we can get to my sacrifice today.

Continue the drama.

However, there are not enough priests to skin all of the animals for burnt offerings. So the Levites help the priests finish skinning the animals until other priests have consecrated themselves. This is because the Levites have done better at keeping themselves pure for Yahweh than the priests have.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing one of the Levites, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am very proud to be able to help the people sacrifice and worship Yahweh; or
* I am surprised because I have never seen this many animals in one place before; or
* I probably will not be able to smell or eat meat for about a month after this.

Continue the drama.

In addition to the large number of burnt offerings, there was the fat of the peace offerings and the drink offerings that went along with the burnt offerings.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing one of the priests, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* There sure is a lot of work to do. I am glad the Levites are helping us skin the burnt offerings; or
* I am so happy to be doing things properly again in the temple; or
* I wish the other priests had been more faithful in keeping themselves ritually clean. We could have used those priests' help today.

Continue the drama.

So, the people began to worship Yahweh again in the temple. Then, Hezekiah and the people rejoiced because God had helped them to do all these things very quickly.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am very happy because I get to be the leader who restarts the worship at Yahweh's temple; or
* I am very thankful to Yahweh, who helped us to be able to worship at the temple again; or
* I am very hopeful that Yahweh will forgive our sins and our ancestors' sins.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing one of the worshippers, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I cannot believe that we are worshiping Yahweh at the temple again; or
* I am happy and sad at the same time; or
* I am happy because I can worship Yahweh together with my fellow Israelites.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 29:31–36 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

**King** Hezekiah says to the people, "You have now **consecrated** yourselves to **Yahweh**. Come and bring **sacrifices** and **thank offerings** to the **temple**, the **house of Yahweh**." A thank offering is a type of freewill offering, or offering that people bring because they want to. Use the same words for king, consecrate, Yahweh, sacrifice, thank offering, and temple or "house of Yahweh" that you have used in previous passages. For more information, refer to king, consecrate, Yahweh, sacrifice, freewill offering, temple, and **house of God** in the Master Glossary.

The number of **burnt offerings** that the "**assembly**," or group of people, brings is 70 bulls, 100 rams, and 200 male lambs. Use the same words or phrases for assembly and for burnt offering as you used in previous passages. For more information on burnt offering, refer to the Master Glossary.

However, there are not enough **priests** to skin all of the animals for burnt offerings. So the **Levites** help the priests finish skinning the animals until other priests have consecrated themselves. Use the same words for priests and Levites that you have used in previous passages. Remember that these words are in the Master Glossary.

In addition to the large number of burnt offerings, there was the fat of the **peace or fellowship offerings** and the **drink offerings** that went along with the burnt offerings. A peace or fellowship offering is a sacrifice someone makes to have a better relationship with Yahweh. In the peace offerings, the priests burned some of the offering on the altar, and the priests and the persons who made the offering ate the rest of the offering. The fat of the peace offerings refers to the fatty animal parts of these fellowship offerings. Use the same word or phrase for "peace offering" that you have used in previous passages. For more information on peace offering, refer to "peace or fellowship offering" in the Master Glossary.

Drink offerings are gifts of wine that priests pour onto the altar along with the other offerings. Wine is fermented grape juice.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **drink offering**. Look up drink offering in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah and the people rejoice because **God** has helped them restore the service of the temple. Use the same word for God as you have used previously. Refer to God in the Master Glossary for more information.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 29:31-36

## 2 Chronicles 30:1–12 (id: eng-2ch-p55-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 30:1–12 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 30:1–12 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 30:1–12 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous story, King Hezekiah, Hezekiah's officials, priests, Levites, and the people started the worship service again at Yahweh's temple. The events in this story happen some time later, but during King Hezekiah's reign. King Hezekiah and his officials invite other Israelites who live in the land of Israel, in the land of Judah, and other regions to come to Yahweh's temple to celebrate the Passover festival and to come back to Yahweh.

King Hezekiah sends a message to all the people in the land of Israel and in the land of Judah, even though King Hezekiah is king in the kingdom of Judah only. King Hezekiah also writes letters to the people in the territories of the tribes of Ephraim and Manasseh. In King Hezekiah's message, King Hezekiah invites all the Israelites to come to Yahweh's temple in Jerusalem and celebrate the Passover to honor Yahweh, the God of Israel.

You will remember that Passover is an important Jewish feast that begins the Festival, or Feast, of Unleavened Bread. During Passover, Jewish people celebrate the time Yahweh rescued the Israelites from Egypt during Moses' time. Yahweh commanded the Israelites through Moses to celebrate the Passover on the 14th day of the month of Nisan, the first month in the Hebrew calendar. If a person is ritually unclean at the time of Passover, that person could celebrate Passover in the second month.

During this year, the people could not celebrate the Passover in the first month because many priests were still ritually unclean and unable to serve in the temple. Also, the people of Judah had not gathered in Jerusalem for the festival. So, King Hezekiah, Hezekiah's officials, and the people gathered in Jerusalem decided to celebrate the Passover in the second month, as Yahweh allows in the Law of Moses. They decided to send a proclamation or announcement throughout all the land of Israel, from the city of Beersheba in the far south to the city of Dan in the far north.

Stop here and look at a map of Judah and Israel during Hezekiah's time. Locate the cities of Jerusalem, Beersheba, and Dan. Pause this audio here.

The people have not celebrated the Passover festival in great numbers, or according to the law that Yahweh gave to Moses. The last time the Israelites celebrated the Passover festival was probably during King Solomon's time. So, King Hezekiah sent messengers who carried letters from King Hezekiah and the princes or officials. These messengers went everywhere in the land of Judah and Israel.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your culture, what are the things a leader usually does when the leader wants to invite others to do something? How does the leader deliver messages to many people in many different places? Pause this audio here.

King Hezekiah's message urges the Israelites to return to Yahweh, the God of Abraham, Isaac, and "Israel," who we also know as Jacob. If the Israelites return to Yahweh, Yahweh might return to the few Israelites who are left, those who have escaped or survived the attacks by the kings of Assyria. King Hezekiah is speaking to the Israelites, whom the kings of Assyria did not kill or take as prisoners when the kings of the Assyrian Empire conquered the northern kingdom of Israel in 721 B.C. King Hezekiah himself is also one of the people the kings of Assyria did not kill.

When Hezekiah tells the people to "return to Yahweh," Hezekiah is telling the Israelites to repent, stop chasing after other gods, and return to worship Yahweh once again.

Hezekiah tells the Israelites that they should not be like their fathers and their brothers, who were unfaithful to Yahweh, the God of the Israelites' fathers. Because the Israelites' fathers and brothers were unfaithful to Yahweh, Yahweh made the Israelites an object of horror. King Hezekiah is saying that Yahweh brought such a terrible punishment on the Israelites that the people from surrounding nations were shocked when they saw how God punished the Israelites. The people of Israel had seen or had known very well about Yahweh's punishment.

Hezekiah tells the Israelites not to be "stiff\-necked," as their fathers were. Hezekiah uses a common phrase to describe the Israelites' ancestors as stubborn and rebellious. Hezekiah tells the Israelites to obey Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Tell a story about someone who was very stubborn and refused to do what he was supposed to do. Pay attention to the ways the storyteller describes this stubborn person. Pause this audio here.

Then, Hezekiah urges the Israelites to come to Yahweh's sanctuary, the temple which Yahweh has set apart for worship. Hezekiah tells the Israelites to serve or worship Yahweh, their God, so that Yahweh's fierce anger will turn away from the Israelites. Hezekiah tells the Israelites that if they come back to Yahweh, the Assyrian captors will show compassion to their Israelite brothers and their children and allow them to return to Israel because Yahweh is kind and merciful.

Hezekiah tells the Israelites that Yahweh will not abandon the Israelites if they return to Yahweh. If the Israelites come back to Yahweh, Yahweh will welcome and not reject the Israelites.

The messengers go from town to town in the territories of the tribes of Ephraim and Manasseh, and as far north as the region where the tribe of Zebulun lives. Many people who live there laugh at and mock the messengers. However, some people from the tribes of Asher, Manasseh, and Zebulun humble themselves and go to Jerusalem. You will remember that these tribes all separated from Judah and Benjamin when the kingdom of Israel divided.

Stop here and look at a map of the territory of the tribes of Ephraim, Manasseh, Zebulun, and Asher during King Hezekiah's time. Pause this audio here.

The storyteller says that the "hand" of Yahweh, or Yahweh's power, is on the people of Judah and gives the people one heart, unified for one purpose: to obey King Hezekiah and his officials and follow Yahweh's instructions.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 30:1–12 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has three scenes.

**In the first scene:** The assembly of Israelites in Jerusalem has not been able to celebrate the Passover festival at the regular time. So, King Hezekiah, King Hezekiah's officials, and the people who have gathered in Jerusalem decide to celebrate the Passover festival in the second month. King Hezekiah sends messengers with a message to other Israelites who are in the land of Israel and Judah, urging the Israelites to come back to Yahweh.

**In the second scene:** The messengers go to all the places King Hezekiah commands the messengers to go and give King Hezekiah's message to the people who live in those places. Many people laugh at and mock the messengers.

**In the third scene:** Some people humble themselves and go to Jerusalem. Yahweh leads the people in the land of the tribe of Judah to be unified for one purpose.

The characters in this passage are:

* King Hezekiah
* King Hezekiah's officials
* The assembly in Jerusalem
* King Hezekiah's messengers
* People who live in the former kingdom of Israel, including people who live in the territory of the tribe of Ephraim, Manasseh, Asher, and Zebulun
* And people who live in the kingdom of Judah

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

The passage begins by saying that King Hezekiah writes a message to send to all of Israel and Judah, including Ephraim and Manasseh. Hezekiah tells the people that the people should come to Jerusalem and celebrate the Passover on the 14th day of the second month. The storyteller then says why King Hezekiah has decided to wait until the second month instead of celebrating Passover in the first month. The Israelites have not been able to celebrate the Passover festival in great numbers at the regular time because not enough priests have consecrated or purified themselves, and the people have not assembled in Jerusalem. So, King Hezekiah, King Hezekiah's officials, and the people who have gathered in Jerusalem decide to celebrate the Passover festival in the second month. Everyone in Jerusalem agrees that the plan to celebrate the Passover festival in the second month seems like the right thing to do.

\[If, in your language, you do not give information in this way, you may decide to reorder the passage and give the reason first. You would first tell how the people were not able to celebrate the Passover in the first month because the priests had not consecrated themselves, and not many people came to Jerusalem in time to celebrate. Then give King Hezekiah's decision to wait until the second month.]{.mark}

Stop here and discuss how you will tell the order of the events in this passage so that the people in your culture will understand clearly. Pause this audio here.

So, King Hezekiah and all the people decide to send an announcement throughout the land of Israel. People will take the announcement from the city of Beersheba in the far south to the city of Dan in the far north, meaning the entire territory of the southern kingdom of Judah and the northern kingdom of Israel.

Stop here and look at the map of Judah and Israel during Hezekiah's time. Locate the cities of Jerusalem, Beersheba, and Dan, if needed. Discuss as a group: How will you translate this so that people will understand that Hezekiah was sending the message to the entire country? Pause this audio here.

In this announcement, Hezekiah calls all the Israelites to come to Yahweh's temple in Jerusalem and celebrate the Passover. You will remember that the Passover is one of the festivals that Jewish law requires the people to travel to Jerusalem to celebrate. Israelites have not celebrated the Passover festival in large numbers according to the Law of Moses. The last time all the Israelites celebrated the Passover festival was probably during King Solomon's time.

Then King Hezekiah commands messengers to take letters from the king and his officials to the people who live throughout the land of Israel and Judah. In the original language, the word for these messengers is similar to the word "to run" and shows that these messengers were to take the message quickly and possibly run to each location. You can also translate this word as "runners."

Stop and discuss: In your community, how do you send messages quickly to many places? How will you translate the idea of this kind of messenger in your translation? Pause this audio here.

In King Hezekiah's letter, Hezekiah urges the Israelites to return to Yahweh, the God of Abraham, Isaac, and Israel, so that Yahweh might return to the Israelites who are left, who have escaped from the "hand," or power, of the kings of Assyria. "The hand of the kings of Assyria" refers to the Assyrian conquest of the northern kingdom of Israel. Here, Hezekiah is speaking to everyone who has escaped or survived the Assyrian army's attacks.

Hezekiah tells the Israelites that they should not be like their fathers and their brothers who were unfaithful to Yahweh, the God of their fathers. Hezekiah is not speaking about the Israelites' actual fathers and brothers, but about the Israelites' ancestors. Hezekiah says that the Israelites' ancestors were unfaithful to Yahweh because they disobeyed and rebelled against Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your language, how would you say that someone is unfaithful to God, gods, or spirits? Pause this audio here.

Yahweh made the Israelites' fathers and brothers an object of horror, as the people of Israel have seen. Hezekiah is possibly referring to two things. First, Hezekiah is saying that Yahweh severely punished the disobedience of the Israelites' ancestors. Secondly, Yahweh's punishment was so severe that the people who saw it were very surprised. The people of Israel have seen how horrible their punishment is, and so have the other nations.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your language, how would you say that Yahweh made the ancestors and the relatives of the Israelites an object of horror? Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah tells the Israelites that they should not be "stiff\-necked" or stubborn as their fathers were, but the people of Israel should submit to Yahweh. The term "stiff\-necked" is special language. You will remember that in the story of the Exodus, Yahweh frequently described the Israelites as stiff\-necked. The original meaning of the term "stiff\-necked" probably comes from a farming expression. Farm animals wore yokes on their necks, and animals that stiffened their necks were stubbornly refusing to accept the yoke.

Stop here and remember your discussion from step two. How did you describe a very stubborn person, and how will you describe "stiff\-necked" people in this passage? Pause this audio here.

Then, Hezekiah invites the people of Israel to come to Yahweh's sanctuary or temple, which Yahweh has consecrated, or made holy, forever. Hezekiah tells the people of Israel to serve and worship Yahweh, their God, so that Yahweh's fierce anger will turn away from the people. Hezekiah says that if the Israelites come back to Yahweh, the Israelites' captors, the Assyrians, will show compassion to their brothers and their children, and allow their brothers and children to return to the land of the Israelites.

Yahweh, the Israelites' God, is gracious and compassionate. Yahweh will not turn Yahweh's face from the Israelites if the Israelites return to Yahweh. Here, Hezekiah is using special language. In the Old Testament, people often used the image of Yahweh turning his face toward or away from a person to mean that Yahweh was either accepting or rejecting someone. Hezekiah said that if the Israelites return to Yahweh, Yahweh will accept the Israelites rather than reject them.

Stop here and discuss: How will you translate the idea that Yahweh will accept and not reject the people if they return to Yahweh? Pause this audio here.

In scene two, the messengers go from town to town in the territory of the tribe of Ephraim and in the territory of the tribe of Manasseh, as far as the region where the tribe of Zebulun lives. But people who live in those places laugh at and mock the messengers.

Stop here and, as a group, look at a map that includes all the places the messengers went. Pause this audio here.

In scene three, the storyteller says that even though some people mock the messengers, some people from the tribes of Asher, Manasseh, and Zebulun humble themselves and go to Jerusalem. The storyteller begins the next idea by showing that just as Yahweh was at work among the people of the tribes of Asher, Manasseh, and Zebulun in the north, so was Yahweh at work among the people of Judah in the south. The storyteller says that the hand of Yahweh is also on the people in the land of Judah. The "hand of Yahweh is on the people" is special language, meaning that Yahweh blesses or guides the people. Yahweh blesses the people with one heart, which shows that the people were unified in their purpose. The people were unified in their obedience to what King Hezekiah and his officials had ordered and followed the word, or message, of Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your language, how will you describe the idea that Yahweh blesses or guides the people to be unified in purpose? Pause this audio here.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 30:1–12 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has three scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* King Hezekiah
* King Hezekiah's officials
* The assembly in Jerusalem
* King Hezekiah's messengers
* People who live in the former kingdom of Israel, including people who live in the territory of the tribe of Ephraim, Manasseh, Asher, and Zebulun
* And people who live in the kingdom of Judah

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

The Israelites have not been able to celebrate the Passover festival in great numbers at the regular time, according to the Law of Moses, because not enough priests have consecrated themselves, and the people have not assembled in Jerusalem. So, Hezekiah and his officials and the whole assembly in Jerusalem decide to celebrate the Passover festival in the second month. The plan seems right to both King Hezekiah and the whole assembly.

So, King Hezekiah and all the people decide to send an announcement throughout the land of Israel. People will take the announcement to all the cities, from Beersheba in the far south to Dan in the far north. King Hezekiah, his officials, and the assembly invite all the Israelites to come to Yahweh's temple in Jerusalem and celebrate the Passover festival to Yahweh, the God of Israel.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am looking forward to celebrate the Passover festival together will other Israelites for the first time during my rule to honor Yahweh; or
* I hope Yahweh will no longer be angry with us if many of us change our lifestyles and come to Jerusalem to celebrate the Passover festival to honor Yahweh at Yahweh's temple; or
* I hope spiritual revival will happen in every place where Israelites live.

> Continue the drama.

Then, King Hezekiah and his officials write letters to all the people in the land of Israel and people in the land of Judah, including the people in the territory of the tribe of Ephraim and in the territory of the tribe of Manasseh. King Hezekiah commands messengers to take these letters to the people who live throughout the land of Israel and Judah.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Hezekiah's messengers, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am very excited to give this invitation to other Israelites; or
* I am very happy because I will soon be able to celebrate the Passover festival with other Israelites; or
* I am a bit afraid because I do not know how other Israelites who are not under King Hezekiah's rule will respond to the invitation.

> Continue the drama.

In King Hezekiah's message, Hezekiah says: "People of Israel, return to Yahweh, the God of Abraham, Isaac, and Israel, so that Yahweh might return to the Israelites who are left, those of us who have escaped from the hand of Assyria's kings. Do not be like your ancestors and relatives who were unfaithful to Yahweh, the God of their fathers. Yahweh made your fathers and brothers an object of horror, as you yourselves have seen. Do not be stubborn like your ancestors were, but submit yourselves to Yahweh. Come to Yahweh's temple, the special place Yahweh has forever made holy and set apart. Worship and serve Yahweh your God so that Yahweh's fierce anger will turn away from you.

"If you return to Yahweh, then the Assyrian captors will show compassion on your brothers and children. The captors will allow your brothers and children to return to this land. Yahweh your God is gracious and merciful. Yahweh will not continue to turn his face from you and reject you if you return to Yahweh."

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing one of the people from the territory of the tribe of Ephraim, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I wonder what secret plan King Hezekiah has because King Hezekiah is not even my king; or
* I have my own idols to worship at home. Why should I spend money to go to Jerusalem to worship Yahweh? or
* I am very happy because I will soon be able to celebrate the Passover festival at Yahweh's temple for the first time in many years.

> Continue the drama.

The messengers go from town to town in the territories of the tribes of Ephraim and Manasseh, and as far as the region where the tribe of Zebulun lives. But most of the people who live in those places laugh at and mock the messengers. However, some people from the tribes of Asher, Manasseh, and Zebulun humble themselves and go to Jerusalem.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Hezekiah's messengers, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am very sad because many of my fellow Israelites do not want to obey Yahweh; or
* I am very hurt because I was expecting to receive a warm welcome as I am urging people to obey Yahweh; or
* Even though many people reject the invitation I have brought with me, I am happy that there are some Israelites who want to obey Yahweh.

Ask the person playing one of the people from the tribe of Asher, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am very sad because many of my fellow Israelites do not want to obey Yahweh; or
* King Hezekiah reminds me to obey Yahweh and not be like my ancestors; or
* Even though many people reject King Hezekiah's invitation, I am happy about this message and look forward to celebrating the Passover again.

> Continue the drama.

The storyteller says that Yahweh also blesses and guides the people of Judah. Yahweh gives the people one heart to obey what King Hezekiah and his officials have ordered, following the word of Yahweh.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing people who live in the kingdom of Judah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am thankful to Yahweh because I will soon be able to celebrate the Passover festival to honor Yahweh; or
* I am sad because many of my fellow Israelites do not want to celebrate the Passover festival as the Law of Moses requires; or
* I do not care what other Israelites do, but as for me, I will teach my family to worship and obey Yahweh by trying to be a good example.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 30:1–12 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

**King** Hezekiah sends a message to all the people in the land of **Israel** and in the land of **Judah**. King Hezekiah also writes letters to the people in the territories of the tribes of Ephraim and Manasseh. In King Hezekiah's message, Hezekiah invites the Israelites to come to the **temple** of **Yahweh** in **Jerusalem** and celebrate the Passover to honor Yahweh, the **God** of Israel. Use the same words or phrases for king, Israel, Judah, temple, Jerusalem, God, and Yahweh as you used in previous passages. For more information on king, Israel, temple, Jerusalem, God, and Yahweh, refer to the Master Glossary.

Passover is an important Jewish feast that begins the Festival, or Feast, of Unleavened Bread. During Passover, Jewish people celebrate the time Yahweh rescued the Israelites from Egypt during Moses' time. Yahweh commanded the Israelites through Moses to celebrate the Passover on the 14th day of the month of Nisan, the first month in the Hebrew calendar. If a person is ritually unclean at the time of Passover, that person could celebrate Passover in the second month.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **Passover**. Look up Passover in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

King Hezekiah and his high **officials**, or princes, and the whole **assembly** in Jerusalem decide to celebrate the Passover festival in the second month. The people have not been able to celebrate the Passover festival at the regular time because not enough **priests** have **consecrated** themselves, and the people have not assembled in Jerusalem. Priests were people who worked in Yahweh's temple in Jerusalem. When you consecrate someone, you set the person apart for special service to Yahweh. Use the same words or phrases for official, assembly, priest, and consecrate as you used in previous passages. For more information on priest and consecrate, refer to the Master Glossary.

King Hezekiah commands messengers or runners to take letters from Hezekiah and his officials to all the people living in Israel and Judah. In the original language, the word the storyteller used for these messengers is similar to the word "to run," which shows that these messengers were to take the message quickly and that they possibly ran to each location. You can also translate this word as "runners."

Stop here and discuss what word or phrase you will use for **messengers**. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

In King Hezekiah's letter, Hezekiah tells the Israelites not to be like their **fathers** and **brothers** who were faithless and stiff\-necked. When Hezekiah said "fathers and brothers," Hezekiah was referring to the Israelites' ancestors. Use the same words for fathers and brothers that you have used in previous passages for ancestors.

The Israelites' ancestors were "stiff\-necked" or stubborn, just like a farm animal that refused to be placed in a yoke.

Stop here and discuss what word or phrase you will use for **stiff\-necked**. If you have already translated this idea in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah tells the Israelites to submit to or obey Yahweh, come to Yahweh's **sanctuary**, which Yahweh has made holy forever, and serve Yahweh, their God. A sanctuary is a holy place—a place that is set apart for the worship of Yahweh. In this context, the word sanctuary refers to the temple of Yahweh, especially the central part of the temple. Use the same word for sanctuary that you have used in previous passages. For more information on sanctuary, refer to the Master Glossary.

Hezekiah says that if the Israelites come back to Yahweh, their captors, the Assyrians, will show compassion to the Israelites' brothers and their children, and allow their brothers and children to return to the Israelites' land. When someone has compassion for someone, that person has a deep affection or love for someone.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **compassion**. Look up compassion in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah tells the Israelites that Yahweh, their God, is gracious and merciful and will not turn away his face from the Israelites if the Israelites return to Yahweh. When someone is gracious to us or shows grace to us, that person does something good for us or gives us something good that we do not deserve.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **gracious** or **grace**. Look up grace in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

God is merciful. This means God is full of mercy; God wants to show mercy to people. The word mercy means goodness, kindness, or compassion. The meaning of mercy can be similar to the meaning of the word grace. Grace means that we receive something that we do not deserve. Grace and mercy go together. It is because of God's mercy that God does not punish us, and then it is because of God's grace that God also gives us good things.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **merciful** or **mercy**. Look up mercy in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

God's hand, or power, was on the people in Judah to give them one **heart** to follow the **word**, or message, of Yahweh\*\*.\*\* You will remember that in this culture, the people believed that the heart was the center of emotion and decision\-making. The word of Yahweh is the message or instruction from Yahweh. Use the same words or phrases for heart and "word of Yahweh" as you have used previously, and refer to heart and word in the Master Glossary for more information.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 30:1-12
