## 2 Chronicles 30:13–20 (id: eng-2ch-p56a-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 30:13–22 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 30:13–22 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 30:13–22 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous story, we learned that in the second month of the Jewish calendar, King Hezekiah sent invitations out from Jerusalem to the people living in the former kingdom of Israel and the kingdom of Judah to celebrate the Passover. The events in this story happen some time after King Hezekiah sent out those invitations. This story is about how the people celebrate the Passover and the Feast of Unleavened Bread in Jerusalem. The Israelites had not celebrated the Passover for many years, although the Law of Moses required the people to celebrate Passover every year.

The people respond to King Hezekiah's invitation. A very large crowd of people gathers in Jerusalem to celebrate the Festival of Unleavened Bread in the second month.

Stop here as a group and look at a map of Jerusalem. Pause this audio here.

The Feast of Unleavened Bread was an important Jewish festival. Jewish people celebrated the Feast of Unleavened Bread after the people celebrated Passover. Passover was a religious feast to celebrate when Yahweh rescued Israel from Egypt in Moses' time. You will remember that when the Israelites left Egypt, the Israelites carried bread dough that did not have yeast. We call the bread people make with this dough "unleavened bread." To remember these events, Moses told the Israelites to celebrate the Passover on the 14th day of the first month in the Hebrew calendar. Then, the people celebrated the Feast of Unleavened Bread from the 15th day to the 22nd day of the month. However, at this time, the Israelites gathered to celebrate the Festival of Unleavened Bread in the second month of the Hebrew calendar because the Israelites were not able to celebrate the festival in the first month.

The Israelites who gathered for the festival searched Jerusalem and removed every altar to foreign gods that the people found. Some altars were places where people made sacrifices, and others were places where people burned incense to these gods. The people destroyed all these altars and threw the pieces into the Kidron Valley. The Kidron Valley is to the east of Jerusalem between the Temple Mount and the Mount of Olives. The Kidron Valley is dry most of the year, but it has strong rushing water in the rainy season.

The people slaughter the Passover lambs on the 14th day of the second month. You will remember that Yahweh commanded the Israelites to remember the original Passover each year. Each family was to slaughter a Passover lamb, just as their ancestors had done during the first Passover.

Stop here and tell the story of the first Passover from Exodus. If you have already translated the book of Exodus into your language, listen to Exodus 12:1–30\. Pause this audio here.

The priests and the Levites are ashamed and consecrate themselves and bring burnt offerings to Yahweh's temple. The priests and Levites are probably ashamed that the people had to delay the Passover celebration because the priests and Levites were slow to purify themselves. The priests and the Levites had delayed the Passover from the first month to the second month.

Yahweh gave instructions through Moses that the priests and Levites had to consecrate themselves, or make themselves pure, before they could do their duty for Yahweh. This means that the priests and the Levites did not have sexual relations with their wives for a certain amount of time, and then the priests and the Levites had to take a special bath and put on fresh clothes. The priests and the Levites were ashamed that they had neglected their duty to Yahweh. The priests and Levites now consecrated themselves so they could perform their duties for the Passover, something the priests and Levites should have done long before.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your culture, how do people prepare themselves to be fit to worship God, gods, or the spirits? What is the process for religious leaders? What is the process for the normal worshipper? Pause this audio here.

Now that the priests and Levites have consecrated themselves to serve in the temple, the priests and Levites bring burnt offerings into the temple. The priests and Levites now take up their regular positions according to the Law of Moses, the man of God. You will remember that in the Law of Moses, Yahweh assigned the priests and the Levites to different duties and places in the tabernacle and temple. For the sacrifice of the Passover lamb, the Levites bring the blood to the priests. Then, the priests sprinkle the blood on the altar.

According to the Law of Moses, the heads of families were to kill the Passover lambs. Many of the ordinary people present for the Passover celebration had failed to go through the proper ritual cleansing that the Law of Moses required. So, the Levites have to kill the Passover lambs for all those who are not ceremonially clean and cannot consecrate, or make holy, their lambs to Yahweh.

Most of the people who have not purified themselves still eat the Passover, which is against the law. Many of these people come from the tribes of Ephraim, Manasseh, Issachar, and Zebulun.

Stop here as a group and look at a map of the land of the tribes of Ephraim, Manasseh, Issachar, and Zebulun during King Hezekiah's time. Pause this audio here.

King Hezekiah prays to Yahweh that Yahweh would forgive the people who sincerely desire to worship Yahweh, even though these people are not clean according to the sanctuary rules. Yahweh answers Hezekiah's prayer and heals the people. In other words, Yahweh forgives the people and does not punish the people.

The Israelites who are present in Jerusalem celebrate the Festival of Unleavened Bread for seven days with great rejoicing. The Levites and priests praise Yahweh every day, and they use musical instruments to help them worship.

Hezekiah encourages and thanks all the Levites for the way that they performed their service to Yahweh. For the seven days, the people celebrate and eat the proper food for the Festival of Unleavened Bread. The people offer fellowship or peace offerings and praise Yahweh, the God of their ancestors.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 30:13–22 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has five scenes.

**In the first scene:** A large crowd of people gathers in Jerusalem. Then, the people remove the altars for sacrifice and the incense altars.

**In the second scene:** The people slaughter the Passover lambs. The priests and the Levites consecrate themselves. The priests and the Levites take their positions in Yahweh's temple. The Levites kill the Passover lambs for the people who are not clean. People eat the Passover meal even though those people are not clean.

**In the third scene:** King Hezekiah prays for the people who sincerely desire to worship Yahweh. Yahweh forgives the people.

**In the fourth scene:** People in Jerusalem celebrate the festival for seven days while the Levites and priests praise Yahweh every day with musical instruments.

**In the fifth scene:** King Hezekiah encourages the Levites. The people eat the assigned food, offer fellowship offerings to Yahweh, and praise Yahweh.

The characters in this passage are:

* Israelites from the kingdom of Judah who have gathered in Jerusalem
* Israelites from the former northern kingdom of Israel who have gathered in Jerusalem
* The priests
* The Levites
* King Hezekiah
* And Yahweh

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In scene one, a very large crowd, or assembly, of people gathers in Jerusalem to celebrate the Festival of Unleavened Bread in the second month.

The people work hard together and remove the incense altars and other altars in Jerusalem and throw them into the Kidron Valley.

Stop here as a group and look at a map and a picture of the Kidron Valley. Pause this audio here.

In scene two, the people slaughter the Passover lambs on the 14th day of the second month. The priests and the Levites are ashamed and consecrate themselves, or make themselves holy and pure, and bring burnt offerings to Yahweh's temple.

Then, the priests and the Levites take up their regular positions according to the Law of Moses. The storyteller calls Moses "the man of God." The Levites bring the blood from the sacrificed animals to the priests. Then, the priests sprinkle or throw the blood. The storyteller does not say where the priests sprinkle the blood. In this context, the storyteller is most likely saying that the priests sprinkle or throw the blood on the altar.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your language, what word or phrase would you use for the word "sprinkle" or "throw"? If you have translated the book of Leviticus, use the same word for sprinkle or throw that you used in Leviticus 1:5\. Pause this audio here.

The storyteller tells us why the Levites are giving the blood from the sacrifices to the priests. The Levites were not usually the people who did this. According to the Law of Moses, the heads of families were to kill the Passover lambs and give the blood to the priests. But since many of the people were not ritually clean, the Levites had to kill the lambs for the people.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your language, how will you make it clear that it is not the normal practice for the Levites to kill the Passover lambs? Pause this audio here.

Many of the people attending the Passover have not consecrated themselves, so these people are ceremonially unclean. When the storyteller calls the people unclean, the storyteller is not saying the people are dirty on the outside and in need of a bath. In the Bible, a clean person is a person who is fit for service to God and able to participate in the worship of God. A person who is unclean is unfit to serve God. An unclean person cannot bring a sacrifice, because that sacrifice would also be unclean.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: Remind yourselves of how you have translated the ideas of people or objects being ceremonially clean and unclean in previous books of the Bible. Pause this audio here.

The storyteller uses a word to show that the opposite of what people expect to happen is happening. Most of the people who had not purified themselves or made themselves ritually clean still ate the Passover, which was against the law. According to the Law of Moses, people who have not purified themselves would not be able to participate in the Passover meal. Many of these people came from the tribes of Ephraim, Manasseh, Issachar, and Zebulun.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your language, what word or phrase would you use to show this kind of contrast, that even though people did not purify themselves, people ate the Passover meal? According to the law, we would expect these people not to participate in the Passover meal. Pause this audio here.

In scene three, because people who were not ritually clean ate the Passover, King Hezekiah prays to Yahweh for these people. King Hezekiah calls Yahweh good and the God of their ancestors. Hezekiah asks Yahweh to forgive everyone who sets that person's heart to seek Yahweh, the God of the Israelites' ancestors. Hezekiah says that these people have set their hearts to seek God, even though the people are not ritually clean according to the sanctuary or temple rules. Hezekiah is saying that the people have sincerely made up their minds and the people desire to worship Yahweh.

Stop here and do this activity as a group: Pretend you want to pray to Yahweh to forgive someone who disobeyed Yahweh. How would you say your prayer to show Yahweh that you care deeply about this person? Pause this audio here.

Stop here and discuss how you will translate King Hezekiah's prayer into your language. Pause this audio here.

And Yahweh hears Hezekiah and heals the people. In other words, Yahweh forgives the people and does not punish the people. Yahweh had said in Leviticus 15:31 that no one should come into the sanctuary unclean, or those people might even become sick. However, you will also remember that Yahweh promised King Solomon earlier in 2 Chronicles that if Yahweh's people humble themselves, pray, and seek Yahweh, then Yahweh will hear the people, forgive the people, and heal the people.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your language, how will you say that "Yahweh forgave the people" or "Yahweh did not harm the people"? Pause this audio here.

In scene four, the Israelites who are present in Jerusalem celebrate the Festival of Unleavened Bread for seven days with great rejoicing. The Levites and priests praise Yahweh every day.

Some scholars believe that the Levites and the priests sang with all their strength to Yahweh. Some believe that the Levites and priests played on loud musical instruments. Others believe that the Levites and priests sang and played on loud musical instruments.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: What version of this part of the passage have you heard before? Which version would people in your area be familiar with? Discuss how you want to translate this part. We recommend that you follow the accepted translation in your area. How will you describe how the Levites and priests praised Yahweh? Pause this audio here.

In scene five, King Hezekiah encourages and thanks all the Levites for their understanding of their service to Yahweh, or the way that the Levites performed their service to Yahweh. It is unclear exactly what Hezekiah is thanking the Levites for. It could be that the Levites did a good job when the Levites taught people about God, or that the Levites understand God and God's laws very well. It could be that Hezekiah appreciates how the Levites sang songs to God with skill and did a very good job leading worship at the temple.

The storyteller says that for the seven days, the people eat proper food for the Festival of Unleavened Bread and offer fellowship offerings and praise Yahweh, the God of their ancestors.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 30:13–22 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has five scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* Israelites from the kingdom of Judah who have gathered in Jerusalem
* Israelites from the former northern kingdom of Israel who have gathered in Jerusalem
* The priests
* The Levites
* King Hezekiah
* And Yahweh

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

A very large crowd of people gathers in Jerusalem to celebrate the Festival of Unleavened Bread in the second month. The people work together and remove the altars and incense altars to pagan gods in Jerusalem. The people throw these altars into the Kidron Valley.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing one of the people who removed the altars for other gods, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am very happy to get rid of these altars for other gods from our land; or
* I do not need to be afraid of King Ahaz, who built these altars; or
* I can feel my fellow Israelites' strong desire to obey Yahweh when I see that the people are getting rid of altars for other gods.

> Continue the drama.

The people slaughter the Passover lambs on the 14th day of the second month. The priests and the Levites are ashamed and consecrate themselves and bring burnt offerings to Yahweh's temple.

Then, the priests and the Levites take up their regular positions in the temple according to the Law of Moses, the man of God. The Levites bring the sacrificial blood to the priests. Then, the priests sprinkle the blood on the altar.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing one of the priests, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am very proud to do this for the people; or
* I am thankful to Yahweh for forgiving our sins; or
* This blood reminds me of the blood of lambs during the first Passover, when Yahweh rescued our ancestors from slavery in Egypt.

Ask the person playing one of the Levites, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am very proud that Yahweh asked us to help the priests; or
* I am very tired now; or
* I am very happy to be able to celebrate the Passover festival again.

> Continue the drama.

Many people in the crowd have not consecrated themselves. So, the Levites have to kill the Passover lambs for all those who are not clean and cannot consecrate their lambs to Yahweh.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing one of the people who did not consecrate themselves, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel very bad for not preparing myself for the festival; or
* I am very thankful for the Levites who are helping me to be able to celebrate the Festival of Unleavened Bread; or
* I am looking forward to worshiping Yahweh.

> Continue the drama.

Most of the many people who have come from the land of the tribes of Ephraim, Manasseh, Issachar, and Zebulun have not purified themselves, yet these people eat the Passover meal. This is against the Law of Moses.

So King Hezekiah prays to Yahweh for these people. Hezekiah says, "Yahweh, you are good, so I beg you to forgive everyone who sincerely wants to obey and worship you, the God of the people's ancestors. Forgive the people, even though these people are not ritually clean according to the rules of the temple." And Yahweh hears King Hezekiah and heals the people.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am afraid that Yahweh might punish many Israelites who did not consecrate themselves but are now eating the Passover meal; or
* I sincerely do not want Yahweh to punish anyone with death during this festival; or
* I am very thankful to Yahweh because Yahweh listened to my prayer.

Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am very pleased with King Hezekiah's attitude and actions; or
* I am very happy for the Israelites because the Israelites are worshipping me again; or
* I like King Hezekiah's prayer.

> Continue the drama.

The Israelites who are present in Jerusalem celebrate the Festival of Unleavened Bread for seven days with great rejoicing. The Levites and priests sing praise to Yahweh every day with loud instruments.

Hezekiah encourages all the Levites, thanking the Levites for their skill and their service to Yahweh. For the seven days, the people eat their assigned portion of the food for the Festival of Unleavened Bread, offer fellowship offerings, and praise Yahweh, the God of the people's ancestors.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 30:13–22 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

A very large crowd of people **assembled** in Jerusalem to celebrate the Festival of Unleavened Bread in the second month. Use the same word or phrase for assembly as you have used in previous passages.

The **Festival of Unleavened Bread** is an important Jewish festival that begins with the Passover Festival, or Feast. This feast reminded the people how Yahweh delivered their ancestors from slavery in Egypt. Use the same word or phrase for Festival of Unleavened Bread as you used in previous passages. For more information on "Festival of Unleavened Bread," refer to the Master Glossary.

The people work together and remove the **altars** in Jerusalem and clear away the **incense** altars. The people throw the altars into the Kidron Valley. An altar is a place where people make offerings or sacrifices to God. An altar is like a table or a platform that people make of earth or large stones. People placed gifts for God on this table. Often, this meant that people then burned the gift. The gift could be an animal, food items, or incense of sweet\-smelling spices. Use the same words or phrases for altar and incense as you used in previous passages. For more information on altar, refer to the Master Glossary.

The people **slaughter** the Passover lambs on the 14th day of the second month. The **priests** and the **Levites** are ashamed and **consecrate** themselves and bring **burnt offerings** to the **temple** of **Yahweh**. Use the same words or phrases for slaughter an animal, priests, Levites, consecrate, burnt offerings, temple, and Yahweh that you have used previously. For more information about priests, Levites, consecrate, burnt offerings, the temple, and Yahweh, refer to the Master Glossary.

Then, the priests and the Levites take up their regular positions in the temple, according to the **Law** of Moses, the man of **God**. The law is the set of instructions that God gave to the people of Israel. Use the same word or phrase for law and God as you used in previous passages. For more information on law and God, refer to the Master Glossary.

Many people in the crowd have not consecrated themselves. So, the Levites have to kill the Passover lambs for all those people who are not ceremonially clean and cannot consecrate their lambs to Yahweh. When the storyteller calls the people unclean, the storyteller is not saying the people are dirty on the outside and in need of a bath. In the Bible, a clean person is a person who is fit for service to God and able to participate in the worship of God. A person who is unclean is unfit to serve God. An unclean person cannot bring a sacrifice, because that sacrifice would also be unclean.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **clean**. Look up **clean and unclean** in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here.

Although most of the many people who have come from the land of the tribes of Ephraim, Manasseh, Issachar, and Zebulun have not purified themselves, yet these people eat the Passover meal. This is not according to the **sanctuary**'s rules of cleanness. A sanctuary is a holy place—a place that is set apart for the worship of God. In this context, the sanctuary is the temple. Use the same word or phrase for sanctuary as you used in previous passages. For more information on sanctuary, refer to the Master Glossary.

So, Hezekiah **prays** for the people who have not purified themselves that Yahweh would **forgive** everyone who sets his **heart** to seek Yahweh. And Yahweh hears Hezekiah and **heals** the people. Use the same words for pray, forgive, heart, and heal that you have used in previous passages. For more information about pray, forgiveness, heart, and heal, refer to the Master Glossary

The Israelites who are present in Jerusalem celebrate the Festival of Unleavened Bread for seven days with great rejoicing. The Levites and priests **praise** Yahweh every day. Use the same word for praise that you have used previously. For more information, refer to the Master Glossary.

For the seven days, the people eat their assigned portion of the food for the Festival of Unleavened Bread, and offer **peace or fellowship offerings** to Yahweh, the God of their ancestors. Use the same word or phrase for peace or fellowship offerings as you used in previous passages. For more information, refer to "peace or fellowship offering" in the Master Glossary.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 30:13-20

## 2 Chronicles 30:21–27 (id: eng-2ch-p56b-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 30:23–27 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 30:23–27 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 30:23–27 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous story, we learned about the celebration of the Passover festival and the Feast of Unleavened Bread in Jerusalem. This story is about how the assembly in Jerusalem celebrated for another seven days and how Yahweh heard his people.

After the people celebrated the Passover festival for one day and the Feast of Unleavened Bread for seven days, the whole assembly of Israelites decided to celebrate for seven more days. So the people continued the festival with great joy for another week. This was a big decision because the people had probably only prepared for a seven‑day celebration, but now the people were staying for an extra week.

Hezekiah, king of Judah, gives the people 1,000 bulls and 7,000 sheep and goats for the offerings. The officials provide the whole assembly with 1,000 bulls and 10,000 sheep and goats.

Stop here as a group and look at pictures of bulls, sheep, and goats if needed. Pause this audio here.

Many more priests consecrate themselves. The people were able to celebrate the Festival of Unleavened Bread for seven more days because King Hezekiah and his officials were very generous. These leaders gave the animals that people needed for the extra sacrifices. More priests were also ready to serve because many had set themselves apart and become ceremonially clean.

All the people of Judah were filled with joy. The priests, the Levites, and everyone who had come from the northern kingdom of Israel celebrated with the people. The assembly also included foreigners, or non\-Israelites, who lived in Israel and had come from Israel for the festival, as well as resident foreigners who lived in Judah. You may remember that Yahweh told Moses that if a foreigner was living among the Israelites and wanted to celebrate Passover to honor Yahweh, people had to circumcise all of the males in that household. After that, the Israelites were to treat the foreigner like someone born in Israel, and Yahweh allowed the foreigner to join the festival.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your culture, what do people think about foreigners or strangers? How do people behave toward foreigners? How do people treat a foreigner who worships God, gods, or spirits that your people worship? Pause this audio here.

People in Jerusalem were very happy. The people had not celebrated like this since the time of

King Solomon, son of David. King Solomon lived more than 250 years before King Hezekiah.

The priests and Levites stood before the people and blessed the people. You will remember that Yahweh gave Moses a blessing that the priests were to pray for the people. Just as Yahweh promised, God hears the prayers of blessing from the holy place where God lives in heaven.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your culture, what are the things a person usually does or says to bless others? If you have already translated the book of Numbers, listen to a recording of Numbers 6:22–27 in your language.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 30:23–27 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has two scenes.

**In the first scene:** The whole assembly agrees to celebrate the festival for seven more days. King Hezekiah and King Hezekiah's officials provide animals for the additional days of celebration.

**In the second scene:** Many more priests consecrate themselves. The Israelites and non\-Israelites in Jerusalem are very happy. The priests and Levites bless the people, and Yahweh hears their prayers.

The characters in this passage are:

* Israelites from the kingdom of Judah who have gathered in Jerusalem
* Israelites from the northern kingdom of Israel who have gathered in Jerusalem
* The priests
* The Levites
* King Hezekiah
* Yahweh
* King Hezekiah's officials
* Non\-Israelites who live in the kingdom of Judah
* And non\-Israelites who live in the northern kingdom of Israel

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In the first scene, the whole assembly, or gathering of Israelites, decided to celebrate for seven more days. So the people continued the festival with great joy for another week. King Hezekiah gave the people 1,000 bulls and 7,000 sheep and goats, and the officials gave 1,000 bulls and 10,000 sheep and goats. The storyteller does not say directly that the people offered these animals as sacrifices, but that is probably what the people did.

The people had likely only prepared for a seven‑day celebration, but now the people were staying an extra week. The people needed animals for sacrifices during those extra days. You may remember that the people ate part of the peace or fellowship offerings, so these gifts from the king and the officials also provided food for everyone in the assembly.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: How will you translate the gifts of the king and the officials so that people will understand that the people used the gifts as offerings? Pause this audio here.

In scene two, many more priests consecrate themselves. There are now more priests ready to serve because these priests had set themselves apart and become ceremonially clean.

All the people of Judah were filled with joy. The priests, the Levites, and everyone who had come from the northern kingdom of Israel celebrated with the people. The assembly also included foreigners, or non\-Israelites, who lived in Israel and had come from Israel for the festival, as well as foreigners who lived in Judah.

A person is a foreigner in a country when that person is in a country that is not where that person was born. These kinds of foreigners are people who are foreigners in a country and temporarily live in that country, even though that country is not where the person was born. It is important to remember that these foreigners have chosen to follow the Law of Moses and worship Yahweh. If these foreigners did not follow the Law of Moses, the Israelites would not allow the foreigners to celebrate the festival with the Israelites.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your language, what word or phrase will you use for the foreigners, or non\-Israelites? Pause this audio here.

There is great joy in Jerusalem. Since the days of Solomon, son of David, king of Israel, an event like this has not happened in Jerusalem.

The priests and Levites, or Levitical priests, rise to bless the people. In the original language, it is a little unclear who is doing the blessing. Some earlier versions seem to refer to "Levitical priests," since the priests were descendants of Levi. Others believe that both the priests and the Levites who are not priests bless the people. However, according to the Law of Moses, priests were usually the ones who blessed the people.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: What version of this part of the passage have you heard before? Which version would people in your area be familiar with? Discuss if you will translate this section as "priests and Levites" or "Levitical priests." We recommend that you follow the accepted translation in your area. Pause this audio here.

The storyteller says that the priests and Levites "arose." This does not mean the priests and Levites were sitting or lying down before they blessed the people. Instead, the storyteller may be saying that the priests stood in front of the people when they gave the blessing.

In the original language, people sometimes use the word "rise" together with another action word to emphasize that action. Here, the storyteller is emphasizing that the priests did the action of blessing the people.

Stop here and discuss this question as a group: In your language, how will you emphasize the priests' action when you tell the story? Pause this audio here.

God hears the priests because the priests' prayers of blessing reached heaven, which is the holy place where God lives.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 30:23–27 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has two scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* Israelites from the kingdom of Judah who have gathered in Jerusalem
* Israelites from the northern kingdom of Israel who have gathered in Jerusalem
* The priests
* The Levites
* King Hezekiah
* Yahweh
* King Hezekiah's officials
* Non\-Israelites who live in the kingdom of Judah
* And non\-Israelites who live in the northern kingdom of Israel

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

The whole assembly agrees to celebrate seven more days. So the people continued the festival with great joy for another week.

Hezekiah, king of Judah, provides 1,000 bulls and 7,000 sheep and goats to the assembly for their offerings. The officials give the assembly 1,000 bulls and 10,000 sheep and goats.

Many more priests consecrate themselves for service to Yahweh.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing one of King Hezekiah's officials, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am very happy because I get to contribute what I have for the Festival of Unleavened Bread to honor Yahweh; or
* I am very happy to be able to support many Israelites who sincerely want to obey Yahweh; or
* I just lost a lot of wealth, but I believe that Yahweh can bless me.

> Continue the drama.

All the people of Judah were filled with joy. The priests, the Levites, and everyone who had come from the northern kingdom of Israel celebrated with the people. The assembly also included foreigners who had come from Israel for the festival, as well as foreigners who lived in Judah.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing one of the non\-Israelites who are celebrating the Festival of Unleavened Bread, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel great joy as I worship Yahweh with the Israelites; or
* I am very thankful for all the priests who bless us; or
* I am very surprised to see a lot of people who still want to worship Yahweh.

Continue the drama.

People in Jerusalem were very happy. The people had not celebrated like this since the time of

King Solomon, son of David. The Levitical priests rise to bless the people. God hears the priests because the priests' prayers of blessing reach heaven, the holy place where God lives.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I am listening to the prayers of the Levitical priests because they are doing things to please me; or
* I like the way the people worship together; or
* I cannot ignore the prayers of the people who are doing what is right. The people are doing what I have commanded them to do.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 30:23–27 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

The whole assembly agrees to celebrate seven more days. So Hezekiah, **king** of **Judah**, provides 1,000 bulls and 7,000 sheep and goats for the assembly for their **offerings**. Use the same words for king and Judah that you have used previously. If you decide to use the word offering in your translation, use the same word for offering or fellowship offering that you have used previously. For more information, refer to king, Judah, offering, and **peace or fellowship offering** in the Master Glossary.

The **officials** give the assembly 1,000 bulls and 10,000 sheep and goats. A great number of **priests consecrate** themselves, or purify themselves so they can help in worship to Yahweh. Use the same words for officials, priests, and consecrate that you have used previously. For more information on priest and consecrate, refer to the Master Glossary.

Use the same words for bulls, sheep, and goats as you have used in previous passages.

All the people of Judah were filled with joy. The priests, the **Levites**, and everyone who had come from the northern kingdom of **Israel** celebrated with the people. The assembly also included **foreigners** who had come from Israel for the festival, as well as foreigners living in Judah. A person is a foreigner in a country when that person is in a country that is not where that person was born. The foreigners in this story are people who are foreigners in a country and temporarily live there, even though that country is not where the person was born. Use the same words or phrases for Levites, Israel, and these kinds of foreigners as you used in previous passages. For more information on Levites, Israel, and foreigners, refer to the Master Glossary.

The people in **Jerusalem** are very happy because the people have not celebrated like this since the days of Solomon, son of King **David**. Use the same words for Jerusalem and David as you have used previously. Refer to the Master Glossary for more information about Jerusalem and David.

The Levitical priests rise to **bless** the people. When a person speaks a blessing over another person, they are praying that Yahweh will do good things for this other person. Use the same word or phrase for bless as you used in previous passages. For more information on bless, refer to the Master Glossary.

**God** hears the priests because their **prayer** of blessing reaches **heaven**, the holy place where God lives. When a person prays, that person is communicating with Yahweh. In this context, heaven refers to the place where God lives with his angels. Use the same words or phrases for God, prayer, and heaven as you used in previous passages. For more information, refer to God, pray, and heaven in the Master Glossary.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 30:21-27

## 2 Chronicles 31:1–10 (id: eng-2ch-p57-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 31:1–10 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 31:1–10 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 31:1–10 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous passage, many Israelites traveled to Jerusalem and celebrated the Passover festival. In this passage, the Israelites leave Jerusalem and remove all the idols from the land, and then the people go home. King Hezekiah organizes the priests and Levites for the work in the house of Yahweh. Then Hezekiah tells the people to donate items to the house of Yahweh.

The Israelites celebrated the Passover festival for the first time in a long time. After the celebration was over, it seems that many of the Israelites felt a greater desire to obey Yahweh. These Israelites showed their dedication to Yahweh by removing all the sacred pillars, Asherah poles, high places, and altars in their land. The people had used all these things to worship false gods.

Stop here and discuss these questions: How common is idol worship in your culture? How do people represent the false gods they worship? What might happen if a group of people came and destroyed these idols? Pause this audio here.

The sacred pillars in Israel were large stones that people had placed in certain areas. People usually did not carve or shape the stones very much.

Stop here and look at a picture of a sacred stone in Israel. Does the stone look like anything that exists in your culture? Pause this audio here.

The Israelites removed the idols, high places, and altars throughout the territory of Judah, Benjamin, Ephraim, and Manasseh.

Stop here and look at a map that shows the territory of Judah, Benjamin, Ephraim, and Manasseh. Pause this audio here.

The people of each Israelite tribe lived in their assigned territory. Within that territory, there were further divisions by clans and families. Most Israelites inherited the territory that belonged to their family.

Stop here and discuss these questions: In your culture, how do people decide where they will live? How do people decide who owns the land? Can a piece of land belong to just one person, or can the land belong to a family, a clan, or an entire tribe? How easy or difficult is it to move to a different place? Pause this audio here.

Next, Hezekiah told different groups of priests and Levites what jobs to do in the house of Yahweh. Many years before, King Solomon organized the priests and the Levites according to King David's instructions. Hezekiah most likely reestablished David's organization system for the priests and Levites.

The priests and Levites needed many things to do the work in the house of Yahweh. The priests and Levites needed animals to sacrifice, flour and oil to make bread, wine, incense, wood, materials for the priests' clothing, and food for the priests and Levites to eat. King Hezekiah contributed a portion of the animals that people needed to make the burnt offerings and peace or fellowship offerings.

Stop here and discuss these questions: Describe a time or an event when the leaders or rich people in your people group give things for the entire community to use. Why do these people do this? Is this a requirement, or do the people do it because they want to? Pause this audio here.

When the storyteller lists the sacrifices the priests make in the house of Yahweh, the storyteller starts with the offerings that the priests make every day, then the offerings that the priests make once a week on the Sabbath. Next, the storyteller mentions the offerings that the priests make once per month on the night of the new moon. Finally, the storyteller mentions the appointed festivals, or yearly festivals that Yahweh appointed, or chose, for the Israelites to celebrate. One group of festivals happens in the first part of the Israelites' year, while another group of festivals happens in the last part of the Israelites' year.

Stop here and discuss these questions: In your people group, when do people make sacrifices and offerings? What kinds of things do people use for sacrifices and offerings? What are the reasons why your people do this? How similar or different are these reasons from the reasons that the Israelites made sacrifices and offerings in Yahweh's temple? Pause this audio here.

The festivals that people celebrated toward the beginning of each year were Passover, the Festival of Unleavened Bread, the Festival of Firstfruits, and the Festival of Weeks.

In Israel, the people began to harvest their crops at the end of the rainy season. This time corresponds to May or early June in the modern calendar. During this time, the Israelites harvested grains like wheat and barley. The Israelites continued to harvest their crops until about four months into the dry season. This time corresponds to September or October in the modern calendar. At this time, the Israelites harvested fruits like grapes, figs, and olives.

Stop here and discuss these questions: In your area, what time of year do you harvest your crops? Do you harvest different crops at different times of the year? What holidays or festivals happen right before, during, or just after the harvest season? Pause this audio here.

After the harvest was finished, the Israelites celebrated the later festivals. These are the Festival of Trumpets, the Day of Atonement, and the Festival of Booths.

King Hezekiah also told the people who lived in Jerusalem to give goods to the priests and Levites. When each group of priests and Levites was on duty in the temple, they spent all their time doing the work of the temple. The priests and Levites did not have time to prepare food or do other kinds of work. So the people of Jerusalem gave the priests food to eat.

Stop here and discuss these questions: In your culture, under what circumstances might your people come together and give food or other goods to workers, religious leaders, or someone else who needed it? How do the people feel about this? What roles or jobs always depend on donations to provide for those people's daily living? Pause this audio here.

Azariah, the high priest, is from the house of Zadok. This means that Azariah is a descendant of a priest named Zadok. Some Bible scholars think the storyteller means the Zadok who was the high priest during Solomon's reign, while other Bible scholars think that Azariah's grandfather's name was Zadok.

King Hezekiah and his officials were amazed at how many items the people of Israel gave to the priests and Levites. It was more than Hezekiah and the officials expected, and it made the men very happy. The people brought grain, new wine, oil, honey, and animals for the sacrifices, but the people also brought sacred, or holy, gifts to give to the priests. Nobody knows exactly what the sacred gifts were. It might mean anything that people did not use to make a sacrifice to Yahweh. All of these sacred gifts were in addition to the items that Yahweh required the Israelites to give to the priests and Levites. The people put all the gifts in "heaps," or large piles.

Stop here and discuss these questions: When do you expect the community to give something, whether it is money, food, or other items? Who benefits from the things that the people give? How often does the amount fall short of what the people need? How often do you have enough? How often do you have more than you need? When you have more than you need, how do the people in your community feel?

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 31:1–10 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has four scenes.

**In the first scene:** The Israelites remove all the idols and pagan altars from their land, then go home.

**In the second scene:** Hezekiah assigns the tasks of temple work to different groups of priests and Levites.

**In the third scene:** The Israelites bring goods to supply the house of Yahweh, the priests, and the Levites.

**In the fourth scene:** Hezekiah and his officials are very happy when they see that the people have given a great deal of goods to the priests and Levites.

The characters in this passage are:

* All the Israelites who came to the Passover festival
* King Hezekiah
* The divisions of priests
* The divisions of Levites
* The people who live in Jerusalem
* Hezekiah's officials
* And Azariah the high priest

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In the first scene, the Passover festival had just finished. All the people who traveled to Jerusalem left the city and went to other places in the territories of Judah, Benjamin, Ephraim, and Manasseh. Then the people destroyed all the things that the people had previously used to worship idols. The people tore down the sacred pillars and the wooden Asherah poles. These were objects that people made to represent the false gods the people worshipped. You already chose words for these objects in an earlier story.

If necessary, stop here and listen to the story at the beginning of 2 Chronicles chapter 14\. In this story, King Asa smashed the sacred pillars and Asherah poles. Pay attention to the words you used for "sacred stones" and "Asherah poles." Pause this audio here.

The pillars and poles were probably tall and straight. For this reason, the storyteller says that the people had to pull them down. People made Asherah poles from wood, and we are not sure what the poles looked like. But people have found many sacred stones in Israel. Bible scholars think that some of these stones come from the time of Hezekiah.

Stop here and look at a picture of the sacred pillars that the Israelites might have used in their worship at this time. Pause this audio here.

The people of Israel also destroyed the high places and the altars that the people had used to worship idols. In this scene, the storyteller describes how the Israelites completely destroyed all the things that people used to worship idols.

Stop here and look at a picture of an altar. Also, look at a picture of what a high place in ancient Israel might have looked like. In the picture of the high place, look for any objects that you recognize. Pause this audio here.

After the people finished tearing down all the idols, each person went to his own possession. This means that each person went back to the land that belonged to his family. Remember that each tribe of Israelites lived in a certain area, and that area was further divided into territory for different clans and families. The easiest way to express this is to simply say that each person went home.

In the second scene, King Hezekiah assigns duties to the divisions of the priests and Levites. There were many jobs that people needed to do in the house of Yahweh. Hezekiah probably followed the same plan that King David gave Solomon for the organization of the priests and Levites. The priests and the Levites did different jobs in the house of Yahweh as part of their service to Yahweh. "Service" describes a person's duty or area of responsibility.

Stop and discuss the words you have used in previous stories for "service" or "duty." Decide which word you will use here. Pause this audio here.

The priests made burnt offerings every morning and evening. In contrast, the priests only made peace offerings during major celebrations and big festivals like Passover. The priests and the Levites had a special responsibility to minister in the house of Yahweh, in order to please Yahweh and for the benefit of the people of Israel. To minister means to serve or to help others. In previous stories, you already chose a word for "minister."

If necessary, stop here and listen to the beginning of 1 Chronicles chapter 15 to remember the word you chose for "minister." Pause this audio here.

"The camp of the Lord," or "the camp of Yahweh," technically refers to the tabernacle, or the Tent of Meeting, which was the large tent that the Israelites carried with them before Solomon built the house of Yahweh. Although the house of Yahweh was not the same thing as the Tent of Meeting, sometimes the storyteller uses the term "the camp of the Lord" or "the camp of Yahweh" when the storyteller is talking about the house of Yahweh. A camp is a temporary place to live. This term emphasizes that the place on earth where the Israelites minister to Yahweh is a temporary place for Yahweh. To make the meaning of this phrase clear, but to also keep the unusual way of saying it, we recommend that you say something that means "the house of Yahweh, the place where Yahweh camps on earth."

Stop here and discuss what words you will use to make it clear that this phrase refers to the house of Yahweh, but also means that Yahweh lives in a temporary place on earth. Pause this audio here.

In the third scene, the storyteller describes how the king and the people provided goods so that the priests and Levites could do their work in the house of Yahweh. King Hezekiah contributed some of his own animals for the sacrifices at the temple. The priests made sacrifices in the morning and evening every day, then added extra sacrifices on the Sabbath and at the New Moon Festival. "New moon" refers to the night when the moon is completely dark in the night sky.

In the Bible, a festival is a day that people set aside for special spiritual or religious purposes. In ancient Israel, the people usually celebrated a festival to remember something that Yahweh did for them, such as the Passover. Or a festival might be a time when the Israelites were required to do a special religious activity, such as on the Day of Atonement. In a previous story in Chronicles, you chose a word for "festival."

If necessary, stop here and listen again to the last part of 1 Chronicles 23\. In the story in 1 Chronicles 23, the storyteller mentions the Levites' duties. Pay attention to the end of the story, when the storyteller mentions the yearly festivals. Pause this audio here.

King Hezekiah told the people who live in Jerusalem to provide the portion due to the priests and Levites. The Israelites gave the firstfruits of their grain, new wine, olive oil, honey, and the produce from their fields. "Firstfruits" means the first part of a harvest. The Israelites often thought that the first items that people harvested were the best part of the harvest. So this word implies that the people should give the best part of their harvest to Yahweh. If you have already translated the book of Exodus, then you have already chosen a word for "firstfruits."

Stop here and listen to the middle portion of Exodus chapter 23, if it is available in your language. Pay close attention to the word you used for firstfruits. If you have not translated Exodus yet, start discussing which word or phrase you could use for firstfruits. Pause this audio here.

Grain is a general term for the small part of plants like wheat, oats, rice, barley, and sorghum that you can eat. This term could also include beans and peas.

Stop here and discuss what word you could use here for "grain." Pause this audio here.

"New wine" refers to any wine that people freshly produce after the grape harvest. This word shows that as soon as the Israelites produced wine, the Israelites were supposed to give a portion of the wine to Yahweh. Most of the oil that the Israelites used was olive oil. While the Israelites did eat honey, which comes from bees, the word in this scene probably means a sweet syrup that people made from dates or other fruits.

Stop here and decide how you will describe the sweet syrup that the Israelites gave for the work in the house of Yahweh. If honey is common in your culture, you may use your word for honey. If honey is not common in your culture, decide what word you will use. Pause this audio here.

Next, the storyteller says that the people of Israel and Judah who lived in the cities of Judah brought a tithe of cattle, sheep, and sacred gifts. The storyteller makes it clear that these people did not live in Jerusalem, which was in the territory of Benjamin. These people lived in Judah, which was south of the territory of Benjamin. The word "tithe" means one\-tenth, or one part out of ten. This means that if an Israelite had ten sheep, the Israelite was supposed to give one sheep as an offering to Yahweh. If an Israelite had ten kilograms of grain, the Israelite was supposed to give one kilogram of grain as an offering to Yahweh. If you have already translated the book of Numbers or the book of Deuteronomy, you have already chosen a word for tithe.

Stop here and listen to Numbers chapter 18 or Deuteronomy chapter 12, if they are available in your language, to remember the word you chose for tithe. If you have not translated either of these books yet, start to discuss what word you could use for tithe. Pause this audio here.

In this scene, "cattle" is a general term for larger livestock, such as bulls, cows, buffalo, and oxen. "Sheep" is a general term for small livestock animals, such as sheep and goats. If your language has general terms for large livestock and small livestock, you may use those here. Otherwise, it is also acceptable to use your words for cows and sheep.

Stop here and decide how you will describe the animals that the Israelites brought to the house of Yahweh. Pause this audio here.

The Israelites also brought sacred, or holy, gifts for the priests and Levites to the house of Yahweh. No one knows exactly what these gifts were. It is possible that the sacred gifts might have included the ingredients for the incense that the priests burned in the house of Yahweh, or material and precious stones to make the clothes that the priests wore. The word "sacred" simply means that the people gave these gifts for the purpose of doing the work in the house of Yahweh. "Consecrated" has the same meaning.

Stop here and decide what words you will use to say "sacred gifts." Pause this audio here.

Finally, the storyteller lets us know that the Israelites began to bring all these gifts for the priests and Levites in the third month and finished bringing things in the seventh month. This timeframe starts at the beginning of the harvest and finishes at the end of the harvest season.

In the fourth scene, King Hezekiah and some leaders come to see the piles of goods that the people have given to the priests and Levites. These leaders were probably government officials. Then King Hezekiah and his officials bless Yahweh and Yahweh's people. When the storyteller says that Hezekiah and his officials blessed Yahweh, it means that these men praised Yahweh or gave thanks to Yahweh. The men also praised, or thanked, the people of Israel. In some languages, it may be impossible or unnatural to say that people blessed Yahweh. If this is the case with your language, you may say that Hezekiah praised Yahweh or thanked Yahweh.

Stop here and decide whether you will say that Hezekiah and his officials blessed Yahweh, praised Yahweh, or thanked Yahweh for all the goods the people gave. Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah was so amazed by the amount of goods the people of Israel gave that Hezekiah asked the priests and Levites about the goods. Azariah, the chief priest, answered King Hezekiah. The chief priest was the leader of all the priests. The storyteller mentions that Azariah was from the house of Zadok. This means that Azariah was a descendant of a man named Zadok. The storyteller is probably talking about the Zadok who was a priest while King David and King Solomon were alive. The descendants of Zadok had a reputation for faithfully obeying Yahweh, even when other priests were not faithful to Yahweh. When the storyteller mentions that Azariah was from the house of Zadok, the storyteller wants us to understand that Azariah was probably a faithful and trustworthy priest. If it is more clear in your language, you may say that Azariah was a descendant of Zadok, or that Zadok was an ancestor of Azariah.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 31: 1–10 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has four scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* All the Israelites who came to the Passover festival
* King Hezekiah
* The divisions of priests
* The divisions of Levites
* The people who live in Jerusalem
* Hezekiah's officials
* And Azariah the high priest

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

When the Passover festival was over, all the Israelites who had attended the festival went out to the towns of Judah. The Israelites smashed the sacred pillars and cut down the Asherah poles. The Israelites destroyed the pagan places of worship and removed the altars. The Israelites did this in all the territory of Judah and Benjamin, and also in all the territory of Ephraim and Manasseh, until the Israelites had destroyed all these things. Then everyone went home.

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing the Israelites who went to the festival, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* I did not realize before how much Yahweh loves our people and how much Yahweh has done for us. Yahweh is truly amazing! or
* I feel sad that so many people in our country worship idols. We need to do something about it! or
* I feel glad that we are getting rid of all these filthy idols! We should have done this long ago.

Ask the people playing the Israelites who stayed home from the festival, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* Why are these people coming home and destroying our places of worship? What happened at that festival? or
* I feel sad that I could not go to the festival. The festival must have been really good! or
* I feel angry that people are tearing down our places of worship. We need those gods to help us! or
* I am glad that our people are finally tearing down these filthy idols.

Continue the drama.

King Hezekiah re\-established the divisions of the priests and the Levites according to their service in the house of Yahweh. The priests and the Levites were to make the burnt offerings and peace offerings, to minister, to give thanks to Yahweh, and to sing praise in the gates of the house of Yahweh, the place where Yahweh camps on the earth.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel relieved that our people will worship Yahweh correctly again; or
* I feel proud that I am the one who is making all these good things happen; or
* I feel glad that I have the opportunity to correct the wrong things that my father did.

Ask the people playing the priests and the Levites, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We feel so happy that we are going back to do our work in the house of Yahweh again; or
* Our fathers told us all about what it was like to serve in the house of Yahweh. We are so happy that now we get to serve Yahweh ourselves; or
* We feel grateful to King Hezekiah for making sure that everything in the house of Yahweh is operating correctly again; or
* We feel excited to see the house of Yahweh repaired and looking beautiful!

Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel happy that my people are making sacrifices to me again in the right way; or
* I feel pleased that my people want to worship me again; or
* It is wonderful that my people are honoring me again.

Continue the drama.

> King Hezekiah provided animals from his own herds and flocks to use for the morning and evening sacrifices, as well as for the sacrifices on the Sabbath, at the New Moon festivals, and the yearly festivals as required by the Law of Yahweh. Hezekiah told the people of Jerusalem to contribute the portion for the priests and Levites, so that the priests and Levites could devote all their time to fulfilling the requirements of the Law of Yahweh. As soon as Hezekiah gave the order, the Israelites generously gave the first portion of their grain harvest, their new wine, olive oil, and honey, and everything that the fields produced. The Israelites abundantly brought in one\-tenth of everything the Israelites had. The Israelites and people of Judah who lived in the towns of Judah also brought one\-tenth of their cattle and sheep and one\-tenth of the sacred gifts, which were set aside for Yahweh. The people made piles of all these items. The people started making the piles in the third month, at the beginning of the harvest, and the people finished by the seventh month, at the end of the harvest.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I want the people to see that it is important to obey Yahweh's laws, so I will give many of my own animals to make sure there are enough to make all the sacrifices; or
* I hope that Yahweh will be pleased with all the work I have done and all the things I have given to the house of Yahweh; or
* I feel excited to see people starting up all the sacrifices and rituals in the house of Yahweh again.

Ask the people playing the people of Jerusalem and the people of Judah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We are glad to contribute our portion to make sure the priests and Levites have everything they need; or
* It is a good thing that the priests are making sacrifices in the house of Yahweh again. We will give the priests everything they need; or
* We wish it did not cost so much to keep the house of Yahweh running! or
* My harvest has been wonderful this year! It is not too much trouble to give one\-tenth of my harvest to the house of Yahweh.

Ask the people playing the priests and Levites, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We have all the supplies we need! This is amazing! or
* We are amazed that the people have been so generous. The people must truly love Yahweh; or
* We are very happy that we can make sacrifices and worship Yahweh in Yahweh's house again!

Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel happy that my people are so generous to the priests and the Levites; or
* This is how I always meant for things to be. Everyone contributes a small part, and everyone has enough; or
* It is wonderful that the people gave even more than they had to. The people are truly showing how much they value my house.

Continue the drama.

When Hezekiah and his officials came and saw the piles, they praised Yahweh and Yahweh's people, the people of Israel. Then Hezekiah asked the priests and the Levites about the piles of goods. Azariah the high priest, who was a descendant of Zadok, said to the king, "Since the people began to bring the contributions into the house of Yahweh, we have had enough to eat, with plenty left over, because Yahweh has blessed Yahweh's people, and this great quantity is what is extra."

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* This is exactly what I hoped would happen. I gave generously to the house of Yahweh, and the people have done the same; or
* I am proud of the people because the people gave so generously; or
* I am grateful to Yahweh, for Yahweh is the one who really inspired the people to give so much.

Ask the people playing Hezekiah's officials, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We are happy that the people are supporting the priests and the Levites so well; or
* We are relieved that the people did their part, and we did not have to force the people to support the house of Yahweh; or
* It is good that the people have been so generous, but what will happen in the future if the harvest is not as good?

Ask the person playing Azariah, the high priest, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* With so many supplies, it will be easy to make sure that everyone has enough; or
* There is so much left over that we will be able to save some for the future; or
* I feel like the people truly value the work we do!

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 31:1–10 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

Now, when all this was finished, all of **Israel** who were present went out to the cities of **Judah** and broke the sacred pillars into pieces and cut down the Asherah poles. The people also tore down the high places and the **altars**. The **Israelites** did this throughout the territory of Judah, Benjamin, Ephraim, and Manasseh until they had destroyed all of these things. Be sure to translate Israel, Judah, altar, high place, and Israelite the same way you did in previous passages. See the Master Glossary for more information on Israel, Judah, altar, and Israelite.

Be sure to translate **sacred pillars** and **Asherah poles** the same way you did in previous stories.

Then the Israelites returned to their own **cities**. Use the same word or phrase for cities as you have used in previous passages.

**King** Hezekiah assigned the **priests** and **Levites** to divisions, or special groups, each of them according to their service. The priests and Levites were to offer **burnt offerings** and **peace or fellowship offerings**, to minister, to give thanks, and to sing **praise** at the gates, or entrances, of the **house of Yahweh**, the place where **Yahweh** lives on earth. Be sure to use the same words for king, priest, Levite, burnt offering, peace or fellowship offering, house of Yahweh, and Yahweh that you used in previous stories. See the Master Glossary for more information on king, priest, Levite, praise, burnt offering, peace or fellowship offering, **house of God**, **temple**, and Yahweh.

Hezekiah also established the king's portion of the items for the offerings, for the morning and evening **burnt offerings**, and the burnt offerings for the **Sabbaths**, the **New Moon festivals** and for the yearly festivals. Hezekiah did this in accordance with the **Law** of Yahweh. Hezekiah also commanded the people who lived in **Jerusalem** to give the portion due to the priests and Levites. Be sure to use the same words for burnt offering, Sabbath, New Moon festival, law, and Jerusalem that you have used in previous stories. See the Master Glossary for more information on burnt offering, Sabbath, **festival of New Moon**, law, and Jerusalem.

As soon as Hezekiah's order spread, the sons of Israel provided in abundance the **firstfruits** of grain, new wine, olive oil, honey, and all the produce of their fields. They brought in abundantly the **tithe** of all. The sons of Israel and Judah who lived in the towns of Judah also brought the tithe of oxen and sheep, and the tithe of sacred gifts which were **consecrated** to Yahweh their **God**, and put them in piles. Be sure to use the same words for first fruits, tithe, consecrated, and God that you used in previous stories. See the Master Glossary for more information on firstfruits, tithe, consecrated, and God.

The people started making the piles in the third month, at the beginning of the harvest, and the people finished by the seventh month, at the end of the harvest.

Stop here and discuss how you will say that the Israelites began bringing gifts in the third month, at the start of harvest season, and finished in the seventh month, at the end of harvest season. Pause this audio here.

When Hezekiah and the leaders came and saw the piles, they **blessed** Yahweh and Yahweh's people Israel. Then Hezekiah asked the priests and the Levites about the piles. Azariah, the **high priest** of the house of Zadok, answered Hezekiah. Be sure to use the same words for blessed and high priest that you used in previous passages. For more information on bless and high priest, refer to the Master Glossary.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 31:1-10
