## 2 Chronicles 32:1–8 (id: eng-2ch-p59-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 32:1–8 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 32:1–8 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 32:1–8 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous passage, the storyteller explained how the priests and Levites gave out all the items that the people of Israel donated for the priests and Levites. In this passage, King Sennacherib of Assyria invades Judah and threatens Jerusalem. King Hezekiah and the people make preparations to defend Jerusalem, and King Hezekiah encourages the people to trust Yahweh their God to protect the people. The storyteller shows how God rescued King Hezekiah and his people from the army of Assyria and saved the city of Jerusalem.

Assyria was a large and powerful country northeast of Judah and Israel. At the time that Hezekiah was king of Judah, everyone was afraid of the Assyrian army. Assyria had already conquered other countries around Judah, and now Assyria began to attack Judah.

Stop here and look at a map that shows the locations of Judah, Israel, and Assyria. Pause this audio here.

Sennacherib and his army began by attacking Judah's fortified cities. Remember that King Solomon and other kings of Judah had built fortified cities all over their territory. The kings had built walls around the cities so that outsiders could not get in and attack the people who lived in those cities. King Hezekiah realized that Sennacherib was going to attack Jerusalem, so Hezekiah did everything Hezekiah could to protect Jerusalem and the people inside the city. The first thing Hezekiah and his men did was to stop up the springs around Jerusalem. That part of Israel got rain during the cooler part of the year, but during the hot part of the year, the people depended on springs for drinking water. A spring is a place where water comes up from underground and forms a small stream or a pool.

Stop here and talk about where your people get water to drink. What would your people do if you did not have these places to get water to drink? Pause this audio here.

Springs are very good sources of water to drink. The storyteller says that there was more than one spring around Jerusalem, but the main spring that we know about was the Gihon spring on the east side of Jerusalem.

Stop here and look at a picture of a spring and a map of Jerusalem that shows the location of the Gihon spring. Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah did more things to strengthen the defenses of Jerusalem. Hezekiah gathered the people to repair damage to Jerusalem's wall, to build a new wall, and to build towers along the wall. By climbing to the top of a tower, the people inside the city could see far away and see if Sennacherib's army was getting close to Jerusalem. Finally, Hezekiah's workers made more weapons and shields.

Stop here and talk about what it might be like to be in a city when you know people are coming to attack. How do you think you would feel? Maybe you have been in a similar situation. What was that situation like? Did your people have a leader who was able to protect you? Pause this audio here.

Finally, Hezekiah gathered the people together and spoke to the people. Hezekiah told the people to be strong and courageous. Hezekiah said the people should not be afraid or discouraged because Yahweh was with the people. Hezekiah was convinced that Yahweh was on the side of the people of Jerusalem and would help the people. Hezekiah's words helped the people to feel brave and hopeful.

Stop here and tell a story about a time when someone was facing a bad situation, but another person said something that caused the first person to see the situation in a better way. This can be a folk tale or a story from your own life. Talk about the situation, why it was a bad situation, and how the person's encouraging words made a difference.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 32:1–8 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has two scenes.

**In the first scene:** The Assyrian army invades Judah and attacks the fortified cities of Judah. Hezekiah organizes workers to improve the defenses around Jerusalem.

**In the second scene:** Hezekiah gathers the people together and tells them not to be afraid, because Yahweh is on the people's side. The people feel encouraged.

The characters in this passage are:

* King Hezekiah
* King Sennacherib of Assyria
* The Assyrian army
* Hezekiah's officials and military advisers
* The people of Jerusalem
* And Yahweh

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

In scene one, the storyteller begins the story by reminding us that Hezekiah had faithfully done many good works for Yahweh. The storyteller is referring to the fact that Hezekiah restarted worship in the house of Yahweh, celebrated the Passover festival, inspired the Israelites to destroy all the idols in the land, and required the Israelites to provide the tithes and offerings to support the priests and Levites.

Sennacherib was both the king of Assyria and the leader of the army. If you have already translated the book of 2 Kings, then you have already heard a version of this story and chosen a name for Sennacherib.

Stop here and listen to 2 Kings chapter 18, starting from the middle of the chapter. This story begins during the 14th year of King Hezekiah's reign. Pause this audio here.

In the original language, the storyteller uses special language and says, "Sennacherib's face was for war against Jerusalem." This means that Sennacherib intended to fight against the people of Jerusalem and to capture the city. The idea is that Sennacherib is looking at Jerusalem, and this shows that Sennacherib intends to come to Jerusalem. Sennacherib wants to fight against the people of Jerusalem, overthrow the king, and take control of the city himself.

Stop here and discuss these questions: What sayings do you have in your language that talk about the way a person faces something, or turns their face toward something, and how that action shows what the person plans to do? What other sayings use a different body part to show a person's intentions or plans? Pause this audio here.

Sennacherib's army laid siege to Judah's fortified cities. A siege is a military strategy to conquer a fortified city. To do a siege, the attacking army simply surrounds the fortified city so that the people on the inside cannot go in and out. Eventually, the people inside the city will run out of food and water. At that point, the people inside the city have three choices: fight their way out, starve to death, or surrender. In a previous story, you described the idea of doing a siege.

If necessary, stop here and listen to the beginning of 1 Chronicles chapter 20 to remember how you described doing a siege. Pause this audio here.

Apparently, Sennacherib did not want to wait for the people inside the fortified cities of Judah to surrender. Sennacherib gave his army instructions to break down the walls of the fortified cities. This was also a normal battle plan if the attacking army was strong enough to do it. The storyteller says that Sennacherib did this because Sennacherib intended to break into and conquer the cities for himself. This simply means that Sennacherib wanted to conquer and control Judah's cities.

Stop here and discuss the best way in your language to say that Sennacherib wanted to conquer and control Judah's cities. Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah saw what Sennacherib was doing and made efforts to defend Jerusalem against the Assyrian army. When the storyteller says that Hezekiah saw what Sennacherib had done, the storyteller means that Hezekiah was aware of Sennacherib's actions and that Hezekiah understood what Sennacherib intended to do. Hezekiah realized that Sennacherib intended to take over all of Judah by conquering Jerusalem.

Stop here and discuss what the best way in your language is to say that Hezekiah knew what Sennacherib was doing and Hezekiah understood what Sennacherib's intentions were. Pause this audio here.

The first thing Hezekiah did was to talk to Hezekiah's government officials and military advisers. The men decided to stop the supply of water outside Jerusalem so that the Assyrians could not use it. Remember that the people of Jerusalem depended on fresh water from springs to get drinking water. We do not know exactly how Hezekiah and his officials stopped the supply of water from the springs, but the purpose was to make it hard for the Assyrian army to get water.

Many people came and helped Hezekiah's officials stop the flow of water from the springs. The people also stopped the flow of a stream that ran through the region. A stream is a small, shallow river. There are not very many streams in Jerusalem. This stream may have had the Gihon spring as its source. Again, the purpose was to make it difficult for the Assyrian army to get water.

To make it clear why the people stopped up the springs and the stream, the storyteller says that the people asked a question to which they did not expect an answer. The people say, "Why should the kings of Assyria come and find abundant water?" First, the phrase "kings of Assyria" probably refers to the Assyrian army's military leaders. These military leaders represent the entire army of Assyria. Second, this question means, "We do not want the Assyrian army to come here and find plenty of water to drink" or "We are not going to make it easy for the Assyrian army to come here and find plenty of water to drink."

Stop here and discuss how you will say that the people of Judah said that they would not make it easy for the Assyrian army to come and find plenty of water to drink. Pause this audio here.

The storyteller continues to describe all the things that King Hezekiah did to prepare for an attack by the Assyrian army. First, the storyteller says that Hezekiah took courage, or strengthened himself. This means that Hezekiah both felt brave and took strong action.

Stop here and discuss what words or sayings you have in your language that mean a person felt brave and took strong action. Then decide which word or saying you could use here. Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah rebuilt parts of the wall of Jerusalem that were broken. The capital city of Jerusalem had a wall for protection, but parts of the wall were broken. Hezekiah did not fix the wall himself but ordered Hezekiah's men to fix the wall. Hezekiah also ordered his men to build towers. Most city walls in ancient Israel had towers around or in the walls. People who stood in the towers to defend the city could see an attack coming from far away. We do not know exactly where these towers were. The towers were probably close to the wall or possibly even part of the wall itself.

Stop here and look at an image of a city wall with towers. What word could you use in your language for "tower"? Pause this audio here.

King Hezekiah also told his men to build another wall outside the original city wall of Jerusalem. This second wall made it even harder for the Assyrian army to attack Jerusalem. King Hezekiah's men also strengthened the Millo. The Millo was a structure in the City of David, which was one of the older parts of Jerusalem. We are not sure exactly what the Millo was. Some people think the Millo was terraces, while other people think the Millo was a building. A terrace is an area of land that people fill with dirt to make flat. There are usually several layers of terraces, almost like stair steps. In an earlier story, you already decided how you want to talk about the Millo.

Stop here and listen to the beginning of 1 Chronicles chapter 11\. In this story, David becomes king of Israel, moves into Jerusalem, and builds the Millo. Pay close attention to how you decided to talk about the Millo. Pause this audio here.

Finally, Hezekiah made many weapons and shields for his people to use to fight against the Assyrian army. Once again, Hezekiah did not make these himself, but rather Hezekiah told his men to make the weapons and shields, and the men did. A weapon was any object that a person could use to hurt another person. In ancient Israel, swords, spears, bows, and arrows were common weapons. A shield was an object that a person could use to protect themself from those weapons.

In the second scene, King Hezekiah appoints more military officers over the people of Jerusalem. The word "people" here might refer to the army and not to the non\-army people of Jerusalem. Then Hezekiah told the people to gather together in the square by the city gate. It is not clear if the storyteller means that Hezekiah assembled all the people of Jerusalem in the square at the city gate, or only the military officers.

A square was a wide open space in a city where people could gather. This square in Jerusalem was next to the city gate.

Stop here and discuss these questions: In your language, what do you call a wide open space in a city where people could gather? What word could you use for this space in your translation? Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah assembled the people and spoke to the people to encourage them. To encourage means to make someone feel better, have hope, or feel brave. The original language says that Hezekiah spoke to the people's hearts. This means that Hezekiah wanted to make the people feel good emotions rather than bad emotions.

Stop here and discuss these questions: How could you say in your language that Hezekiah spoke to the people in order to help the people feel better, have hope, or feel brave? Pause this audio here.

The first thing Hezekiah said was "Be strong and courageous." The word "strong" probably does not mean to be physically strong, but to be determined mentally and emotionally to do what people need to do. Hezekiah wanted the people to think that they could fight this battle and win.

Next, Hezekiah told the people, "Do not be afraid or discouraged because of the king of Assyria and the large army that is with the king." Discouraged means the opposite of encouraged. A person who is discouraged feels hopeless, sad, and afraid.

Next, Hezekiah states the reason why the people did not need to feel afraid or discouraged. Hezekiah says, "There is a greater power with us than with the king of Assyria." The greater power is Yahweh. Hezekiah means that Yahweh is on the side of the people of Israel and that Yahweh is more powerful than Assyria's army.

Stop here and discuss how you could say that the Israelites have a greater power on their side than the Assyrians have. Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah continues to describe how Yahweh is more powerful than Assyria's army. Hezekiah says that Sennacherib, the commander of the Assyrian army, only has an arm of flesh or mere human strength. Remember that in the Bible, someone's arm represents that person's power. And in the Bible, flesh represents the state of being human or mortal. Hezekiah means that Sennacherib only has ordinary human power to help Sennacherib. Hezekiah contrasts this with the spiritual power that the Israelites have because Yahweh is on the Israelites' side.

Stop here and discuss these questions: What sayings do you have in your language that mean that someone has only human power as opposed to spiritual power? Could you use any of these sayings here? Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah says that Yahweh our God is with us to help us and to fight our battles. This means that Yahweh will protect the people of Israel. Yahweh will help the Israelites to have enough power to win the battle against the Assyrian army.

Stop here and discuss how you will say "Yahweh our God is with us to help us and to fight our battles." Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah's speech to the people worked. The people felt encouraged and gained confidence.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 32:1–8 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has two scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* King Hezekiah
* King Sennacherib of Assyria
* The Assyrian army
* Hezekiah's officials and military advisers
* The people of Jerusalem
* And Yahweh

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

After King Hezekiah had faithfully done all these things, King Sennacherib of Assyria came and invaded Judah. Sennacherib laid siege to all the fortified cities of Judah. Sennacherib gave orders to his army to break down the city walls and take control of the cities. When Hezekiah realized that Sennacherib intended to attack Jerusalem, Hezekiah consulted with his officials and military commanders. The men decided to stop the flow of the springs around Jerusalem. Many people came and stopped up all the springs and the stream that flowed through the region. The people did this so that it would not be easy for the Assyrian army to find water when the army came near Jerusalem.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I did all these things to please Yahweh, but now Yahweh is allowing Sennacherib to attack Judah. Did I do something wrong? or
* I feel confident that Yahweh will not allow such an evil army to conquer the city where Yahweh's Name lives; or
* I will do everything in my power to make sure that Sennacherib does not conquer Jerusalem; or
* One of the best ways to defeat Sennacherib's army is to make sure the army has no water to drink.

Ask the person playing King Sennacherib, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Judah is a good land full of good farms and prosperous cities. I want that land! or
* It makes me happy to think of my people ruling over the towns of Judah; or
* My army is much stronger than Hezekiah's army. I am not afraid of Hezekiah's army!

Ask the people playing the Assyrian army, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We feel proud to conquer new lands for our king; or
* This is good land. It will benefit our people to control this land; or
* We are happy to fight to show everyone that our army is the strongest army in the world!

Ask the people playing Hezekiah's officials and military commanders, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We feel very nervous. The Assyrian army is strong and terrifying; or
* We are sad that we are losing our land to the Assyrians; or
* We are angry that the Assyrians have attacked our country. How dare the Assyrians hurt Yahweh's people! or
* We will do everything possible so that the Assyrians cannot conquer Jerusalem!

Ask the people playing the workers in Jerusalem, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We feel afraid. Sennacherib has conquered so many other towns and cities. Will Sennacherib conquer Jerusalem, too? or
* We feel confident in our king. Look at all the things our king is doing to protect our city; or
* We are determined to make it as hard as possible for the Assyrian army to conquer Jerusalem; or
* We feel confused. We have been more faithful to Yahweh than the previous generation. Why is this happening to us now?

Continue the drama.

Then Hezekiah took courage and instructed his men to work hard to repair all the broken sections of the wall around Jerusalem. The men also built towers on the wall. Hezekiah's men built another wall outside the first wall and reinforced the Millo in the City of David. Hezekiah also made a large number of weapons and shields.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* We can defend this city. We just need to fix a few things; or
* I am proud of my people for working so hard; or
* I feel confident that our defenses will be strong enough; or
* I know that Yahweh is with us. Yahweh will not let this city fall.

Ask the people playing Hezekiah's workers, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We feel confident in our king. Our king is doing the right things to defend the city; or
* We feel nervous. We are doing a lot of work, but will it be enough? or
* Surely Yahweh will not let the Assyrians destroy Jerusalem; or
* If the Assyrians do conquer this city, what will happen to me and my family?

Continue the drama.

Hezekiah appointed military officers over the people and told them to assemble before Hezekiah in the square at the city gate. Hezekiah encouraged the people, saying, "Be strong and courageous. Do not be afraid or discouraged because of the king of Assyria and the large army with him, because there is a stronger power on our side than there is on the king of Assyria's side. The king of Assyria only has human power, but Yahweh our God is on our side to help us and fight this battle for us." The people felt better after listening to what Hezekiah, the king of Judah, said.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing King Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Yahweh has helped our people many times. Yahweh will help again; or
* I want my people to know that they do not need to be afraid, because Yahweh will protect us; or
* I feel safe and secure. Yahweh is better protection than any wall or shield.

Ask the people playing the people listening to Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* Hezekiah is right! Yahweh is God over everything, and Yahweh will protect us; or
* We feel better. We just need to trust in Yahweh; or
* We feel excited to see how Yahweh will rescue us; or
* Yahweh is stronger than the Assyrians. We do have a chance to win here.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 32:1–8 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

After **King** Hezekiah had faithfully done all these things, King **Sennacherib** of **Assyria** came and invaded **Judah**. Sennacherib laid **siege** to all the **fortified cities** of Judah. Sennacherib gave orders to his army to break down the city walls and take control of the cities for Sennacherib. Be sure to use the same words for king, Sennacherib, Assyria, Judah, siege and fortified city as you have used in previous stories. See the Master Glossary for more information on king, Judah, and siege.

When Hezekiah realized that Sennacherib's face was for war against **Jerusalem**, Hezekiah consulted with his **officials** and **military commanders**. Use the same words for Jerusalem, officials, and military commanders that you have used in previous passages. Refer to the Master Glossary for more information about Jerusalem.

Stop here and discuss how you will say that Sennacherib's **face was for war** against Jerusalem, or that Sennacherib intended to attack Jerusalem. Pause this audio here.

The men decided to stop the flow of the **springs** around Jerusalem. Many people came and stopped up all the springs and the stream that flowed through the region. The people said to each other, "We do not want it to be easy for the Assyrian army to find water when they arrive here." Use the same word for springs that you have used in previous passages.

Then Hezekiah took courage and instructed his men to work hard to repair all the broken sections of the wall around Jerusalem. The men also built **lookout towers**. Use the same word for lookout towers as you have used previously, and refer to "lookout towers" in the Master Glossary for more information.

Stop here and decide how you will say that King Hezekiah **took courage**, or strengthened himself. Pause this audio here.

The men also built towers on the wall around Jerusalem. Hezekiah's men built another wall outside the first wall and reinforced the **Millo**, or **terraces,** in the City of David. Hezekiah also made a large number of weapons and **shields**. Be sure to use the same words for the Millo, or terrace, and shield as you have used in previous stories.

Stop here, if needed, and look at a picture of a spring. Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah appointed military officers over the people and told them to assemble before Hezekiah in the square at the city gate. Then Hezekiah "spoke to the people's hearts." Remember that this means that the king wanted the people to feel better, stop being afraid, and feel encouraged.

Stop here and decide how you will say that King Hezekiah **spoke to the people's hearts**. If needed, refer to **heart** in the Master Glossary for more information. Pause this audio here.

The King told the people, "Be strong and courageous. Do not be afraid or discouraged because of the king of Assyria and the large army with him, because there is a stronger power on our side than there is on his side. The king of Assyria only has an arm of flesh, but **Yahweh** our **God** is on our side to help us and fight this battle for us." Be sure to use the same words for Yahweh and God as you have used in previous stories. See the Master Glossary for more information on Yahweh and God.

Stop here and decide how you will say that the king of Assyria has only an "**arm of flesh**," which means that the King of Assyria is only depending on human power, as opposed to God's power. Pause this audio here.

The people felt better after the people listened to what Hezekiah, the king of Judah, said.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 32:1-8

## 2 Chronicles 32:9–23 (id: eng-2ch-p60-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 32: 9–23 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 32: 9–23 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 32: 9–23 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous passage, King Hezekiah prepared to defend Jerusalem from an attack by the Assyrian army. In this passage, King Sennacherib of Assyria sends messengers to Jerusalem to try to scare Hezekiah and the people so that they will surrender to the Assyrian army. But Yahweh kills many Assyrian soldiers, and Sennacherib is the one who gives up instead.

Remember that by the time Hezekiah became the king of Judah, the Assyrian army had already completely conquered and occupied the northern kingdom of Israel. Some time after Hezekiah finished preparations for Sennacherib's attack, the Assyrian army attacked and captured many cities in Judah. When this story happens, the main Assyrian army, along with Sennacherib, is attacking a city called Lachish. Lachish was a very important city in Judah. You may remember that Lachish was one of the cities that King Rehoboam built up and fortified. Lachish was about 48 kilometers southeast of Jerusalem. Lachish was on a major road that led from Egypt to Jerusalem. Since Sennacherib came from the north, if he successfully captured Lachish, then Sennacherib's army would have Jerusalem surrounded.

Stop here and look at a map that shows the locations of Judah, Assyria, Jerusalem, and Lachish. Pause this audio here.

It must have been terrifying for the people of Judah to think the Assyrian army was very close to surrounding the people. The Assyrian army was very cruel. But even though the Assyrian army was powerful, it would still be easier for Sennacherib if King Hezekiah and the people of Judah just surrendered. To surrender means to give up or to stop fighting and allow the other side to win. Leaders sometimes chose to do this if they thought their army had no chance to win. Sennacherib probably sends his messages to Hezekiah and the people of Judah to convince the people to give up without fighting.

Stop here and tell a story about someone who decided to give up without fighting. How did that decision benefit people? How did that decision harm people? Do you think the person was wise or unwise to give up? Pause this audio here.

In those days, many people believed that the gods of their country, the spirits of their ancestors, or other local spirits were supposed to protect the people in their country. People believed that if two countries fought each other, the country with the stronger gods, ancestors, or spirits would win. This is probably one reason why many people groups worshipped many gods, spirits, and the spirits of ancestors. People believed that if they worshipped more spiritual beings, those beings would fight for the people. Sennacherib strongly believes that his gods are stronger than Yahweh, Judah's God.

Stop here and discuss these questions: In your culture, how common is it for people to believe in other gods besides Yahweh? What kinds of things do people believe the false gods do? How common is it for people to believe that some false gods are more powerful than others? Pause this audio here.

In those days, many people also believed that the more high places, altars, and idols people built for their false gods, the happier those false gods would be. So Sennacherib thought that Hezekiah offended Yahweh when Hezekiah tore down all of the high places and altars in Judah, except for the altar in the house of Yahweh. Sennacherib did not understand that Yahweh wanted only one altar, in the house of Yahweh in Jerusalem.

Stop here and discuss these questions: How common is it in your culture for people to believe that other gods want many altars, shrines, or temples? How might people feel if someone damaged or destroyed those places? Pause this audio here.

Sennacherib's messengers delivered this first message, but the message did not cause King Hezekiah to surrender. So, Sennacherib wrote letters to Hezekiah and the people of Judah. These letters contained the same message. Sennacherib said, "The gods of the other nations could not save them from me, and your God, Yahweh, cannot save you either." The storyteller points out Sennacherib's key mistake: Sennacherib thinks that Yahweh, the God of Jerusalem, is exactly like the gods of the other peoples. Sennacherib does not realize that Yahweh is the one true God.

Stop here and talk about how Yahweh, the one true God, is different from the false gods or spirits that the people in your area worship. Pause this audio here.

After Sennacherib insulted Yahweh, King Hezekiah and Isaiah the prophet prayed to heaven about the situation. Heaven is the place where Yahweh lives, so this is another way to say that Hezekiah and Isaiah prayed to Yahweh. Isaiah was one of the most important prophets in the history of Israel and Judah. The relationship between King Hezekiah and Isaiah was good. Yahweh responded to Hezekiah and Isaiah's prayers and sent a messenger to help Hezekiah and the people of Judah. Most people call this kind of messenger from Yahweh an angel. An angel is a supernatural being who does what Yahweh wants.

Stop here and discuss these questions: How common is it in your culture for people to believe in angels? What do people believe about angels? What do people believe that angels do? Pause this audio here.

The angel that Yahweh sent killed all the mighty warriors, commanders, and officers in the Assyrian army. Because of this, Sennacherib left and went back to his own country in disgrace. Later, Sennacherib's own sons murdered Sennacherib. Yahweh not only saved King Hezekiah and the people of Judah from the Assyrian army but also from all others who could be a threat to the people of Judah. For the rest of Hezekiah's lifetime, the people of Judah lived in peace.

Stop here and tell some stories about a time when your people were worried that something bad might happen to your people, but it did not happen. This could be a story of a time you thought that an army might attack your people; that a natural disaster like a flood, fire, drought, or a bad storm might come; or perhaps you feared that many people might get sick and die. What did your people do to prepare for the bad thing? Do you know why the bad thing never happened? How would your lives be different if the bad thing had happened? Pause this audio here.

After Sennacherib left Judah, people brought offerings to Jerusalem for Yahweh and gifts, or valuable items, for King Hezekiah. We are not sure if these people were all from Judah or if some of these people came from other countries. At the end of this story, the storyteller says that people in other nations honored and admired King Hezekiah, so it is possible that some of these people came from other nations. The people who lived in countries like Moab, Ammon, and Edom were also very afraid of the Assyrian army, and their leaders were probably very happy to hear that the entire Assyrian army died in Judah.

Stop here and tell stories about a time when your people celebrated something good that happened. This good thing could be anything, such as a good harvest, a victory in a sport, the end of a war, the end of a drought, the election of a leader, or anything else that was good for your community. What did you do to celebrate this good thing? How did any of the people who live around you react or join in the celebration?

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 32:9–23 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has three scenes.

**In the first scene:** Sennacherib sends messengers to Jerusalem to tell Hezekiah and the people that Yahweh cannot save them from Sennacherib's attack.

**In the second scene:** Sennacherib writes letters with the same message. Sennacherib's servants read the letters out loud near the walls of Jerusalem, where all the people of Jerusalem could hear.

**In scene three**: King Hezekiah and Isaiah the prophet pray to Yahweh, and Yahweh saves Jerusalem. Sennacherib leaves Judah. People bring gifts to Yahweh and Hezekiah.

The characters in this passage are:

* Sennacherib, King of Assyria
* Sennacherib's messengers
* The Assyrian army
* King Hezekiah
* The people of Jerusalem
* Yahweh
* Isaiah the prophet
* The angel of Yahweh
* Sennacherib's sons
* And the people who brought gifts to Yahweh and Hezekiah

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

The first scene takes place after Hezekiah spoke to the people of Jerusalem to encourage the people. At this time, Sennacherib and his army were besieging the city of Lachish. You will remember that a siege happens when an army attacks a city with tall walls and the people of the city go inside and close the gates. The army surrounds the city so that the people inside cannot get out to get food and water. Armies in ancient times used sieges to conquer cities quite often.

Sennacherib did not go to Jerusalem himself. Sennacherib probably stayed near Lachish and sent messengers or government officials to give King Hezekiah a message. But Sennacherib also wanted all the people of Judah to hear Sennacherib's message. Sennacherib's messengers begin the message with a special statement to show that the words that come after are King Sennacherib's exact words.

Stop here and discuss these questions: In your language, how do you show that a messenger is saying the exact words that someone else told the messenger to say? Could you use any of these here? Pause this audio here.

Most of the statements in Sennacherib's message are questions that no one expects anyone to answer, or questions that everyone already knows the answer to. Remember that people in Bible times used these kinds of questions to emphasize something important. Each question in Sennacherib's message was intended to mock, insult, and frighten Judah's people, King Hezekiah, and Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss these questions: Are there any special ways you can use your language to insult someone, mock someone, or frighten someone? Could you use any of these in this scene? Pause this audio here.

The first question in Sennacherib's message is, "What are you trusting in that makes you think you can survive under siege in Jerusalem?" This question means something like, "It is foolish that you think you can survive under siege in Jerusalem."

Stop here and discuss how you will say Sennacherib's first question in your language. Pause this audio here.

The second question in Sennacherib's message is long and includes a quotation of something Hezekiah said. Sennacherib says, "Is not Hezekiah misleading you, so that you will die from hunger and thirst, when Hezekiah says, 'Yahweh our God will save us from the attack by the King of Assyria'?" Sennacherib's first claim is that Hezekiah is misleading the people of Jerusalem. Sennacherib also says that the result of Hezekiah's misleading the people is that the people will die of hunger and thirst. It was common that if an attacking army laid siege to a city, the people inside the city died of hunger and thirst. So what Sennacherib said was a very real threat.

There are several ways you could communicate this sentence's meaning. You could say something like, "Hezekiah is misleading you when Hezekiah says that Yahweh your God will save you from the King of Assyria. As a result, you will die of hunger and thirst." In the original language, the greatest emphasis is on the statement "Hezekiah is misleading you." Sennacherib wants the people inside Jerusalem to stop trusting their king and to feel afraid that the people will die of hunger and thirst.

Stop here and discuss how you will say Sennacherib's second question. Pause this audio here.

The third question in Sennacherib's message is also long and includes a quotation of something Hezekiah said. Sennacherib says, "Was it not Hezekiah himself who removed Yahweh's high places and altars and then told the people of Judah and Jerusalem, 'You must worship before one altar and burn incense on it?'" Sennacherib does not mean that Hezekiah personally went around the land and removed every altar and high place. Rather, Sennacherib means that Hezekiah told the people to go all over the land and remove the high places and altars, and because Hezekiah was the king, the people obeyed Hezekiah. Sennacherib blames Hezekiah for this. Sennacherib thinks that Yahweh will be angry that Hezekiah told the people to remove all the high places and altars, and that the people could only worship and burn incense in front of the altar in the house of Yahweh. Sennacherib does not realize that Hezekiah was actually obeying Yahweh when Hezekiah did these things.

For the first part of this statement, you could say something like, "Hezekiah is the one who was responsible for removing Yahweh's high places and altars." For the second part of this statement, you could say something like, "Hezekiah is the one who told the people of Judah and Jerusalem that you may only worship in front of one altar and burn incense on it."

Stop here and discuss how you will say Sennacherib's third question. Pause this audio here.

The fourth question in Sennacherib's message is shorter and simpler. Sennacherib says, "Do you not know what my fathers and I did to all the people of other lands?" When Sennacherib says "My fathers," Sennacherib means "my ancestors" or "my predecessors," who were Assyria's previous kings. When Sennacherib says "all the people of other lands," Sennacherib means the people who lived in the countries that the Assyrian army had already conquered. This question means something like, "You should know what my fathers and I did to all the people of other lands," or possibly, "You are stupid if you do not know what my fathers and I did to other lands."

Stop here and discuss how you will say Sennacherib's fourth question. Pause this audio here.

The fifth question in Sennacherib's message is, "Were the gods of the nations of the lands at all able to rescue their land out of my hand?" This question means, "The gods that those people worshipped were not able to do anything to save their land from me." The word "nations" means people groups other than the people of Judah. In this question, Sennacherib emphasizes that the gods of the other nations were not able to do even one very small thing to save their land from the Assyrian army.

Stop here and discuss how you will say Sennacherib's fifth question. Pause this audio here.

The sixth question in Sennacherib's message is similar to the fifth question. Sennacherib says, "Who among all the gods of these nations—which my fathers completely destroyed—could rescue his people from my hand?" This question means, "None of the gods of those nations could save the people from my power—my ancestors completely destroyed those people."

Stop here and discuss how you will say Sennacherib's sixth question. How will you make it different from the fifth question? Pause this audio here.

The seventh question in Sennacherib's message is, "How much less is your God able to rescue you from my hand?" This question means, "Your God will not be able to save you from me either."

Stop here and discuss how you will say Sennacherib's seventh question. Pause this audio here.

Finally, Sennacherib stops using questions that everyone already knows the answer to. Now, in his message, Sennacherib just says what Sennacherib wants the people of Jerusalem to do. First, Sennacherib uses a word that means "because of the things I just said, this is what you should do."

Stop here and discuss what words you have in your language that mean "because of the things I just said, this is what you should do." Pause this audio here.

Sennacherib tells the people of Jerusalem not to let Hezekiah deceive or mislead the people. "Deceive," or "lie," and "mislead" have very similar meanings, but the storyteller uses two words for emphasis. Unless it is very unnatural in your language to use two words with a similar meaning, we recommend that you use two words here to emphasize what Sennacherib says Hezekiah is doing to Hezekiah's people.

Stop here and discuss how you will describe what Sennacherib says Hezekiah is doing to Hezekiah's people. Pause this audio here.

Next, Sennacherib says, "Do not believe him." Sennacherib is talking about Hezekiah. Sennacherib says the people should not believe Hezekiah because no god of any nation or kingdom has ever been able to save people from the power of the kings of Assyria. When Sennacherib says "god" here, Sennacherib is not talking about Yahweh, the one true God. Sennacherib is talking about the false gods that the other people worshipped. "Nations" means people groups, but a "kingdom" means a territory that a king rules over. A kingdom at that time was more like a country is now.

The final statement in Sennacherib's message is, "How much less will your God rescue you from my hand!" This statement means something like, "Your God is not able to save you from my attack either."

Stop here and discuss how you will say Sennacherib's final statement. Pause this audio here.

At the end of scene one, Sennacherib's servants, or messengers, speak against Yahweh God, and against Yahweh's servant Hezekiah. To speak against someone means to say bad things about that person, or to say things that are not true. Sennacherib's messengers say bad things about both Yahweh and Hezekiah.

Stop here and discuss these questions: In your language, how do you say that someone said bad things or untrue things about another person? Which of these could you use here to say that Sennacherib's servants spoke against Yahweh and Hezekiah? Pause this audio here.

In the second scene, Sennacherib writes letters to insult Yahweh, the God of Israel. Once again, Sennacherib tries to convince the people in Jerusalem that Yahweh cannot save the people from the Assyrian army. Although the storyteller does not say so, it sounds like Sennacherib's messengers read the letters out loud from a place very close to the wall of Jerusalem. Sennacherib's messengers were close enough that the fighters who were standing on top of the wall could hear what Sennacherib's messengers said. And Sennacherib's messengers used the language of Judah when they read the letters out loud.

In ancient times, people built walls around their cities to protect the city and the people inside. These walls were not like the walls of a house or a hut. People built the walls very thick and very tall. One ancient wall near Jerusalem was seven meters thick! On the inside, people built stairs so that soldiers could go to the top of the wall. It was not uncommon for people to stand or sit on the wall around the city to witness important events taking place on the ground below. If an army was attacking the city, soldiers could stand on the wall to shoot arrows or throw things at enemy soldiers outside the wall.

Stop here and look at a picture that shows soldiers standing on a city wall. Then discuss how you will make it clear to your hearers that these city walls were not like the walls of a house or a hut, but were very tall and thick and able to support many people standing on the walls. Pause this audio here.

Sennacherib says, "The gods of the nations of the lands have not rescued their people from my hand, or power." Sennacherib is once again talking about the false gods that the people who lived in the countries all around Judah worshipped. Sennacherib calls Yahweh "the God of Hezekiah." This means that Yahweh was the God whom Hezekiah worshipped. The storyteller also calls Yahweh "the God of Jerusalem." This could mean "the God that the people in Jerusalem worshipped," or "the God whose temple is in Jerusalem," or "the God who rules over Jerusalem."

Stop here and discuss how you will say "the God of Jerusalem" in your language. Pause this audio here.

The storyteller calls false gods "the work of men's hands" or "idols made by human hands." In this statement, the word "men" means human beings. This is a way to say that the false gods are not the one true Creator God. People used their hands to carve wood or stone images and called those images gods, but those images are not really gods.

Stop here and discuss how you could describe the false gods in your language to show that they are not real but made by human beings. Pause this audio here.

In the third scene, King Hezekiah and Isaiah the prophet cry out to heaven in prayer. "Cry out to heaven" is another way to say that Hezekiah and Isaiah prayed to Yahweh. Yahweh responds to Hezekiah and Isaiah's prayers and sends an angel to help them. This angel, or messenger, has supernatural power.

The angel of Yahweh destroys, or kills, every mighty warrior, commander, and officer in the camp of the king of Assyria. This means that the angel killed all the leaders and the best fighters in the Assyrian army. So Sennacherib returned in disgrace, or shame, to Sennacherib's own land. There were many reasons for Sennacherib to feel disgraced. Sennacherib's army was not able to conquer Jerusalem. All of Sennacherib's soldiers died. The Assyrian people may very well have wondered how anyone could lose an entire army. Sennacherib probably felt embarrassed, confused, and afraid.

Stop here and discuss how you will say that Sennacherib returned home in disgrace. Pause this audio here.

The Assyrian people worshipped many gods and built temples for those gods. One day, Sennacherib was worshipping in the house, or temple, of Sennacherib's god. Some of Sennacherib's own children came into the temple and struck Sennacherib down with swords. This means that Sennacherib's children killed Sennacherib by stabbing Sennacherib with swords. In the original language, the storyteller uses a special phrase for Sennacherib's children. The storyteller calls the children "those who came from his inner parts." This phrase emphasizes that the children came from Sennacherib's body.

Stop here and discuss these questions: In your language, what words or phrases do you have for children that emphasize that those children come from the father's body? Could you use any of these phrases here? Pause this audio here.

The storyteller finishes the story by saying that this is how Yahweh saved Hezekiah and the people of Jerusalem from Sennacherib and from the hand, or power, of all others. This means that no one else was able to frighten or threaten the people of Judah again during Hezekiah's lifetime.

The next statement about Yahweh is hard to understand in the original language. Some people think the statement means that Yahweh allowed the people of Judah to be at rest, or at peace, during this time. It is possible that the storyteller says the same thing in two different ways for emphasis. However, some people think this statement means that Yahweh guided Hezekiah and the people in every way. Yahweh made sure that Hezekiah and the people of Judah made the right decisions and were successful. And some other people think that this means that Yahweh provided in every way for Hezekiah and the people of Judah. Yahweh made sure that Hezekiah and the people had everything they needed. We recommend that you either say that Yahweh guided Hezekiah and the people in every way or that Yahweh provided for Hezekiah and the people in every way.

Stop here and decide how you will say that Yahweh guided Hezekiah and the people in every way or that Yahweh provided for Hezekiah and the people in every way. Pause this audio here.

Finally, the people bring gifts, or valuable items, for Yahweh and Hezekiah. It is not clear whether the storyteller means that the people of Judah brought these gifts or if people from other nations also brought gifts. The people of all nations did have great esteem or respect for King Hezekiah.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 32:9–23 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has three scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* Sennacherib, king of Assyria
* Sennacherib's messengers
* The Assyrian army
* King Hezekiah
* The people of Jerusalem
* Yahweh
* Isaiah the prophet
* The angel of Yahweh
* Sennacherib's sons
* And the people who brought gifts to Yahweh and Hezekiah

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

Later, when Sennacherib and his army were laying siege to the fortified city of Lachish, Sennacherib sent messengers to Jerusalem with a message for Hezekiah, the king of Judah and for all the people of Judah who were in Jerusalem. The message from King Sennacherib of Assyria said: "It is foolish that you think you can survive under siege in Jerusalem! Hezekiah is misleading you when Hezekiah says that Yahweh your God will save you from the king of Assyria. Instead, you will die of hunger and thirst! Hezekiah is the one who made you destroy Yahweh's high places and altars. And Hezekiah is the one who told you, people of Judah and Jerusalem, that you may only worship in front of one altar and burn incense on it."

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Sennacherib, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I know I can conquer Jerusalem, but it would be easier if the people in the city just gave up. I will try to scare the people into surrender; or
* Hezekiah made a big mistake when Hezekiah destroyed all the high places and altars of his God, except for one. Hezekiah's God must be very angry with Hezekiah; or
* I feel certain that this is going to be an easy victory.

Ask the people playing Sennacherib's messengers, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We are enjoying speaking our king's message to Hezekiah and the people in Jerusalem; or
* We feel powerful. Our army has defeated so many people, we do not have to worry about losing; or
* This message must really make the people inside Jerusalem feel afraid! The people should just give up now; or
* We do not understand why any king would destroy all the high places and altars of that king's god except for one.

Ask the person playing Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Sennacherib thinks that just because he conquered other lands, that means Sennacherib can conquer Yahweh's land. Sennacherib is wrong; or
* I know Yahweh will protect us, but the way this arrogant man talks about Yahweh makes me feel angry! or
* I am confident that Yahweh will help us, but what if the people believe the things that Sennacherib is saying?

Ask the people playing the people of Judah in Jerusalem, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We have confidence that Yahweh will do something to save us from the Assyrian army; or
* We feel nervous. What if Sennacherib is right? or
* If the Assyrian army captures Lachish, we will be trapped. We will not be able to get away from Sennacherib; or
* Sennacherib is foolish. Sennacherib does not understand that when we destroyed all the high places and altars, it made Yahweh happy, not angry.

> Continue the drama.

Sennacherib's message continued: "Surely you know what my fathers and I did to all the people of other lands. The gods of those people were not able to do one thing to save their land from our armies. None of the gods of those nations could stop me from taking over their country. My ancestors completely destroyed those nations. And your God will not be able to save you from me either. Therefore, do not let Hezekiah deceive or mislead you. Do not believe what Hezekiah says, for no god of any people or kingdom has ever been able to save people from what the kings of Assyria want to do to them. Your god is not able to save you either." And Sennacherib's messengers said more vile things about Yahweh God and about Yahweh's servant Hezekiah.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Sennacherib, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* My ancestors and I killed the people of many other nations. These people of Judah can never win against us; or
* If Hezekiah is smart, he will surrender. If Hezekiah is not smart, then we will show Hezekiah who has the more powerful army; or
* Yahweh, the God of Israel, cannot do anything to me. I am more powerful! or
* I am looking forward to marching into Jerusalem and capturing the city.

Ask the people playing Sennacherib's messengers, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We feel relieved that we are on the side of the strongest army; or
* The people of Jerusalem are not very smart if they do not surrender to us; or
* If the people of Jerusalem do not give up, we will just kill them all.

Ask the person playing Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel angry that Sennacherib said that I am lying to my own people; or
* I am a little worried that my own people might feel afraid of the Assyrian army; or
* I am looking forward to seeing how Yahweh saves us from this situation.

Ask the people playing the people of Judah and Jerusalem, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We really hope that Yahweh saves us! or
* Hezekiah told us to be brave. We have to do what our king told us to do; or
* With Yahweh on our side, we can defeat the Assyrian army.

Continue the drama.

King Sennacherib also wrote letters to insult Yahweh, the God of Israel, and to lie about Yahweh. Sennacherib's letters said, "Just like the gods of the other people have not saved their people from my power, so the God that Hezekiah worships will not save you either." Sennacherib's messengers shouted these things in the language of Judah to the people of Jerusalem who were standing on Jerusalem's wall. Sennacherib's messengers did this to frighten and terrify the people of Jerusalem, so that it would be easier to capture Jerusalem. Sennacherib's messengers spoke about the God who lives in Jerusalem as if God were just like one of the pagan gods that humans made with their hands.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Sennacherib, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Hezekiah is foolish to not surrender. I will give Hezekiah one more warning; or
* Why do the people of Jerusalem continue to trust in their God? The people should know I will destroy them if the people do not give in; or
* I feel a little unhappy that King Hezekiah has not surrendered yet.

Ask the people playing Sennacherib's messengers, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* The people of Jerusalem should not believe that their god can save them from us; or
* Hezekiah should know better than to get his people into an impossible fight; or
* We are content to wait for our king to come and conquer this city.

Ask the people playing the people of Judah and Jerusalem, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* The Assyrians are speaking about Yahweh as if Yahweh were one of the false gods of our neighbors. The Assyrians have no idea what Yahweh is really like; or
* We hope that Yahweh will do something amazing, and then these Assyrians will see that Yahweh is more powerful than they are; or
* We feel nervous. Hezekiah says that Yahweh will protect us, but what if Yahweh does not?

> Continue the drama.
> 
> Then King Hezekiah and the prophet Isaiah, son of Amoz, prayed to God in heaven and cried out to God for help. So Yahweh sent an angel who killed all the mighty soldiers and officers in the Assyrian army. So Sennacherib was forced to return to his own land in disgrace. Later, when Sennacherib went into the house of his god, some of Sennacherib's own sons killed Sennacherib with swords. This is how Yahweh rescued Hezekiah and the people of Jerusalem from King Sennacherib of Assyria and from all the others who threatened them. And Yahweh provided for the people in every way. Many people came to Jerusalem and brought gifts for Yahweh and for King Hezekiah. From then on, the people in all the surrounding nations highly respected King Hezekiah.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel angry that Sennacherib insulted Yahweh! I want Yahweh to do something to Sennacherib; or
* This is a bad situation. I should get a prophet to help me; or
* I do not want to listen to Sennacherib's insults anymore. I want Yahweh to make the Assyrian army leave; or
* I hope that Yahweh will show everyone just how powerful Yahweh is!

Ask the person playing Isaiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Sennacherib does not understand who Yahweh is. Sennacherib is in for a big surprise; or
* I feel angry that Sennacherib is threatening our people; or
* I feel confident that Yahweh will protect us; or
* This is an opportunity for Yahweh to show the whole world how powerful Yahweh is!

Ask the person playing the angel, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Now Sennacherib will see how powerful Yahweh, the God of Israel, is; or
* Yahweh told me to kill the soldiers. I am glad to do it so I can protect the people of Jerusalem; or
* It is sad that so many people have to die, but I must kill these soldiers to protect the people of Judah!

Ask the person playing Sennacherib, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel terrified! All my soldiers and leaders are dead! or
* This is the worst thing that has ever happened to me; or
* The people will think that I am a terrible military commander and a terrible king! I might not be safe anymore.

Ask the people playing Sennacherib's sons, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* Our father got an entire army killed. Our father must have done something really bad; or
* If we allow our father to live, the entire country will be weak; or
* It would probably be better if someone other than our father were leading the country.

Ask the people playing the people of Jerusalem, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We are amazed that Yahweh killed the entire Assyrian army; or
* We are grateful that Yahweh did such an amazing miracle to save us; or
* After this, we know that we can trust Yahweh in any situation! or
* King Hezekiah was right about Yahweh! Yahweh really is more powerful than all the other gods!

Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Now the Assyrians know that there is no one like me, Yahweh, in all the earth; or
* This will help the people of Jerusalem to understand how important they are to me; or
* I am glad that there is no one left to threaten the people of Judah and Jerusalem; or
* Now the people of Judah will be able to live in peace.

Ask the people playing the people who brought gifts, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The people might answer things like:

* We do not know how Hezekiah made the Assyrians run away. Hezekiah's God must be very powerful; or
* We are happy that the Assyrian army is no longer a threat; or
* Hezekiah must be a wise and powerful king. We will show Hezekiah that we want to be on good terms with Hezekiah; or
* King Hezekiah was right about Yahweh! We will bring some gifts to Yahweh in Jerusalem.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 32:9–23 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

Afterward, **King** Sennacherib of Assyria sent some servants, or military officers, to **Jerusalem** while Sennacherib and his army were laying **siege** to Lachish. The storyteller calls King Sennacherib's military officers his "servants," but you should use whatever term you have been using for military officers here. Sennacherib sent a message for Hezekiah, the King of **Judah** and for all the people of Judah who were in Jerusalem. Be sure to use the same words for siege, Jerusalem, king, and Judah that you used in previous stories. See the Master Glossary for more information on siege, Jerusalem, king, and Judah.

This is what King Sennacherib of Assyria said: "You should not trust that you can survive under siege in Jerusalem! Hezekiah is misleading you, so that you will die of hunger and thirst, saying, '**Yahweh** our **God** will save us from the power of king of **Assyria**.' Hezekiah himself destroyed Yahweh's **high places** and **altars** and told you, people of Judah and Jerusalem, 'You may only **worship** in front of one altar and burn **incense** on it.'" Be sure to use the same words for Assyria, Yahweh, God, high place, altar, worship, and incense that you used in previous stories. See the Master Glossary for more information on Yahweh, God, altar, and worship.

"Surely you know what my ancestors and I did to all the people of other lands. The **gods** of the **nations** were completely unable to save their people from my **hand**, or power." Be sure to use the same words for gods and nations that you used in previous stories. See the Master Glossary for more information on gods and nations. You will remember that in this culture, people used the idea of hands to represent power. Use the same phrase for "hand" or power here that you have used previously.

Sennacherib continued and said, "Therefore, do not let Hezekiah deceive or mislead you like this. Do not believe what Hezekiah says, for no god of any nation or **kingdom** has ever been able to save people from either me or my predecessors." Use the same word for kingdom that you have used previously. For more information, refer to kingdom in the Master Glossary.

King Sennacherib also wrote letters to insult Yahweh, the God of **Israel**, and to speak against Yahweh. Be sure to use the same word for Israel that you used in previous stories. See the Master Glossary for more information on Israel.

Sennacherib's messengers shouted in Hebrew, the language of Judah, to the people of Jerusalem who were standing on the wall of Jerusalem. Then King Hezekiah and the **prophet** **Isaiah**, son of Amoz, **prayed** and cried out to **heaven**. Be sure to use the same words for prophet, Isaiah, pray and heaven as you did in previous stories. See the Master Glossary for more information on prophet, pray and heaven.

Stop here and discuss as a group how you will say that the messengers shouted in the language of the people of Judah. You may say that the messengers shouted in **Hebrew**, or you may say that the messengers shouted in the language that the people of Judah spoke. For more information about Hebrew, refer to the Master Glossary. Pause this audio here.

So Yahweh sent an **angel** who killed every mighty warrior, commander, and officer in the camp of the king of Assyria. So Sennacherib was forced to return to his own land in disgrace. Be sure to use the same word for angel that you used in previous stories. See the Master Glossary for more information on angel.

Later, when Sennacherib went into the house of his god, some of Sennacherib's own sons, those who came from his inner parts, killed Sennacherib with swords.

Stop here and decide how you will say "sons, or those who came from his inner parts." Pause this audio here.

This is how Yahweh rescued Hezekiah and the people of Jerusalem from the attack of King Sennacherib of Assyria and from all the others who threatened them. And Yahweh guided the people in every way. Many people came to Jerusalem and brought **offerings**, or gifts, for Yahweh and valuable gifts for King Hezekiah. From then on, the people in all the surrounding nations greatly respected King Hezekiah. If you use the word for offerings for Yahweh, refer to offerings in the Master Glossary for more information.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 32:9-23
