## 2 Chronicles 32:24–33 (id: eng-2ch-p61-v1)

Hear and Heart
--------------

In this step, hear 2 Chronicles 32:24–33 and put it in your hearts.

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 32:24–33 three times, in three different translations, if possible.

Pause this audio here.

Now, as a group, discuss the following questions:

1. What do you like in this passage? Pause this audio here.
2. What do you not like or not understand in this passage? Pause this audio here.
3. What does this passage tell us about God? Pause this audio here.
4. What does this passage tell us about people? Pause this audio here.
5. How does this passage affect our daily lives? Pause this audio here.
6. Who do you know who needs to hear this passage?

Setting the Stage
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 32:24–33 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In the previous story, King Sennacherib of Assyria invaded Judah and threatened to destroy Jerusalem. But King Hezekiah and Isaiah the prophet prayed to Yahweh, and Yahweh saved Judah from Sennacherib. In this story, Hezekiah almost dies from an illness, but Yahweh gives him a miraculous sign and heals Hezekiah. Then Yahweh blesses Hezekiah and makes him very rich. After Hezekiah dies, Hezekiah's son Manasseh becomes the next king.

If you have already translated the book of 2 Kings, then you have already translated one version of this story. If the book of 2 Kings is available in your language, stop here and listen to 2 Kings chapter 20 to help you remember the events of that story. Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah becomes very sick around the same time that King Sennacherib invades Judah. The king and the people are probably very afraid. The people of Judah need a strong leader to help them survive Sennacherib's attack. But Hezekiah is so sick that everyone thinks Hezekiah might die. However, Hezekiah prays to Yahweh, and Yahweh speaks to Hezekiah. Yahweh gives Hezekiah a miraculous sign, then Hezekiah recovers from his illness.

Stop here and tell a story about a miracle you have witnessed. What was happening when the miracle occurred? How did the people involved feel about it? What might have happened if this miracle had not taken place? Pause this audio here.

But even though Yahweh gives Hezekiah a miraculous sign and heals Hezekiah, Hezekiah does not respond in the right way. This is because Hezekiah is proud. Because Hezekiah is proud and ungrateful, Yahweh becomes angry at Hezekiah and the people of Judah.

Stop here and tell a story about a person who received a gift or a blessing but was not grateful. How do you describe the attitude of the person who was ungrateful? Pause this audio here.

However, when Hezekiah realizes that Yahweh is angry, Hezekiah does the wise thing and humbles himself. Humble means the opposite of proud. Hezekiah shows Yahweh that Hezekiah is not proud anymore. The people of Judah also humble themselves and show Yahweh that the people are sorry. So Yahweh is no longer angry at Hezekiah and the people, and Yahweh does not punish Hezekiah and the people of Judah while Hezekiah is still alive.

Next, the storyteller explains that Hezekiah became richer and more famous. The people highly honor Hezekiah. The storyteller explains that Hezekiah has so many valuable things that Hezekiah has to build special buildings to keep his possessions in. Hezekiah owns a great deal of silver, gold, precious stones, spices, shields, and other items.

Stop here and discuss these questions: What material goods do your people think are the most valuable? Where do your people keep the most valuable goods? Who owns the goods that your people think are very valuable? Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah also builds storehouses for extra grain, wine, and oil. Hezekiah probably stores these food and drink items to use in case drought, famine, or attacks by an enemy cause Hezekiah's country to run out of those things.

Stop here and discuss these questions: How often do your people have extra food available? When you have extra food, what do you do with that food? If drought or famine happens, how do you make sure that your people will have enough food? Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah builds stalls for his cattle and pens for his flocks of sheep. While cattle and sheep can live in the open fields quite well, people can protect their cattle and sheep by putting them in a stall or a pen. This kind of stall for cattle had a fence around it and a roof over it. The roof could protect the cattle from bad weather and provide shade during hot weather. A pen was a fenced area for sheep that was not covered by a roof. The pen kept all the sheep in one place. Hezekiah builds new stalls and pens because Hezekiah has so many flocks and herds of animals.

Stop here and discuss this question: In your area, what makes a person rich? What kind of things do rich people gather, such as money, land, or animals? Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah changes the flow of the waters of the Gihon spring so the water comes inside Jerusalem. A spring is a place where fresh water bubbles up from under the ground. In a hot and dry place like Jerusalem, it was very important for people to make sure they had access to water. During the dry season, other sources of water dried up, but the waters of Gihon kept flowing. So the Gihon spring was the most important source of water for people in Jerusalem.

Stop here and look at a map that shows the city of Jerusalem and the location of the Gihon spring. Pause this audio here.

In Hezekiah's time, the Gihon spring was located outside Jerusalem's city walls. If an enemy came and attacked Jerusalem from that direction, the people of Jerusalem would not be able to get water. Hezekiah realizes this is a problem, so Hezekiah stops up one part of the spring and orders his workers to dig an underground tunnel to bring water from the spring into the city. The tunnel was more than half a kilometer long and went through the rock underneath Jerusalem. The water flowed down through the tunnel and into a pool inside Jerusalem.

Stop here and discuss these questions: In your area, how do people get water? What might happen if your people could no longer get water in this way? Pause this audio here.

King Hezekiah is successful in everything he does. But one time, God tests Hezekiah. After God gives Hezekiah a miraculous sign, people in other countries hear about it. Some officials from Babylon come to ask Hezekiah about the miraculous sign. The storyteller says that at that moment, God leaves Hezekiah. This means that God does not tell Hezekiah what to do but lets Hezekiah make his own choices. God wants to see what Hezekiah will do without God's help. Sadly, as you may remember from listening to this story in 2 Kings chapter 20, Hezekiah makes a foolish choice. This choice shows what is really in Hezekiah's heart. Remember, the Israelites believed that a person's actions show what is inside that person's heart—if your heart is good, your actions will be good, but if your heart is bad, your actions will be bad.

Stop here and tell a story about someone who did something that showed that his heart was good or that his heart was bad. What did other people think when they saw the way that person acted? Pause this audio here.

The storyteller ends this story by telling the audience that they can find other stories about Hezekiah in other books, or scrolls. The name of one book, or scroll, is "The History of the Kings of Judah and Israel." The name of the other book is "The Vision of the Prophet Isaiah, son of Amoz." Some Bible scholars think these are two different books. But some Bible scholars think that "The Vision of the Prophet Isaiah" might be a smaller book that is part of "The History of the Kings of Judah and Israel." Nobody knows where these books are now. After Hezekiah died, the people buried Hezekiah in the tombs of David's descendants. Then Hezekiah's son Manasseh became the next king.

Defining the Scenes
-------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 32:24–33 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will define the scenes, characters, and setting of this passage. Then the group will visualize this passage.

This passage has four scenes.

**In the first scene:** Yahweh heals Hezekiah from a deadly illness. At first, Hezekiah is not grateful for this healing, and Yahweh becomes angry. But Hezekiah repents of his pride, and Yahweh is no longer angry.

**In the second scene:** The storyteller describes how wealthy Hezekiah was. Hezekiah builds an underground tunnel so that water can flow from the Gihon spring into the city of Jerusalem.

**In the third scene:** Some ambassadors from Babylon visit Hezekiah, and God withdraws from Hezekiah in order to test Hezekiah.

**In the fourth scene:** Hezekiah dies, and the people bury Hezekiah in the royal tombs. Hezekiah's son Manasseh becomes the next king of Judah.

The characters in this passage are:

* Hezekiah
* Yahweh
* The people of Judah and Jerusalem
* The Babylonian ambassadors
* And Manasseh, Hezekiah's son

As a group, pay attention to these parts of the passage's setting:

The events of the first scene happen around the same time as the Assyrian invasion of Judah. Hezekiah became very sick. Hezekiah was so sick that his family and servants were afraid that Hezekiah was going to die.

Stop here and discuss this question: In your language, how do you talk about a person who is so sick that they might die from the illness? Which of these words or expressions could you use here? Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah prayed to Yahweh, and Yahweh spoke to Hezekiah. Yahweh gave Hezekiah a miraculous sign. A sign is something unusual that is happening for a special reason. A sign can be something supernatural, like a miracle, or a sign can be something natural that happens after Yahweh told someone it was going to happen. If you have already translated the book of 2 Kings, then you have already translated the story of Hezekiah's illness and recovery and chosen a word for "sign."

Stop here and listen again to the beginning of 2 Kings chapter 20, if it is available in your language. Pay close attention to the word you used for "sign." Pause this audio here.

The storyteller assumes that the listener understands that the miraculous sign meant that Hezekiah would get well. The rest of the story shows that Hezekiah did get well and lived for many more years. But in some languages, it might be awkward to not say that the sign meant that Hezekiah would get well. It might also be awkward to not say that Hezekiah recovered from the illness. It is an acceptable translation choice to include this information in the story.

Stop here and discuss these questions: For your listeners, would it be better to say that God sent the sign to let Hezekiah know that Hezekiah would get well? Would it be better to say that Hezekiah did get well? Or would it be better to leave these things unsaid? Pause this audio here.

Next, the storyteller says that Hezekiah did not respond appropriately to the kind thing that Yahweh had done for Hezekiah. Hezekiah was ungrateful, even though Yahweh had saved Hezekiah from death.

Stop here and discuss these questions: Think of all the words or phrases you can to describe a person who is ungrateful for the good things they have. Which of these could you use here to describe King Hezekiah? Pause this audio here.

The storyteller says that Hezekiah's heart was proud. Remember, the Israelites believed that a person feels and thinks things in his heart. So this phrase simply means that Hezekiah was proud. Because Hezekiah was proud, Yahweh's wrath was against Hezekiah and the people of Judah and Jerusalem. Wrath is very strong anger, and wrath is Yahweh's response to sin. Yahweh was angry because Hezekiah's proud attitude was sinful. The storyteller does not mention how King Hezekiah and the people living in Judah and Jerusalem knew that Yahweh was angry with them. The storyteller only says that Yahweh's wrath was against them.

Fortunately, Hezekiah realized that Yahweh was angry because of Hezekiah's pride. So Hezekiah humbled himself, and the people of Jerusalem did the same thing. This means that they realized that their pride was wrong and they stopped being proud. The storyteller does not describe how Hezekiah and the people of Jerusalem showed Yahweh that they were not proud anymore. The storyteller just tells us the result of their repentance. Yahweh was no longer angry with Hezekiah and the people of Judah, and Yahweh's wrath did not come on them while Hezekiah was still alive. For the rest of Hezekiah's life, there was peace and prosperity in Judah.

Stop here and discuss these questions: in your culture, how does a person show that he is not proud? How does a person show that he has repented when he realizes he has done something wrong? What words and phrases do you use to describe these situations? Could you use any of those words or phrases here? Pause this audio here.

In the second scene, the storyteller says that Hezekiah was very rich and became even more rich over the course of Hezekiah's life. The storyteller also tells us that the people felt great respect for Hezekiah.

Stop here and discuss these questions: in your language, how do you say that people feel a lot of respect for someone? How do you say that people give great honor to someone? Which of these words or phrases could you use here? Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah became very wealthy while he was king, and he needed buildings to store all the material goods he had. Hezekiah's workers built special buildings called storehouses or treasuries to keep his material goods in. In addition to silver, gold, precious stones, spices and shields, Hezekiah had many other valuable items. You will remember that shields are large, flat items that soldiers use to protect themselves in battle, so these were very valuable to armies.

Stop here and remember your discussion in the previous step about the material goods that your people think are most valuable. What words or phrases do you use in your language to refer to valuable items as a group? Which of these could you use here? Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah also built special buildings to store extra grain, wine and oil. This shows that during Hezekiah's reign, Judah had many good harvests and had more grain, wine and oil than the people needed. Hezekiah stored this extra grain, wine and oil in case they needed it in the future.

Stop here and discuss these questions: in your language, what special words do you have for a building where people store extra food and drink? If you do not have a word for this, how could you describe this kind of building for your listeners? Pause this audio here.

In this scene, the storyteller uses a word that is not the usual word for "wine". Bible scholars think this word might mean "new wine" or "unfermented wine". You will remember that people make wine from squeezing the juice from grapes and letting the juice sit for some time so that it becomes fermented.

Hezekiah collected a large number of herds of cattle, or large animals like cows, bulls, and oxen, and flocks of sheep. He built stalls for his cattle and pens for his sheep.

Stop here and discuss these questions: what do you call a fenced\-in area with a roof where people keep cattle? What do you call a fenced\-in area where people keep sheep? If you do not have special words for these things, how can you describe them for your listeners? Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah also built new cities. Hezekiah might have built these cities for all the things mentioned in this scene: his treasuries and storehouses. Some Bible scholars also think Hezekiah might have built new cities to place soldiers near the treasuries, storehouses, herds and flocks in order to protect them.

King Hezekiah changed the flow of the waters of the Gihon spring so that the water would come inside the walls of Jerusalem.

Stop here and discuss these questions: what do you call a spring, or a place where water bubbles up from under the ground? If your language does not have a word for this, how can you describe this so your listeners will understand? Pause this audio here.

The storyteller emphasizes that it was Hezekiah's idea to do this, and that Hezekiah planned and carried out the building project. Hezekiah probably did not do the work himself, but he told his workers to dig the tunnel.

Stop here and discuss these questions: in your language, how do you emphasize that a particular person did something? How could you emphasize that it was Hezekiah who started and completed this building project? Pause this audio here.

The Gihon spring was on the east side of Jerusalem, outside the city walls. Hezekiah changed the direction of the water by stopping the water from flowing out on the east side of the city, and then Hezekiah's workers dug an underground tunnel to bring the water into the city.

Stop here and look at a picture of the tunnel that Hezekiah's workers dug through the rock under Jerusalem to bring water from the Gihon spring into the city. Pause this audio here.

In scene three, the princes, or rulers, of Babylon sent some officials to Jerusalem to ask Hezekiah about the wonder, or miracle, that had happened in the land. Remember, when Hezekiah was sick, Yahweh showed Hezekiah a miraculous sign to prove that Hezekiah would get well. "Wonder" and "miracle" are both different words for a sign. It is something that happened that was unusual and seemed impossible.

Stop here and discuss these questions: what words do you have in your language that have a similar meaning to "miracle" or "wonder"? Which of these could you use here? Pause this audio here.

When the storyteller says that God left Hezekiah, it does not mean that God abandoned Hezekiah forever. It also does not mean that God did not love Hezekiah anymore. God only left Hezekiah for a short time in order to test Hezekiah. The storyteller means that God did not intervene or stop Hezekiah from doing what Hezekiah wanted to do, because God wanted to know what Hezekiah's true character was like.

Stop here and discuss these questions: Tell a story about a time that a person in authority, such as a parent, teacher or boss, tested someone by allowing that person to do whatever the person wanted to do. How do you talk about this situation? What words or phrases can you use to describe when a parent, teacher, or boss could step in to help, but doesn't, because they want to see what the person will do on their own? Which of these could you use here? Pause this audio here.

In the fourth scene, the storyteller mentions a book or scroll that people called The Vision of the Prophet Isaiah, son of Amoz, and a book that people called the Book of the Kings of Judah and Israel. There are more stories about Hezekiah in these books. Unfortunately, these books do not exist anymore. And Bible scholars do not agree whether they were two separate books or scrolls, or if the Vision of the Prophet Isaiah was a smaller book inside the Book of the Kings of Judah and Israel. The Vision of the Prophet Isaiah is a different book from the Book of Isaiah in the Bible.

A vision is a kind of revelation from God. When a prophet has a vision, he sees something that other people do not see or hears something that other people do not hear.

The storyteller says that Hezekiah did things that showed that he was faithful to Yahweh and completely devoted to Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss these questions: what words do you have for actions that show that someone is faithful to another person? Which of these could you use here? Pause this audio here.

Hezekiah died and his people buried him in the upper part of the tombs where the people buried the descendants of David. The people of Judah gave Hezekiah special honor when Hezekiah died. The storyteller does not tell us exactly what this special honor was. Then Hezekiah's son Manasseh became the next King of Judah.

Now, the group should storyboard, draw, or use objects to visualize the passage and the action in it.

Embodying the Text
------------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 32:24–33 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will dramatize the passage.

This passage has four scenes.

The characters in this passage are:

* Hezekiah
* Yahweh
* The people of Judah and Jerusalem
* The Babylonian ambassadors
* And Manasseh, Hezekiah's son

As a group, act out the passage twice. You should act out the passage in your own language.

First, act out the passage without stopping. Pay attention to the dialogue, flow, plotline, and chronology of the passage. Make sure you do not skip acting out the difficult or important parts of the passage. Help each other remember every part of the passage.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage.

The group should act out this passage a second time. At certain points in the drama, pause the drama and ask the person playing the character, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person playing the character should answer what they would be feeling or thinking if they were that character. Then continue the drama.

Pause this audio here and act out the passage a second time.

The following is an example of the drama and possible responses.

About that time, Hezekiah became deathly ill. Hezekiah prayed, and Yahweh gave Hezekiah a miraculous sign to show Hezekiah that he would get better. But Hezekiah was proud and was not grateful for the kindness that Yahweh had shown to Hezekiah. So Yahweh's great anger came against Hezekiah and also against Judah and Jerusalem. But Hezekiah humbled himself and repented of his pride, and the people of Jerusalem did the same thing. Therefore, Yahweh was no longer angry and did not bring judgment on Judah at that time, and things were good for the rest of Hezekiah's reign.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I nearly died, but Yahweh saved me! I must be very special and valuable to this country. What would these people do without me? or
* Look at everything I have done for my people! Look at how Yahweh saved me so that I could keep helping my people. I am truly one of the greatest kings of Judah; or
* I feel awful that Yahweh is angry at our people because of my pride. I must humble myself and ask Yahweh for mercy, and the people should too! or
* I am grateful to Yahweh not only for saving me from illness, but also for saving me from my own foolish pride.

Ask the people playing the people of Judah and Jerusalem, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* What a miraculous sign that Yahweh gave our king! Yahweh is the only God who can do such great things! or
* We feel relieved that Yahweh saved our king. We need Hezekiah to protect us from the Assyrians; or
* Perhaps we have not shown Yahweh how thankful we are for Yahweh's help. We must humble ourselves before Yahweh; or
* We are grateful that Yahweh spared us from his wrath, in spite of our pride. Yahweh is a very merciful God.

Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* Poor Hezekiah is so afraid of dying. I will give Hezekiah a sign to show Hezekiah that he will not die from this illness; or
* I am pleased that Hezekiah prayed to me to heal his illness. That shows that Hezekiah knows I am his healer and the one who gives Hezekiah life; or
* I feel angry that Hezekiah is so proud and ungrateful for all I have done for him. Not only that, but the people are just as proud as Hezekiah is! or
* I am very happy that Hezekiah and the people have repented of their pride. Because the people have done this, I will not punish them at this time.

> Continue the drama.

Throughout Hezekiah's rule, Hezekiah was very wealthy, and the people highly honored Hezekiah. Hezekiah built special treasury buildings for his silver, gold, precious stones, spices, shields, and other valuable items. Hezekiah also built many storehouses for his grain, new wine, and oil. Hezekiah made many stalls for his cattle and pens for his flocks of sheep. Hezekiah built many towns and gathered many large herds of cattle and flocks of sheep because God had given Hezekiah great wealth.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel grateful that Yahweh has blessed our nation with so much material wealth; or
* I am pleased with how rich the nation has become! Maybe we are not quite as rich as we were during the days of Solomon, but we are doing very well! or
* All these possessions are wonderful, but I am nervous that someone might try to come and steal them all.

Ask the people playing the people of Judah and Jerusalem, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* We feel safe and secure knowing that our nation has everything we need and more; or
* We feel proud of our nation and our king. We are so glad that we live in Judah; or
* Yahweh has been very gracious to us since we humbled ourselves and repented of pride. We must remember to tell our children that this is the way to receive Yahweh's blessings.

Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel happy that my people have been humble and have received all my blessings with gratitude; or
* I am pleased with my servant Hezekiah. Hezekiah is taking very good care of the nation; or
* I love to provide for my people.

> Continue the drama.

It was Hezekiah who closed the upper outlet of the Gihon spring and brought the water by tunnel to the west side of the City of David. And Hezekiah succeeded in everything Hezekiah did. But one day, some ambassadors arrived from Babylon to ask Hezekiah about the miraculous sign that had occurred in the land. At that time, God let Hezekiah make his own choices in order to test Hezekiah's character.

Pause the drama. Ask the person playing Hezekiah, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* It worries me that our only year\-round source of water is outside the walls of Jerusalem. We need to find a way to get that water inside the city; or
* I am so pleased with my workers! My workers found a way to tunnel through rock to bring the waters of the Gihon spring into Jerusalem; or
* I am so proud that these messengers from Babylon have come to see how great our nation is.

Ask the people playing the people of Judah and Jerusalem, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* It is wonderful that our king dug a tunnel to bring water into the city! Now we do not have to wonder where we will get water from if an enemy attacks us; or
* Look! Even the Babylonians have heard about the miraculous sign that Yahweh did in our country. Now the Babylonians know what a great God Yahweh is; or
* Yahweh has done many great things for us while Hezekiah has been king. Surely our people will continue to be safe and rich!

Ask the person playing Yahweh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* I feel glad that Hezekiah made a tunnel to bring water inside the city for my people. This makes Jerusalem more secure; or
* Hezekiah has been wise in the ways that he has prepared Jerusalem to stand strong if there are problems; or
* Here come some Babylonian ambassadors. This will be a good time to test Hezekiah to see if Hezekiah's heart is truly wise and if he is thinking about what is best for the nation.

> Continue the drama.

The rest of the events in Hezekiah's reign and Hezekiah's acts of faithfulness to Yahweh are in "The Vision of the Prophet Isaiah son of Amoz," and in "The Book of the Kings of Judah and Israel." When Hezekiah died, the people buried Hezekiah in the upper part of the royal tombs. All of Judah and Jerusalem honored Hezekiah when he died. And Hezekiah's son Manasseh became the next king.

Pause the drama. Ask the people playing the people of Judah and Jerusalem, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* We feel sad that King Hezekiah has died. Hezekiah was such a good king; or
* Hezekiah was a good king. Surely Hezekiah's son Manasseh will also be good to us; or
* King Hezekiah built a strong and prosperous country for us. Perhaps things will be even better with Manasseh as our king!

Ask the person playing Manasseh, "What are you feeling or thinking?" The person might answer things like:

* My father did great things for this nation, and I hope that I will be even greater than my father; or
* Now it is my turn to rule and I get to do the things that I think are best; or
* I feel sad that my father has died, but happy that I have a chance to make our country even greater than it already is.

Filling the Gaps
----------------

Listen to an audio version of 2 Chronicles 32:24–33 in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this step, the group will discuss key terms and words in this passage.

In those days, Hezekiah became sick and almost died. Hezekiah **prayed** to **Yahweh**, and Yahweh spoke to Hezekiah and gave him a sign to show that Hezekiah would get well. But Hezekiah was not grateful for the kindness he had received, because Hezekiah's heart was so proud. Be sure to translate pray and Yahweh the same way you did in previous passages. See the Master Glossary for more information on pray and Yahweh.

Stop here and discuss as a group what word or phrase you will use for **sign**. Look up sign in the Master Glossary for more information. If you have already translated this word in another book of the Bible, use the same word that you have used there. Pause this audio here

Therefore, Yahweh's **wrath**, or strong anger, came against Hezekiah and on **Judah** and **Jerusalem**. However, Hezekiah humbled the pride in Hezekiah's heart, as did the people living in Jerusalem. And so the wrath of Yahweh did not come on the people during the days of Hezekiah. Be sure to translate wrath, Judah, and Jerusalem the same way you did in previous passages. See the Master Glossary for more information on wrath, Judah, and Jerusalem.

Then Hezekiah had great wealth and great honor. Hezekiah made treasuries for his silver, gold, precious stones, spices, shields, and other valuable items. Hezekiah built storehouses for grain, wine, and oil. And Hezekiah made stalls for all kinds of cattle and sheep pens for his flocks. Hezekiah built cities for himself and gained many flocks and herds because **God** had given Hezekiah great wealth. Be sure to translate God the same way you did in previous passages. See the Master Glossary for more information on wine and God.

Remember, in this scene, the storyteller uses a word for wine that probably means "new wine" or "unfermented wine."

Stop here and decide what word you will use for **wine.** Pause this audio here.

It was Hezekiah who stopped the upper outlet of the waters of the Gihon spring and directed the waters down to the west side of the City of **David**. And Hezekiah prospered in everything he did. But when it came to the matter of the ambassadors from **Babylon**, who came to Hezekiah to ask about the **miracle** that had happened in the land, God left Hezekiah alone to test him and see what was in Hezekiah's **heart**. Be sure to use the same words for **David**, **Babylon**, **miracle**, and **heart** that you used in previous stories. See the Master Glossary for more information on **David**, **miracle**, and **heart**. Remember, a miracle is similar to a sign.

As for the rest of the events in Hezekiah's reign and his deeds of faithful love, they are in the **Visions** of the **Prophet Isaiah** son of Amoz, and in the book, or **scroll**, of the **kings** of Judah and **Israel**. Hezekiah died, and the people buried Hezekiah in the upper part of the **tombs** of David's **descendants**. All of Judah and the inhabitants of Jerusalem honored Hezekiah when he died. And Hezekiah's son Manasseh became the next king. Be sure to use the same words for vision, prophet, Isaiah, scroll, king, Israel, tomb, and descendants that you used in previous stories. See the Master Glossary for more information on vision, prophet, scroll, king, Israel, tomb, and descendants.

Speaking the Word
-----------------

Listen to an audio version of the passage in the easiest\-to\-understand translation.

Pause this audio here.

In this session, retell the passage, as a group, in your own language.

First, the whole group should practice telling the passage in your own language. Remember to include the things you decided in previous steps, like chronology, words, phrases, emotions, or anything else you discussed.

One group member should tell a few sentences of the passage and pause. Another group member should tell the next part of the passage and pause. Do this until the group has retold the whole passage. The group members can correct each other in this step.

Pause this audio here.

Next, each member of the group should tell the whole passage without interruption at least one time. A group member can use random objects, motions, or their storyboard to help them remember the passage.

Pause this audio here.

Next, the group can decide whose version of the passage you like the most. You may decide that you liked the way one group member told one scene but you liked the way a different group member told another scene.

Pause this audio here.

Finally, the group can put together a final version of the passage that everyone agrees on. Each group member should practice telling this final version of the passage.

* **Associated Passages:** 2 Chronicles 32:24-33
